Author’s Preface and Methodological Introduction

Why Study Muslim Philosophy and Philosophers?

The history of philosophy in Muslim societies is often approached through a narrow selection of celebrated figures or through a simplified account of the reception of Greek thought. Such approaches can introduce important names and events, but they may leave the reader with an incomplete picture: the range of questions debated, the diversity of intellectual traditions, and the sustained philosophical activity that continued long after the classical period.

Muslim Philosophy and Philosophers is an independent intellectual project intended to offer a broad, accessible, and critically engaged introduction to this history. It explores the arguments, methods, contexts, and intellectual exchanges that shaped philosophical thought across the Muslim world. It does not treat the tradition as a single uniform school, nor does it assume that every thinker discussed belonged to the same religious, linguistic, or philosophical community. Rather, it examines a connected intellectual world in which Muslim thinkers, alongside Christian, Jewish, and other scholars, contributed to debates conducted across languages, regions, and generations.

The central concern of this work is philosophy as inquiry. The thinkers considered here addressed enduring questions about existence, knowledge, causation, the soul, reason, revelation, ethics, political life, and the nature of the natural world. Their answers were not always compatible. They disagreed with one another, revised inherited positions, developed new conceptual tools, and sometimes challenged the assumptions of the traditions from which they had begun. These disagreements are not obstacles to understanding the history; they are among its most important features.

Four Methodological Commitments

1. Intellectual breadth beyond a single school. The history presented here is not limited to the Peripatetic tradition associated with al-Kindī, al-Fārābī, Avicenna, and Ibn Rushd. It also considers kalām, Muʿtazilī and other theological debates, Illuminationist philosophy, philosophical Sufism, ethical and political thought, and later traditions of metaphysical inquiry. These fields had distinct aims and methods, but their discussions frequently intersected. Their relationships must therefore be studied rather than forced into artificial separation.

2. Serious attention to post-classical thought. The history of philosophy in Muslim societies did not end with Ibn Rushd. Later scholars continued to examine and transform questions inherited from earlier philosophers and theologians. The post-classical traditions of verification (taḥqīq), commentary, logic, metaphysics, and philosophical theology deserve attention in their own right. This work therefore follows developments across later Iranian, Central Asian, Ottoman, and South Asian intellectual settings, while recognising that the history of each region requires careful study and cannot be reduced to a single continuous school.

3. A geographically and linguistically diverse account. Philosophical activity took place across a wide area, from the eastern Mediterranean and North Africa to Iran, Central Asia, Anatolia, and South Asia. Arabic was a major language of scholarship, but Persian and other languages also played important roles. The movement of texts, teachers, students, and arguments connected different intellectual centres, while local circumstances gave philosophical debates distinctive forms. A history that concentrates on a few famous cities or language traditions cannot adequately represent this diversity.

4. Engagement with arguments and primary sources. Philosophical history should not be reduced to biographies, labels, or summaries of conclusions. It requires attention to what thinkers actually argued, the premises they used, the distinctions they drew, and the objections they faced. Primary texts are therefore essential. Translations and scholarly studies can help readers approach these works, but they should not be confused with the original texts themselves. Where translated excerpts and further-reading suggestions are provided, they are intended as starting points for closer examination, not substitutes for independent study.

Reason, Revelation, and Intellectual Disagreement

One recurring theme in this history is the relationship between reason and revelation. Philosophers, theologians, jurists, and mystics approached this relationship in different ways. Some sought to demonstrate the compatibility of philosophical inquiry and religious belief; others questioned particular philosophical doctrines or the limits of human reasoning. Still others developed approaches in which demonstration, interpretation, and spiritual experience were understood as complementary or competing paths to knowledge.

This book does not assume that these debates produced one final consensus. Nor does it judge a thinker’s philosophical significance solely by whether later scholars accepted that thinker’s conclusions. The aim is to understand positions in their historical and conceptual settings, to reconstruct their arguments as fairly as possible, and to distinguish a thinker’s own claims from later interpretations of them.

Terminology, Transliteration, and Dates

The expressions “Islamic philosophy,” “Muslim philosophy,” and “philosophy in the Islamic world” are not perfectly interchangeable. “Islamic philosophy” can refer to a broad intellectual tradition shaped by Islamic civilisation and its debates, while “Muslim philosophers” ordinarily refers to philosophers who were themselves Muslims. The intellectual world under discussion also included non-Muslim scholars who worked in the same languages, institutions, and networks. The terminology used in this manuscript should therefore be understood in its historical context rather than as a claim that every contributor shared the same religious identity or philosophical commitments.

Arabic and Persian names and technical terms are generally presented with scholarly transliteration, including diacritical marks where appropriate. Familiar English forms, such as Avicenna and Averroes, may appear alongside the corresponding Arabic names. Dates are generally given in the Common Era (CE); Hijrī (AH) dates may be included where they help clarify historical context. Transliteration and naming conventions will be reviewed for consistency as the manuscript develops.

How to Read This Work

This manuscript is arranged broadly in chronological and thematic sequence, but its subjects are connected by recurring problems. Readers may follow the historical development from early debates and the translation movement through the Peripatetic tradition, kalām, Illuminationism, philosophical Sufism, later metaphysics, and modern engagements. Alternatively, they may trace a particular question—such as the nature of existence, causality, the intellect, prophecy, or ethical life—across several periods.

Readers are encouraged to approach each argument actively. Ask what assumptions a philosopher begins with, whether the conclusion follows from those assumptions, what objections might be raised, and how another thinker might respond. Where the manuscript offers primary-source excerpts, discussion questions, glossaries, or further-reading references, use them as aids to that process. The purpose is not simply to memorise names and doctrines, but to develop the ability to read, compare, question, and evaluate philosophical arguments.

Muslim Philosophy and Philosophers remains a work in progress. Its interpretations, references, transliterations, and historical details require continuing scrutiny against primary texts and reliable scholarship. The manuscript is offered as an invitation to study a rich and diverse intellectual history with curiosity, intellectual honesty, and a willingness to revise one’s understanding in the light of evidence.

Part I: Foundations and Early Debates

Chapter 1What is Islamic Philosophy?

A Necessary Question

A book carrying the title Muslim Philosophy and Philosophers must, before it says anything else, ask what those words mean. The question is not pedantic; it shapes every choice that follows. Does "Islamic philosophy" refer to philosophy produced by Muslims, or philosophy that is Islamic in character? Is it a geographical designation, a

linguistic one, or a religious one? Do we mean the tradition called falsafa, or do we include kalām (dialectical theology), ḥikma (integrative wisdom), uṣūl al-fiqh (legal hermeneutics and philosophy of language), and al-ʿirfān al-naẓarī (theoretical gnosis) alongside philosophical poetry? While the terminological debate between "Islamic philosophy" and "Arabic philosophy" is a modern nineteenth- and twentieth-century historiographical controversy, the underlying boundary questions — how the "sciences of the ancients" (ʿulūm al-awāʾil) relate to the Islamic sciences and to prophetic revelation — were already being debated in tenth-century Baghdad when al-Fārābī mapped the hierarchy of demonstration, dialectic, and law, and they remain alive today when thinkers like Seyyed Hossein Nasr and Mohammed Arkoun interpret the legacy in sharply different ways. This chapter lays the conceptual groundwork. It defines the three most important terms — falsafa, ḥikma, and kalām — and situates them in relationship to one another. It examines the Qur'anic impetus for rational reflection. It maps the overlapping territories of philosophy and theology, and it confronts directly the problem of naming: should we speak of "Islamic philosophy," "Arabic philosophy," "philosophy in the Islamic world," or something else? By the end of this chapter, you will have a clear sense of what this book includes, and why.

1. The Qur'anic Stimulus to Reason Long before the translations of Aristotle and Plotinus reached Baghdad, the foundational text of Islam was issuing a sustained call to think. The Qur'an does not present itself as an irrational mystery to be accepted blindly. It repeatedly appeals to observation, reflection, and argument. In perhaps the most emblematic passage, it asks: "Do they not ponder (afalā yatadabbarūna) the Qur'an? Had it been from other than God, they would surely have found therein much discrepancy (ikhtilāfan kathīran)" (Q 4:82). Rather than empirical falsifiability in the modern Popperian sense, the verse advances a classical conditional syllogism (what Muslim logicians call qiyās istithnāʾī, corresponding to modus tollens) grounded in the rational test of internal coherence (ʿadam al-ikhtilāf) and deep meditative scrutiny (tadabbur): if a text of this scale originated from finite, changing human minds, it would exhibit internal contradiction; since contemplative examination finds coherence rather than contradiction, its source transcends contingent authorship. Even where the Qur'an demands wholehearted existential submission (islām) to God, it grounds the recognition of divine truth in rational scrutiny rather than blind imitation (taqlīd). The rhetorical interrogatives afalā taʿqilūn ("do you not use your intellect?") and afalā tatafakkarūn ("do you not reflect?"), alongside the declarative refrain li-qawmin yatafakkarūn / yaʿqilūn ("for a people who reflect / understand"), echo through dozens of verses. Significantly, the root ʿ-q-l occurs forty-nine times in the Qur'an exclusively in verbal forms (such as taʿqilūn, yaʿqilūn, and naʿqilu) rather than as the abstract noun al-ʿaql — presenting rationality in scripture as a dynamic moral and cognitive activity of discerning signs (āyāt) and binding one's conduct from folly, before the noun al-ʿaql was reified by the translators and philosophers into a cosmic or psychological substance (rendering Greek nous). Similarly, the famous report much quoted by later Muslim Neoplatonists, "The first thing God created was the intellect (awwalu mā khalaqa Allāhu al-ʿaql)," originated in eighth-century ascetic and theological circles (such as Dāwūd ibn al-Muḥabbar's Kitāb al-ʿAql) and was graded weak or fabricated (mawḍūʿ) by classical hadith critics (including Ibn Ḥajar and al-Suyūṭī), yet its adoption by the falāsifa and Ismāʿīlī thinkers allowed them to naturalize Plotinus's First Intellect within Islamic cosmology. This Qur'anic disposition created a cultural soil in which philosophical inquiry could thrive. When Greek, Persian, and Indian texts arrived in the eighth and ninth centuries, they did not fall into a vacuum. They entered a civilisation already convinced that God's creation was orderly, intelligible, and deserving of systematic study. The task of the philosopher would be to integrate the fruits of demonstrative reason with the data of scripture — a task that has never been completed without tension, but one that the text of the Qur'an itself seemed to authorise.

2. Three Key Terms: Falsafa, Ḥikma, Kalām Any serious study of Muslim philosophy must learn to navigate three Arabic words. Translating them simply as "philosophy," "wisdom," and "theology" captures something of their meaning but obscures the ways they jostled, overlapped, and distinguished themselves historically.

Falsafa The word falsafa is a direct Arabicisation of the Greek philosophia. Its practitioners, the falāsifa (singular faylasūf), were self-consciously heirs to the Hellenistic curriculum. They studied logic, natural philosophy, metaphysics, ethics, and politics as the ancients had organised them. Al-Kindī, the first major Muslim philosopher in this mould, adapted the six canonical Alexandrian definitions of philosophy in his treatise On the Definitions and Descriptions of Things (Risāla fī Ḥudūd al-Ashyāʾ wa-Rusūmihā): among them, philosophy from the standpoint of its object is "the knowledge of eternal, universal things, their existence, their quiddities, and their causes, insofar as lies within human capacity," and from the standpoint of its ultimate ethical and spiritual telos — echoing Plato's Theaetetus (176b) — it is "assimilation to the actions of God, may He be exalted, insofar as lies within human capacity" (altashabbuh bi-afʿāl Allāh taʿālā bi-qadr ṭāqat al-insān). Its purpose was human perfection in both theoretical truth and practical virtue. For al-Fārābī, philosophy was the pursuit of certain, demonstrative truth wherever it was found, and its highest branch was metaphysics, the study of being as being. Falsafa was, from the beginning, an elite enterprise. Its texts were studied in royal courts and among circles of scholars rather than in mosque study-halls. It was often viewed with suspicion by jurists and theologians who saw it as a foreign import. Yet the falāsifa themselves argued vigorously that philosophy was not foreign but universal. In the celebrated prologue to On First Philosophy (Fī al-Falsafa al-Ūlā), al-Kindī insisted that "we ought not to be ashamed of appreciating the truth and acquiring it from wherever it comes, even if it comes from distant races and nations different from us" — a cosmopolitan philosophical manifesto that later Muslim writers frequently paired with the famous maxim attributed in the hadith literature (Tirmidhī 2687) and in Nahj al-Balāgha (Saying 80, attributed to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib) that "the word of wisdom is the lost camel (ḍālla) of the believer; wherever he finds it, he has the greatest right to it." For al-Kindī, the ancient Greeks were earnest predecessors in the cumulative human quest for the very truth that prophetic revelation had now confirmed.

Ḥikma The Qur'an uses the word ḥikma repeatedly and positively: "He gives wisdom to whom He wills, and whoever has been given wisdom has certainly been given much good" (Q 2:269). Ḥikma is wisdom in the deepest sense — an insight that combines knowledge, right action, and spiritual perception. The term never carried the foreign taint that clung to falsafa. Because it was a Qur'anic word, it could be claimed by mystics, ethicists, and philosophers alike. Over time, ḥikma became the preferred label for a certain kind of integrative philosophy that refused to separate discursive reasoning from spiritual experience. The high point of this usage came with the School of Illumination founded by Shihāb al-Dīn Suhrawardī, whose Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq presented philosophy as the fruit of both logical demonstration and mystical vision. Later, Mullā Ṣadrā's monumental system was called al-Ḥikma al-Mutaʿāliya — Transcendent Wisdom — signalling that it offered a path beyond the plain Peripateticism of the early falāsifa. In modern Iran, philosophy is still taught as ḥikma, a living tradition in which the philosopher is as much sage as analyst.

At the same time, the boundary between falsafa and ḥikma was historically fluid rather than a rigid dichotomy: Avicenna himself titled his concise Peripatetic manuals ʿUyūn al-Ḥikma (Fountains of Wisdom) and al-Ḥikma alʿArūḍiyya, using ḥikma as a direct synonym for demonstrative philosophy, before post-Avicennan thinkers in the Illuminationist and Ṣadrian traditions increasingly reserved ḥikma for an integrative wisdom uniting burhān (logical demonstration), ʿirfān (spiritual unveiling), and qurʾān (revealed scripture). This book will respect both usages, and Part V in particular will show how the later wisdom tradition absorbed and transformed the Peripatetic inheritance.

Kalām Kalām literally means "speech" or "discourse." It came to designate dialectical theology — the rational defence and elaboration of Islamic doctrine. The earliest practitioners were the Muʿtazila, who appeared in the eighth century and built a systematic theology using tools of argumentation that were, in part, shaped by encounters with Christian and Manichaean disputants. Kalām focused on questions such as God's unity and justice, free will, the status of the grave sinner, and the createdness of the Qur'an. Is kalām philosophy? The relationship has been hotly contested. The falāsifa generally looked down on kalām as mere apologetics: its practitioners began from revealed premises rather than purely rational ones, and their dialectical methods fell short of demonstrative certainty. In the fifth chapter of his Enumeration of the Sciences (Iḥṣāʾ al-ʿUlūm), al-Fārābī defined the art of kalām as a dialectical habitus (malaka) by which a scholar defends the specific doctrines and actions stipulated by the founder of a religious community (milla) and refutes anyone who seeks to subvert them by argument — ranking it below universal demonstrative philosophy (burhān) because its premises are taken from a particular revealed law rather than primary universals. Al-Ghazālī, himself a master of Ashʿarī kalām, similarly compared dialectical theology to a defensive guard protecting the faith of the community along the pilgrimage road and to medicine tailored for those afflicted by doctrinal doubts, while recognizing that after Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī and Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī, post-classical kalām and falsafa merged into a unified discipline of rational verification (taḥqīq). Alongside falsafa, ḥikma, and kalām, two further indigenous disciplines contributed decisively to Islamic philosophical life: uṣūl al-fiqh (legal theory), which developed one of the premodern world's most sophisticated philosophies of language, deontic logic, and epistemic probability, and alʿirfān al-naẓarī (theoretical gnosis), which articulated a rigorous mystical ontology of Being and manifestation. And yet, kalām is impossible to exclude from a comprehensive history of Muslim philosophy. Its major thinkers — ʿAbd al-Jabbār, al-Bāqillānī, al-Juwaynī, Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī — developed sophisticated theories of causality, atomism, language, and ethics. Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī's commentaries on Avicenna are so philosophically rich that later thinkers often studied philosophy through his lens. The boundary between kalām and falsafa became increasingly porous, especially after al-Ghazālī's critique forced the falāsifa to address theological concerns and the theologians to assimilate philosophical logic. By the post-classical period, it is often difficult to say whether a text belongs to one tradition or the other. This book therefore includes kalām not as a separate, inferior genre, but as a genuine and original mode of rational inquiry that shaped and was shaped by falsafa.

3. Philosophy and Theology: Distinctions and Overlaps Even with these definitions in hand, the border between philosophy and theology in Islam is a moving line. One way to draw it is by subject matter: philosophy investigates being, knowledge, causality, and the soul through universal reason; theology starts from revelation and defends its propositions with rational argument. This works in broad strokes but quickly crumbles before the reality of texts. Avicenna's Metaphysics is a philosophical masterpiece, yet its final chapters are devoted to prophecy, religious ritual, and prayer — topics that belong

squarely to theology in any conventional division. Al-Ghazālī's Moderation in Belief is a kalām manual, yet its preface makes a sustained philosophical case for the desirability of doubt. A more fruitful approach is to see philosophy and theology as two overlapping rationalities, distinguished more by their epistemic starting points than by their conclusions. Philosophy began from the Greek sciences and moved toward the God of the philosophers. Theology began from the God of revelation and moved outward to construct a coherent metaphysics. Where they met — and they met constantly — produced the most creative moments in the tradition. The Muʿtazila's insistence on divine justice forced every later philosopher to grapple with the problem of evil. Avicenna's proof for the Necessary Existent compelled theologians to sharpen their own arguments for God's existence. Ibn Rushd's defence of philosophy as a religious obligation redefined the very terms of the debate. This book will not enforce a rigid separation. It will, instead, treat the interaction between philosophy and theology as one of the central stories of the tradition. When we examine al-Ghazālī, we will be reading a theologian who absorbed philosophy to its marrow. When we examine Mullā Ṣadrā, we will be reading a philosopher who treats Qur'anic exegesis as an integral part of his metaphysics. The conversation is the point.

4. The Problem of Periodisation Just as the borders between disciplines are fluid, the temporal borders of "Muslim philosophy" are contested. Does it begin with the first translation of Greek texts into Arabic? Or with the Qur'an itself? Does it end with Ibn Rushd, as some nineteenth-century European historians claimed, or does it continue through the Safavid madrasas and into the present day? The old nineteenth- and early twentieth-century Orientalist narrative — popularized by Ernest Renan in Averroès et l'averroïsme (1852) and codified by T. J. de Boer in The History of Philosophy in Islam (1901) — held that Islamic philosophy was essentially a passive transmission belt: the Arabs borrowed Greek learning, preserved it, commented on it, and handed it back to Latin Europe before succumbing after al-Ghazālī (d. 1111) and Averroes (d. 1198) to mystical irrationalism. That narrative has been thoroughly dismantled by modern scholarship, which has revealed the astonishing philosophical creativity of the post-Avicennan period and the living traditions of Iran, India, and the Ottoman Empire. It is now clear that philosophy did not die with Averroes. It was reborn in the Illuminationism of Suhrawardī, the Transcendent Philosophy of Mullā Ṣadrā, the comprehensive synthesis of Shāh Walī Allāh, and the modern engagements of Iqbāl, Soroush, and many others. This book therefore adopts a maximalist approach to periodisation. It begins with the intellectual soil of the Qur'an and the early Muslim community. It marches through the translation movement, the Peripatetic synthesis, the great critiques, and the subsequent mystical and Illuminationist turns. It devotes substantial space to the later Shīʿī philosophical renaissance, to the Ottoman philosophical tradition, and to the South Asian synthesis. And it ends with a survey of modern and contemporary voices, because the tradition is still being written. Muslim philosophy is not an antique object in a museum case. It is a living conversation.

5. "Islamic" or "Arabic"? The Naming Debate Perhaps the most vexed modern question is simply what to call this tradition. The phrase "Islamic philosophy" suggests a philosophy determined by the religion of Islam, an enterprise whose theorems are constrained by dogma. Some thinkers reject it for that very reason. The Egyptian scholar Fu'ād Zakarīyā, for instance, argued that philosophy is by definition free inquiry, and "Islamic philosophy" is a contradiction in terms — there is only philosophy, and its practitioners happen to be Muslims. Others object to the religious label on historical grounds:

the most famous philosophers in Arabic, they note, included Christians (such as Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī), Jews (such as Maimonides, who wrote in Arabic), and even outright free-thinkers (such as the physician-philosopher al-Rāzī). An alternative, "Arabic philosophy," emphasises the linguistic vehicle. Most major philosophical texts of the classical period were composed in Arabic, even when their authors were Persian, Central Asian, or Andalusian. This label has the advantage of including non-Muslims and highlighting the unifying role of the Arabic language as a medium of intellectual exchange. Its disadvantage is that it obscures the substantial Persian and, later, Turkish traditions of philosophical writing. Avicenna's Dānishnāma-yi ʿAlāʾī is Persian philosophical prose at its finest; Nāṣir-i Khusraw wrote all his philosophical works in Persian; Mullā Ṣadrā wrote primarily in Arabic but also in Persian; Iqbāl wrote in Persian, Urdu, and English. To call all of this "Arabic philosophy" is misleading — and while Marshall Hodgson proposed "Islamicate" for the wider cultural matrix and Shahab Ahmed (What Is Islam?, 2016) later critiqued Hodgson by arguing that philosophical exploration along the "Balkans-to-Bengal complex" is directly Islamic in meaning-making, "Islamic philosophy" remains the most inclusive and accurate term when understood in this civilizational and conceptual sense. Other formulations have been proposed: "philosophy in the Islamic world," "philosophy in the lands of Islam," "Islamicate philosophy" (a term borrowed from Marshall Hodgson, who used "Islamicate" to refer to the broader cultural contexts arising under Muslim rule without requiring personal faith). These are accurate but cumbersome. They also run the risk of sounding as though the religious and intellectual commitments of the thinkers were incidental to their philosophy, which they manifestly were not. Avicenna's Metaphysics would look very different without the Islamic context of revelation and prophecy. Ibn ʿArabī's entire system would be inconceivable without the Qur'an. This book does not enforce a single terminological orthodoxy. I shall use "Islamic philosophy" to refer to the tradition as a whole, while acknowledging that it includes non-Muslim participants and that its "Islamic" character is a matter of context, culture, and creative tension, not of dogmatic closure. When I speak of "Muslim philosophers," I mean the philosophers who were Muslims, and they are the primary subjects of this book. But I will not exclude their Christian, Jewish, and other colleagues who were integral to the same intellectual world. The aim is not to police boundaries but to understand a tradition in its full richness — a tradition that is both deeply Islamic and genuinely philosophical, both rooted in revelation and committed to reason.

6. The Architecture of This Book This chapter has tried to establish that Muslim philosophy is not a single, stable object but a field of debates — about reason and revelation, about method and authority, about the very name of the endeavour. The chapters that follow will trace these debates historically and thematically, giving each major figure and school room to speak in its own voice while drawing out the connecting threads. We shall begin in Part I with the textual and theological foundations: the translation movement that brought Greek learning into Arabic, and the Muʿtazilī rationalists who forged the first systematic Islamic theology. Part II follows the great Peripatetic synthesis and its tenth-century rivals across seven chapters: from al-Kindī (Chapter 4) and alFārābī (Chapter 5) to the empiricist freethinker Abū Bakr al-Rāzī, the Christian-Muslim Baghdad School of Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī and al-Sijistānī, and the Khurāsānian synthesis of al-ʿĀmirī (Chapter 6), the Brethren of Purity (Chapter 7), the ethical humanism of Ibn Miskawayh (Chapter 8), and the towering summit of Avicenna's metaphysics, psychology, logic, and Eastern Wisdom (Chapters 9 and 10). Part III (Chapter 11) examines al-Bīrūnī's comparative methodology and philosophy of science. Part IV (Chapters 12–16) reconstructs the full arc of classical Sunnī kalām (Ashʿarī and Māturīdī metaphysics in Chapter 12), al-Ghazālī's critique (Chapter 13), Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī's method

of verification, post-Rāzian kalām, and Ibn Taymiyya's nominalist critique (Chapter 14), the Andalusian vision of Ibn Bājja and Ibn Ṭufayl (Chapter 15), and Ibn Rushd's defense of demonstrative philosophy (Chapter 16). Part V (Chapters 17–19) explores Suhrawardī's School of Illumination, Ibn ʿArabī and the systematic Akbarian school (alQūnawī, al-Qayṣarī, al-Jīlī, and Jāmī), and Rūmī's philosophical poetry, while Part VI (Chapter 20) turns to Ibn Khaldūn's new science of human civilization (ʿilm al-ʿumrān). Part VII (Chapters 21–24) moves into the post-classical eastern renaissance: Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī and the Maragha revival (Chapter 21), Mīr Dāmād and the School of Isfahan (Chapter 22), Mullā Ṣadrā's Transcendent Philosophy (Chapter 23), and Post-Ṣadrian and Ottoman philosophy from Mullā Fanārī and Tashköprüzāde to Sabzavārī and ʿAllāma Ṭabāṭabāʾī (Chapter 24). Part VIII (Chapter 25) examines early modern South Asian metaphysics across Shāh Walī Allāh al-Dihlawī, Shaykh Aḥmad Sirhindī, and the rationalist maʿqūlāt curriculum of Mullā Maḥmūd Jaunpūrī and Muḥibb Allāh al-Bihārī. Finally, Part IX (Chapters 26–30) brings the story through nineteenthcentury reform (Chapter 26) and Muḥammad Iqbāl (Chapter 27) to the twentieth- and twenty-first-century revival in the seminary and academy — Nasr, Muḥammad Bāqir al-Ṣadr, Muṭahharī, al-Attas, and al-Fārūqī (Chapter 28) — and to contemporary hermeneutics, feminist thought, Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān's trusteeship ethics, analytic Islamic philosophy (Chapter 29), and living ethical and political philosophy (Chapter 30). The structure is chronological in its broad sweep, but it is also designed to highlight recurring philosophical problems: God and being, causality and freedom, soul and intellect, ethics and politics, knowledge and illumination. Readers who wish to follow a single theme across periods will find signposts throughout.

A Note on Reading Philosophy Before we step into the history, a word of practical advice. Philosophy is not a collection of results to be memorised; it is an activity of thinking along with the thinker. The Muslim philosophers are not museum pieces to be admired from a distance; they are interlocutors. When Avicenna argues that existence (wujūd) is extrinsic and superadded (ʿāriḍ / zāʾid) to contingent quiddity (māhiyya) — while carefully insisting in al-Taʿlīqāt that it is not an ordinary categorical accident inhering in a pre-existing substance — he wants you to object, to test the claim, and to trace its consequences. When al-Ghazālī satirises the philosophers' inability to prove God's attributes, he is inviting a response. Reading philosophy requires patience; the arguments are dense, the terminology can be alien, and the cultural assumptions are not always our own. But the reward is genuine intellectual engagement with minds of the first order, minds that shaped the history of human thought far beyond the Islamic world. I encourage you to read the primary texts wherever possible. This book will guide you through them, explaining the contexts and clarifying the arguments, but no secondary source can replace the experience of opening Avicenna's Remarks and Admonitions or Ibn Ṭufayl's Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān and encountering the philosopher's own voice. Philosophy lives in the first person. With these clarifications in place, we are ready to begin the story proper. The next chapter will examine the translation movement that brought Aristotle and his Alexandrian commentators into the Arabic-speaking world — the single most consequential event in the prehistory of Muslim philosophy, and a tale of scholars, patrons, and books that transformed a civilisation.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “Surely in the creation of the heavens and the earth, and the alternation of night and day, there are signs (āyāt) for those who possess intellect (ūlī al-albāb) — those who remember God standing, sitting, and on their sides,

and reflect (yatafakkarūna) upon the creation of the heavens and the earth: 'Our Lord, You have not created this in vain!'” — Qurʾān 3:190–191

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How do the three classical Arabic terms falsafa, ḥikma, and kalām differ in their starting premises, methods of argumentation, and conceptions of the relationship between human reason and prophetic revelation? 2. Why did nineteenth- and early twentieth-century European historians of philosophy construct the narrative that Islamic philosophy 'died' after Ibn Rushd (d. 1198), and what historical evidence from the post-classical madrasa tradition refutes that thesis? 3. Compare the methodological merits and limitations of the labels 'Islamic philosophy,' 'Arabic philosophy,' and Marshall Hodgson's 'Islamicate philosophy.' How should a historian classify non-Muslim thinkers such as Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq, Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī, and Maimonides? 4. In what ways did the Qur'anic vocabulary of cosmic signs (āyāt), intellect (ʿaql), and wisdom (ḥikma) prepare the conceptual ground for the reception of ancient Greek, Persian, and Indian sciences?

Chapter 2The Translation Movement and the Graeco-Arabic Heritage

A Civilisation in Search of Knowledge

In the middle of the eighth century, a new city was rising on the banks of the Tigris. Baghdad, founded in 762 by the ʿAbbāsid caliph al-Manṣūr, was designed to be the capital of an empire stretching from the Atlantic to Central Asia. But more than palaces and barracks, it was conceived as a centre of learning — a place where the intellectual wealth of earlier civilisations would be gathered, translated, mastered, and put to new uses. Within a century, it had become the stage for one of the most ambitious translation movements in human history. Greek philosophy and science, Persian wisdom literature, Indian mathematics and medicine, and elements of Syriac and Coptic scholarship were systematically rendered into Arabic. The consequences have shaped world philosophy ever since. The translation movement was not an accident or a whim of a single ruler. It was the result of converging forces: the material resources and cosmopolitan ambitions of the ʿAbbāsid state, the presence of educated bilingual communities (particularly Syriac-speaking Christians), the demand for technical expertise in medicine, astrology, and administrative science, and a theological and cultural confidence that allowed Muslims to seek truth from non-

Muslim sources without feeling threatened. Al-Kindī, whom we shall meet properly in Chapter 4, would capture the mood in a famous saying: "We should not be ashamed to acknowledge truth and to assimilate it from whatever source it comes to us, even if it is brought to us by earlier generations and foreign peoples. For the seeker of truth nothing takes precedence over truth." This chapter tells the story of that assimilation: the patrons, the translators, the texts they found and sometimes misidentified, and the philosophical landscape they built for al-Fārābī, Avicenna, and all who came after. To read Muslim philosophy without understanding the translation movement is like reading Aquinas without knowing about Aristotle's rediscovery. The books made the philosophy possible.

1. Baghdad and the Bayt al-Ḥikma The institution most closely associated with the translation movement is the Bayt al-Ḥikma, the House of Wisdom. Its name has acquired a legendary aura, and in popular imagination it is often pictured as an academy on the Athenian model — a university with salaried scholars, formal research programmes, and systematic teaching. The historical reality was both more modest and more interesting. The Bayt al-Ḥikma (or Khizānat al-Ḥikma, "Treasury of Wisdom") originated under Hārūn al-Rashīd and was expanded under his son al-Maʾmūn (reigned 813–833). As modern historical scholarship (notably Dimitri Gutas) has demonstrated against older romanticized legends, the Bayt al-Ḥikma was not a modern-style university or a centralized academy built specifically for Graeco-Arabic translation, nor was it an astronomical observatory (alMaʾmūn's famous observatories were established separately at al-Shammāsiyya in Baghdad and on Mount Qāsiyūn outside Damascus). Rather, modeled on Sasanian royal palace libraries, it functioned primarily as a caliphal library, bureau for Pahlavi-to-Arabic historical and courtly translations, and center for mathematical and astrological research, while the bulk of Graeco-Arabic philosophical and medical translation was funded by a broad network of caliphs, viziers, courtiers, and scholar-patrons (such as the Banū Mūsā and Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa's documented patrons) across Baghdad. Most importantly, it was a signal of caliphal patronage. To work there was to have the state's backing. Scholars were paid, manuscripts were sought out and purchased at great expense, and translation was treated as a prestigious and sometimes lucrative activity. Al-Maʾmūn is said to have sent a delegation to Constantinople to acquire Greek manuscripts, a story that captures the deliberate, state-sponsored nature of the enterprise. Yet the Bayt al-Ḥikma was far from the whole story. Translation also took place in private households, in monasteries, in the homes of wealthy patrons, and at the courts of provincial governors. The physician's family of the Bukhtīshūʿ, the astronomer's circle around Thābit ibn Qurra, the vast network of Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq — all operated with significant independence. The House of Wisdom provided legitimacy and funding, but the translation movement was a broad cultural phenomenon, not the project of a single building. What drove it? Practical needs came first. The ʿAbbāsid court required astrologers who could cast horoscopes and astronomers who could compute prayer times and the lunar calendar; these sciences were far more advanced in Greek, Persian, and Indian texts than in Arabic. Physicians trained in the Galenic tradition were in demand to treat the caliph and his household. Administrators versed in Persian statecraft and Greek logic were needed to manage an empire of astonishing diversity. But alongside the practical motives, there was genuine intellectual curiosity. The caliphs of the early ninth century, al-Maʾmūn above all, saw themselves as patrons of wisdom in the grand style of the Sasanian kings and the Hellenistic monarchs. Knowledge was power, but it was also glory, a marker of cultural supremacy.

2. The Syriac Intermediaries The translators did not generally work directly from Greek into Arabic. Between the two languages lay a crucial intermediary: Syriac, a dialect of Aramaic that had been the intellectual and liturgical language of Eastern Christians for centuries. Long before the rise of Islam, Syriac-speaking scholars had undertaken their own translation efforts, rendering Greek philosophical, medical, and theological texts into their own tongue. Figures like Sergius of Reshaina (died 536) had translated Galen and Aristotle into Syriac, and the school of the monastery of Qenneshre on the Euphrates was a major centre of Greek-Syriac learning. The tradition of Aristotelian logic in Syriac was particularly strong; it was studied as a propaedeutic for theological disputation. When the ʿAbbāsid translators began their work, they found a ready-made lexicon of philosophical terms already developed in Syriac. Many of the Arabic philosophical terms that became standard — words for substance, accident, essence, existence, category, syllogism — were calqued from Syriac precedents. The translators themselves were overwhelmingly Syriac-speaking Christians: Nestorians and Jacobites whose communities had preserved Greek learning for centuries and who now found employment and prestige in the Muslim courts. They were not merely mechanical transmitters. They were scholars in their own right, who made interpretive choices that shaped how Greek philosophy was understood in Arabic. The most famous of them was Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq.

3. Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq: The Prince of Translators Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq (809–873) was an Arab Christian of the Nestorian church, born in al-Ḥīra in southern Iraq. He studied medicine in Baghdad under the eminent physician Yuḥannā ibn Māsawayh and then travelled, according to his own account, to Greek-speaking lands in search of manuscripts and philological expertise. By the time he returned to Baghdad, he was the foremost medical translator of his generation, and his influence extended far beyond medicine into philosophy and science. Ḥunayn's method was exacting. He collated multiple Greek manuscripts to establish the best text. He did not translate sentence by sentence in a slavish way; he aimed for a flowing, idiomatic Arabic that conveyed the sense of the original without distortion. The results are remarkable: his Arabic version of Galen's On the Usefulness of the Parts reads like an original Arabic composition, yet it preserves the medical detail with precision. He compiled glossaries, corrected earlier translations, and established a school of translators including his son Isḥāq ibn Ḥunayn and his nephew Ḥubaysh. Together they produced a corpus of translated works that became the standard Arabic Galen and covered much of Aristotle. His son Isḥāq ibn Ḥunayn (c. 830–910) became an even more important translator of philosophy. It was Isḥāq who translated Aristotle's Categories, On Interpretation, Physics, On the Soul (De Anima), Nicomachean Ethics, and Book Alpha Elatton (α) of the Metaphysics into crystalline Arabic — complementing the earlier translation of the main body of Aristotle's Metaphysics (Books Beta through Nu) executed by Usṭāth (Eustathius) for al-Kindī, as well as translations of Book Lambda (Λ) by Abū Bishr Mattā and Isḥāq himself. Isḥāq's translations are technically precise and philosophically sophisticated; Avicenna and Ibn Rushd encountered Aristotle's psychology, physics, and ethics largely through Isḥāq's versions alongside the Greek commentaries translated in his circle. The father-son partnership ensured that the most advanced medical and philosophical knowledge of antiquity was available in Arabic within two generations.

4. Thābit ibn Qurra and the Sabian Contribution The other towering figure of the early translation movement was Thābit ibn Qurra (c. 836–901), a Sabian from Ḥarrān in northern Mesopotamia. The Sabians were a religious community who traced their roots to the ancient paganism of the Near East, with a strong focus on star worship; they had preserved Greek mathematical and astronomical texts with particular devotion. Ḥarrān was a centre of Greek learning long after it had faded elsewhere, and Thābit grew up bilingual in Syriac and Arabic, literate in Greek, and deeply trained in geometry, astronomy, and Platonic philosophy. Thābit translated works by Archimedes, Apollonius of Perga, Euclid, Ptolemy, and many others. His translations were so competent and so highly regarded that some Greek originals lost today survive only through his Arabic. But Thābit was far more than a translator. He was a mathematician of the first rank — he developed a theory of amicable numbers, extended the Pythagorean theorem, and wrote on infinitesimals — and an astronomer who composed influential handbooks. He also wrote philosophical works in Arabic: treatises on the soul, on the nature of sleep and dreams, on ethics. His family, including his son Sinān and grandsons Ibrāhīm and Thābit, continued the tradition into the tenth century, forming a dynasty of Sabian scholar-scientists who contributed mightily to the intellectual life of Baghdad. Thābit's presence reminds us that the translation movement was not a Muslim monopoly. It was a multicultural, multi-religious enterprise in which Christians, Sabians, and a smaller number of Jews and Zoroastrians played indispensable roles. The cosmopolitanism of ʿAbbāsid Baghdad made the philosophical tradition possible. Philosophy in the Islamic world was, from the beginning, the product of a civilisation that saw itself as heir to multiple pasts.

5. The Key Corpora: What Was Translated The texts that reached the Muslim philosophers through this movement formed a distinctive intellectual library. They were not identical to the corpus studied in medieval European universities, and the differences matter enormously for understanding why Islamic philosophy took the shape it did. Aristotle. The bulk of Aristotle's surviving works were available in Arabic by the end of the tenth century: the Organon (logical works, including the Rhetoric and Poetics as parts of the syllabus), the Physics, On the Heavens, On Generation and Corruption, the Meteorology, On the Soul, the Metaphysics, the Nicomachean Ethics, and the Politics (though the latter only partially and in a poor translation, which is why Muslim political philosophers relied more on Plato). The Aristotelian corpus was never read as a pure, uninterpreted text; it arrived wrapped in a thick layer of Greek commentaries, especially those of Alexander of Aphrodisias, Themistius, and John Philoponus. These commentaries shaped the Muslim understanding of Aristotle at a structural level. Philoponus's arguments against Aristotle on the eternity of the world, for instance, became standard ammunition for kalām theologians and were adopted wholesale by al-Ghazālī. Plato. Far less of Plato's integral dialogue text was available than of Aristotle's treatises. Rather than a verbatim rendering of the complete Greek dialogues, Plato's Timaeus (the most cosmologically influential Platonic work), Republic, and Laws circulated in Arabic primarily through Galen's Synopses of the Platonic Dialogues (such as Galen's Compendium of the Timaeus, Jawāmiʿ Kitāb Ṭīmāwūs, translated in Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq's circle) alongside paraphrases and translations associated with Yaḥyā ibn al-Biṭrīq and Ḥunayn. Muslim philosophers often knew Plato through doxographies — handbooks of the opinions of the philosophers — and through the works of his Neoplatonic interpreters. The effect was that Plato was understood less as the author of aporia-laced dialogues and more as a sage who taught the creation of the world by a divine Demiurge and the immortality and pre-existence of

the soul. The political Plato — the Plato of the Republic — was, however, taken with great seriousness by al-Fārābī and Ibn Rushd, who both wrote commentaries on parts of it. The Neoplatonic Pseudepigrapha. Perhaps the single most consequential textual transmission — and certainly the most philosophically fateful — was of two works that were not what they claimed to be. The Theology of Aristotle was in fact a paraphrase of Plotinus's Enneads IV–VI, with some editorial adjustments to turn the pagan philosopher's teachings into a monotheistic schema. The Book of the Pure Good, known in Latin as the Liber de Causis, was an extract from Proclus's Elements of Theology, again heavily edited. Both texts were attributed to Aristotle, and both were read as the summit of the Master's metaphysical teaching. The consequences of this misattribution were enormous. The real Aristotle is a philosopher of concrete substances and immanent form; the Aristotle known to the Muslim falāsifa was, especially in metaphysics, a Neoplatonic emanationist who taught that the universe proceeds from God in a descending hierarchy of intellects that terminates in the active intellect governing the sublunary world. Plotinus's One and Proclus's triadic procession and reversion were merged with the Qur'anic Creator and the Aristotelian prime mover. The resulting synthesis, first fully articulated by al-Fārābī and systematised by Avicenna, is a philosophy that is neither purely Greek nor purely Islamic but a new and creative hybrid. Without the Theology of Aristotle and the Liber de Causis, the cosmology of Avicenna's Metaphysics — and even the Illuminationist cosmology of Suhrawardī — would be unrecognisable. Other Sciences. The translation movement also brought into Arabic the works of Ptolemy (the Almagest, the Tetrabiblos, the Geography), Euclid (the Elements), Galen (over a hundred medical and philosophical treatises), Hippocrates, Dioscorides on medicine and botany, Archimedes, and many others. The mathematical and medical sciences were integrated with philosophy in a unified curriculum: logic was studied first, then the mathematical sciences (arithmetic, geometry, astronomy, music), then natural science, and finally metaphysics. A typical Muslim philosopher was often also a physician, mathematician, or astronomer, and the boundaries between these disciplines were porous in a way that modern specialisation finds difficult to grasp.

6. Persian and Indian Contributions While Greek heritage dominated philosophy and the natural sciences, the translation movement also drew heavily on Persian and Indian traditions, particularly in ethics, statecraft, and wisdom literature. Persian. The Sasanian Empire (224–651) had cultivated a rich courtly culture in which practical philosophy — the art of governing, the ethics of the ruler, the management of the soul — was prized. Works of ancient Iranian gnomologia (andarz) and royal statecraft — translated from Middle Persian (Pahlavi) into Arabic by figures such as Ibn al-Muqaffaʿ (d. c. 756), who rendered Kalīla wa-Dimna and the Khwāday-Nāmag, and later synthesized by Ibn Miskawayh in his Jāvīdān Khirad (al-Ḥikma al-Khālida, Eternal Wisdom), which frames a Pahlavi wisdom text attributed to the primeval sage-king Hūshang within an ecumenical anthology of Persian, Indian, Greek, and Arab ethical maxims — entered the bloodstream of Islamic practical philosophy. The genre of "mirrors for princes" literature, which advised rulers on justice, virtue, and the behaviour befitting a king, entered Islamic thought through Persian intermediaries and profoundly influenced later ethicists like Ibn Miskawayh and al-Ṭūsī. Persian wisdom was associated with the figure of the wise counsellor, the adīb, who combined literary culture, moral insight, and practical sagacity. This tradition would eventually merge with the philosophical ethics of the Greek tradition to produce works like the Akhlāq manuals. The most important single figure in the transmission of Persian material was Ibn al-Muqaffaʿ (c. 720–756), a Persian convert to Islam who translated the Kalīla wa Dimna — a collection of animal fables that originated in

India's Panchatantra — from Middle Persian into Arabic. His translation is a masterpiece of early Arabic prose and was widely read for its political and ethical wisdom. Ibn al-Muqaffaʿ also translated Persian works on statecraft and composed original treatises on law and ethics that show a sophisticated blend of Persian and Islamic ideas. His alAdab al-Kabīr (The Great Book of Conduct) is a manual for the cultivation of the self and the navigation of courtly life that draws unabashedly on the Persian heritage while giving it an Islamic colouring. Indian. Direct contact with Indian science was also significant. In the late eighth century, an Indian astronomical work, the Siddhānta of Brahmagupta, was translated into Arabic as the Sindhind by Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm alFazārī. The translation introduced Indian numerals, the concept of zero, and trigonometric methods (sine, cosine) to the Islamic world, laying the groundwork for the great achievements of Muslim astronomers and mathematicians like al-Khwārizmī and al-Bīrūnī. Indian medical texts, notably the Caraka Saṃhitā and the Suśruta Saṃhitā, were also translated, and Indian physicians were employed at the ʿAbbāsid court. Al-Bīrūnī's Kitāb al-Hind, written in the eleventh century, is a towering monument to the philosophical engagement with India. Though beyond the early translation movement chronologically, it was made possible by the earlier transmission of Indian works and by the culture of empirical curiosity the translation movement had fostered. AlBīrūnī studied Sanskrit, read Indian philosophical texts with care, and attempted a cross-cultural description of Hinduism that remains astonishing in its depth and sympathy. His work shows that the intellectual horizons of Muslim philosophers were not limited to the Greek Mediterranean; they extended, at least for some, to the Indic world and beyond.

7. The Legacy of the Translation Movement By the middle of the tenth century, the major works of Greek philosophy and science were available in Arabic, often in multiple translations and with extensive commentaries. An Arabic philosophical vocabulary had been forged — terms like wujūd (existence), māhiyya (quiddity), jawhar (substance), ʿaraḍ (accident), ʿaql (intellect) — which would serve as the precision instruments of all subsequent philosophical analysis. A class of professional scholars, the falāsifa, had emerged, trained in logic and the sciences and committed to the pursuit of truth through demonstrative reason. And a set of problems — the eternity of the world, the nature of the soul, the relationship between God and the universe, the place of revelation within a rational cosmology — had been placed firmly on the agenda. The translation movement was also a cultural event of the first magnitude. It established that the intellectual life of Islam would be, from its earliest self-conscious moment, global. Greek philosophy was not a foreign invasion, as its later detractors charged, but a deliberately gathered inheritance, brought into the Arabic world by Muslim, Christian, and Sabian scholars working under Muslim patronage. The confidence that allowed this — the conviction that truth belongs to humanity and that the believer has nothing to fear from it — was itself part of the legacy of the Qur'anic call to reason explored in Chapter 1. There were, of course, texts that did not cross the bridge. The Greek tragedies, the historians Herodotus and Thucydides, the Latin literature of Rome — these remained outside the horizon of Arabic culture. The library was selective, and the selection shaped the range of what could be thought. But within the chosen boundaries, the riches were immense. The philosophers who walk through the doors opened by the translation movement — al-Kindī, alFārābī, Avicenna, Averroes, Suhrawardī, Mullā Ṣadrā — would each, in their own way, grapple with the Greek inheritance and transform it into something distinctly their own. The next chapter turns to the first major school of rational thought indigenous to Islam, the Muʿtazila, who were already developing their own sophisticated theology in parallel with the translation movement, and whose ideas

about God's justice and unity would set the terms of debate for centuries. Then we meet al-Kindī, the man who first brought the new Aristotelian and Neoplatonic philosophy into an explicit harmony with the God of the Qur'an.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “Regarding Galen's book On Demonstration (Fī al-Burhān): none of our contemporaries up to this day had obtained a complete Greek manuscript of it, though Gabriel ibn Bakhtīshūʿ searched furiously for it, as did I myself throughout the lands of Mesopotamia, all of Syria, Palestine, and Egypt until I reached Alexandria, collating the surviving fragments first into Syriac and then into precise Arabic according to the sense of the whole sentence rather than word-for-word substitution.” — Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq, Risāla ilā ʿAlī ibn Yaḥyā fī Dhikr mā Turjima min Kutub Jālīnūs (Epistle on the Translations of Galen's Works)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. What political, administrative, and ideological factors motivated the ʿAbbāsid caliphs and Baghdadi elite families to fund the two-century Graeco-Arabic translation movement? 2. Why was the misattribution of the Theology of Aristotle (paraphrasing Plotinus's Enneads IV–VI) and the Book of Causes (paraphrasing Proclus's Elements of Theology) to Aristotle so consequential for the metaphysical trajectory of classical falsafa? 3. Compare the word-for-word translation method attributed to Yūḥannā ibn al-Biṭrīq with the sense-for-sense philological method developed by Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq and his workshop. How did Ḥunayn's method shape technical Arabic philosophical prose? 4. Beyond Greek philosophy, how did Pahlavi ethical-political literature (such as Jāvīdān Khirad and Kalīla waDimna) and Sanskrit astronomical-mathematical treatises (such as the Sindhind) enrich early Islamic scientific thought?
