Chapter 3The Muʿtazila: The First Rationalist School

Between Faith and Reason

As the translators were rendering Aristotle and Galen into Arabic, a parallel and distinctly Islamic rationalism was already taking shape in the mosques and markets of Basra and Baghdad. Its authors were not philosophers in the

Greek mould — they did not call themselves falāsifa and they began from scripture, not from a syllabus of ancient texts — but they were philosophers in the deeper sense that they sought to understand God, the world, and human responsibility through systematic reason. They were the Muʿtazila, a school of dialectical theologians (mutakallimūn) whose name elicits both admiration and controversy to this day. The Muʿtazila have often been called the "rationalists of Islam," a phrase that captures something of their spirit but risks oversimplifying their commitments. They were rationalists in the sense that they held reason to be a Godgiven faculty capable of knowing moral truths independently of revelation. But they were also deeply scriptural thinkers, who believed that their positions were firmly grounded in the Qur'an and the Prophet's example. Their project was not to replace revelation with reason but to use reason to understand revelation, to defend it against its critics, and to eliminate the contradictions they believed arose when scripture was read without rational discipline. Why begin a book on Muslim philosophers with a school of theologians? Because the questions the Muʿtazila raised — about God's unity and justice, about free will and causality, about the relation of reason to faith — became the permanent agenda of Islamic thought. Al-Kindī, the first faylasūf proper, wrote his earliest works in conversation with Muʿtazilī patrons and arguments. Al-Ghazālī, four centuries later, was still debating the same problems the Muʿtazila had sharpened to a fine point. Even when the school itself declined politically, its methods and ideas were absorbed into the mainstream of both Sunnī and Shīʿī intellectual life. To skip the Muʿtazila is to miss the opening movement of the symphony into which later philosophers would enter as soloists.

1. Origins: The Political and Theological Crucible The Muʿtazila emerged from the political chaos that followed the Prophet Muḥammad's death in 632. Within a generation, the Muslim community had split over the legitimacy of its leaders, and these divisions — which became the Sunni-Shīʿī schism — were also theological. Who was a true Muslim? Was the grave sinner still a believer? Could a corrupt or sinful ruler legitimately lead the community? The founding moment of Muʿtazilism is traditionally linked to the figure of Wāṣil ibn ʿAṭāʾ (c. 700–748), a student of the distinguished scholar al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī in Basra. The story goes that when al-Ḥasan was asked about the status of a Muslim who commits a grave sin, Wāṣil interrupted with a position that differed from both his teacher's and the Khārijites', who declared the grave sinner an unbeliever. Wāṣil argued that such a person was neither a believer nor an unbeliever but occupied an intermediate position — al-manzila bayn al-manzilatayn. He then "withdrew" (iʿtazala) from al-Ḥasan's circle to develop his own teaching, giving the school its name: the Muʿtazila, "those who withdraw." Whether this origin story is historically accurate or a later rationalisation matters less than what it reveals. The Muʿtazila were, from the beginning, a movement defined by a moral seriousness that refused to reduce complex human realities to binary categories. The question of the grave sinner was not a remote technicality; it went to the heart of how God's justice and human responsibility could be reconciled, and to the social question of who could be trusted in a community riven by political violence. Their answer — that the grave sinner is suspended between belief and unbelief, and that his ultimate fate is a matter for God's just judgement — was a mediating position that reflected a broader intellectual temperament: one that sought to hold competing truths in tension and to submit them all to rational scrutiny. Alongside Wāṣil, the second founding figure was ʿAmr ibn ʿUbayd (died 761), a respected ascetic and scholar who gave the movement its spiritual depth. Together they established Basra as the first centre of Muʿtazilī thought, soon rivalled by a school in Baghdad. The Basran school tended to be more rigorous in its natural philosophy and atomism; the Baghdadi branch, influenced by the proximity of court politics and Greek texts, was often more

flexible and politically engaged. The rivalry was creative, generating a steady stream of refinements, rebuttals, and new syntheses.

2. The Five Principles Beneath the surface of individual disputes, the Muʿtazila developed a unified theological system organised around five core principles. To be a Muʿtazilī was to affirm them all. Divine Unity (al-tawḥīd). The affirmation that God is absolutely one is foundational to Islam, but the Muʿtazila pressed it to its logical extreme. They argued that describing God through attributes — knowledge, power, life, will — introduced a plurality into the divine essence. If God's attributes were real entities distinct from His essence, then there would be multiple eternal beings, a violation of pure monotheism. Their solution was to hold that God knows, wills, and is powerful by His essence, not by separate attributes inhering in it. The Qur'anic descriptions of God's "hand," "face," or "throne" were not to be understood literally — that would be anthropomorphism — but interpreted metaphorically or allegorically. God, for the Muʿtazila, is a being of pure simplicity, utterly unlike creation. This rigorous anti-anthropomorphism set them sharply against those traditions that were content to accept scriptural language without asking "how" (bi-lā kayf). It also created an intellectual space that philosophy would later fill: if God is pure simplicity, how does a diverse world emerge from Him? The Neoplatonic emanation scheme of al-Fārābī and Avicenna would be one sophisticated answer to a Muʿtazilī question. Divine Justice (al-ʿadl). If the God of the Qur'an is "the most just of judges" (Q 10:109) and "wrongs not His servants" (Q 3:182), then, the Muʿtazila reasoned, He cannot be the author of evil or injustice. Human beings must be genuinely free creators of their own acts; otherwise God would be punishing people for actions He Himself compelled them to perform, a manifest injustice. This led to the doctrine of qadar — human power and free will — and the famous self-designation of the Muʿtazila as "the People of Justice and Unity" (ahl al-ʿadl wa-l-tawḥīd). God does not create evil acts; humans do. God's role is to command the good, forbid the evil, and hold individuals accountable. This commitment to divine justice also underwrote a robust ethical objectivism: acts are good or evil intrinsically, and God commands what is good because it is good, not the other way around. The Muʿtazila opposed the voluntarism that would later characterise Ashʿarī theology, in which good and evil are defined solely by God's command. For the Muʿtazila, justice was a real quality that human reason could discern, and God's justice consisted in His acting in accordance with it. The Promise and the Threat (al-waʿd wa-l-waʿīd). This principle concerns eschatology. God, being just, must fulfil His promises and His threats exactly as He has announced them. The grave sinner who dies without repentance cannot simply be pardoned arbitrarily; God's threat of punishment is not nullified by divine whim. At the same time, because the sinner still professes faith, he is not consigned to eternal hell in the manner of unbelievers. The Muʿtazila thus held that the grave sinner would suffer punishment for a finite period and then be released, a middle position between the eternity of punishment for unbelievers and the unconditional universal salvation of some later mystics. This rigorous insistence that divine threats matter reinforced the ethical seriousness of the school. Faith was not a magic talisman that rendered deeds irrelevant. The Intermediate Position (al-manzila bayn al-manzilatayn). As the founding story suggests, this principle defined the status of the grave sinner in this world. He is neither muʾmin (believer) nor kāfir (unbeliever), but fāsiq (sinner), a category with its own legal and social implications. The Khārijites, by contrast, declared the grave sinner an apostate worthy of death, while the Murjiʾa deferred judgement entirely to God and insisted on the sinner's continued membership in the community of believers. The Muʿtazilī intermediate position was a work of political

theology, an attempt to carve out a space for moral judgement without tearing the community apart. It would influence legal and political debates for centuries. Commanding Right and Forbidding Wrong (al-amr bi-l-maʿrūf wa-l-nahy ʿan al-munkar). The final principle concerns the obligation to act in society to promote virtue and combat vice. All Muslims acknowledge this Qur'anic duty, but the Muʿtazila interpreted it as a collective obligation that could at times require active opposition to unjust rulers. This made the Muʿtazila, in certain periods, a politically activist movement. During the ʿAbbāsid caliphate of al-Maʾmūn, Muʿtazilī scholars were closely allied with the state and used its power to enforce their theological views — a use of force that would later contribute to their downfall when political winds shifted. The principle itself, however, remained a permanent feature of Islamic social ethics, debated and refined by Shīʿī and Sunnī jurists alike.

3. Metaphysics and Natural Philosophy: Atomism, Generated Effects (Tawlīd), and Latency (Kumūn) Beyond theology, the Muʿtazila constructed the first systematic Islamic philosophy of nature. Most Basran and Baghdadi Muʿtazila argued that the corporeal world is composed of indivisible atoms (jawāhir farda or ajzāʾ lā tatajazzaʾ) and accidents (aʿrāḍ) — such as color, taste, motion, rest, composition, pain, knowledge, and will — that inhere in them. Crucially, unlike later Ashʿarī occasionalists (who would claim that no accident can endure for two moments of time and that God directly re-creates every accident at every instant), early Muʿtazilī atomists distinguished between instantaneous accidents (such as motions and sounds) and enduring accidents (such as colors, life, and spatial composition, which endure across time until replaced by a contrary accident or annihilated by God). Moreover, to safeguard human moral responsibility, the Muʿtazila developed the theory of generated effects (tawlīd): when a voluntary human agent performs a primary act within their locus of power (such as drawing a bowstring or throwing a stone), that act causally generates secondary physical effects in the external world (the flight of the arrow and the striking of the target), for which the human agent is morally and legally accountable. This classical kalām atomism was pioneered by Abū al-Hudhayl al-ʿAllāf (c. 750–c. 840), the first great systematic metaphysician of the Basran Muʿtazila. Abū al-Hudhayl taught that the created universe is finite in spatial extension and temporal duration, that every physical body is an aggregate of indivisible dimensionless particles (he held that a minimum of six atoms arranged along the six spatial directions — top, bottom, front, back, left, right — is required to form a three-dimensional body), and that human beings are genuine creators of their voluntary actions and their generated effects (mutawallidāt) through the power (qudra) God has vested in them beforehand. His purpose was to secure the absolute power and uniqueness of God while also making room for human free will by treating human acts as accidents created by the human agent under God's general empowerment. He was an ingenious builder of concepts whose ideas set the stage for the entire subsequent trajectory of kalām. A brilliant and eccentric critic within the school was Ibrāhīm al-Naẓẓām (c. 775–c. 845). Al-Naẓẓām rejected atomism. Instead he developed a theory of kumūn (latency): all things are created by God at once and latent within each other, and the history of the world is the gradual emergence of what was already there. An oil lamp's flame was not created in the moment of lighting but had been hidden in the oil from the first creation. Even more controversially, because al-Naẓẓām rejected indivisible atoms and affirmed the actual infinite divisibility of every continuous body (holding that there is no part of matter that does not have a further half-part ad infinitum), his atomist teacher Abū al-Hudhayl challenged him with a version of Zeno's dichotomy paradox: how can an ant creeping across a rock ever traverse an infinite number of spatial sub-intervals in a finite time? To solve this paradox of the continuum without surrendering infinite divisibility, al-Naẓẓām proposed his famous theory of the leap (al-ṭafra): a moving body traverses a continuous distance containing infinitely many potential divisions by

crossing finite sub-spans through discrete spatial leaps without having to actualize and touch every infinite midpoint sequentially. Al-Naẓẓām was also a poet, a literary stylist, and a deeply original thinker whose ideas challenged his fellow Muʿtazila as vigorously as they challenged outsiders. His intellectual independence was legendary, and his works — now mostly lost — were still being debated centuries later. The Muʿtazilī engagement with natural philosophy was not a detour from theology but an integral part of it. What is the world, that a just God might hold humans accountable within it? What are bodies, such that a transcendent God can create them? These were philosophical questions being answered with tools forged in-house, before the impact of Greek natural philosophy had fully been absorbed. That absorption, when it came through al-Kindī and the falāsifa, would not replace the Muʿtazilī framework overnight; it would enter into dialogue with it, and the dialogue would reshape both.

4. The Centrality of Reason: Epistemology and Ethics The Muʿtazila's most enduring and controversial contribution was their epistemology — their theory of how we know, and how reason relates to revelation. They held that reason (ʿaql) is capable of arriving at moral and theological truths independently of scripture. The goodness of justice and the badness of lying, for instance, are grasped by reason and in no need of prophetic confirmation. God's commands in the Qur'an confirm what reason already knows; they do not constitute it from nothing. This led to the much-debated doctrine that the first obligation upon every human being, before the arrival of any prophet, is rational inquiry into God's existence and the truth of prophecy. A human being who fails to use reason to arrive at the knowledge of God is culpable, even if no messenger has reached him. Conversely, a prophet's claim must be tested by reason: miracles and other signs can be evaluated rationally, and a message that contradicts reason cannot be from God. This gave the Muʿtazila a clear criterion for interpreting scripture: wherever a literal reading of the Qur'an conflicts with a rational truth, the text must be interpreted allegorically. Reason is the judge, not the defendant. This rationalist optimism also produced the doctrine of al-ṣalāḥ wa-l-aṣlaḥ — that God does what is best and most salutary for His creatures. God's grace is not arbitrary; it is rationally structured for the benefit of human beings. Suffering that cannot be justified in terms of recompense or moral education is incompatible with divine justice. The Muʿtazila therefore wrote extensive theodicies, explaining the existence of natural evil and human suffering in ways that preserved God's perfect goodness. Their arguments were not always convincing to outsiders, but the attempt itself — to hold God accountable to a humanly intelligible standard of justice — was a philosophical watershed. Al-Ghazālī's later critique of the philosophers' inability to prove God's concern for particulars was, in effect, a turn of the same Muʿtazilī screw.

5. Key Figures: A Gallery of Minds The Muʿtazila movement spanned more than four centuries and encompassed a remarkable range of personalities and specialisms. A full roll call would fill a volume, but a few figures apart from those already named deserve brief attention. Al-Jāḥiẓ (c. 776–868) was perhaps the most original literary mind in the history of Arabic letters. A Muʿtazilī of the Basran school, he wrote on theology, zoology, rhetoric, psychology, and a thousand other subjects in a prose that combined clarity, humour, and encyclopaedic curiosity. His Kitāb al-Ḥayawān (Book of Animals) is a sprawling work of natural history that also mounts theological arguments for design in creation, while his Kitāb al-Bukhalāʾ

(Book of Misers) is a satirical masterpiece that doubles as a study of human social behaviour. Al-Jāḥiẓ represents the Muʿtazilī spirit at its most expansive: all of creation is a text to be read with the lens of an inquisitive reason. Abū ʿAlī al-Jubbāʾī (c. 849–915) and his son Abū Hāshim al-Jubbāʾī (c. 861–933) were the great systematisers of Basran Muʿtazilism. The father, Abū ʿAlī, was a formidable debater who refined the atomistic theory and argued against al-Ashʿarī, who had been his student before breaking away to found what became Sunnī kalām. The son, Abū Hāshim, introduced the influential but highly abstract notion of "states" (aḥwāl) to explain divine attributes without compromising unity — an idea that generated volumes of commentary. It was a memorable moment in the history of ideas when the brilliant student turned against his master, and al-Ashʿarī's subsequent career as the architect of Sunni occasionalism was built on the foundation of the Muʿtazilī tools he had learned from the Jubbāʾīs. ʿAbd al-Jabbār (c. 935–1025) was the last great Muʿtazilī systematiser. His vast al-Mughnī fī Abwāb al-Tawḥīd wal-ʿAdl (The Sufficer Concerning the Topics of Unity and Justice) is a twenty-volume summa of Muʿtazilī theology, ethics, and polemics. Writing under Buyid patronage in Rayy, near modern Tehran, ʿAbd al-Jabbār defended the school in its mature form, responding to Ashʿarī critiques and incorporating elements of philosophical ethics and physics. His work preserved Muʿtazilism for later Shīʿī thinkers, who adopted much of his ethical framework. Without ʿAbd al-Jabbār, much of what we know about classical Muʿtazilism would be lost.

6. The Miḥna: Theology and State Power No account of the Muʿtazila would be complete without the Miḥna — the inquisition instituted by the ʿAbbāsid caliph al-Maʾmūn in 833. Al-Maʾmūn, a deeply intellectual ruler, had been drawn to Muʿtazilī theology, particularly its doctrine of the createdness of the Qur'an. The Qur'an, the Muʿtazila argued, is God's speech, but speech is an act, and acts are created; therefore the Qur'an is created in time, not co-eternal with God. To say otherwise, they argued, was to posit a second eternal entity alongside God — a violation of tawḥīd. Al-Maʾmūn, shortly before his death, ordered that judges and scholars be interrogated on this point, and those who refused to affirm the createdness of the Qur'an be dismissed from office. The policy was continued by his successors al-Muʿtaṣim and al-Wāthiq, and some scholars were imprisoned or flogged for their refusal. The most famous resister was Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal (780–855), the founder of the Ḥanbalī legal school and a staunch defender of the uncreated Qur'an. His principled endurance under interrogation made him a folk hero to traditionalist Muslims and cemented the image of the Muʿtazila as an authoritarian, rationalist elite willing to use state power to crush dissent. The Miḥna ended in failure around 848, when the caliph al-Mutawakkil reversed the policy. Its legacy was deep and ambiguous. For the Muʿtazila, it was a short-lived victory that turned into a long-term catastrophe. The school became associated with state coercion, and its subsequent decline was in part a popular and scholarly backlash against its methods. At the same time, the Miḥna demonstrated that theological ideas had real political stakes and that the caliphs could not simply impose a doctrine by decree. The autonomy of the scholarly class from the state was reinforced, an outcome with profound implications for the development of Sunnī Islam.

7. Legacy: The Transformation of Kalām and Philosophy The Muʿtazila declined as a distinct school in Sunni Islam after the eleventh century, but their intellectual legacy persisted everywhere under new names and in new forms. The most direct heir — and the most powerful critic — was Abū al-Ḥasan al-Ashʿarī (c. 874–936), who trained in Muʿtazilī theology before breaking with his teachers and founding the Ashʿarī school of kalām. Al-Ashʿarī retained the rationalist methods he had learned — counter-arguments, dialectical reasoning, conceptual analysis — but

subordinated reason to revelation and affirmed a strong divine voluntarism in ethics. Ashʿarism became the dominant theological school of Sunnī Islam, and much of its sophistication was a mirror image of the Muʿtazila it opposed. Whenever an Ashʿarī theologian argued against free will or the objectivity of ethics, he was arguing against a Muʿtazilī position that had forced him to sharpen his own. The Māturīdī school, founded in Samarqand by Abū Manṣūr al-Māturīdī (c. 853–944), took a middle path closer to Muʿtazilī ethics while remaining firmly Sunnī. Māturīdīs accepted a role for reason in moral knowledge and adopted a more moderate occasionalism. Through the Māturīdīs, Muʿtazilī ideas percolated into the theology of the Ottoman Empire and Central Asia. Māturīdism became the dominant theological school in Central Asia, the Ottoman lands, and much of South Asia, and its more robust rationalism — closer to Muʿtazilī positions than Ashʿarism on questions of free will and the objectivity of ethics — ensured that the Muʿtazilī legacy survived in institutional form long after the Muʿtazila themselves had ceased to exist as a distinct school. Key later Māturīdī thinkers such as Abū al-Muʿīn al-Nasafī (d. 1114) and Najm al-Dīn al-Nasafī (d. 1142) developed sophisticated ontologies and epistemologies that deserve a place in any comprehensive history of Islamic philosophy. Within Shīʿī Islam, Muʿtazilī rationalism found a lasting home. Twelver Shīʿī theologians, especially from the Buyid period onward, absorbed Muʿtazilī ethical theory and integrated it into their own frameworks. When we meet Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī in Chapter 21, we will encounter a Shīʿī philosopher-theologian who drew deeply on Muʿtazilī arguments. The Zaydī Shīʿa of Yemen also maintained a continuous Muʿtazilī tradition that survives to the present. Beyond Islam, the Muʿtazila influenced Jewish thinkers such as Saʿadya Gaʾon (882–942), the head of the Babylonian Jewish academy, who imported Muʿtazilī arguments into Jewish theology. Some scholars have found Muʿtazilī echoes in the Christian Arabic literature of the period as well. The school's cosmopolitan rationalism travelled far. For the falāsifa proper, the Muʿtazila created an intellectual environment in which rational inquiry into God, nature, and ethics was both legitimate and prestigious. Al-Kindī, the first Muslim to wear the title faylasūf without apology, owed his earliest patronage to Muʿtazilī-inclined court circles. His project — to demonstrate the harmony of Greek philosophy and Islamic truth — would have been far more difficult without the Muʿtazilī precedent that reason was a trusted ally of revelation. And when al-Ghazālī, in his devastating Incoherence of the Philosophers, took aim at the Aristotelian fusion of God and world, the venom in his pen was brewed in a tradition of dialectical kalām that the Muʿtazila had begun. The Muʿtazila were the first to make rational theology a systematic Islamic science. They were right about some things, wrong about others, but above all they were serious. Their claim that God is a just and unified reality knowable in part by human reason, and that human beings are morally responsible agents — this conviction remained at the centre of Islamic thought long after the school bearing their name had faded. The next chapters will show how the falāsifa took up these same questions with new tools, arriving at systems of extraordinary scope and beauty which we now, with the Muʿtazilī opening in our ears, are ready to hear as participants in a single, continuous conversation.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “Knowledge of God can be attained only through rational speculation (al-naẓar al-ʿaqlī), and rational speculation is the very first obligation (awwal al-wājibāt) incumbent upon every morally responsible human being, prior to the arrival of revelation; for the veracity of revelation itself cannot be known until one first knows that the Sender of the prophet is Wise and incapable of deceit or injustice.” — Al-Qāḍī ʿAbd al-Jabbār, Sharḥ alUṣūl al-Khamsa (Exposition of the Five Principles)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How do the first two principles of the Muʿtazila — Divine Unity (al-tawḥīd) and Divine Justice (al-ʿadl) — logically entail their doctrines of the createdness of the Qurʾān and human free creation of voluntary acts (khalq al-afʿāl)? 2. Contrast Abū al-Hudhayl al-ʿAllāf's theory of indivisible atoms (al-jawhar al-fard) and discrete accidents with his nephew al-Naẓẓām's anti-atomist theory of infinite divisibility, latency (kumūn), and the leap (ṭafra). 3. Explain the Muʿtazilī thesis of moral objectivism (al-ḥusn wa-l-qubḥ al-ʿaqliyyān). According to ʿAbd al-Jabbār, why is an act good or evil in itself, and what role remains for prophetic revelation if human reason can already discern fundamental moral duties? 4. What did the historical episode of the Miḥna (833–848 CE) under Caliph al-Maʾmūn and his successors reveal about the tension between rationalist state orthodoxy and traditionalist religious authority led by Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal?

Moral Agents and Their Deserts: The Character of Muʿtazilite Ethics. Princeton: Princeton University Press, 2008. Van Ess, Josef. Theology and Society in the Second and Third Centuries of the Hijra. Part II: The Peripatetic Synthesis and Its Tenth-Century Rivals

Chapter 4Al-Kindī: The First Muslim Philosopher

A Philosopher at the Court

In the early decades of the ninth century, as the translation movement was reaching its peak and the Muʿtazila were refining their rational theology, a man of noble Arab lineage and immense intellectual ambition began to write philosophical treatises in a language that had never before been used for systematic philosophy. His name was Abū Yūsuf Yaʿqūb ibn Isḥāq al-Kindī (c. 801–c. 873). Posterity would call him Faylasūf al-ʿArab — "the Philosopher of the Arabs" — a title that honoured both his ethnic distinction (he claimed descent from the ancient Arabian tribe of Kinda) and his pioneering role in forging an Arabic philosophical idiom. He stands at the head of the tradition this book traces, the first to bring the Greek sciences and the Islamic revelation into a deliberate and sustained philosophical synthesis. Al-Kindī was not a translator, though he worked closely with the translators and sometimes supervised their efforts. He did not know Greek himself, which meant he depended on the Syriac-Arabic translations for his knowledge of Aristotle, Plato, the Neoplatonic pseudepigrapha, and the Hellenistic commentaries. Yet his dependence was also his liberation: because he encountered Greek philosophy as a body of Arabic texts, he could treat it as material to be reshaped for distinctively Islamic purposes. He was, in a sense, the first to do philosophy in Arabic, and the vocabulary he developed — for being, for intellect, for eternity and creation, for the soul's ascent — became the permanent toolkit of the tradition. His life was bound up with the ʿAbbāsid court in Sāmarrāʾ and Baghdad, where he served as tutor to the caliph alMuʿtaṣim's son and enjoyed the patronage of al-Muʿtaṣim and, later, al-Wāthiq. He was a polymath: he wrote on geometry, optics, music, medicine, astrology, and the manufacture of swords and perfumes, alongside his core philosophical and metaphysical treatises. Of his nearly two hundred and fifty known works — many of them short epistles rather than large books — only a fraction survive, but what has come down to us reveals a mind of extraordinary range, confidence, and synthetic power. In a period of intellectual ferment, when the newly translated Greek texts could dazzle or disorient, al-Kindī provided a coherent vision: philosophy and revelation are two languages speaking one truth.

1. The Harmony of Revelation and Reason Al-Kindī's fundamental conviction, repeated across his works, was that revealed knowledge and philosophical knowledge cannot ultimately conflict. Truth is one, and the Creator of the universe is also the Giver of scripture. God gives truth to prophets through revelation (waḥy) and to philosophers through demonstrative reason; both are divine gifts, distinguished by manner and speed rather than content. The prophet acquires knowledge "without effort, without inquiry, without the passage of time, and without logical procedure," while the philosopher must labour through the stages of demonstration. But the truth they reach is the same truth. This position was more than apologetic strategy. It was a genuine epistemology of revelation. In his treatise On the Quantity of Aristotle's Books and What Is Required for the Attainment of Philosophy, al-Kindī explained that philosophy was the "knowledge of things in their true natures, insofar as this is possible for man." Its goals are the purification of the soul and the knowledge of God — goals that scripture also commands. To seek wisdom through philosophy was thus to obey God's instruction to reflect on His signs in creation. The philosopher does not stand outside religion; he pursues its deepest rational dimension. Because of this harmony, al-Kindī felt no hesitation in using philosophical concepts to interpret scriptural passages that seemed opaque or anthropomorphic. The Qur'an says that God "established Himself upon the Throne" (Q 7:54) and that He has "hands" (Q 38:75). For al-Kindī, these expressions were figurative, and their proper interpretation required philosophical analysis. The Throne signified God's dominion over the cosmos; the hand signified His power. The literalist who insists on a bodily Throne imagines a God who takes up space and sits on furniture, thereby confusing the Creator with the created. Reason, by clarifying what it means to be a transcendent cause, protects the purity of monotheism. In this al-Kindī aligned naturally with the Muʿtazila, whose antianthropomorphism drew on the same rationalist instinct. Yet al-Kindī went further than most Muʿtazilī theologians in one crucial respect: his philosophical defence of creation out of nothing. The Greek philosophical tradition, particularly in its Aristotelian mainstream, had held the world to be eternal — a position that directly contradicted the Qur'anic doctrine of a created universe with a temporal beginning. The Muʿtazila had argued for temporal creation on theological grounds, but their philosophical tools for doing so were still elementary. Al-Kindī undertook to prove temporal creation using the full arsenal of Greek logic, geometry, and physics. This was a genuinely original philosophical project, and it earned him, alongside his harmonising instincts, the suspicion of those who thought he was recruiting philosophy to do theology's work.

2. Metaphysics: Oneness and Creation The metaphysical centre of al-Kindī's system is a sustained meditation on unity and multiplicity. God, for al-Kindī, is the True One — a unity without parts, without attributes distinct from His essence, without genus or species, without quantity or quality, beyond description in the language of created things. He is not an entity in the universe but the cause of the universe, and the fundamental relationship between Creator and creation is that God brings unity to multiplicity: He is the cause by which many things exist, are unified, and are sustained. The concept of "oneness" (al-waḥda) is the master key to his metaphysics. Everything other than God is, in itself, characterised by multiplicity: a body has parts, a quality inheres in a substance, a moment of time follows another. Multiplicity, al-Kindī argues, is accidental, not essential to being. The existence of anything multiple requires a cause that imparts unity to it. Since an infinite regress of causes is impossible — this is another point al-Kindī argues with tools borrowed from Aristotle — the chain must terminate in a cause that is pure unity, not mixed with any multiplicity whatsoever. That cause is God.

This argument is not a mere restatement of Muʿtazilī atomism. It is a metaphysical deduction that took the Aristotelian prohibition on infinite regress and the Neoplatonic intuition that unity precedes multiplicity, and fused them into a single rational proof for the existence and nature of God. Al-Kindī's treatise On First Philosophy, dedicated to the caliph al-Muʿtaṣim, is the first sustained work of philosophical theology in Arabic, and it remains one of the most elegant. Its first sentence announces the project: "The highest and noblest of human arts is the art of philosophy, which is defined as the knowledge of things in their true natures, insofar as man's capacity for such knowledge extends." First philosophy, which studies God and ultimate causes, is its crown. Al-Kindī's most audacious philosophical argument was his proof that the world cannot be eternal. He constructed a series of proofs based on the impossibility of actual infinites. The most famous runs as follows: If the world were eternal, then an infinite number of past moments would have elapsed up to the present. But an infinite cannot be traversed; one can never pass through an infinite series. Since the present moment does exist, the past must be finite. Therefore, the world had a beginning. The argument is philosophically controversial — later thinkers from Avicenna to modern set theorists have challenged it — but its historical importance is immense. It showed that a Greek-trained philosopher, using Greek logical and mathematical tools, could argue against Aristotle's eternity of the world and for the creationist metaphysics of the Qur'an. The argument would be refined, criticised, and defended for a thousand years, appearing in al-Ghazālī's critique of the philosophers, in Maimonides's wrestling with creation, and in the debates of the Latin scholastics. Al-Kindī had placed a philosophical torpedo under the Aristotelian hull, and it was not easily disarmed. He also argued for the finitude of space, the finitude of time, and the finitude of all bodies in the universe. These were not separate theses but aspects of a single metaphysical vision: God is infinite in the sense of being uncaused, necessary, and pure unity; all else is finite in duration, extension, and causal dependence. The cosmos is a bounded sphere of created multiplicity, suspended from the One like a lamp from a chain.

3. The Theory of the Intellect One of al-Kindī's most influential contributions was his theory of the intellect, which he laid out in a short treatise curiously titled On the Intellect (Risāla fī al-ʿAql). The treatise distilled a complex commentary tradition — chiefly Alexander of Aphrodisias's interpretation of Aristotle's On the Soul — into a clear fourfold scheme that would be taken up, modified, and debated by every major Muslim philosopher after him. In his epistle On the Intellect (Risāla fī al-ʿAql), following the exact order and terminology of his Arabic text, alKindī distinguished four kinds or stages of intellect: The First Intellect that is Always in Act (al-ʿaql al-awwal alladhī bi-l-fiʿl abadan): Listed first by al-Kindī, this is the transcendent, separate cosmic Intelligence outside the human soul that eternally possesses the universal intelligible forms in pure actuality and serves as the cause that brings human potentiality into act (what the later Peripatetic tradition would call the Active or Agent Intellect, al-ʿaql al-faʿʿāl). The Intellect in Potentiality (al-ʿaql alladhī bi-l-quwwa): This is the innate rational capacity of the human soul prior to actualizing knowledge — an unactualized readiness to receive universal intelligible forms that belongs to the human soul by nature. The Intellect that has Passed in the Soul from Potentiality to Actuality (al-ʿaql alladhī kharaja fī al-nafs min alquwwa ilā al-fiʿl): When the potential intellect is illuminated by the First Intellect and grasps universal truths (which al-Kindī calls the "acquired" possession, al-mustafād, because the soul acquires it from the First

Intellect), the soul now possesses a stable habitual disposition (malaka) — like a skilled scribe who possesses the art of writing even when not actively writing — able to summon intelligible forms whenever it wills. The Second or Manifest Intellect (al-ʿaql al-thānī / al-ʿaql al-ẓāhir): This fourth stage in al-Kindī's scheme is the intellect in active exercise and outward manifestation — when the soul brings its stored habitual knowledge forth into immediate contemplation and demonstrative action, just as the scribe actively writes. Al-Kindī's agent intellect is strongly Neoplatonic in colouring. It is not merely a psychological faculty but a transcendent being, the bridge between the sublunary world of generation and corruption and the celestial world of eternal intelligences. The soul's ultimate felicity lies in being united with the agent intellect, or at least in ascending to the plane where the agent intellect's light shines without obstruction. The exact details of this union — whether it is an identity, a vision, or a participation — were left ambiguous in al-Kindī's surviving works, and the ambiguity would generate a rich tradition of interpretation. Al-Fārābī would refine the scheme; Avicenna would give it a radically new epistemological twist; Ibn Rushd would argue over it with his Andalusian predecessors. All of them began from al-Kindī's fourfold map. Crucially, al-Kindī used this theory of intellect to give philosophical precision to the Qur'anic concept of the soul's ascent. The soul, imprisoned in the body during earthly life, actualises its intellectual potential through study and moral purification. At death, it separates and returns to its true home in the intelligible realm, where the veils of matter no longer obstruct its vision of truth. Al-Kindī's treatise On the Device for Dispelling Sorrows — one of the earliest works of philosophical consolation in Arabic — counselled that attachment to bodily things is the source of all grief. The wise person lives in the body as a temporary resident, training the soul for its eternal home. This ethical and spiritual dimension of his epistemology would become as important as the technical scheme itself.

4. Ethics and the Purification of the Soul Al-Kindī's ethics is inseparable from his metaphysics of soul and intellect. The human being is a composite of an immortal rational soul and a perishable material body. The soul's proper activity is intellectual contemplation; the body's appetites and passions drag it downward into the realm of transient goods. The moral life consists in the soul's turning away from its bodily attachments — pleasure, wealth, status, even the body's own health as an end in itself — and orienting itself towards the enduring intelligible realm. Philosophy is the therapy of the soul; the philosopher is the physician of his own spirit. On the Device for Dispelling Sorrows is a remarkable work of practical philosophy, closer in spirit to the Stoic meditations and the Hellenistic consolation literature than to the more systematic ethical treatises of later figures like Ibn Miskawayh. Al-Kindī argues that sorrow arises from the loss of what we love, or the frustration of what we desire. If we desire only what is within our control — namely, the cultivation of our souls — we become immune to sorrow. External goods can always be taken away; the goods of the intellect are inalienable, because nothing in the material world can touch them. The wise person, therefore, practises a kind of detachment that is at once philosophical, medical, and spiritual. He also wrote on the definition of virtue, on justice, on the duties of parents to children and children to parents, and on the proper conduct of rulers. These works show that he was not only a metaphysician but also a practical moralist engaged with the ethical challenges of court life and family obligation — a philosopher in the world, not a hermit beyond it. Al-Kindī's ethical outlook was deeply indebted to the Platonic and Neoplatonic traditions, especially the Phaedo's portrayal of philosophy as the practice of dying. But he gave it a Qur'anic colouring: the soul is God's trust, and its

purification is a return to the divine presence. "Whoever purifies it has succeeded, and whoever corrupts it has failed" (Q 91:9–10) — these verses could serve as the motto of al-Kindī's ethical project.

5. The Range of His Thought It is worth pausing to register the sheer breadth of al-Kindī's intellectual output, because it was not yet specialised in the narrow modern sense. He wrote on optics, correcting Greek theories of vision and formulating the earliest known explanation of how the eye perceives light — a theory based on the rectilinear propagation of rays. He wrote on music theory, analysing the mathematical ratios of intervals and the therapeutic effects of melody on the soul; his Risāla fī al-Luḥūn is the earliest Arabic treatise on the subject. He wrote on pharmacology, on the manufacture of swords, on the tides, on the causes of thunder and lightning, on the rainbow, on the determination of distances to the stars. This was not dilettantism. It was the expression of a unified conception of knowledge, within which physics, mathematics, medicine, and metaphysics formed a single curriculum of ascent to the divine. The same mind that deduced the finitude of time and body also wrote treatises on spherical geometry, astronomical instruments, and the sphericity of the earth and the elements — working in the very milieu of al-Maʾmūn's astronomers (such as the Banū Mūsā and Sanad ibn ʿAlī, who measured the terrestrial degree and computed the circumference of the earth on the plain of Sinjār) — and argued that prophetic revelation and demonstrative reason converge on the same truth. Al-Kindī was a man who saw no contradiction between measuring the sublunary world and contemplating the One; the two activities were complementary exercises of the same intellect, the same ʿaql that God had given as humanity's greatest gift.

6. Legacy: The Beginning of a Tradition Al-Kindī's immediate philosophical legacy was considerable but complicated. He attracted brilliant immediate disciples — including Aḥmad ibn al-Ṭayyib al-Sarakhsī (d. 899) in Baghdad and Abū Zayd al-Balkhī (850–934) in Khurāsān — and while his style of harmonizing creationist theology with Neoplatonic-Aristotelian philosophy was soon eclipsed in Baghdad by al-Fārābī's stricter Peripateticism, an influential eastern Kindian tradition flourished for over a century in Khurāsān through al-Balkhī, Ibn Farīghūn, and Abū al-Ḥasan al-ʿĀmirī (d. 992), whom we shall examine in Chapter 6. His political fortunes declined after the mid-ninth century, when his patrons lost power and his library was confiscated by rivals. He did not, like al-Fārābī and Avicenna, build a systematic edifice that subsequent generations could study and comment upon as a whole. His works, many short and occasional, were dispersed rather than collected into a canon. Yet al-Kindī's influence was pervasive in subtler ways. He created the Arabic philosophical vocabulary. He established the principle that Greek philosophy and Islamic revelation were not enemies but allies in the pursuit of truth. He placed creation, the soul's ascent, and the purification of the intellect at the centre of the philosophical agenda. And he demonstrated, by example, that a Muslim philosopher could argue from first principles rather than simply parroting the ancients. When al-Fārābī took up the task of systematising logic and political philosophy, when Avicenna crafted his vast metaphysical synthesis, they were walking through doors al-Kindī had opened. The medieval Latin tradition knew him, slightly, via translations of a few works, and referred to him as "Alkindus" — a name that appears in the margins of scholastic manuscripts on optics and astrology. But in the Islamic world he was never forgotten; Mullā Ṣadrā in the seventeenth century still referred to him respectfully, and modern Arab intellectuals have reclaimed him as a founding figure of a rationalist, cosmopolitan Islam.

With al-Kindī, Muslim philosophy discovers its voice: still raw in places, over-ambitious, sometimes uncritical, but unmistakably its own. The next phase of the story belongs to al-Fārābī, who would take al-Kindī's scattered insights and forge them into a systematic Peripatetic philosophy — with a logic, a metaphysics, and a political theory of extraordinary coherence and ambition. The "Philosopher of the Arabs" had sown the seeds; the "Second Teacher" would harvest them.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “We ought not to be ashamed of appreciating the truth and of acquiring it wherever it comes from, even if it comes from races distant and nations different from us. For the seeker of truth nothing takes precedence over the truth, and the truth is neither disparaged nor belittled by the one who speaks it or the one who conveys it — rather, no one is demeaned by the truth; all are ennobled by it.” — Abū Yūsuf Yaʿqūb ibn Isḥāq al-Kindī, Fī alFalsafa al-Ūlā (On First Philosophy)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How does al-Kindī argue in On First Philosophy (Fī al-Falsafa al-Ūlā) for the substantive harmony between Greek philosophy and prophetic revelation, and how does he answer traditionalist jurists who attacked philosophy as a foreign innovation? 2. Why did al-Kindī break with Aristotle and Proclus to defend the temporal creation of the universe ex nihilo (ibdāʿ) and the impossibility of an actual infinite magnitude or infinite past time? 3. Examine al-Kindī's Neoplatonic demonstration of the True One (al-Wāḥid al-Ḥaqq). How does he prove that every multiplicity in the sensible world depends on a transcendent Unity that contains no internal composition whatsoever? 4. In his Epistle on the Device for Dispelling Sorrows (Risāla fī al-Ḥīla li-Dafʿ al-Aḥzān), how does al-Kindī adapt Stoic and Platonic cognitive therapy to argue that grief arises not from external events, but from our attachment to perishable possessions?

Chapter 5Al-Fārābī: The Second Teacher

Between Two Masters

In the history of philosophy, certain figures are so comprehensive, so original in their synthesis, that they earn a permanent honorific. Abū Naṣr Muḥammad ibn Muḥammad al-Fārābī (c. 870–950) was called al-Muʿallim alThānī — the Second Teacher — a title that placed him directly after Aristotle, the First Teacher, and signalled that only Aristotle had surpassed him in the depth and breadth of philosophical understanding. No later Muslim philosopher, not even Avicenna, would rival al-Fārābī in the scope of his systematic ambition: he wrote authoritatively on logic, the philosophy of language, physics, metaphysics, psychology, political philosophy, and the theory of prophecy, and he unified them all in a single architectonic vision. If al-Kindī had planted the flag of philosophy on Islamic soil, al-Fārābī built the city. He was born in Fārāb, in present-day Kazakhstan, but spent most of his productive life in Baghdad and, later, at the Ḥamdānid court in Aleppo. He studied under Christian teachers, learned logic from the Alexandrian tradition, and immersed himself in the newly translated corpus of Greek philosophy. Legend has it that he knew seventy languages; more reliably, he was described by contemporaries as an ascetic who lived simply, avoided worldly distractions, and devoted himself entirely to study and writing. He died in Damascus around 950, reportedly accompanying the entourage of the ruler Sayf al-Dawla, for whom he had composed works on music and politics. He left behind a body of work of astonishing range and rigour — commentaries on Aristotle, original treatises, and synthetic masterpieces — that set the agenda for the next three centuries of Islamic philosophy. Al-Fārābī's overarching project was to demonstrate that the philosophy of the Greeks, properly understood, was not only compatible with the religion of the Muslims but provided the rational core that made religion intelligible and universally accessible. He did not argue this as a polemical outsider; he developed it patiently, working out the logical structure of knowledge, the nature of demonstration, the hierarchy of the sciences, the order of the cosmos, the dynamics of the soul, and the structure of the ideal state. By the time he was finished, he had produced the first systematic Islamic philosophy — a coherent Aristotelian-Neoplatonic synthesis that would serve as the foundation for Avicenna, Ibn Rushd, and Maimonides, and that would echo through the Latin scholastics centuries later.

1. The Organon and the Hierarchy of the Sciences Al-Fārābī's philosophy begins not with God or the cosmos but with language and logic. He believed, with Aristotle, that logic is the instrument (organon) of all sciences — the tool that distinguishes truth from error and certain from merely probable knowledge. Without logic, philosophy collapses into opinion. His works on logic are voluminous: commentaries on the entire Aristotelian Organon, including the Categories, On Interpretation, Prior Analytics, Posterior Analytics, Topics, and Sophistical Refutations, along with the Rhetoric and Poetics. His Kitāb alḤurūf (Book of Letters) is a monumental study of the relationship between language, thought, and being that has no real parallel in Greek. Al-Fārābī's most original contribution to logic was his expansion of the syllogistic framework to include the rhetorical and poetic syllogisms, thus incorporating rhetoric and poetics within the demonstrative sciences. Each kind of discourse — demonstrative, dialectical, rhetorical, poetic, sophistical — corresponds to a different level of cognitive certainty and a different audience. Demonstration yields certainty for the expert; dialectic yields persuasive probability for the disputant; rhetoric produces conviction in the multitude through the arousal of emotions and the use of enthymemes; poetry works through imaginative representation (takhayyul) to move the soul. This continuum of logical arts would underpin his political philosophy and his theory of religion, in which

prophets and lawgivers deploy all the logical instruments to guide human communities of varied intellectual capacities. From logic, al-Fārābī moved to the classification of the sciences themselves. In his Iḥṣāʾ al-ʿUlūm (Enumeration of the Sciences), he provided the first systematic map of knowledge in Arabic. The sciences are divided into five broad domains: Linguistic Science: grammar, lexicography, prosody — the study of language as the medium of all other sciences. Logic: the instrument of thought, divided into its parts along the Aristotelian model. Mathematical Sciences: arithmetic, geometry, optics, astronomy, music, the science of weights, and mechanics. Natural Science: physics, the study of the heavens and the sublunary world, the soul, minerals, plants, and animals. Political Science and Metaphysics: the study of human association, law, and governance, culminating in the knowledge of God and the separate intellects. This hierarchy is not merely a catalogue. It reflects a pedagogical and epistemological order. The student moves from the linguistic and logical instruments, through the mathematical and physical disciplines, to the practical science of politics and the theoretical summit of metaphysics. Religion (milla) and jurisprudence (fiqh) are positioned as practical arts subordinate to political philosophy: they are the concrete forms in which philosophical truths are embodied for particular communities and times. This was a bold and controversial claim, for it implied that the philosopher, whose knowledge is demonstrative and universal, stands in a position of epistemic authority over the jurist and the theologian, whose knowledge is dialectical and tied to a specific revealed tradition.

2. Emanation and the Celestial Intellects Al-Fārābī's cosmology is a masterwork of synthesis. He took the Aristotelian vision of a universe moved by an Unmoved Mover and wove it together with the Neoplatonic doctrine of emanation from the Theology of Aristotle and the Liber de Causis — both of which he took, as virtually everyone did, to be genuine works of Aristotle. The result was a hierarchical universe in which existence flows from God through a descending chain of intellects, each of which is simultaneously a cause and a consequence of the one above it. The structure unfolds as follows. God, the First Being, is pure actuality, perfect, simple, and self-sufficient. Because He is perfect, His self-knowledge does not remain sterile; it overflows. From God's contemplation of Himself there proceeds, necessarily and eternally, a First Intellect (al-ʿaql al-awwal). This First Intellect itself exhibits a twofold modal structure — it is necessary through the First Cause (wājib bi-l-awwal) and possible in itself (mumkin bidhātihi) — and therefore has a pluralized object of intellection: insofar as it intellects the First Cause (and its own necessity through the First), it gives rise to the Second Intellect; insofar as it intellects its own contingent essence, it gives rise to the soul and body of the outermost celestial sphere. The Second Intellect, in turn, contemplates God and its own essence, producing a Third Intellect and the sphere of Saturn. The process cascades downward through Jupiter, Mars, the Sun, Venus, Mercury, and the Moon — each intellect generating the next intellect and its associated celestial sphere. The tenth and final intellect in the cosmic descent is the Active Intellect (al-ʿaql al-faʿʿāl), which governs the sublunary world of generation and corruption and which plays the central role in human knowledge that al-Kindī had already described.

This emanationist scheme solved a number of philosophical problems simultaneously. It explained how a perfectly simple God, without deliberation or temporal action, could be the source of a complex and diversified universe. It provided a rational astronomy, in which the movements of the celestial spheres were the visible expressions of intellectual contemplation. It linked cosmology to psychology through the Active Intellect, which stands at the boundary between the celestial and the terrestrial and mediates both existence and knowledge. And it accounted for the hierarchical organisation of being in a way that both Aristotle and the Qur'an could be said to confirm: all things come from God, and the higher rules the lower. Notably, al-Fārābī's emanation is eternal. The universe has no temporal beginning; it flows from God with the same necessity that a conclusion flows from premises. This position, which al-Kindī had denied, would become one of the central issues in the confrontation between the falāsifa and the theologians. Al-Ghazālī would later charge that alFārābī and Avicenna, by affirming the eternity of the world, had effectively denied God's creative freedom and rendered creation indistinguishable from God's own necessity. Al-Fārābī, for his part, did not see this as a problem: God's freedom, he argued, consists precisely in His being the perfect and necessary cause of all that is, not in His being able to choose arbitrarily between alternatives. A God who deliberates and changes His mind is an imperfect God. The perfect God creates eternally and necessarily, and that is the highest form of freedom.

3. The Active Intellect and Human Knowledge The Active Intellect is the pivot of al-Fārābī's epistemology, just as it was for al-Kindī, but al-Fārābī gives it a far more detailed and systematic treatment. The human intellect, in its potential state, is a blank receptive capacity. It becomes actual when the Active Intellect illuminates it — when the intelligible forms that reside in the Active Intellect are impressed upon the human mind. The analogy is with light and vision: just as the sun makes potentially visible objects actually visible by casting light upon them, so the Active Intellect makes potentially intelligible objects actually intelligible by casting its light upon the human soul. Al-Fārābī elaborated the stages of intellection with greater precision than al-Kindī. The human intellect moves from pure potentiality, through the actualisation of particular concepts and propositions, to the level of the acquired intellect (ʿaql mustafād), at which the soul becomes a repository of universal knowledge and approaches the condition of the Active Intellect itself. At the highest stage, when the soul has actualised all its intellectual potential, it achieves a kind of union (ittiṣāl) with the Active Intellect. Whether this union is an identity of substance or a perfected contact is a question al-Fārābī leaves somewhat open, but the goal is clear: the human being who perfects his intellect becomes, in a sense, a terrestrial image of the celestial intelligences, a mind in which the order of the cosmos is reflected and understood. This doctrine had profound implications for the understanding of prophecy. If the Active Intellect is the source of all human knowledge, and if the differences between human beings in knowledge are differences in receptivity to its light, then the prophet differs from the philosopher in degree and scope rather than in kind. The prophet receives the illumination of the Active Intellect in a more complete and immediate way, and the imaginative faculty translates that intellectual overflow into images, narratives, and laws that can guide the community. We shall return to this theory of prophecy shortly.

4. Political Philosophy: The Virtuous City Al-Fārābī's political philosophy is arguably his greatest and most original achievement. He was the first Muslim philosopher to give politics the systematic centrality it had in Plato and Aristotle, and he did so by fusing the Platonic ideal city with the Islamic concept of a community governed by a prophetic lawgiver. His major political works — al-Madīna al-Fāḍila (The Virtuous City), al-Siyāsa al-Madaniyya (The Political Regime), and Fuṣūl

Muntazaʿa (Selected Aphorisms) — are treatises on the principles of political association, the types of cities, the qualities of the ruler, and the conditions for human flourishing. Human beings, al-Fārābī argues, are political by nature. They cannot achieve happiness — which is the perfection of the intellect and the soul's ultimate union with the Active Intellect — in isolation. They require the division of labour, the mutual assistance, and the moral education that only an organised community provides. The city (madīna) is the natural context for the full realisation of human potential. Cities differ according to the conception of happiness they adopt. The virtuous city is one in which the citizens cooperate to achieve true happiness, which is intellectual knowledge of God and the separate intelligences. The ruler of the virtuous city must be a philosopher-king in the fullest sense: he must possess theoretical knowledge of the sciences, practical wisdom regarding governance and law, the physical and moral excellence to lead by example, and — critically — the capacity to translate philosophical truths into the symbolic and persuasive language that the majority of citizens can understand. He must be, in al-Fārābī's terminology, a prophet, a philosopher, a king, and a lawgiver all at once. Al-Fārābī's description of the virtuous ruler is one of the most demanding job descriptions ever written. He must have natural intelligence, a good memory, love of learning, truthfulness, contempt for bodily pleasures, magnanimity, courage, and justice. He must be physically sound and an eloquent speaker. If such a man cannot be found — and al-Fārābī acknowledges the difficulty — rule can pass to a group of individuals who collectively embody the requisite qualities. If even that fails, the virtuous city will decline, and other forms of regime will take its place. The ignorant city (al-madīna al-jāhiliyya) is the generic type of all non-virtuous cities. Its citizens pursue false conceptions of happiness — wealth, pleasure, honour, power, or mere liberty. Al-Fārābī provides a typology that includes the city of necessity, where citizens seek only the bare minimum of survival; the city of wealth, where greed predominates; the city of pleasure, devoted to sensual gratification; the city of honour, where glory and reputation are the ultimate goods; the city of domination, where power and conquest are the aims; and the democratic city, where freedom is valued above all and people are permitted to pursue whatever they wish. Al-Fārābī's treatment of democracy is ambivalent: it is not virtuous, but because it tolerates diversity, it can become the soil in which philosophy and virtue might accidentally flourish. Philosophers, like plants in a meadow, can sometimes grow among the weeds. Beyond the city lies the perfect association of all cities — a federation or world-state under a single philosopherprophet — which is the ultimate political horizon. This universalistic vision, rooted in the ideal of a single human community united under true knowledge, echoes the Islamic concept of the umma while simultaneously transcending it in the direction of a cosmopolitan reason.

5. Prophecy as Perfected Imagination Al-Fārābī's theory of prophecy is the crowning integration of his epistemology, psychology, and political philosophy. It is arguably the first systematic philosophical account of prophecy in any language, and it set the terms for all subsequent discussions in Islam and, through Maimonides, in Judaism. Prophecy, for al-Fārābī, operates at the intersection of intellect and imagination. The Active Intellect floods the prophet's rational soul with intelligible truths. But these pure abstract truths are inaccessible to most human beings, who think in images, narratives, and sensory representations. The prophet's imagination (mutakhayyila), which is naturally of extraordinary power, takes the intellectual overflow and translates it into vivid sensory

symbols — visions, voices, parables, laws, rituals. The prophet's legislation is thus a translation of philosophical universals into the concrete practical terms that a community can understand and follow. The Qur'an itself, on this reading, is the imaginative representation of demonstrative truth, adapted to human language and human categories so that all levels of intellect — the philosophical elite, the dialectical theologians, and the rhetorical multitude — can be addressed. This theory performed several vital functions in al-Fārābī's system. It explained how the same Active Intellect that educates the philosopher also inspires the prophet, preserving the unity of truth. It accounted for the symbolic and narrative character of scripture without reducing it to philosophical allegory. It assigned philosophy the role of understanding what religion means, not of disproving or abandoning it. The philosopher who has attained demonstrative knowledge sees that the symbols of religion are images of the truth he knows directly; the ordinary believer sees the symbols and is guided to virtue through them. Religion is thus philosophy for the many; philosophy is religion understood for the few. This theory was both deeply reverent and profoundly destabilising. It granted religion a crucial and permanent social function — without it, the multitude could not be led to happiness — but it also made religion, in its cognitive content, a derivative and lesser form of knowledge compared to philosophy. The prophet is superior to the philosopher in his practical, imaginative, and political capacities; he is the philosopher's equal in intellectual content, but the content comes from the same Active Intellect. Al-Fārābī would not have put it so crudely, but the implication was clear: the Prophet's revelation and Aristotle's demonstration are two expressions of the same cosmic intelligence. This thesis would earn the philosophers the enduring hostility of those for whom revelation was not a symbolic translation but a unique and irreplaceable divine speech.

6. Music, Logic, and the Arts of the Soul A brief note on an aspect of al-Fārābī's thought that is often overlooked: his philosophy of music. His Kitāb alMūsīqā al-Kabīr (Grand Book of Music) is one of the most important works on music theory in the medieval world, and it was no mere technical manual. Al-Fārābī saw music as a logical art — a branch of the mathematical sciences that also engaged the soul at its deepest layers. Music, like poetry, could move the imagination, arouse emotions, and inculcate virtues or vices. The virtuous ruler would therefore legislate music as carefully as he legislated law, understanding that the soul's formation is not only a matter of argument but also of rhythm, melody, and tone. In this, al-Fārābī echoed Plato's Republic and anticipated the extensive Islamic literature on the spiritual effects of music that would culminate in the Sufi samāʿ.

7. The Second Teacher's Legacy Al-Fārābī died around 950, leaving behind a philosophical edifice whose influence was almost impossible to overstate. Avicenna read him carefully and credited him with finally making the Metaphysics of Aristotle intelligible; his own work, for all its originality, stands on Fārābīan foundations. Ibn Rushd, in distant Andalusia, drew on al-Fārābī's logical treatises and his defence of the harmony between philosophy and religion. Maimonides, writing to a Jewish audience, declared that all the works of al-Fārābī were "fine flour" and that his logical writings in particular were indispensable. In the Latin West, al-Fārābī was known as "Alpharabius" and his Enumeration of the Sciences was translated and studied, shaping scholastic classifications of knowledge. But perhaps his most profound legacy was the intellectual confidence he bequeathed to the generation after him. Al-Kindī had shown that philosophy could speak Arabic and think Islamically. Al-Fārābī showed that it could do so systematically, comprehensively, and with an authority that challenged the other claimants to wisdom — the jurists, the theologians, the grammarians, the mystics — on their own ground. He placed philosophy at the summit

of the sciences and made the philosopher the ideal ruler of the ideal state, not as a usurper of the prophet's mantle but as the one who understood it best. He left the tradition with a map of reality — logical, physical, metaphysical, political — whose coordinates would guide all who came after. Before turning in Chapter 7 to the popular Neoplatonic-Pythagorean encyclopaedism of the Brethren of Purity (Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ) and in Chapter 8 to the ethical humanism of Ibn Miskawayh, we turn next in Chapter 6 to the vibrant tenth-century philosophical rivals and successors of al-Fārābī: the radical empiricist and anti-Aristotelian physician-philosopher Abū Bakr al-Rāzī, the interconfessional Baghdad Peripatetic School of Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī and Abū Sulaymān al-Sijistānī, and the eastern Kindian synthesis of Abū al-Ḥasan al-ʿĀmirī.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “When the Active Intellect emanates intelligible truths onto the passive intellect of this supreme human ruler, he becomes a wise philosopher; and when it emanates those same truths onto his perfected imaginative faculty (alquwwa al-mutakhayyila), which translates them into vivid sensory symbols and laws capable of guiding the multitude toward happiness, he becomes a prophet and warner (nabī mundhir).” — Abū Naṣr al-Fārābī, Mabādiʾ Ārāʾ Ahl al-Madīna al-Fāḍila (Principles of the Views of the Citizens of the Virtuous City)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. Why was al-Fārābī honored across the Islamic world as 'The Second Teacher' (al-Muʿallim al-Thānī), and how did his Enumeration of the Sciences (Iḥṣāʾ al-ʿUlūm) position jurisprudence (fiqh) and dialectical theology (kalām) relative to demonstrative philosophy? 2. Trace the structure of al-Fārābī's ten-intellect emanationist cosmology. How does the First Intellect's twofold contemplation (of the First Cause and of its own essence) generate both the subsequent intellects and the celestial spheres? 3. How does al-Fārābī's psychological theory of the imaginative faculty (al-takhayyul) explain how one and the same universal philosophical truth can be grasped demonstratively by philosophers and represented symbolically as religion (milla) for the general public? 4. Compare al-Fārābī's taxonomy of the Virtuous City (al-madīna al-fāḍila) and the four defective cities (ignorant, wicked, errant, and turncoat) with Plato's Republic. What happens to the souls of citizens of the ignorant cities after death in al-Fārābī's account?

Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1985. Al-Fārābī. The Political Writings: 'Selected Aphorisms' and Other Texts. Translated by Charles E. Butterworth. Ithaca, NY: Cornell University Press, 2001.
