Chapter 6Freethinkers, Empiricists, and the Tenth-Century Baghdad School: Abū Bakr al-Rāzī, Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī, al-Sijistānī, and al-ʿĀmirī

Beyond the Fārābīan Highway

Standard textbook narratives of classical Islamic philosophy often move in a single straight line from al-Kindī to alFārābī and from al-Fārābī to Avicenna, leaving the impression that tenth-century philosophy in the Islamic heartlands was monolithic in its Aristotelian-Neoplatonic commitments. Nothing could be further from the historical reality. The tenth century — dubbed by Joel Kraemer and Adam Mez the 'Renaissance of Islam' under the Buyid amirate — was the most pluralistic, experimental, and combative century in early Islamic intellectual history. Alongside al-Fārābī's austere Peripateticism, at least three major philosophical currents competed for intellectual allegiance in Rayy, Baghdad, and Nishapur: the radical anti-Aristotelian empiricism and Platonic atomism of Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī; the interconfessional Christian and Muslim Aristotelian salon culture of the Baghdad School led by Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī and Abū Sulaymān al-Sijistānī; and the Neo-Kindian apologetic synthesis championed in eastern Iran by Abū al-Ḥasan al-ʿĀmirī.

1. Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī (Rhazes): The Empiricist Philosopher-Physician Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī (c. 865–925 CE), known to medieval Latin Europe as Rhazes, was born in Rayy near modern Tehran and directed the major hospitals of Rayy and Baghdad. In the history of medicine, his encyclopedic al-Ḥāwī fī al-Ṭibb (Liber Continens) and his clinical treatise on smallpox and measles (Kitāb al-Judarī wa-l-Ḥaṣba) established him as the greatest clinical observer of the medieval world. Yet al-Rāzī insisted that he was not merely a physician who dabbled in philosophy, but a true philosopher who walked in the footsteps of Socrates and Plato rather than Aristotle. When critics accused him of failing to follow the ascetic lifestyle of Socrates, he penned his delightful philosophical apologia al-Sīra al-Falsafiyya (The Philosophical Way of Life), arguing that the mature Socrates participated in civic life, raised children, and pursued moderation rather than Hindu or Manichaean self-mortification.

The Metaphysics of the Five Eternals (al-Qudamāʾ al-Khamsa) Against both the Muʿtazilī theologians who defended creation ex nihilo and the Peripatetics who defended the eternal emanation of the cosmos from a single Necessary First Cause, al-Rāzī formulated a bold pluralist ontology grounded in the Platonic Timaeus and pre-Socratic atomism. He argued that a world characterized by temporal origin, imperfection, pain, and physical mutability cannot proceed directly from a purely wise and immutable Creator without pre-existing principles. He therefore posited Five Eternal Principles (al-qudamāʾ al-khamsa): The Creator (al-Bāriʾ): Purely wise, merciful, and omniscient, from whom order, life, and intelligence proceed. The Universal Soul (al-Nafs al-Kulliyya): Living and active, yet originally ignorant and enamored of matter; its passionate desire to unite with prime matter to experience sensible pleasure is the cosmic trigger for the creation of the physical universe. Prime Matter (al-Hayūlā al-Ūlā): Composed of indivisible atoms (ajzāʾ lā tatajazzaʾ) dispersed in empty space; prior to the Soul's intervention, matter is unformed and inert. Absolute Space (al-Makān al-Muṭlaq / al-Khalāʾ): Against Aristotle's definition of place as the inner boundary of the containing body, al-Rāzī defended a self-subsistent, infinite three-dimensional void that exists independently of the bodies occupying it.

Absolute Time (al-Zamān al-Muṭlaq / al-Dahr): Against Aristotle's claim that time is merely the measure of motion, al-Rāzī distinguished between relative, measured time (zamān maḥṣūr) and absolute, eternal duration (dahr / mudda) that flows independently of celestial motion. In al-Rāzī's cosmic theodicy, when the ignorant Soul tangled itself in Prime Matter and fell into suffering, the merciful Creator intervened by endowing the world with regular natural order and sending the Intellect (al-ʿaql) — not as a sixth eternal principle, but as a divine gift of awakening — to remind human souls of their true immaterial home. Philosophy is the sole vehicle of salvation: through rational inquiry and ethical self-mastery, the soul emancipates itself from bodily addiction and returns to its pure spiritual state.

Al-Ṭibb al-Rūḥānī, Methodological Doubts, and the Critique of Prophecy In his ethical masterpiece al-Ṭibb al-Rūḥānī (The Spiritual Physic), al-Rāzī developed a clinical cognitive therapy for the passions, treating arrogance, envy, anger, greed, excessive erotic attachment (ʿishq), and the fear of death as psychological pathologies to be healed by rational analysis. Even more revolutionary was his philosophy of science in al-Shukūk ʿalā Jālīnūs (Doubts Concerning Galen). Opening the book with a defense of intellectual independence, al-Rāzī declared that philosophy and medicine are cumulative sciences: to revere ancient authorities like Galen or Aristotle by refusing to test their claims against new empirical observations is a betrayal of those masters' own spirit. It was this radical egalitarianism of reason that led al-Rāzī to his most controversial position, preserved in the refutation Aʿlām al-Nubuwwa (Signs of Prophecy) by his fellow citizen, the Ismāʿīlī philosopher Abū Ḥātim al-Rāzī (d. c. 934). Abū Bakr al-Rāzī argued that God, in His universal justice and mercy, has endowed all human beings equally with innate reason (ʿaql) capable of discovering practical arts, moral truths, and metaphysical realities. Singling out specific individuals as infallible prophets, he contended, introduces sectarian warfare and blind obedience (taqlīd), whereas universal human reason unites humanity across cultures. Though fiercely rejected by Sunnī, Shīʿī, and Peripatetic thinkers alike, al-Rāzī's arguments forced subsequent Islamic philosophers to construct far more rigorous epistemological defenses of revelation.

2. The Tenth-Century Baghdad School: Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī, al-Sijistānī, and al-Tawḥīdī While al-Rāzī championed Platonic empiricism in Rayy, Baghdad remained the capital of Aristotelian logic and commentary through an extraordinary interconfessional circle of Christian and Muslim philosophers. When alFārābī departed Baghdad for Syria in 942, the leadership of the Baghdad Peripatetic school passed to his foremost disciple, the Syriac Jacobite Christian philosopher and theologian Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī (893–974 CE). Trained under both Mattā ibn Yūnus and al-Fārābī, Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī compiled critical editions of the Arabic Organon, employed Aristotelian categories of intellect, intelligizer, and intelligible (ʿāqil, ʿaql, maʿqūl) to defend the Christian Trinity against al-Kindī's polemics, and authored a celebrated treatise on Tahdhīb al-Akhlāq (The Reformation of Morals). In the Tahdhīb, Yaḥyā articulated a cosmopolitan humanist ideal: all human beings share a single rational soul (nafs nāṭiqa) and therefore constitute a single universal family bound by the duty of mutual affection (maḥabba) and moral refinement. After Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī's death, the mantle of the Baghdad philosophical circle passed to his Muslim student Abū Sulaymān Muḥammad ibn Ṭāhir al-Sijistānī (c. 912–985 CE), known as al-Manṭiqī ('the Logician'). Convening a celebrated salon at his home in Baghdad patronized by the Buyid ruler ʿAḍud al-Dawla and King Abū Jaʿfar Bānūya of Sīstān, al-Sijistānī drew a firm boundary between philosophy and religious law. Critiquing the Brethren of Purity (Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ) for trying to blend Greek philosophy and Islamic sharīʿa into a single syncretic alloy, al-Sijistānī argued that philosophy is the autonomous quest of human reason moving from below upward through

demonstration, whereas prophecy is a divine gift descending from above through revelation; each has its own integrity, and confusing their methods corrupts both. The vibrant conversations of al-Sijistānī's circle were immortalized by his devoted student and literary companion Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī (c. 930–1023 CE) in two masterpieces of Arabic philosophical prose: Kitāb al-Imtāʿ wa-lMuʾānasa (The Book of Enjoyment and Bonhomie, recording thirty-seven nightly philosophical sessions with the Buyid vizier Ibn Saʿdān) and al-Muqābasāt (The Borrowings, recording over a hundred philosophical discussions among Baghdad's leading minds). Together with his exchange of questions and answers with Ibn Miskawayh in alHawāmil wa-l-Shawāmil, al-Tawḥīdī's writings show that tenth-century falsafa was not an isolated monastic exercise, but a public, dialogical humanism deeply engaged with grammar, aesthetics, psychology, and social ethics.

3. Abū al-Ḥasan al-ʿĀmirī and the Eastern Kindian Tradition In the eastern provinces of Khurāsān and Transoxiana, meanwhile, al-Kindī's legacy was carried forward by his brilliant student Abū Zayd Aḥmad ibn Sahl al-Balkhī (850–934 CE) — a polymath geographer, psychologist, and author of Maṣāliḥ al-Abdān wa-l-Anfus (Sustenance for Bodies and Souls, which pioneered the clinical distinction between endogenous clinical depression and situational sadness) — and by al-Balkhī's student Abū al-Ḥasan Muḥammad ibn Yūsuf al-ʿĀmirī (d. 992 CE) of Nishapur. Unlike the Baghdad Aristotelians who kept philosophy and theology in separate compartments, al-ʿĀmirī dedicated his career to demonstrating the substantive harmony between Neoplatonic-Aristotelian metaphysics and orthodox Sunnī Islam. In al-Amad ʿalā al-Abad (On the Afterlife), he defended both the individual immortality of the rational soul and the reality of bodily resurrection against radical Peripatetics. In his landmark comparative treatise Kitāb al-Iʿlām bi-Manāqib al-Islām (An Exposition of the Merits of Islam), al-ʿĀmirī constructed a systematic philosophy of religion, comparing Islam with Judaism, Christianity, Zoroastrianism, Manichaeism, and polytheism across rational, ethical, political, and civilizational criteria, and arguing that authentic demonstrative wisdom (ḥikma) serves as the handmaiden and confirmation of prophetic revelation. It was in the libraries of Bukhara and Khurāsān stocked with al-ʿĀmirī's and al-Fārābī's works that the young Avicenna would soon begin his own philosophical education.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “The Creator — exalted be His name — gave us the intellect only so that we might attain through it the utmost of what lies within human power in this world and the next. Whoever looks deeply into the subtle sciences and exercises his own rational judgment, even if he errs in certain particulars, is closer to God's pleasure than one who blindly imitates authorities without investigation; for if the ancients had not examined the errors of their predecessors, no science would ever have reached completion.” — Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī, al-Sīra al-Falsafiyya (The Philosophical Way of Life) and al-Shukūk ʿalā Jālīnūs (Doubts Concerning Galen)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How does Abū Bakr al-Rāzī's doctrine of the Five Eternals (al-qudamāʾ al-khamsa) attempt to solve the problem of evil and temporal origination without accepting either creation ex nihilo or Neoplatonic emanation? 2. Why did Abū Sulaymān al-Sijistānī criticize the Brethren of Purity (Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ) for attempting to merge Greek philosophy and Islamic religious law (sharīʿa) into a single system? Do you find al-Sijistānī's separation thesis or al-ʿĀmirī's integrationist thesis more philosophically persuasive? 3. In what ways does Abū Bakr al-Rāzī's al-Shukūk ʿalā Jālīnūs (Doubts Concerning Galen) anticipate modern falsificationist and cumulative models of the philosophy of science?

4. How did Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī use Aristotelian psychology (the identity of intellect, intelligizer, and intelligible) to construct a Christian philosophical theology in Arabic, and what does his leadership of the Baghdad School reveal about the interconfessional nature of classical falsafa?

Provo, UT: Brigham Young University Press, 2011. Ibn ʿAdī, Yaḥyā. The Reformation of Morals (Tahdhīb al-Akhlāq). Translated by Sidney H. Griffith. Provo, UT: Brigham Young University Press, 2002. Chapter 7The Brethren of Purity: The Philosophical Encyclopaedists

A Mystery in Fifty-Two Epistles

Sometime in the middle of the tenth century — the exact date is disputed, but the most careful scholarship places it around 960 to 980 — a remarkable set of texts began to circulate in the intellectual circles of Basra and Baghdad. They were called the Rasāʾil Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ wa Khullān al-Wafāʾ, the Epistles of the Brethren of Purity and the Faithful Friends. Their authors were anonymous, their tone was urgent and missionary, and their scope was breathtaking: fifty-two epistles covering mathematics, logic, natural philosophy, psychology, metaphysics, and the mystical sciences, all presented as a unified curriculum of salvation. The Rasāʾil were, in effect, a popular encyclopaedia of philosophy and science, written not for the specialist scholar but for the literate, curious, spiritually restless public. They represent the democratisation of philosophy in the Islamic world — an attempt to make the wisdom of the ancients, harmonised with Islamic piety, accessible to all who were willing to learn. Who were the Brethren of Purity? They did not sign their names. They described themselves only as a group of friends united in the pursuit of truth, who had formed a kind of intellectual and spiritual brotherhood. They met in secret, they studied together, and they produced their epistles as a collective work, deliberately suppressing individual authorship. Over the centuries, speculation about their identity has been intense: were they Ismāʿīlī missionaries, using philosophy as a cover for esoteric Shīʿī doctrines? Were they Muʿtazilī rationalists? Were they simply independent intellectuals, disillusioned with the sectarian conflicts of their time and seeking a universal wisdom that transcended all schools? Scholars still debate the question, but the consensus is that the Brethren were likely associated with, or at least sympathetic to, the Ismāʿīlī movement, though their epistles were too eclectic, too open-minded, and too widely popular to be simply party propaganda. They were philosophers of a populist and spiritual temper, and their influence far exceeded the boundaries of any single sect.

This chapter explores who the Brethren were, what they taught, and why their encyclopaedic project mattered. Their work is less rigorous than al-Fārābī's, less original in its metaphysics than Avicenna's would be, but it is a unique window into how philosophy was received by the educated middle classes of the Islamic world — the merchants, the secretaries, the courtiers, the curious — and how it was woven into a vision of spiritual ascent that combined Pythagoras and the Qur'an, Plotinus and the Prophet, in a single colourful tapestry.

1. The Structure and Purpose of the Epistles The Rasāʾil comprise fifty-two epistles organised into four large sections, a structure that mirrors the hierarchical order of knowledge the Brethren had inherited from the Alexandrian schools and systematised in the spirit of alFārābī: The Mathematical Sciences (14 epistles): number, geometry, astronomy, music, geography, the theoretical and practical arts, and the nature of crafts and professions. The Natural Sciences (17 epistles): matter and form, generation and corruption, minerals, plants, animals, the human body, the senses, life and death, and the structure of the cosmos. The Rational Soul (10 epistles): the intellectual principles, the nature of the human soul, the levels of knowledge, the relationship between microcosm and macrocosm, and the path of spiritual purification. The Divine-Legal Sciences (11 epistles): belief and doctrine, the meaning of prophethood, the nature of religious law, the spiritual meanings of ritual, the occult sciences, and the final stages of the soul's journey back to God. The Brethren prefaced the whole with an introductory epistle that set out their educational philosophy. They argued that the human being, made in the image of the cosmos, carries within himself all the levels of being: the mineral, the vegetable, the animal, the rational, and the angelic. Education is the process of actualising these latent capacities, moving by disciplined stages from the lower sciences to the higher. The epistles were to be studied in order, with each section preparing the soul for the next. The goal was nothing less than the complete transformation of the student — from a creature driven by appetite and convention to a perfected soul capable of returning to its divine origin. The Brethren were acutely aware that they were writing for a public that might be suspicious of philosophy. They took pains to present their teachings as a recovery of ancient wisdom — the wisdom of the prophets, the sages of India and Persia, the Greek philosophers, and the Qur'an itself — which had become fragmented and obscured by sectarianism and literalism. Their ambition was to reassemble the scattered fragments into a single, universal science, which they called ḥikma, wisdom. In a famous passage, they described the ideal human being as "a Persian in lineage, an Arab in faith, a Ḥanīf in religion, an Iraqi in culture, a Hebrew in experience, a Christian in conduct, a Syrian in piety, a Greek in knowledge, an Indian in discernment, and a Sufi in way of life." This was not syncretism for its own sake; it was a philosophical cosmopolitanism rooted in the conviction that truth is one, and that the wise recognise it wherever it appears.

2. Neoplatonic and Pythagorean Cosmology The cosmology of the Brethren is an elaborate and colourful variation of the Neoplatonic emanation scheme. At the summit is God, the One, who is beyond all description, beyond being, beyond intellect — pure unity from which everything flows. From God emanates the Universal Intellect (al-ʿaql al-kullī), the first created being, in which the forms of all things are contained. From the Universal Intellect emanates the Universal Soul (al-nafs al-kulliyya), which animates the cosmos and is the intermediary between the intellectual and the material worlds. From the

Universal Soul emanates Prime Matter (al-hayūlā al-ūlā), the receptive substrate of all bodies. And from Prime Matter, shaped by the forms descending through the Soul, come the four elements, the minerals, the plants, the animals, and finally the human being, who recapitulates the whole cosmic hierarchy within himself. What distinguishes the Brethren's version of this familiar scheme is its deep saturation in Pythagorean number symbolism and its vivid, almost poetic, sense of cosmic sympathy. They were fascinated by the mathematical structure of reality. Numbers were not mere abstractions but the principles of created things. The number one is the principle of unity; two, of duality and opposition; three, of the first complete whole; four, of the four elements and the four humours; seven, of the planets and the days of the week; twelve, of the signs of the zodiac — and so on through an intricate web of correspondences that linked arithmetic to geometry, music to astronomy, and the movements of the planets to the humours of the body and the faculties of the soul. The universe, in their vision, is a vast musical instrument, tuned to mathematical ratios, and the wise person is one who has learned to hear the harmony. This Pythagorean and Neoplatonic framework was given an Islamic colouring. The Universal Intellect was identified with the Pen (al-qalam) mentioned in the Qur'an ("He taught by the Pen," Q 96:4), and the Universal Soul with the Guarded Tablet (al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ, Q 85:22) on which all destinies are inscribed. The descent of being from God was thus reframed as a divine act of writing and inscription, a metaphor that would have deep resonance in the Islamic tradition. The cosmos itself is a revelation; the study of nature, for the Brethren, is a reading of God's signs that complements and illuminates the verbal revelation of scripture.

3. The Human Soul: Microcosm and Ascent The central drama of the Brethren's philosophy is the story of the human soul — its fall into matter, its forgetfulness of its true home, and its gradual return through knowledge and purification. The human being is a microcosm (ʿālam ṣaghīr), a little world in which all the forces of the greater cosmos are concentrated. The body corresponds to the earth, the senses to the planets, the rational soul to the celestial intelligences, and the spirit to the divine light. To know oneself is to know the universe — and ultimately to know God. The soul, in its original state, was a pure intellectual substance dwelling in the intelligible realm. But it descended into the material body, seduced by its attractions, and became trapped in the prison of the senses. The body is not evil — the Brethren were no Gnostics who despised the material world — but it is a veil that obscures the soul's vision. The goal of human life is to wake from the dream of material existence, to recover the memory of one's divine origin, and to undertake the ascent back to the intelligible realm. The path of ascent is structured precisely according to the curriculum of the epistles. The student begins with the mathematical disciplines, which purify the mind from the confusion of the senses and accustom it to abstract thought. He then moves to natural science, which reveals the order and wisdom of creation and awakens awe and love for its Maker. He proceeds to rational psychology, in which he studies the nature of his own soul and learns to distinguish the true self from the bodily appetites. Finally, he enters the divine sciences, in which he contemplates the celestial hierarchies, the nature of prophecy, the symbolic meanings of religious law, and the ultimate reality of God. At the summit of this journey, the soul achieves a state of illumination in which it sees all things in light of the One, its knowledge unified with its knowing, its being merged with the intelligible world. This union, the Brethren insisted, was not a loss of individuality but its perfection — the soul becomes fully itself when it becomes fully united with its source. The Brethren placed particular emphasis on the practical dimension of this ascent. Knowledge without moral purification is sterile and even dangerous. The student must practice asceticism — not the extreme asceticism of

the desert hermit but a disciplined moderation in food, sleep, and worldly pursuits. He must cultivate the virtues of humility, truthfulness, patience, and love. He must associate with like-minded seekers, for the Brethren believed that spiritual progress was a collective enterprise. The very name "Brethren of Purity" expressed their conviction that the bonds of friendship, built on shared pursuit of wisdom, were the truest form of human association. The isolated sage was a contradiction in terms; philosophy was meant to be lived in community.

4. The Animal Fable and Ecological Ethics One of the most famous and striking sections of the Rasāʾil is the lengthy fable known as "The Case of the Animals versus Man," which occupies the epistle on animals in the natural sciences section. It is an extraordinary work of philosophical imagination, and it deserves special attention for its ethical and ecological implications, which were centuries ahead of their time. The fable is set on an island where all the animals have gathered to bring a lawsuit against the human race. The animals complain to the king of the Jinn, who serves as judge, that humans have enslaved them, killed them, eaten their flesh, taken their skins, and treated them with cruel contempt. The humans, they argue, claim superiority by virtue of their intellect, their arts, and their supposed divine favour, but these claims are hollow. The animals point out that they too possess intelligence, social organisation, and moral virtues — often in greater measure than humans. The bee builds more ingeniously than any human architect; the ant shows greater justice and cooperation in its community; the dog exhibits a loyalty that puts human friendship to shame. The animals deploy philosophical arguments, scriptural citations, and moral reasoning to make their case. The humans respond with the classic arguments for human exceptionalism: reason, language, the soul's immortality, the divine command to subdue the earth. But the animals counter each point with wit and wisdom. The debate ranges over theology, cosmology, ethics, and politics, and the Brethren make it clear that the animals often get the better of the exchange. At the climax of the trial (Epistle 22, edited and translated by Lenn E. Goodman and Richard McGregor), after the animal spokespersons have dismantled every human boast regarding physical superiority, political institutions, technology, and worldly sciences, a final speaker rises from the human delegation to point not to kings, jurists, or engineers, but to the friends of God, prophets, and pure-hearted sages (awliyāʾ Allāh wa-l-ḥukamāʾ) — those rare human beings whose purified souls ascend to angelic immortality. On account of the saints and realized sages alone, the King of the Jinn rules that the animals shall remain entrusted to human care until the completion of the cosmic cycle, transforming what began as a claim of anthropocentric dominion into a humbling moral stewardship accountable to the highest spiritual ideal. This epistle has been compared, with justice, to the animal fables of the Indian Pañcatantra and the Greek Aesop, but its philosophical ambition is greater than either. It is not merely a collection of moral tales but a sustained argument for a humane and ecologically sensitive ethic. The Brethren were, in their own way, environmental philosophers. They argued that the cosmos is a single living being, in which all parts are interconnected, and that cruelty to animals is not only a vice but a violation of the cosmic order. Their position was rooted in Neoplatonic and Islamic principles: the world is God's creation, and every creature manifests some aspect of divine wisdom and mercy. To despise any part of creation is to despise its Maker. The influence of this fable was considerable. It was translated into Persian and enjoyed a wide readership across the Islamic world. Its themes resurfaced in later Islamic literature, in the animal fables of Jalāl al-Dīn Rūmī, and in the popular genre of adab (belles-lettres) that used animal speech to convey moral and political critique. For modern readers, it represents one of the earliest and most eloquent voices in a tradition of ecological thought that the world is only now recovering.

5. Knowledge, Salvation, and the Attack on Literalism The Brethren were not only encyclopaedists; they were critics of the society in which they lived. Their epistles contain sustained polemics against the religious scholars of their day — the jurists, the literalist theologians, the dogmatic sectarians — whom they accused of reducing religion to outward ritual and legal hair-splitting, while neglecting the inner, spiritual meanings. They called these scholars "sleepers in the couch of ignorance" and "merchants of religion" who used their learning for worldly status. The Brethren were, in this respect, close to the Sufi tradition, which had long insisted on the superiority of inner purification over external observance, and they were also heirs to the Muʿtazilī critique of literalism. But they went further than either in their open embrace of philosophical allegory as the key to scripture. For the Brethren, the Qur'an and the revealed laws of all prophets were symbolic representations of philosophical truths. Paradise was not a literal garden but the soul's state of intellectual illumination and closeness to God. Hell was not a literal fire but the torment of ignorance and separation. The resurrection was not the reanimation of corpses but the soul's awakening from the death of material attachment. They did not deny the literal meaning of scripture — it had its proper role for the masses — but they insisted that the literal meaning was the outer shell (qishr) and the inner meaning (lubb) was the philosophical kernel. The duty of the wise was to crack the shell and share the kernel with those ready to receive it. This hermeneutical position placed the Brethren at odds with the more literalist trends in Islam and also, to some extent, with the Peripatetic philosophers, who generally maintained a more reserved public posture on the allegorical interpretation of scripture. The Brethren's openness about their esotericism has been one reason why scholars have long suspected Ismāʿīlī connections: the Ismāʿīlīs were famous for their doctrine of the outer (ẓāhir) and inner (bāṭin) meanings of scripture, and for their graded initiation of believers into deeper levels of knowledge. But the Brethren lack the specific doctrinal features of Ismāʿīlism — the imamate, the cyclical history of prophets, the hierarchical structure of the daʿwa — and their tone is consistently universalist and syncretic rather than sectarian. It may be safest to say that they inhabited the broad intellectual current out of which Ismāʿīlism also drew, a current in which Neoplatonic emanationism, Pythagorean number mysticism, and Islamic esotericism flowed together.

6. Legacy: Democratising Philosophy The Rasāʾil enjoyed an extraordinary circulation. They spread from Basra and Baghdad across Persia, Central Asia, and the Maghreb, and were introduced into al-Andalus around the turn of the eleventh century by the mathematician-astronomer Maslama al-Majrīṭī (d. c. 1007) and his disciple Abū al-Ḥakam al-Kirmānī (d. 1066), where they fertilized an already receptive Andalusian esoteric soil prepared earlier by Ibn Masarra (883–931) and profoundly influenced Ibn al-Sīd al-Baṭalyawsī (d. 1127), Ibn Bājja, Ibn Ṭufayl, Ibn ʿArabī, and the Jewish Neoplatonists Solomon ibn Gabirol and Baḥya ibn Paqūda. They were translated into Persian, and sections appeared in Indian and Ottoman libraries. The Spanish Jewish philosopher Baḥya ibn Paqūda, in his Duties of the Heart, drew on the Brethren's ethical psychology. The encyclopaedic impulse they represented — the desire to present all knowledge in a comprehensive and accessible format — had a long afterlife in Islamic civilisation, from the Mafātīḥ al-ʿUlūm of al-Khwārizmī to the great Ottoman encyclopaedias of the seventeenth century. For the history of philosophy, the Brethren's most important contribution was their role as popularisers. The Rasāʾil were not intended as original contributions to the technical debates of the falāsifa; they did not break new ground in logic or metaphysics. But they brought philosophy out of the court and the specialist circle and into the wider literate public. They made it plausible to be a philosophically informed Muslim without being a professional

scholar of Aristotle. They argued, with passion and eloquence, that the pursuit of wisdom was not a dangerous foreign import but the deepest meaning of the Islamic faith itself. In this, they were enormously successful. For every one person who read Avicenna's Shifāʾ, there were probably a hundred who encountered philosophy through the Rasāʾil, even if they did not know the authors' names. The Brethren also represented a distinctive temperament in Muslim thought: one that valued synthesis over criticism, ecumenical spirituality over polemics, and the inner transformation of the soul over the formal mastery of texts. They were philosophers of the soul more than philosophers of the syllogism. Their vision of a universal wisdom, shared by the prophets and the sages of all nations, offered a way of being both a committed Muslim and a citizen of the intellectual world — a combination that would prove attractive to generations of readers and that remains, in many quarters, appealing today.

7. Ismaili Philosophical Neoplatonism: Abū Yaʿqūb al-Sijistānī, Ḥamīd al-Dīn al-Kirmānī, and Nāṣir-i Khusraw Closely related in spirit to the Epistles of the Brethren of Purity — yet distinct in its rigorous theological architecture — was the philosophical tradition of tenth- and eleventh-century Ismaili Neoplatonism. Developed by missionary-philosophers (dāʿīs) operating across Khurāsān, Sīstān, Rayy, Iraq, and the Fatimid capital of Cairo, this movement forged one of the most original metaphysical syntheses in Islamic history by marrying Plotinian negative theology and Fārābīan cosmology to Shīʿī esoteric hermeneutics (taʾwīl). Four thinkers stand at the summit of this tradition: Abū Ḥātim al-Rāzī (d. 934) and Muḥammad ibn Aḥmad al-Nasafī (d. 943): The pioneers of eastern Persian Ismaili Neoplatonism. While al-Nasafī's Kitāb al-Maḥṣūl introduced Neoplatonic emanation into Ismaili cosmology, Abū Ḥātim al-Rāzī's Kitāb al-Iṣlāḥ (The Book of Correction) and Aʿlām al-Nubuwwa (Signs of Prophecy) defended the necessity of prophetic revelation and the hierarchy of cosmic intellects against the empiricist freethinker Abū Bakr al-Rāzī (see Chapter 6). Abū Yaʿqūb al-Sijistānī (d. after 971): In Kitāb al-Yanābīʿ (The Book of Wellsprings) and Kashf al-Maḥjūb (Unveiling of the Hidden), al-Sijistānī formulated a radical apophatic theology of double negation (nafy wanafy al-nafy): to safeguard divine transcendence (tanzīh) against both anthropomorphism (tashbīh) and philosophical conceptualism, one must first negate all corporeal attributes of God ('God is not a thing, not limited, not in time') and then negate even His transcendent negations ('God is not non-thing, not beyondbeing'), because the Originator (al-Mubdiʿ) transcends the very opposition between Being and Non-Being. Creation proceeds not through gradualemanation from God's essence, but through a single, timeless Creative Command (al-amr / al-kalima) or Origination (ibdāʿ) that brings the First Intellect (al-ʿaql al-awwal / alsābiq) into fullness of being at a stroke. Ḥamīd al-Dīn al-Kirmānī (d. after 1020): Chief dāʿī of Iraq and Iran (Ḥujjat al-ʿIrāqayn) summoned to Cairo during the reign of the Fatimid Caliph al-Ḥākim, al-Kirmānī authored the masterpiece Rāḥat al-ʿAql (Peace of the Intellect, 1020). Dissatisfied with the older Plotinian triad of One–Intellect–Soul, al-Kirmānī replaced it with an original ten-intellect cosmology adapted from al-Fārābī and calibrated to the hierarchy of the Fatimid daʿwa, while insisting that the First Intellect is originated out of nothing (ibdāʿ) rather than emanated (fayḍ) from the Divine Essence. Nāṣir-i Khusraw (1004–c. 1077): The greatest Persian-language Ismaili philosopher and poet. Writing all his philosophical works in crystalline Persian from his exile in Badakhshān, Nāṣir-i Khusraw composed Jāmiʿ alḤikmatayn (The Harmony of the Two Wisdoms, 1070) as a systematic commentary on the philosophical poem of

Abū al-Haytham al-Jurjānī, demonstrating point by point how the demonstrative wisdom of the Greek philosophers (ḥikmat-i yūnāniyān) and the esoteric wisdom of prophetic revelation (ḥikmat-i dīnī) converge upon the same universal truth.

8. From the Brethren and the Ismaili Philosophers to Ibn Miskawayh and Avicenna The Brethren of Purity bring to a close the first major phase of Islamic philosophy. We have now seen the translation movement provide the texts, the Muʿtazila erect a rationalist theology, al-Kindī demonstrate the harmony of Greek wisdom and Islamic revelation, al-Fārābī build the first comprehensive philosophical system, and the Brethren popularise philosophy as a spiritual path. The stage is now set for the figure who would take all these threads and weave them into the single most influential philosophical synthesis in the history of Islam: Abū ʿAlī alḤusayn ibn Sīnā, known to the West as Avicenna. But before we turn to Avicenna, we must examine one more figure from the Buyid period who made his mark on a different front: Ibn Miskawayh, the philosopher of ethical refinement. Where al-Fārābī had focused on logic, metaphysics, and the ideal state, Ibn Miskawayh devoted his most important work to the cultivation of individual character — the practical question of how a human being becomes virtuous, happy, and wise. His Tahdhīb alAkhlāq (The Refinement of Character) is the foundational text of Islamic philosophical ethics, and it will provide the transition from the grand systems to the intimate work of the soul. We turn to him next.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “Know, O brother — may God support you and us with a spirit from Himself — that our Brethren have examined all the revealed laws and philosophical sciences without fanaticism, seeing the human being as a microcosm (alʿālam al-ṣaghīr) in whom all the potencies of the macrocosm are gathered, and viewing the material body as a ship upon the ocean of nature by which the rational soul journeys back to its eternal abode in the Realm of the Spirit.” — Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ, Rasāʾil Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ wa-Khullān al-Wafāʾ (Epistles of the Brethren of Purity)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How did the Brethren of Purity (Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ) organize their fifty-two Epistles into a four-stage pedagogical curriculum (mathematical, physical, psychological-intellectual, and theological-legal), and how does this order mirror the soul's spiritual ascent? 2. Why did the Brethren give primacy to Neopythagorean number theory and musical harmony as the gateway to metaphysics, and how did they correlate the numbers 1 through 4 with God, the Universal Intellect, the Universal Soul, and Prime Matter? 3. Analyze the philosophical message of Epistle 22, The Case of the Animals versus Man Before the King of the Jinn. If animals surpass human beings in natural harmony, honesty, and ecological balance, what single quality ultimately justifies human stewardship? 4. How does the Brethren's esoteric hermeneutics (taʾwīl) reinterpret Qur'anic descriptions of Paradise, Hell, and bodily resurrection, and why did both orthodox Sunnī scholars and strict Aristotelians like al-Sijistānī criticize their syncretism?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ. The Case of the Animals versus Man Before the King of the Jinn: An Arabic Critical Edition and English Translation of Epistle 22. Chapter 8Ibn Miskawayh: The Philosopher of Ethical Refinement

A Humanist at the Buyid Court

In the bustling intellectual world of tenth- and early eleventh-century Iraq and Persia, where the Buyid dynasty had created a political space for Shīʿī and rationalist thought, a philosopher named Abū ʿAlī Aḥmad ibn Muḥammad ibn Yaʿqūb ibn Miskawayh (c. 932–1030) set himself a task that was at once ancient and urgently practical: to map the path of moral self-cultivation. He was not, in the manner of al-Fārābī, a systematiser of the universe or a theorist of the ideal state. He was a philosopher of the individual soul — its diseases and its health, its virtues and its vices, its education from childhood to old age. His major work, Tahdhīb al-Akhlāq wa Taṭhīr al-Aʿrāq (The Refinement of Character and the Purification of Dispositions), became the foundational text of Islamic philosophical ethics, a book that would be read, quoted, and reworked for a thousand years, from the Persian court to the Ottoman madrasa to the colonial encounter with European thought. Ibn Miskawayh was, in many respects, the embodiment of the cosmopolitan courtly culture of the Buyid period. He served as a secretary, librarian, and courtier to a succession of Buyid viziers, including the famously learned Abū alFaḍl ibn al-ʿAmīd and the Ṣāḥib ibn ʿAbbād. He was a companion of the great literary philosopher Abū Ḥayyān alTawḥīdī, with whom he shared a deep humanist curiosity about language, society, and the human condition — even if their friendship later soured into acrimonious literary feud. He studied history, medicine, alchemy, and the full Peripatetic curriculum. And he wrote, in a clear and elegant Arabic, works that synthesised the ethical thought of Plato, Aristotle, Galen, and the Islamic tradition into a practical manual of moral education. If al-Fārābī was the Second Teacher, Ibn Miskawayh was the chief instructor of the soul. This chapter examines Ibn Miskawayh's ethical philosophy as presented in the Tahdhīb al-Akhlāq, his concise philosophical summa al-Fawz al-Aṣghar, his philosophy of history in the Tajārib al-Umam, and his enduring influence on the later tradition of akhlāq literature. In Ibn Miskawayh, we encounter a thinker who believed that philosophy was not primarily a body of doctrine but a way of life — a discipline of character that could make ordinary human beings wise, just, and happy.

1. Life and Intellectual Milieu Abū ʿAlī Aḥmad ibn Muḥammad ibn Yaʿqūb Miskawayh (often called Ibn Miskawayh) was born in Rayy, near modern Tehran, around 932 into a cultivated Persian Muslim family (while medieval biographers such as Yāqūt debated whether an earlier ancestor had converted from Zoroastrianism, his father's name Muḥammad ibn Yaʿqūb attests that the family had been Muslim for generations). His name, originally "Miskawayh," was a Persian diminutive meaning "little musk," and his cultural horizon was thoroughly Persian as well as Arabic. He studied in

Baghdad under some of the leading scholars of the day, including the grammarian al-Sīrāfī and the philosopher Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī, the Christian student of al-Fārābī who carried on the Baghdad Peripatetic tradition. Through Ibn ʿAdī, Ibn Miskawayh was connected to the most rigorous philosophical training available, grounded in the Alexandrian commentaries on Aristotle and the Fārābīan synthesis. But Ibn Miskawayh was not an academic philosopher in the secluded sense. He spent his career at court, serving viziers who were themselves distinguished men of letters and patrons of learning. The Buyid court in Rayy and Baghdad was a crucible of intellectual exchange, where Muʿtazilī theologians debated with Shīʿī jurists, where the Rasāʾil of the Brethren of Purity were read and discussed, and where the Greek sciences were pursued with all the seriousness of a state project. Ibn Miskawayh's works reflect this environment: they are erudite without being obtuse, principled without being doctrinaire, and addressed to the educated statesman and secretary as much as to the specialist scholar. His circle included some of the most fascinating literary figures of the age. His complex intellectual partnership with the brilliant litterateur and philosopher Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī (d. 1023) produced both the vivid portrait of Buyid courtly and philosophical circles in al-Tawḥīdī's Kitāb al-Imtāʿ wa-l-Muʾānasa (Book of Enjoyment and Sociability) and, even more directly, their joint philosophical correspondence al-Hawāmil wa-l-Shawāmil (The Rambling Questions and the Comprehensive Answers). In al-Hawāmil wa-l-Shawāmil, the restless, existentially tormented al-Tawḥīdī poses 175 wide-ranging questions ("the wandering camels," hawāmil) on human nature, moral psychology, divine justice, friendship, doubt, and logic, and Miskawayh supplies measured, AristotelianNeoplatonic responses ("the comprehensive sheepdogs," shawāmil) that gather those scattered anxieties into rational order. The portrait that emerges is of a man of deep learning and steady temper, committed to the life of reason but fully aware of its limits.

2. Tahdhīb al-Akhlāq: The Refinement of Character If Ibn Miskawayh had written nothing else, his Tahdhīb al-Akhlāq would secure his place in the history of philosophy. The book is a systematic treatise on ethics, but it is systematic in the manner of the Hellenistic schools rather than of the modern textbook: it aims at the transformation of the reader's soul, not merely the exposition of a theoretical position. It draws on Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics, on the Neoplatonic commentary tradition, and on the ethical and medical works of Galen, but at every point it reshapes these sources to fit an Islamic worldview and to address the practical concerns of the cultivated Muslim reader.

The Framework: Soul, Virtue, and the Mean Ibn Miskawayh opens the Tahdhīb with a Platonic-Aristotelian account of the human soul. The soul has three faculties: the rational (al-quwwa al-nāṭiqa), the irascible or spirited (al-quwwa al-ghaḍabiyya), and the appetitive or concupiscent (al-quwwa al-shahwāniyya). This tripartite division, derived from Plato's Republic and refined by Aristotle, is the psychological foundation of his ethics. Moral health consists in a harmony among these three faculties, with reason ruling and the other two submitting to its guidance. Virtue is the state in which each faculty performs its proper function and none exceeds its bounds. Corresponding to the three faculties are four cardinal virtues, which Ibn Miskawayh defines through the Aristotelian doctrine of the mean: each virtue is a midpoint between two vices, one of excess and one of deficiency. Wisdom (al-ḥikma) is the virtue of the rational faculty. It stands between the excess of cunning or slyness (jarbaza) and the deficiency of stupidity (balāda). Wisdom includes discernment, clarity of thought, the capacity to deliberate well, and the theoretical knowledge of universals.

Courage (al-shajāʿa) is the virtue of the irascible faculty. It stands between rashness (tahawwur) and cowardice (jubn). Courage is not fearlessness but the capacity to face danger rightly, to be bold when boldness is called for and restrained when it is not. Temperance (al-ʿiffa) is the virtue of the appetitive faculty. It stands between licentiousness (sharah) and insensibility (khumūd). Temperance is the moderation of bodily desires — for food, drink, and sex — in accordance with reason. Justice (al-ʿadāla) is the composite virtue that harmonises the other three. It is the state in which wisdom governs, courage enforces, and temperance moderates, producing fairness in dealings with oneself and others. Justice, for Ibn Miskawayh, is not merely one virtue among others; it is the perfection of the whole soul, the condition in which every part receives its due. This scheme was thoroughly Greek in its origin, but Ibn Miskawayh gave it an Islamic grounding. He quoted the Qur'an and the sayings of the Prophet and ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib to illustrate and authenticate each virtue. The just man, he noted, is the one who fulfils the rights of God, the rights of his own soul, and the rights of his fellow human beings — a formulation that echoes the Islamic juridical categories of ḥuqūq Allāh and ḥuqūq al-ʿibād. The philosopher and the Prophet, in his vision, are teaching the same discipline of the soul, one through demonstration and the other through exhortation and law.

The Science of Character: How Virtue Is Acquired A distinguishing feature of Ibn Miskawayh's ethics is his detailed attention to the acquisition of virtue. He was not content to describe the virtues; he wanted to explain how a person — especially a young person — could be brought to possess them. This led him to develop what amounts to an Islamic version of moral psychology and moral education. He argued that children are born neither good nor evil but with natural dispositions (ṭabāʾiʿ) that can be shaped. Moral character is not immutable nature but acquired habit (ʿāda). By repeatedly performing good actions, the soul becomes habituated to virtue, just as the body becomes fit through repeated exercise. The role of the parent, the teacher, and the ruler is to provide the environment in which good habits can be formed — through instruction, through example, through reward and correction, and through the regulation of the child's diet, company, and amusements. Ibn Miskawayh's discussion of child-rearing, in the early sections of the Tahdhīb, is remarkably concrete: he advises on the proper foods, the right stories to tell, the dangers of flattery, the importance of physical exercise, and the gradual introduction of philosophical study. It is one of the earliest and most humane treatises on education in the Islamic tradition. The Greek inspiration here is Galen, whose ethical treatises on the diagnosis and therapy of the soul's passions Ibn Miskawayh knew well. But Ibn Miskawayh integrated Galen's medical model into a broader Islamic moral framework, in which the ultimate purpose of moral education is not merely psychological health but the soul's return to God. The language of spiritual medicine — "diseases of the soul," "therapies," "prescriptions" — pervades the Tahdhīb, bridging the gap between the ethical and the medical, the philosophical and the religious.

Friendship and the Social Nature of the Good No ethical philosophy is complete without an account of human relationships, and Ibn Miskawayh gave friendship (ṣadāqa) a central place in his scheme. Following Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics, he distinguished friendships of utility, friendships of pleasure, and friendships of virtue. Only the last is true friendship: a bond between virtuous

persons who love each other for the sake of their shared goodness. Such friendship is a school of virtue, for the friend is a mirror in which one sees one's own character reflected and an ally in the common pursuit of wisdom. But Ibn Miskawayh went further than Aristotle in one significant respect. For him, friendship was not only a relationship between individuals but a principle of social cohesion. Justice and friendship are complementary: justice regulates the relations between strangers and the less-than-virtuous through law, while friendship binds the virtuous in a community of mutual love. The ideal society is one in which justice provides the framework and friendship fills it with warmth. This vision, which is both Aristotelian in origin and deeply Islamic in its resonance with the Qur'anic ideal of brotherhood (ukhuwwa) and the Prophetic affection (maḥabba) among believers, would be taken up and extended by later ethicists, including Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī.

The Vice of Envy and the Love of Honour Ibn Miskawayh was a keen psychological observer, and one of the most penetrating sections of the Tahdhīb is his analysis of the vices that poison social life. He paid particular attention to envy (ḥasad) and the excessive love of honour (ḥubb al-jāh). Envy, he argued, is a torment of the soul: it is grief at another's good fortune, tinged with the wish to see it destroyed. It arises from a combination of pride and a false estimate of one's own deserts. The envious person misidentifies his own good with the defeat of others, thereby twisting the natural human desire for excellence into a destructive passion. The cure for envy, Ibn Miskawayh proposed, was partly intellectual — one must recognise that another's good does not diminish one's own — and partly spiritual, involving the cultivation of contentment (qanāʿa) and the redirection of ambition from external goods to the goods of the soul. His analysis was both philosophically precise and practically useful, and it would be drawn on by later writers in the Persian ethical tradition, from al-Ghazālī to Saʿdī.

3. Al-Fawz al-Aṣghar: A Concise Philosophical Summa While the Tahdhīb is a work of practical ethics, Ibn Miskawayh also wrote a shorter but systematic treatise of theoretical philosophy called al-Fawz al-Aṣghar (The Lesser Triumph). It is "lesser" in size — a handbook rather than a tome — but it covers the essential topics of Peripatetic philosophy: God, the soul, and prophecy. It represents his mature attempt to reconcile philosophy and Islam in a succinct and accessible format. The work is divided into three parts. The first treats the existence and nature of God, arguing for a Necessary Existent whose essence is existence itself — an argument that anticipates Avicenna's famous proof, though it is less developed. The second treats the human soul, its simplicity, its immortality, and its ascent through intellectual purification. The third treats prophecy, which Ibn Miskawayh explains, in the Fārābīan manner, as the perfection of the rational and imaginative faculties, through which the prophet receives intelligible truths from the Active Intellect and translates them into laws and symbols for the community. What is striking about al-Fawz al-Aṣghar is its blend of Peripatetic rationalism and Islamic piety. Ibn Miskawayh insists that the conclusions of philosophy — the immortality of the soul, the need for moral purification, the reality of reward and punishment after death — are precisely the truths that the prophets have conveyed in the language of revelation. He even offers a philosophical interpretation of the Prophet's nocturnal journey (isrāʾ) and ascension (miʿrāj), reading them as symbols of the soul's intellectual ascent to the divine presence. The book is a testament to his conviction that philosophy and religion, far from being rivals, are two expressions of a single saving truth.

4. Philosophy of History: The Tajārib al-Umam Ibn Miskawayh's intellectual range extended far beyond ethics and metaphysics. He was also one of the most important historians of the Buyid period, and his Tajārib al-Umam (The Experiences of Nations) is a universal history from the creation of Adam down to his own time, with a particular focus on the Buyid dynasty and its court. The work is a major source for the political history of the period, but it is also a work of philosophical reflection on the nature of history and the moral lessons it teaches. For Ibn Miskawayh, history was not a mere chronicle of dates and battles. It was a repository of moral experience, a "laboratory" in which the student of ethics could observe the consequences of virtue and vice played out on the grand scale. The rise and fall of dynasties illustrated the moral laws that govern human affairs: justice and wisdom lead to stability and prosperity; oppression and folly lead to ruin. The wise man, he argued, could learn from history what he might otherwise have to learn through painful personal experience. In this, Ibn Miskawayh was a predecessor of Ibn Khaldūn, whose Muqaddimah would later transform the study of history into a systematic science of society. Ibn Miskawayh lacked Ibn Khaldūn's theoretical genius, but his historical writings were infused with the same conviction that the events of this world are not random but exhibit patterns accessible to reason. The Tajārib also reveals Ibn Miskawayh's interest in the ethical formation of the ruler. By recording the lives of caliphs, viziers, and generals — their virtues, their failings, their moments of justice and of cruelty — he was providing a mirror for princes, a gallery of examples from which the statesman could draw practical wisdom. History was, in this sense, a branch of practical philosophy, and the historian was a moral educator in narrative form.

5. Philosophy, Religious Law, and Adab Culture One of Ibn Miskawayh's most significant achievements was his integration of philosophy with the broader literary and ethical culture of his time — what the Arabs called adab. Adab was a complex ideal: it encompassed refined manners, wide reading in poetry and prose, historical knowledge, and practical wisdom. It was the culture of the secretary, the courtier, the man of letters. Ibn Miskawayh, by writing philosophy in a polished and accessible Arabic, and by addressing subjects of immediate human concern — character, friendship, the conduct of life — brought philosophy into the mainstream of adab. He made it respectable, even indispensable, for the educated Muslim gentleman. He also clarified the relationship between philosophy and religious law (sharīʿa). For Ibn Miskawayh, the sharīʿa was the divinely provided discipline of the soul for the multitude. It commanded the same virtues that philosophy taught demonstratively, but it commanded them through the authority of revelation and the sanctions of law. The philosopher, by contrast, obeyed the sharīʿa not because he required its commands — his reason already directs him to the good — but because he recognises it as a true symbolic expression of the truths he knows intellectually, and because his participation in the community of believers is itself a good. This nuanced position allowed Ibn Miskawayh to be both a committed Muslim and a Peripatetic philosopher without obvious tension. He was not a disguised free-thinker; he was a man who saw ritual worship as a spiritual exercise and communal prayer as a school of friendship.

6. Influence: The Akhlāq Tradition and Beyond The Tahdhīb al-Akhlāq became the fountainhead of a long and distinguished tradition of ethical writing in Persian and, later, Ottoman Turkish. When Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī, the great Shīʿī philosopher and theologian of the thirteenth century, came to write his own monumental ethical work, the Akhlāq-i Nāṣirī (Nasirean Ethics), he took Ibn

Miskawayh's Tahdhīb as his primary model and source. Al-Ṭūsī reorganised and expanded the material, but the core framework — the tripartite soul, the four cardinal virtues, the doctrine of the mean, the practical guidance on child-rearing and the governance of the household — came directly from Ibn Miskawayh. Through al-Ṭūsī, Ibn Miskawayh's ethics passed into the curriculum of Shīʿī madrasas and into the wider Persianate world. The subsequent akhlāq literature — including Jalāl al-Dīn al-Dawānī's Akhlāq-i Jalālī in the fifteenth century and Kınalızāde Ali's Akhlāq-i ʿAlāʾī in the Ottoman sixteenth — continued to build on the Miskawayhian foundation. These works became standard texts in the education of princes, administrators, and scholars across the Ottoman, Safavid, and Mughal empires. The tradition remained alive well into the modern period, shaping the moral discourse of Muslim societies in ways that are only now being fully studied. Ibn Miskawayh's influence also extended beyond the akhlāq genre. Al-Ghazālī, though he would later critique the falāsifa in their metaphysics, drew freely on Ibn Miskawayh's ethical psychology in his Iḥyāʾ ʿUlūm al-Dīn, especially in the books on the training of the soul and the vices of the heart. The vision of the soul's purification through gradual habituation and intellectual illumination, which al-Ghazālī made the centrepiece of his Sufiinflected ethics, owed a great deal to the philosophical ethics of Ibn Miskawayh. The boundary between Sufi and philosophical ethics had become, by the twelfth century, a permeable membrane. In the Jewish tradition, Ibn Miskawayh's influence was also felt. The Spanish Jewish ethicist Baḥya ibn Paqūda, whose Duties of the Heart (c. 1080) is a classic of Jewish pietistic literature, drew on Ibn Miskawayh's psychological and ethical concepts, and the Tahdhīb was cited by later Jewish thinkers such as Shem Ṭov ibn Falaquera. The cosmopolitanism of the age made Ibn Miskawayh a philosopher for all the Abrahamic communities.

7. The Philosopher as Moral Physician With Ibn Miskawayh, Islamic philosophy acquired its first fully developed ethical system — a system that was simultaneously faithful to its Greek sources, adapted to Islamic imperatives, and directed toward the practical cultivation of individual virtue. His achievement was not as grand as al-Fārābī's cosmic symphony, nor as revolutionary as the synthesis that Avicenna was about to construct. But it addressed the question that every reader, then and now, eventually asks: "How should I live?" Ibn Miskawayh offered a disciplined, humane, and deeply intelligent answer to that question, and he did so in a voice that was calm, reasonable, and credibly wise. He believed that philosophy was a therapy of the soul, a medicine that could cure the diseases of pride, envy, greed, and fear. He believed that virtue could be taught, that character could be changed, and that the life of reason was not an arid intellectualism but the fullest flowering of the human potential. And he believed that the revealed law of Islam and the demonstrative wisdom of the Greeks were allies in the long, patient work of making human beings good. In a world of political violence, courtly intrigue, and sectarian strife — his world, like ours, was no utopia — he offered a vision of moral seriousness that did not require abandoning the world but transforming it from within. His legacy is with us still, not only in the pages of the later akhlāq texts but in the intuition, shared by many Muslims across centuries, that the heart of Islam is not dogma or law alone but the beautiful refinement of the human character — ḥusn al-khuluq, as the Prophet said, is the best of all things.

8. The Road to Avicenna Ibn Miskawayh died around 1030, likely in his nineties, having served viziers, written histories, refined his soul, and taught the art of living well. His death coincided with the youth of a prodigy in far-off Bukhara, a boy named Ibn Sīnā who was already devouring the works of al-Fārābī and Aristotle, and who would, within a few decades, produce the single most influential philosophical synthesis in Islamic history. Ibn Miskawayh's ethics would be woven into

that synthesis, as Avicenna gave the Peripatetic tradition its definitive metaphysical shape and its most durable arguments. We turn now to that towering figure, the Master of Philosophy, whose Shifāʾ and Ishārāt would dominate the intellectual landscape of both the Islamic world and the Latin West for half a millennium.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “A character trait (khuluq) is a stable disposition of the soul that causes it to perform its actions spontaneously without needing deliberate calculation at each step. Since the human being is civic by nature (madanī bi-l-ṭabʿ) and cannot attain his perfection or cultivate justice, generosity, and temperance as an isolated hermit in caves or mountains, true virtue can be acquired only within the fellowship and mutual aid of human society.” — Abū ʿAlī Aḥmad ibn Miskawayh, Tahdhīb al-Akhlāq (The Refinement of Character)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How does Ibn Miskawayh synthesize Plato's tripartite psychology (rational, spirited, and appetitive faculties) with Aristotle's doctrine of virtue as the golden mean (al-wasaṭ) in Tahdhīb al-Akhlāq? 2. Why does Ibn Miskawayh reject the ascetic withdrawal of hermits and isolated monks, arguing instead that Islamic communal rituals (such as congregational prayer and the Hajj pilgrimage) are divinely designed instruments for cultivating civic friendship (maḥabba) and justice? 3. How does Ibn Miskawayh adapt Galen's medical model of the 'therapy of the soul' (ṭibb al-nufūs) to diagnose and cure moral vices such as envy (ḥasad), unbridled anger (ghaḍab), and the morbid fear of death? 4. Trace the enduring influence of Ibn Miskawayh's Tahdhīb al-Akhlāq on both al-Ghazālī's Iḥyāʾ ʿUlūm al-Dīn and the Persianate-Ottoman akhlāq tradition (al-Ṭūsī's Akhlāq-i Nāṣirī, al-Dawānī's Akhlāq-i Jalālī, and Kınalızāde's Akhlāq-i ʿAlāʾī).
