Chapter 9Avicenna (Ibn Sīnā): The Master of Philosophy

The Prodigy from Bukhara

Abū ʿAlī al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sīnā (c. 980–1037) — known to the Latin West as Avicenna and to the Islamic world simply as al-Shaykh al-Raʾīs, the Master — was born in the village of Afshana near Bukhara, in what is now

Uzbekistan. His life, like his philosophy, has an almost legendary quality. By the age of ten he had memorised the Qur'an and mastered Arabic literature. By sixteen he had taught himself medicine with such success that he was treating patients, he tells us in his autobiography, without charge. By eighteen he had absorbed the entire philosophical curriculum — logic, mathematics, natural science, and metaphysics — and was already a practising physician and jurist. The works of al-Fārābī, he wrote, rescued him from despair over Aristotle's Metaphysics, which he had read forty times without understanding until al-Fārābī's commentary lit the way. His life was turbulent. After the fall of the Sāmānid dynasty in Bukhara, he became a wandering scholar and statesman, serving as vizier and court physician to a succession of rulers in Persia, falling in and out of favour, sometimes imprisoned, always writing. He died in Hamadān in 1037, at the age of fifty-seven, reportedly from a digestive illness exacerbated by excessive work and pleasure. He left behind a corpus of staggering size and range — over a hundred works, including the monumental al-Shifāʾ (The Healing), a philosophical encyclopaedia that covered logic, physics, mathematics, and metaphysics; the more concise al-Najāt (The Salvation); the late and lapidary al-Ishārāt wa-l-Tanbīhāt (Remarks and Admonitions); and the medical al-Qānūn fī al-Ṭibb (The Canon of Medicine), which would dominate European medical education for five centuries. Avicenna was not merely a commentator or a system-builder. He was a philosopher of extraordinary originality who, while claiming to follow Aristotle, fundamentally transformed the Aristotelian inheritance. His doctrines of existence and essence, the Necessary Existent, the soul as an immaterial substance, and the intellectual nature of divine knowledge set the agenda for all later philosophy in the Islamic world and, through Latin translations, reshaped European scholasticism from Aquinas to Scotus. To read Avicenna is to stand at the summit of the Peripatetic synthesis and to see the whole landscape of medieval philosophy stretching out below.

1. Existence and Essence: The Core Distinction The single most consequential move Avicenna made was to draw a clear and systematic distinction between existence (wujūd) and essence (māhiyya). Every created thing, he argued, has an essence — what it is — and an existence — that it is. Essence answers the question "What is it?"; existence answers the question "Is it?". For every being other than God, essence and existence are distinct. Essence does not, by itself, guarantee existence; a thing's whatness does not entail its thatness. A horse's essence (equinity) does not imply that a horse exists. Existence must come to essence from outside, from a cause that brings the possible into actuality. This distinction was not merely a piece of conceptual analysis. It was the foundation of a new metaphysics. For Avicenna, essence in itself is indifferent to existence or non-existence; it is a mere possibility. In itself, it is neither necessary nor impossible. To become actual, it needs a cause — and that cause, in the final analysis, is God, the one being whose essence is existence, the one being whose whatness is nothing other than its thatness. God is the Necessary Existent (wājib al-wujūd): the being who cannot not exist and whose very nature is existence itself. All other beings are merely possible in themselves (mumkin al-wujūd): they may exist or not exist, and their existence derives entirely from the Necessary Existent. This move transformed the relation between God and the world. In Aristotle's metaphysics, God is the Unmoved Mover, a final cause that attracts the world through desire but does not directly account for the world's being. In Avicenna's metaphysics, God becomes the cause of existence as such — the efficient cause that gives actuality to every essence. The world depends on God not only for motion but for its very being. Avicenna's God is thus far more intimately involved in creation than Aristotle's, and the language of Neoplatonic emanation, which describes the world as flowing from the One, can be reinterpreted as a philosophical account of existence-giving.

The implications of this doctrine radiated through every corner of philosophy. It allowed Avicenna to claim that metaphysics, the study of being as being, has God as its subject and that the proof of God's existence is a task for metaphysics, not physics. It also gave him the resources to argue that universals — like "horseness" — have a threefold status: they exist in the mind (bi-l-ʿaql), in concrete individuals (bi-l-ʿayn), and in themselves, in a purely intelligible realm where "horseness is simply horseness" without universal or particular. This doctrine of the threefold status of essence would profoundly influence later discussions of universals.

2. Proof of the Necessary Existent Avicenna's most famous argument for the existence of God is his proof of the Necessary Existent, which he presents in the Metaphysics of the Shifāʾ and in the Ishārāt. It is an argument from contingency, not from motion or design, and it is arguably the most rigorous such proof in the medieval tradition. The argument runs as follows. Every being is either necessary in itself or possible in itself. If a being is necessary in itself, then no further cause is required: it exists independently. If a being is possible in itself, it needs a cause — external to its essence — that brings it into existence. Now consider the totality of all possible beings. This totality, as a set, cannot be necessary in itself, because a set composed entirely of possible beings would itself be possible and would therefore need a cause. The cause of the set of all possible beings cannot be another possible being (for that would be a member of the set and would be part of what needs explanation). Therefore, the cause must lie outside the set — a being that is necessary in itself, the Necessary Existent. Avicenna presents this as a strictly metaphysical deduction. He does not begin by observing that things move or change; he begins from the sheer fact that things exist and that their existence is not self-explanatory. The argument is a priori in the sense that it requires only the distinction between necessity and possibility, not empirical premises about the world. It aims to demonstrate that the Necessary Existent is one, simple, unique, and the ultimate cause of all other existents. From the oneness of the Necessary Existent, Avicenna deduces further attributes. God must be simple, because composition would imply parts, and parts would imply a cause that unites them. God must be one, because if there were two Necessary Existents, they would either be identical (in which case they are one) or distinguished by something (in which case they are composite and therefore not necessary in themselves). God must be an intellect, because intellect is the perfection of immaterial being, and God, as necessarily existent, possesses all perfections. God must be good, truth, and pure actuality. Avicenna's God is the absolute plenitude of being, overflowing with intelligence and power — a conception at once Neoplatonic in its sweep and Islamic in its insistence on the absolute transcendence and uniqueness of the Creator.

3. The "Floating Man" and the Soul If Avicenna's metaphysics is the skeleton of his system, his philosophical psychology is its heart. His most famous thought experiment, the "floating man," appears in several versions across his works and has become a classic of philosophical anthropology. Imagine, Avicenna says, a human being created all at once in perfect maturity, suspended in empty air, with his limbs separated so that he feels no bodily sensation, his eyes blindfolded so that he sees nothing, and his ears filled so that he hears nothing — in short, a consciousness with no sensory input whatsoever. This human being would still be aware of himself; he would affirm "I exist." But he would affirm nothing about his body, because he has no sensory experience of it. He would not know that he has a body at all. Therefore, the self that he affirms is not

identical with his body or any bodily part. The human soul, the true self, is an immaterial substance distinct from the body. This argument established the soul as a substance in its own right — not a mere form imposed on matter, as in Aristotle, but a spiritual substance that uses the body as an instrument. The soul is not a harmony of bodily humours; it is an incorporeal, rational entity that survives the death of the body. Avicenna developed an elaborate faculty psychology to explain how this immaterial soul relates to the body: the soul has vegetative and animal faculties that operate through bodily organs, but its rational faculty — the intellect — is entirely independent of matter and is the locus of personal identity and immortality. Avicenna's dualism is not the radical dualism of Plato, where the soul merely inhabits the body as a prisoner. For Avicenna, the soul is the perfection of the body and is created by God at the moment the body is ready to receive it. The relationship is one of intimate union; the soul is the substantial form that makes the body what it is. But the soul's ultimate destiny is to detach from the body through intellectual cultivation and to return to its origin in the intelligible world. At death, the soul's condition depends on the degree of intellectual perfection it has achieved. The soul that has actualised its intellect becomes a pure intelligible substance, enjoying a state of eternal contemplation. The soul that has failed to attain intellectual fulfilment remains bound to the body's attachments, suffering a kind of sensory afterlife of pain and longing. Avicenna's eschatology is thus thoroughly philosophical: heaven and hell are states of the soul, not literal gardens and fires.

4. Logic and the Distinction of Mental Existence Avicenna was also a logician of the first rank who made a permanent contribution to the discipline. He reworked the Aristotelian Organon so thoroughly that later logicians in Islam often spoke of "Avicennan logic" rather than Aristotelian. His most original logical doctrine is the distinction between mental existence and concrete existence, which underpins his entire epistemology. A proposition, for Avicenna, is not merely a string of words or a mental image. It has a truth-value that is independent of any particular mind, though it is known only through minds. When I think "Humanity is a rational animal," the form of this proposition exists in my mind, but its content — the relation between humanity and rationality — is what it is irrespective of whether my mind exists. While Avicenna occasionally used the ordinary Arabic phrase fī nafs al-amr ("in the matter itself" or "in reality"), his distinction between indifferent quiddity (māhiyya bi-lā sharṭ), mental existence (wujūd dhihnī), and concrete existence (wujūd ʿaynī) laid the foundations for what Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī (d. 1274) and later post-classical logicians and metaphysicians (such as al-Jurjānī, alDawānī, and al-Qūshjī) would formalize as the technical theory of nafs al-amr ("the fact of the matter" or "the thing in itself") — an objective truth-maker domain that grounds the necessity of mathematical, logical, and negative propositions without positing self-subsistent Platonic Forms. His most famous contribution to syllogistic theory was the discovery that the two-premise syllogism could be systematised in a new way. He distinguished between connective and repetitive syllogisms, developed a sophisticated analysis of modal propositions (necessity, possibility, impossibility), and argued that the syllogism's validity does not depend on the existence of its terms in the external world — logic studies the formal relations of thoughts, not the physical world directly. This independence of logic from ontology allowed logic to become a purely formal discipline, a tool applicable to theology and metaphysics alike. Avicenna's analysis of the categories also moved beyond Aristotle. He argued that the Aristotelian list of ten categories (substance, quantity, quality, relation, etc.) could be reduced: being is primarily divided into substance

and accident, and the other accidents are merely ways in which substance is described. This critical tendency, which always seeks to find simplicity beneath multiplicity, is thoroughly Avicennan.

5. God's Knowledge of Particulars and the Problem of Evil One of Avicenna's most contested doctrines is his account of how God knows the world. For Avicenna, the Necessary Existent is pure intellect whose knowledge is identical with His essence. Because God's essence is the cause of all existence, God knows all things by knowing Himself as their cause. He knows the universe in a universal and eternal manner, through the intellectual forms that emanate from Him, not by acquiring sensory data or experiencing temporal change. But does God know individual, particular events — the fall of a sparrow, the colour of my shirt? Avicenna's answer, which infuriated al-Ghazālī and has been debated ever since, is that God knows particulars but knows them "in a universal way." By this he meant that God's knowledge, being timeless and intellectual, grasps the entire causal chain of events in a single eternal act. God knows that a particular eclipse will occur because He knows the celestial causes that produce it; He knows every particular through its universal cause. God's knowledge is complete, but it is not knowledge of particulars as particular in a temporal sequence. It is knowledge of the necessary order of the universe from the perspective of eternity. This doctrine was accused by al-Ghazālī of effectively denying that God knows particulars in their particularity, which would contradict the Qur'anic insistence that not even an atom's weight escapes God's knowledge. Avicenna's defenders, including later Avicennans like al-Ṭūsī and Mullā Ṣadrā, argued that Avicenna's position preserved divine omniscience without compromising divine simplicity and transcendence — that knowing things in their universal causes is a higher, not a lower, form of knowledge. In Chapter 14 we shall see how Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī subjected this doctrine to a searching critique, and how the debate over divine knowledge became one of the central fault lines of post-classical Islamic thought. The problem of evil is intimately connected to this issue. Avicenna held, as a consequence of his emanationism, that the universe is as perfect as it can possibly be. The Necessary Existent, being pure goodness, produces the best possible order. Evil (sharr) has no positive existence; it is merely the privation of good, the absence of a perfection that should be present. Physical evils — blindness, illness, suffering — are necessary consequences of the operation of natural causes in a world composed of matter subject to generation and corruption. A world without such privations would be a different world, and the actual world is the best of all possible worlds, given the nature of its causes. This theodicy, which is a remarkable fusion of Neoplatonic privation theory and Islamic divine justice, leaves little room for a personal God who intervenes to prevent specific evils. Again, al-Ghazālī would later object that such a God is not the God of the Qur'an, who hears prayers and shows mercy. But Avicenna's system is internally coherent: God's perfection is expressed in the rational order of the cosmos, not in arbitrary interventions. The philosopher's task is to understand that order and to align his soul with it, thereby achieving happiness despite the inevitable sufferings of embodied life.

6. The Master at Work Avicenna wrote, it is said, with incredible speed and concentration. He composed the Shifāʾ in its entirety without consulting a library, relying solely on his memory. He wrote much of the Ishārāt while on horseback during military campaigns. His prose in the Shifāʾ is dense and technical; in the Ishārāt, it is lapidary and almost mystical, a text that would require generations of commentators to unpack. These are the works of a man who lived inside his

philosophy, for whom the distinctions of logic and the structures of metaphysics were as familiar as his own heartbeat. His intellectual self-confidence was monumental. When he had finished the Shifāʾ, he reportedly said, "I have written a book that will be the standard of the sciences." He was right. For six hundred years in the Islamic world, and for three hundred in the Latin West, his works were the starting point for philosophical instruction at the highest level. Thomas Aquinas quoted him constantly, often to disagree, but never to dismiss. Duns Scotus engaged with his arguments on existence and essence with an intensity that produced some of the most acute medieval metaphysics. The Avicennan shadow is long, and it falls across both the medieval and the early modern worlds.

7. The Formal Structure of the Burhān al-Ṣiddīqīn (Proof of the Sincere) in al-Ishārāt To appreciate why Avicenna's proof of the Necessary Existent revolutionized medieval metaphysics across Islam, Judaism, and Christendom, one must examine its exact modal architecture in the fourth class (Namaṭ IV) of alIshārāt wa-l-Tanbīhāt. Unlike Aristotle's physical proof from motion in Physics VIII or the kalām cosmological argument from temporal origination (ḥudūth), Avicenna insists that metaphysics must establish the First Principle purely through the analysis of existence as such (al-mawjūd bi-mā huwa mawjūd), without appealing to sensory facts about motion or temporal change. Avicenna's deduction proceeds through four rigorous steps concerning the Totality (jumla) of contingent beings: 1. Every existent we encounter, considered solely in terms of its own quiddity (māhiyya), is either necessary in itself (wājib al-wujūd bi-dhātihi) or contingent in itself (mumkin al-wujūd bi-dhātihi). If it is necessary in itself, the Necessary Existent is immediately established. 2. Suppose instead that the existent is contingent in itself. Now consider the entire aggregate or totality (jumla / silsila) of all contingent beings, whether that causal chain is finite or stretches back to infinity. What is the modal status of this whole totality? 3. A totality composed exclusively of contingent parts cannot be necessary in itself, since a whole subsists only through its units; if every member lacks intrinsic necessity of existence, the collection of them all remains contingent in itself. 4. Therefore, the entire totality of contingent beings requires a cause that bestows existence (murajjiḥ / mūjib) upon it. That cause cannot lie inside the totality (for then it would be contingent and would have to cause itself); hence it must stand outside the totality of contingent beings. Whatever stands outside the totality of contingent beings is, by definition, Necessary in Itself (Wājib al-Wujūd bi-dhātihi). Crucially, Avicenna pairs this proof with a radical redefinition of true efficient causality (al-ʿilla al-fāʿiliyya). Whereas the natural philosopher thinks of an efficient cause as a prior event that initiates motion (like a builder who builds a house and then walks away while the house remains standing), the metaphysician recognizes that the true ontological cause gives existence (ifāḍat al-wujūd) continuously and coexists simultaneously (maʿan bi-l-dhāt, ontological simultaneity) with its effect. Just as the movement of the hand turns a key simultaneously in time while remaining ontologically prior as its cause, the Necessary Existent is ontologically prior (mutaqaddim bi-l-dhāt) to the cosmos even if the cosmos has no temporal beginning. Because even an infinite temporal series of parents and children consists merely of accidental preparatory causes (ʿilal muʿidda), every moment of existence depends vertically and simultaneously on the Necessary Existent.

8. From the Peripatetic Synthesis to the Eastern Wisdom With Avicenna, the Peripatetic synthesis reaches its apogee. He absorbed al-Kindī's harmony of reason and revelation, al-Fārābī's emanationist cosmology and theory of prophecy, the logical rigour of the Alexandrian commentaries, and the medical and psychological sciences of Galen and Aristotle. He welded them into a single, powerful system that answered almost every question a philosopher of the eleventh century could ask: What is being? What is knowledge? What is the soul? How does God create? Why is there evil? How can prophecy be philosophically understood? And he did it in a way that was recognisably Islamic, drawing on the Qur'an and the language of the prayer niche, while maintaining the universal claims of demonstrative reason. Yet Avicenna's own intellectual journey did not end with the Shifāʾ. In his later years, he turned to a different kind of philosophy — less systematic, more allegorical, more suffused with the language of illumination and mystical ascent. He called this his "Eastern Philosophy" (al-Ḥikma al-Mashriqiyya), and it has been a puzzle for scholars ever since. The next chapter turns to this enigmatic dimension of Avicenna's work: his allegorical narratives, his hints at a philosophy of light, and the legacy that would inspire Suhrawardī, the Master of Illumination.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “Suppose that one of us is created all at once and fully formed in his intellect, yet his sight is veiled from observing any external thing; that he is created floating in empty air or a void so that the resistance of the air does not touch him in any way he would have to feel; and that his limbs are separated so that they neither meet nor touch one another. Then let him reflect whether he affirms the existence of his own self (dhātuhu): he will not doubt in affirming that his self exists, even while affirming neither limb, nor inner organ, nor heart, nor brain.” — Ibn Sīnā (Avicenna), Kitāb al-Shifāʾ: al-Nafs (The Book of Healing: On the Soul, I.1)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. Explain Avicenna's distinction between essence/quiddity (māhiyya) and existence (wujūd) using his example of the triangle or 'horseness in itself' (al-farasiyya min ḥaythu hiya farasiyya). How does this distinction ground his division of being into the Necessary in Itself and the Contingent in Itself? 2. Trace the four steps of Avicenna's Burhān al-Ṣiddīqīn (Proof of the Sincere) in Namaṭ IV of al-Ishārāt wa-lTanbīhāt. Why does Avicenna consider a purely metaphysical proof from the totality of contingent beings superior to Aristotle's physical proof from motion? 3. What is the exact philosophical point of Avicenna's Flying Man (al-rajul al-muʿallaq fī al-hawāʾ) thought experiment? Does it succeed in proving that the human soul is an immaterial substance distinct from the body, or is it vulnerable to the objection that epistemic distinctness does not entail ontological distinctness? 4. Why did Avicenna argue that God knows particular temporal events (such as a specific solar eclipse) 'in a universal manner' (ʿalā naḥw kullī) through their invariable causal chains, and why did this doctrine provoke such fierce opposition from al-Ghazālī?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Avicenna (Ibn Sīnā). The Metaphysics of The Healing (Kitāb al-Shifāʾ: al-Ilāhiyyāt). Translated by Michael E. Marmura. Provo, UT: Brigham Young University Press, 2005. Avicenna (Ibn Sīnā). Remarks and Admonitions (al-Ishārāt wa-l-Tanbīhāt): Physics and Metaphysics. Translated by Shams C. Inati. New York: Columbia University Press, 2014.

Gutas, Dimitri. Avicenna and the Aristotelian Tradition: Introduction to Reading Avicenna's Philosophical Works. 2nd ed. Leiden: Brill, 2014. McGinnis, Jon. Avicenna. Great Medieval Thinkers. Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2010. Wisnovsky, Robert. Avicenna's Metaphysics in Context. Ithaca, NY: Cornell University Press, 2003.

Chapter 10Avicenna's Eastern Wisdom and Legacy

The Sage of the East

If the Shifāʾ represents the systematic, daylight Avicenna — the Peripatetic master laying out the demonstrative sciences in their logical order — there is another Avicenna, one who wrote in twilight. This Avicenna composed short, visionary narratives in which the human soul undertakes an allegorical journey through the cosmos, guided by celestial intelligences, encountering symbolic figures, and ascending toward the divine light. He called these works his ḥikma mashriqiyya, a term that can be translated as "Eastern philosophy," "Oriental wisdom," or "Illuminative wisdom." And he claimed, in the introduction to a now-lost or partially surviving work, that his Eastern philosophy represented the true core of his teaching, as opposed to the Peripatetic doctrines he had presented in the Shifāʾ for the general public. This has produced one of the great debates in Avicennan scholarship. Was Avicenna's Eastern philosophy a radically different system — a mysticism in philosophical dress — that he withheld from the uninitiated? Or was it a more advanced and esoteric presentation of the same metaphysics, intended for an elite who had already mastered the Peripatetic curriculum? The surviving texts are fragmentary, and Avicenna's own statements are deliberately obscure. But what is clear is that the Eastern turn in Avicenna's thought had an extraordinary impact on later Islamic philosophy, especially on the Illuminationism of Suhrawardī and the Transcendent Philosophy of Mullā Ṣadrā. This chapter explores Avicenna's allegorical-mystical works, the puzzle of the "Oriental Philosophy," and the formation of the Avicennan tradition in both Sunnī and Shīʿī milieux. It is a story of texts, exegeses, and creative misreadings — of how the Master's hint became a whole new school.

1. Allegorical Narratives: Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān and His Companions Avicenna wrote three major allegorical narratives that dramatise the soul's journey from the world of matter to the world of intellect. The earliest and most influential is Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān (Living, Son of Awake), named after its central figure, an ageless sage who meets the narrator (the soul) and guides him through the geography of the cosmos. The story is cast as a first-person encounter, and its style is deliberately reminiscent of the Qur'anic miʿrāj literature and the Neoplatonic allegorical ascent. Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān describes himself as a traveller who knows all the lands of the world — the West, the East, and the hidden realms in between. The West represents the world of matter, darkness, and the body; the East represents the world of pure light, intellect, and God. The narrator asks Ḥayy to guide him to the truth, and Ḥayy explains that the journey is difficult, requiring the soul to detach from the body and purify its intellect. The narrative then proceeds through a series of cosmic stations: the soul must pass the sphere of the moon, the sphere of Mercury, and so on, each governed by a celestial intellect that imparts a specific wisdom, until it reaches the outermost sphere and the divine presence.

The other two narratives — Risālat al-Ṭayr (The Epistle of the Bird) and Salāmān wa Absāl — continue this allegorical mode. In Risālat al-Ṭayr, the soul is portrayed as a bird trapped in a net, who escapes with the help of other birds and flies back to the King's court. In Salāmān wa Absāl, Avicenna presents a Neoplatonic allegory of the soul's relation to the Active Intellect and the body, using a story of two lovers to represent the soul's desire for intellectual perfection. These narratives were enormously popular, inspiring a long tradition of philosophical allegory in Persian and Arabic, including the famous Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān of Ibn Ṭufayl in al-Andalus (which, despite the same title, is a very different kind of book) and the symbolic tales of Suhrawardī. What do these allegories teach that the Shifāʾ does not? Nothing, strictly speaking, in terms of doctrinal content — the metaphysics, the cosmology, the intellectual ascent are all there in the demonstrative works. But the allegories teach in a different mode. They address the imagination rather than the syllogistic intellect, and they aim not only to inform but to transform the reader's soul. Avicenna believed that the highest knowledge cannot be fully communicated in propositions; it must be tasted (dhawq), directly experienced by the soul. The allegories are an invitation to that experience. They are the rhetoric of the intelligible, the poetry of philosophy.

2. The Enigma of the "Oriental Philosophy" In the programmatic prologue to Manṭiq al-Mashriqiyyīn (The Logic of the Easterners, the surviving opening part of his book al-Mashriqiyyūn or al-Ḥikma al-Mashriqiyya), Avicenna contrasts his popular Peripatetic books written for the common run of Aristotelians with the uncompromised philosophy of the "Easterners" (almashriqiyyūn). The Western philosophy, he says, is what he has presented in the Shifāʾ — the standard Peripatetic system. The Eastern philosophy is something else, reserved for "those who are worthy" and who have the capacity for direct intellectual vision. The terminology is spatial but also symbolic: the East is the place of sunrise, illumination, light; the West is the place of sunset, darkness, matter. Scholars have speculated endlessly about what this Eastern philosophy contained. The fragmentary state of the texts prevents a definitive reconstruction, but some conclusions are widely held. As Dimitri Gutas has demonstrated through a philological reconstruction of the surviving fragments, Avicenna's Eastern Philosophy (al-Ḥikma alMashriqiyya) was not an anti-rationalist mysticism, nor did it employ Suhrawardī's later terminology of 'knowledge by presence' (al-ʿilm al-ḥuḍūrī). Rather, it was a streamlined, non-partisan presentation of Avicenna's own mature system stripped of pedantic dialectical refutations, reorganizing the sciences (dividing metaphysics into Universal Science, ʿilm kullī, and Divine Theology, ilāhiyyāt) and highlighting intellectual intuition (al-ḥads) — the mind's rapid, direct grasping of middle terms through conjunction with the Active Intellect. Geographically, Avicenna's title al-Mashriqiyyūn ('The Easterners') referred specifically to the independent philosophers of Khurāsān and Transoxiana (the Islamic East, centered in Bukhara, Nishapur, and Rayy) in contrast to the rigid, literalist Christian and Muslim Aristotelian commentators of Baghdad (the Islamic West, such as Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Ṭayyib). Notably, Avicenna himself never claimed to be reviving the wisdom of the ancient preIslamic Persian sages; it was Suhrawardī a century and a half later who transmuted Avicenna's geographical contrast between Khurāsān and Baghdad into a sacred symbolic geography of Light (ishrāq), explicitly criticizing Avicenna in Kitāb al-Mashāriʿ wa-l-Muṭāraḥāt (§207) for failing to recover the ancient 'Oriental source' (al-aṣl alsharqī) of the Persian sage-kings (ḥukamāʾ al-Furs). Although the bulk of al-Mashriqiyyūn was lost during the sack of Isfahan in 1034, the idea of an 'Eastern Philosophy' exercised a potent attraction over later centuries. Ibn Ṭufayl in twelfth-century Andalusia framed his philosophical novel Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān as an unveiling of the secrets of Avicenna's 'Eastern Philosophy'; Suhrawardī used Avicenna's unfinished project as a foil to launch his own Philosophy of Illumination (Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq); and

Mullā Ṣadrā integrated Avicenna's late pointers on mystical realization (Namaṭ IX–X of the Ishārāt) into his Transcendent Philosophy.

3. The Formation of the Avicennan Tradition Avicenna's direct influence was immediate and overwhelming. Within a generation of his death, his works were being taught, commented upon, and fiercely debated in the madrasas of Persia, Iraq, and Syria. The Ishārāt became the central text of philosophical study, attracting a long series of commentaries, the most famous by Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī (whose commentary is actually a thorough critique) and Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī (whose commentary is a defence, rebutting al-Rāzī point by point). The Rāzī-Ṭūsī exchange became a standard text in madrasa curricula, and through it, Avicennan philosophy became the common property of educated men across the Islamic East, whether they identified primarily as theologians, philosophers, or mystics. In the Sunnī world, Avicenna's reception was complex. The Ashʿarī theologians, led by al-Ghazālī, attacked his doctrines of the eternity of the world, God's knowledge of particulars, and bodily resurrection. Al-Ghazālī's Tahāfut al-Falāsifa (The Incoherence of the Philosophers) was largely aimed at Avicenna, though it named al-Fārābī as well. Yet al-Ghazālī's critique did not succeed in displacing Avicenna. Instead, it forced later Sunnī thinkers to engage with Avicenna more deeply, often accepting his logic and psychology while rejecting his metaphysics. Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, the greatest Sunnī theologian-philosopher of the twelfth to thirteenth centuries, wrote a massive commentary on the Ishārāt that treated Avicenna with a mixture of respect and dismantling critique. Through alRāzī, as we shall see in Chapter 14, Avicennan concepts entered the bloodstream of Sunnī kalām, producing a "philosophical theology" that was Avicennan in structure even when anti-Avicennan in conclusion. In the Shīʿī world, Avicenna's legacy was even more profound. Shīʿī scholars, from Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī onward, adopted Avicenna as their primary philosophical authority and integrated his system with Shīʿī theology and the teachings of the Imams. Ṭūsī's commentary on the Ishārāt is a masterpiece of philosophical reconstruction that saved the Ishārāt for Shīʿī education. Later, Mullā Ṣadrā would draw on Avicenna's existential metaphysics, his theory of the soul, and his Eastern hints to create the Transcendent Philosophy, which claimed to fulfil what Avicenna had only hinted at. The Ottoman and Mughal worlds also received Avicenna through the madrasa curriculum. Ottoman professors wrote super-commentaries on the Ishārāt and the Shifāʾ well into the nineteenth century. In Iran, the Avicennan tradition never died; it simply transformed, flowing into the Illuminationist and Transcendent currents, and it remains a living part of the philosophical curriculum in Qom today.

4. The Latin Avicenna Avicenna's influence was not confined to Islam. In the twelfth century, his major works — especially the Shifāʾ's sections on physics, metaphysics, and the soul — were translated into Latin in Toledo and became central to the philosophical revolution of the thirteenth-century universities. The "Latin Avicenna" was, for a time, as influential as the Latin Aristotle, and his doctrines on existence and essence, the soul, and the nature of God provoked intense debate in Paris and Oxford. Thomas Aquinas acknowledged Avicenna as one of his principal sources and interlocutors. He accepted Avicenna's distinction between existence and essence (though he modified it to suit his Christian doctrine of creation), and he engaged critically with Avicenna's theory of the Active Intellect and his emanationism. Duns Scotus developed his own theory of univocity of being in part through grappling with Avicennan metaphysics. The Avicennan argument from contingency for the existence of God was taken up by Leibniz and by later rationalist theology. Avicenna's

medical Canon remained the standard textbook in European universities until the seventeenth century. It is no exaggeration to say that Avicenna was, alongside Aristotle, the most important philosophical authority in the medieval West after Augustine. But the Latin Avicenna was also a truncated Avicenna, stripped of his Islamic context and his Eastern wisdom. The allegories, the mystical hints, the integration with Qur'anic concepts — all were invisible to Latin readers. The Avicenna known to Europe was the Peripatetic system-builder of the Shifāʾ. The full Avicenna, philosopher and visionary, would only be recovered by modern scholarship and by the living tradition of Islamic ḥikma, which continued to read the allegories and the Eastern fragments alongside the demonstrative works.

5. The Immediate Avicennan Lineage and Twelfth-Century Independent Critics: Bahmanyār, ʿUmar Khayyām, Abū al-Barakāt al-Baghdādī, and Bābā Afḍal Kāshānī Between Avicenna's death in 1037 and the rise of Suhrawardī and Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī in the late twelfth century, philosophy in the Islamic East was far from dormant. Alongside a direct school of Avicennan disciples who transmitted and defended the Master's system, the twelfth century produced bold independent philosophers who subjected Avicenna's physics, psychology, and metaphysics to searching internal critique: Bahmanyār ibn al-Marzubān (d. 1066) and Abū al-ʿAbbās al-Lawkarī (d. c. 1123): Avicenna's foremost direct student, a Zoroastrian convert from Azerbaijan for whom Avicenna composed al-Mubāḥathāt (The Discussions), Bahmanyār authored Kitāb al-Taḥṣīl (The Book of Validation), the most authoritative eleventhcentury summa of Avicennan logic, physics, and metaphysics. His student al-Lawkarī established the teaching of Avicennan philosophy across Khurāsān through his compendium Bayān al-Ḥaqq bi-Ḍamān al-Ṣidq. ʿUmar Khayyām (1048–1131): World-famous for his Rubāʿiyyāt (Quatrains) and his geometric solution of cubic equations (Risāla fī al-Barāhīn ʿalā Masāʾil al-Jabr wa-l-Muqābala), Khayyām was also a penetrating Avicennan philosopher who described himself as a student of Avicenna. In five concise Arabic and Persian philosophical treatises — notably al-Risāla al-Ḍiyāʾiyya (Light on the Subject of Universal Science) and Risāla fī Kulliyyat al-Wujūd — Khayyām argued that generic concepts and existence (wujūd) are conceptual attributes (iʿtibāriyya) in the mind rather than superadded entities in the external world, directly anticipating Suhrawardī's critique of Peripatetic essentialism. Abū al-Barakāt Hibat Allāh al-Baghdādī (c. 1080–1165): A Jewish physician-philosopher of Baghdad who converted to Islam late in life, Abū al-Barakāt authored Kitāb al-Muʿtabar fī al-Ḥikma (The Book of What Has Been Established by Personal Reflection) — the most original twelfth-century philosophical critique of Avicenna before Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī. Rejecting blind adherence to Aristotle and Avicenna, Abū al-Barakāt advanced three revolutionary doctrines: (1) in psychology, he rejected Avicenna's partitioning of the soul into separate vegetative, animal, and rational faculties, arguing that the unitary 'I' (al-dhāt al-wāḥida) perceives both sensory particulars and universal concepts directly; (2) in physics, he refuted Aristotle's theory of projectile motion by proving that a thrower imparts a self-expending violent inclination (mayl qasrī) directly into the projectile (anticipating fourteen-century impetus mechanics); and (3) in metaphysics, he redefined time (zamān) not as the measure of physical motion, but as the measure of existence (miqdār al-wujūd) itself, concluding that God's own existence and creative will operate in real duration while dismantling Avicenna's ten-intellect emanation scheme. Afḍal al-Dīn Muḥammad Maraqī Kāshānī, known as Bābā Afḍal (d. c. 1213): Writing almost exclusively in luminous, idiomatic Persian prose (Madārij al-Kamāl, Jāwīdān-nāma, and ʿArḍ-nāma), Bābā Afḍal recentered philosophy around self-knowledge (khūd-āgāhī). Dividing all reality into 'mere being' (būdan,

corporeal nature) and 'finding / awareness' (yāftan, conscious intellect), Bābā Afḍal argued that because the human soul is present to itself prior to all external sensation, rigorous reflection on the self's own luminous awareness is the royal road to knowing the Universal Intellect and the Divine Origin.

6. The Bridge from Avicenna to Al-Bīrūnī With Avicenna, the Peripatetic synthesis reaches its full maturity, and its inner currents — logical, metaphysical, psychological, and spiritual — flow into the broad river of subsequent Islamic thought. But before we follow that river into the turbulence of al-Ghazālī's critique, the systematic verification of Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, and the defence of Ibn Rushd, we must pause to consider a contemporary of Avicenna's who represents a different — and in some ways more radical — mode of philosophical engagement with the world. Abū Rayḥān al-Bīrūnī (973–1048) was a polymath and philosopher of science who refused to subordinate empirical investigation to metaphysical deduction. Where Avicenna built a grand cosmic system from which particular truths could be deduced, al-Bīrūnī insisted on the primacy of observation, measurement, and cross-cultural comparison. He was, in many ways, the first philosopher to practice what we would now call the comparative study of religion and the philosophy of science, and his masterpiece Kitāb al-Hind is one of the most remarkable works of crosscultural philosophy ever written. The next chapter turns to al-Bīrūnī — the observer of India, the critic of authority, the empirical conscience of Islamic philosophy.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “What we have set forth in the books of The Healing (al-Shifāʾ) is calculated for the common run of Peripatetic associates; as for those who seek the pure truth without partisan attachment to Greek custom, we have composed for them The Eastern Philosophy (al-Ḥikma al-Mashriqiyya), and at the close of our Pointers (al-Ishārāt) we have unveiled the stations of the Gnostics (maqāmāt al-ʿārifīn) for those endowed with penetrating insight.” — Ibn Sīnā (Avicenna), Prologue to Manṭiq al-Mashriqiyyīn (Logic of the Easterners) and al-Ishārāt wa-l-Tanbīhāt (Namaṭ IX)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. Compare Henry Corbin's mystical-theosophical interpretation of Avicenna's 'Eastern Philosophy' (al-ḥikma almashriqiyya) with Dimitri Gutas's historical-critical interpretation. How do Avicenna's three visionary recitals (Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān, The Bird, and Salāmān and Absāl) relate to his syllogistic works? 2. In Namaṭ IX of al-Ishārāt wa-l-Tanbīhāt ('On the Stations of the Gnostics'), how does Avicenna distinguish the true Gnostic (al-ʿārif) from the ascetic (al-zāhid) and the ritual worshipper (al-ʿābid)? 3. Why did Avicenna's Kitāb al-Shifāʾ and al-Ishārāt wa-l-Tanbīhāt completely displace Aristotle's original Greek texts as the foundational philosophical textbooks in the eastern Islamic lands after the eleventh century? 4. Trace the impact of the 'Latin Avicenna' on thirteenth-century European scholasticism, focusing on Thomas Aquinas's adoption of the essence-existence distinction in De Ente et Essentia and Duns Scotus's concept of the univocity of being.

Part III: Philosophy of Science and Cross-Cultural Thought

Chapter 11Al-Bīrūnī: Comparative Methodology and the Philosophy of Science

The Observer from Khwarezm

Abū Rayḥān Muḥammad ibn Aḥmad al-Bīrūnī (973–1048) was born in the region of Khwarezm, south of the Aral Sea, and died in Ghazna, in present-day Afghanistan, after a life of extraordinary scientific productivity. He was a contemporary of Avicenna, with whom he exchanged a famous series of philosophical letters — sharp, sometimes contentious, always brilliant — on topics ranging from the nature of light to the eternity of the world. But where Avicenna was the architect of a grand metaphysical system, al-Bīrūnī was the tireless empiricist, the measurer, the observer, the comparativist. He wrote over a hundred and fifty works, covering astronomy, mathematics, geodesy, pharmacology, mineralogy, history, and the scientific study of religion and culture. His Kitāb al-Hind (Book of India), completed around 1030, is a landmark in the history of anthropology, comparative religion, and crosscultural philosophy — a work that has no real parallel in the Middle Ages and that anticipates the methods of modern ethnography and the philosophy of science. Al-Bīrūnī was not a faylasūf in the narrow Peripatetic sense; he did not write treatises on the Necessary Existent or the syllogism. But he was a philosopher of science and a philosopher of culture of the first order. His body of work

raises, in an Islamic context, the most fundamental questions of epistemology: How do we know what we know? What distinguishes scientific knowledge from dogma, gossip, and wishful thinking? Can one understand a foreign culture without prejudice, and what methods does such understanding require? Can religion itself be studied scientifically? Al-Bīrūnī's answers to these questions remain remarkably fresh, and his practice remains a model of intellectual integrity. This chapter examines his comparative methodology, his empirical epistemology, and his enduring relevance as a philosopher of science.

1. The Encounter with India Al-Bīrūnī's opportunity to study India arose from political subjugation. He was attached to the court of Maḥmūd of Ghazna, the powerful ruler whose invasions of northern India in the early eleventh century brought immense wealth and captives back to Ghazna. Al-Bīrūnī, whether by choice or necessity, accompanied the military campaigns and spent several years in the Punjab, where he learned Sanskrit, studied with Indian scholars, and immersed himself in the religious, philosophical, and scientific literature of Hindu India. His Kitāb al-Hind is the fruit of that immersion. What makes the Kitāb al-Hind remarkable is not merely its vast learning — it covers Indian religion, philosophy, literature, customs, law, astronomy, mathematics, and geography — but its methodological self-consciousness. AlBīrūnī opens the book with a long prologue in which he reflects on the difficulties of understanding another culture. The obstacles, he notes, are many: language barriers, the tendency of people to be attached to their own customs and to despise those of others, the deliberate distortions introduced by partisanship and propaganda, and the sheer complexity of the material. The scholar who wishes to understand must cultivate a set of virtues: patience, linguistic competence, a suspension of judgement, a willingness to let the subjects speak for themselves, and a commitment to record accurately even what one finds religiously or philosophically objectionable. Al-Bīrūnī explicitly criticises earlier Muslim accounts of India for their inaccuracy and prejudice. He notes that most previous authors had simply repeated hearsay or had allowed their own cultural and religious assumptions to colour their descriptions. He, by contrast, will report what the Indians themselves say, citing their own books and their own teachers. He will not pass judgement on the truth or falsehood of their religious doctrines; his goal is to understand and describe them as they are, not to refute or convert. This is a breathtakingly modern methodological principle — a principle of descriptive neutrality that anthropologists and phenomenologists of religion would articulate only many centuries later.

2. The Scientific Study of Religion and Society Al-Bīrūnī applied this principle across the entire spectrum of Indian civilisation. He described the Hindu concept of God, the doctrines of karma and rebirth, the caste system, the ritual practices, the dietary laws, the philosophical schools (he gives a remarkably accurate account of Sāṃkhya, Yoga, and Vedānta), the astronomical and mathematical sciences, and the literary epics. His descriptions are detailed, systematic, and remarkably free of polemic. He compares Indian doctrines with Greek philosophy (finding parallels between the Hindu ātman and the Platonic soul, for instance) and with Islamic and Christian ideas, not to claim superiority but to illuminate difference and similarity. His treatment of the caste system is particularly instructive. Al-Bīrūnī describes it with ethnographic precision: the four varṇas, the rules of purity and pollution, the hereditary occupations, the status of the caṇḍālas (outcastes). He does not editorialise. But implicit in his description is a sociological observation: the caste system, like all social hierarchies, is a human construct maintained by religious sanction, and it varies in its details from region to region.

This cool, analytical gaze is that of the naturalist who has turned his attention from minerals and stars to human societies. What motivated al-Bīrūnī to undertake such a study? Partly, it was sheer intellectual curiosity. He was a polymath who found all knowledge delightful. But partly it was a philosophical conviction: that the study of other cultures is a branch of natural science, because human beings are part of nature and their beliefs and practices can be observed, described, and compared. This conviction, which underlay his entire scientific practice, set him apart from many of his contemporaries, who regarded foreign religions as mere error to be refuted.

3. Empirical Epistemology: Observation Against Authority Al-Bīrūnī's approach to the natural sciences was governed by the same empiricism. He was a vocal critic of armchair science — the tendency to rely on textual authority rather than direct observation and measurement. In his astronomical and geographical works, he repeatedly insisted that the true scientist tests and verifies. His Tahdīd Nihāyāt al-Amākin (The Determination of the Coordinates of Cities), for instance, is a work of mathematical geography in which he corrected the longitudes and latitudes of hundreds of locations by combining reports from travellers with mathematical calculations, always checking and cross-checking. He was particularly severe on astrologers who made unsupported claims. In his Kitāb al-Tafhīm li-Awāʾil Ṣināʿat alTanjīm (Book of Instruction in the Elements of the Art of Astrology), he distinguished between astronomy, which is a demonstrative science based on mathematics and observation, and astrology, which he regarded as largely conjectural. He did not reject astrology outright — he believed the stars had influences — but he insisted that astrological claims must be tested empirically, and that most of the astrological lore circulating in his day was unreliable. One famous episode illustrates al-Bīrūnī's empiricism vividly. In his correspondence with Avicenna, he challenged Avicenna's Aristotelian physics on several points, including the impossibility of other worlds and the existence of void space. Avicenna, defending the established Peripatetic position, argued deductively from first principles. AlBīrūnī countered with empirical observation and with an appeal to the limits of human knowledge: just because we have not observed other worlds does not mean they do not exist; the universe may be far larger and stranger than our philosophical systems suppose. This willingness to challenge authority with observation, and to hold philosophical systems accountable to empirical data, is the hallmark of al-Bīrūnī's scientific temperament.

4. Rejection of Mere Authority and the Ethic of Scepticism Al-Bīrūnī was not a sceptic in the radical sense of refusing to affirm any knowledge. But he was a methodological sceptic: he believed that intellectual integrity requires constant questioning of received opinions, a habit of distinguishing between what is known by demonstration, what is transmitted by reliable testimony, and what is merely conjectured or dogmatically asserted. In this, he stood in the tradition of the Hellenistic empirical schools of medicine and the Ptolemaic astronomical tradition, but he radicalised their principles and applied them to fields where scepticism was rarely welcomed — particularly religion and culture. His Kitāb al-Hind contains a striking passage in which he reflects on the credulity of the common people and the responsibility of the scholar. People, he says, are prone to believe what they are told by those in authority, and they repeat it without verification. The scholar's duty is to resist this universal human tendency, to doubt, to investigate, to refuse to transmit what he has not himself confirmed. This ethical dimension of al-Bīrūnī's epistemology — the idea that intellectual honesty is a moral virtue — is one of his most important legacies. He was, in a deep sense, a philosopher of intellectual conscience.

His ethical epistemology extended to his own work. When he reported the doctrines of the Hindus, he explicitly stated that he was faithfully transmitting what they believed, not what he believed. This was a way of honouring the distinction between testimony and endorsement, a distinction that modern readers take for granted but that was not at all standard in medieval inter-religious writing. Al-Bīrūnī was, in this respect, far ahead of his time.

5. A Model of Philosophical Objectivity Al-Bīrūnī's work offers a model of philosophical objectivity that is neither a naive positivism nor a cold detachment. He was a deeply religious man — a Muslim who believed in the truth of Islam — and he did not hide his own commitments. In the Kitāb al-Hind, he sometimes notes where Indian doctrines contradict Islamic teachings and expresses his disagreement. But his disagreement does not contaminate his description. He practises what we would now call "methodological agnosticism": for the purposes of understanding, the truth or falsehood of the subject's beliefs is bracketed. The task is to see the world as they see it, and to do so in a way that they would recognise as accurate. This capacity for intellectual empathy — for entering into the mind of another culture without losing one's own critical faculties — is exceedingly rare in any age. It is what makes al-Bīrūnī a philosopher of science in the richest sense: a thinker who not only does science but reflects deeply on what science is and what it requires of the scientist. He understood that the empirical study of human beings demands as much rigour and self-discipline as the study of the stars, and that the temptation to prejudice is far greater.

6. Al-Bīrūnī in the History of Philosophy Al-Bīrūnī's contemporaries did not fully grasp the philosophical implications of his comparative and empirical methodology. He was admired as an astronomer, a mathematician, and a historian, but his philosophy of science did not form a school in the way that Avicenna's metaphysics did. The Peripatetic and Illuminationist traditions continued to dominate Islamic philosophy, and al-Bīrūnī's works, for all their brilliance, remained somewhat on the periphery of the philosophical curriculum. Yet his influence can be traced in unexpected places. Ibn Khaldūn, the great philosopher of history of the fourteenth century, adopted a strikingly similar historical methodology: he too insisted on the need to verify reports, to understand the social and natural conditions that shape human events, and to approach the study of history with the critical spirit of a natural scientist. Whether Ibn Khaldūn had read al-Bīrūnī is uncertain, but the methodological parallels are strong. Al-Bīrūnī's comparative approach to religion also found direct continuators in Abū al-Maʿālī's Bayān al-Adyān (1092), al-Shahrastānī's Kitāb al-Milal wa-l-Niḥal (Book of Religions and Sects, c. 1127), Rashīd al-Dīn Faḍl Allāh's Jāmiʿ al-Tawārīkh in Ilkhanid Iran, and the Ottoman polymath Kātib Çelebī (1609– 1657), whose Irshād al-Ḥayārā ilā Tārīkh al-Yūnān wa-l-Rūm wa-l-Naṣārā (1654) applied a comparative historical and institutional method to the study of European Christian nations and their religious and political orders. In the modern period, al-Bīrūnī has been rediscovered by scholars seeking to show that the scientific spirit is not a European monopoly. His Kitāb al-Hind has been hailed as a foundational text of anthropology, and his empirical critique of Avicenna's physics has been cited as evidence that the Islamic scientific tradition contained internal resources for moving beyond Aristotelianism — resources that, for complex historical reasons, were not fully exploited. Al-Bīrūnī stands as a reminder that the history of Islamic philosophy is not only the story of falsafa and ḥikma but also the story of a vigorous and self-reflective scientific empiricism, grounded in a distinctively Islamic cosmopolitanism.

7. The Turn to Critique and Response With al-Bīrūnī's comparative philosophy of science, the broad landscape of early Islamic thought attains a remarkable variety. We have now followed the tradition from the first translations of Greek texts, through the rational theology of the Muʿtazila, the founding of the Peripatetic tradition by al-Kindī and al-Fārābī, the popular encyclopaedism of the Brethren of Purity, the ethical humanism of Ibn Miskawayh, the towering synthesis of Avicenna, and the empirical conscience of al-Bīrūnī. The stage is now set for Part IV, which opens by reconstructing the systematic metaphysics and epistemology of classical Sunnī kalām — the Ashʿarī school of al-Ashʿarī, alBāqillānī, and al-Juwaynī alongside the Central Asian Māturīdī school of Abū Manṣūr al-Māturīdī and Abū alMuʿīn al-Nasafī (Chapter 12) — before turning to the dramatic confrontation between Abū Ḥāmid al-Ghazālī (Chapter 13), the systematic verification (taḥqīq) of Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī and the post-Rāzian tradition (Chapter 14), the Andalusian philosophical vision of Ibn Bājja and Ibn Ṭufayl (Chapter 15), and the Aristotelian defense mounted by Abū al-Walīd Ibn Rushd (Averroes, Chapter 16). Al-Ghazālī's Tahāfut al-Falāsifa (The Incoherence of the Philosophers) will challenge the very foundations of the Avicennan cosmology and claim that the philosophers have failed to demonstrate the eternity of the world, God's knowledge of particulars, and the possibility of bodily resurrection — charges that, if sustained, would render philosophy not merely incomplete but religiously dangerous. Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī will absorb al-Ghazālī's critique and transform it into a new, more rigorous synthesis of Avicennism and Ashʿarism, a "verification" that would define the terms of philosophical theology for centuries. Ibn Rushd's response, Tahāfut al-Tahāfut (The Incoherence of the Incoherence), will defend philosophy's right to exist and will attempt to reconcile the Qur'an with the Aristotelian tradition in a way that differs significantly from Avicenna's. The next part of this book turns to that great debate, which would shape the destinies of philosophy in the Islamic West and East for centuries to come.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “This book is not a polemical treatise of refutation and debate, in which we set out the arguments of an opponent in order to demolish them; it is a straightforward record of testimony (kitāb ḥikāya), in which I state the doctrines of the Indians exactly as they themselves hold them, adding the parallel teachings of the Greek philosophers and the Sufis where they shed light upon the subject, for truth is served only when one strips the soul of partisan passion, habitual prejudice, and blind obedience to authority.” — Abū Rayḥān al-Bīrūnī, Kitāb Taḥqīq mā li-l-Hind (Verifying All That the Indians Recount, Reasonable and Unreasonable)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. In his eighteen-question correspondence with the young Avicenna (al-Asʾila wa-l-Ajwiba), how did al-Bīrūnī challenge Aristotle's On the Heavens regarding the possibility of elliptical planetary orbits, the existence of a vacuum, and the plurality of worlds? 2. What methodological rules of comparative cross-cultural scholarship did al-Bīrūnī establish in Kitāb al-Hind, and why did he insist on learning Sanskrit to read the Bhagavad Gītā, Patañjali's Yoga-Sūtras, and Sāṃkhya texts in the original? 3. How did al-Bīrūnī diagnose the sociological and linguistic cleavage between the philosophical monotheism of the educated Sanskrit elite (khāṣṣa) and the anthropomorphic idol-veneration of the common multitude (ʿāmma), and how did he compare this to Greek and Islamic society? 4. In what respects does al-Bīrūnī's integration of observational testing, mathematical modeling, and historical source criticism represent an alternative empiricist tradition within classical Islamic philosophy?

Part IV: Classical Kalām, the Ghazālian Critique, Post-Rāzian Taḥqīq, and Andalusia

Chapter 12Classical Sunnī Kalām as Philosophy: Ashʿarī and Māturīdī Metaphysics from al-Māturīdī and al-Bāqillānī to al-Juwaynī and al-Nasafī

Why Sunnī Kalām Belongs at the Center of Islamic Philosophy

Between the height of the Muʿtazilī school in the ninth century (Chapter 3) and al-Ghazālī's confrontation with Avicennan Peripateticism at the turn of the twelfth century, two major schools of Sunnī dialectical theology (ʿilm alkalām) — the Ashʿarī school founded in Iraq by Abū al-Ḥasan al-Ashʿarī (c. 874–936 CE) and the Māturīdī school founded in Transoxiana by Abū Manṣūr al-Māturīdī (c. 853–944 CE) — forged complete, rival philosophical systems of ontology, epistemology, philosophy of nature, and moral agency. For too long, histories of philosophy written under the spell of nineteenth-century classification schemes relegated Ashʿarī and Māturīdī thought to 'dogmatic theology' while reserving the title 'philosophy' exclusively for Greek-style falsafa. Yet the classical mutakallimūn did not merely quote scripture; recognizing that scriptural authority itself presupposes a prior rational proof of the existence, unity, and veracity of the divine Speaker, they constructed rigorous foundationalist epistemologies, an atomist physics of discrete space and time, modal proofs from contingency and particularization (takhṣīṣ), and subtle analyses of causality and human action.

1. Abū al-Ḥasan al-Ashʿarī and the Founding of the Ashʿarī School Born in Basra, Abū al-Ḥasan ʿAlī ibn Ismāʿīl al-Ashʿarī (c. 874–936) studied for forty years as the star pupil of the head of the Basran Muʿtazila, Abū ʿAlī al-Jubbāʾī. Around the year 912, however, al-Ashʿarī experienced a profound intellectual crisis over the Muʿtazilī claim that human reason can bind God to act according to humanly conceived rules of 'what is best' (al-ṣalāḥ wa-l-aṣlaḥ). In the famous pedagogical parable of the Three Brothers — a righteous adult believer, a wicked adult unbeliever, and a child who dies in infancy — al-Ashʿarī exposed the internal contradiction of Muʿtazilī rationalist theodicy: if God took the child's life early 'for the child's own good' because He foresaw that the child would grow up to sin, then the wicked adult brother in Hell can rightly ask why God did not grant him the same early death. Abandoning the Muʿtazilī circle in the Great Mosque of Basra, al-Ashʿarī vowed to turn the sharp weapons of rational dialectic (kalām) to the defense of Sunnī orthodoxy. In treatises such as Kitāb al-Lumaʿ (The Book of Flashes) and Istiḥsān al-Khawḍ fī ʿIlm al-Kalām (A Vindication of the Practice of Dialectical Theology), al-Ashʿarī staked out a middle path between Muʿtazilī rationalism and extreme Ḥanbalī literalism: Real Eternal Attributes (Ṣifāt al-Maʿānī): Against the Muʿtazilī reduction of God's attributes to His bare essence, al-Ashʿarī held that God possesses eternal, real attributes of Knowledge (ʿilm), Power (qudra), Life (ḥayāt), Will (irāda), Hearing (samʿ), Sight (baṣar), and Speech (kalām) that subsist in His essence — they are 'neither identical with His essence nor other than He' (lā hiya huwa wa-lā hiya ghayruhu). Uncreated Inner Speech (al-Kalām al-Nafsī): Distinguishing between the physical ink, sounds, and letters (ḥurūf wa-aṣwāt) of the recited Qurʾān (which are created) and the eternal inner meaning subsisting in the

divine essence (al-kalām al-nafsī), al-Ashʿarī resolved the theological deadlock of the Miḥna. The Doctrine of Acquisition (Kasb / Iktisāb): To preserve both divine omnipotence and human moral accountability without making humans autonomous creators of being alongside God, al-Ashʿarī argued that God creates every human act together with a created, momentary human power (qudra ḥāditha) at the very instant of the action, through which the human agent 'acquires' (yaktasibu) the act and becomes legally and morally responsible for it.

2. Classical Ashʿarī Atomism, Occasionalism, and the Argument from Particularization: AlBāqillānī and al-Juwaynī Over the next century and a half, two intellectual giants transformed al-Ashʿarī's insights into a systematic metaphysical edifice: the Mālikī judge of Baghdad Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn al-Ṭayyib al-Bāqillānī (d. 1013 CE, author of Kitāb al-Tamhīd and al-Inṣāf) and the Nishapur master Abū al-Maʿālī ʿAbd al-Malik al-Juwaynī (1028– 1085 CE, known as Imām al-Ḥaramayn and teacher of al-Ghazālī, author of al-Shāmil fī Uṣūl al-Dīn and Kitāb alIrshād).

The Ontology of Atoms (Jawāhir), Accidents (Aʿrāḍ), and Continuous Re-Creation To prove the temporal origination of the physical universe (ḥudūth al-ʿālam) against Aristotelian eternalism, alBāqillānī systematized an ontology in which everything other than God (mā siwā Allāh) consists exclusively of two categories: indivisible, dimensionless atoms (al-jawāhir al-farda) that become extended bodies (ajsām) only when combined, and transient accidents (aʿrāḍ — such as motion, rest, color, composition, and life) that inhere in those atoms. Al-Bāqillānī established three foundational ontological premises: 1. Accidents are real entities distinct from atoms, and no accident can endure across two moments of time (alʿaraḍ lā yabqā zamānayn); every accident ceases to exist instantaneously upon its creation unless God creates a fresh replica of it at the next discrete instant. 2. No atom can ever exist stripped of all positive or privative accidents (al-jawhar lā yakhlū ʿan al-aʿrāḍ), such as motion or rest, combination or separation. 3. Whatever cannot precede that which is temporally originated is itself temporally originated (mā lā yasbiqu alḥādith fa-huwa ḥādith). Since every atom in the cosmos is inseparable from temporally originated accidents, the entire physical universe must have an absolute temporal beginning. This discrete, quantum-like ontology directly entailed radical occasionalism: because no created accident endures from instant t1 to instant t2, finite things possess no intrinsic causal natures (ṭabāʾiʿ) capable of necessitating future effects. What we perceive as natural causality — fire burning cotton, bread satisfying hunger, light following sunrise — is the habitual custom of God (ʿādat Allāh / ijrāʾ al-ʿāda), who freely re-creates the world's atoms and accidents at every discrete moment. Al-Bāqillānī even defended the famous thesis of the Equivalence of Proofs (inʿikās al-adilla): if an argument for a theological conclusion is invalid, the conclusion itself would be false — a bold principle that al-Juwaynī and Ibn Khaldūn later overturned by distinguishing between the invalidity of a particular proof and the falsity of the proposition proved.

Al-Juwaynī's Modal Turn: Particularization (Takhṣīṣ) and the Assimilation of Avicenna Working in Nishapur only decades after Avicenna's death, Imām al-Ḥaramayn al-Juwaynī was the first great Ashʿarī theologian to engage directly with Avicenna's modal metaphysics. In al-Irshād and al-ʿAqīda al-Niẓāmiyya, al-Juwaynī shifted the cosmological argument from pure atomism to the Principle of Particularization

(takhṣīṣ): every feature of the universe — its coming to exist at one time rather than another, the specific sizes and trajectories of celestial bodies, and the exact configurations of matter — is equally possible (jāʾiz / mumkin) relative to its opposite. Because a bare possibility cannot tip the scales in favor of its own existence over non-existence, every contingent state requires a free, willing Particularizer (Mukhaṣṣiṣ Murīd) who selects one possibility out of an infinity of equally possible alternatives by sheer volition (irāda) rather than natural necessity (ījāb dhātī). This argument became the direct foundation for al-Ghazālī's critique of Avicenna in the Tahāfut.

3. Abū Manṣūr al-Māturīdī and the Central Asian Māturīdī Tradition: Epistemology and Moral Realism While Ashʿarism came to dominate the Shāfiʿī and Mālikī madrasas of Iraq, Syria, Egypt, and North Africa, an equally sophisticated Sunnī philosophical theology flourished in Samarqand and Bukhara among the Ḥanafī scholars of Transoxiana: the Māturīdī school, founded by Abū Manṣūr Muḥammad ibn Muḥammad al-Māturīdī (c. 853–944 CE) and brought to its classical zenith by Abū al-Muʿīn Maymūn ibn Muḥammad al-Nasafī (1046–1114 CE) in his monumental Tabṣirat al-Adilla (The Enlightenment of Proofs).

Al-Māturīdī's Foundational Epistemology in Kitāb al-Tawḥīd Al-Māturīdī's Kitāb al-Tawḥīd is the earliest surviving systematic Sunnī treatise to open with a comprehensive theory of knowledge (maʿrifa). Writing against skeptics (Sūfisṭāʾiyya), dualists (Manichaeans, Bardaisanites, and Marcionites), and naturalists (Dahriyya), al-Māturīdī established three irreducible, mutually reinforcing sources of human knowledge (asbāb al-ʿilm): Sense Perception (al-ʿiyān / al-ḥawāss): Yielding immediate, indubitable knowledge of particular empirical objects; anyone who denies sensory truths in debate should be subjected to physical pain until he admits he feels it. Authentic

Reports

and

Testimony

(al-akhbār):

Including

both

multiply

transmitted

historical/geographical knowledge (tawātur) that compels certainty and the testimony of prophets authenticated by evidentiary signs. Rational Reflection and Inference (al-naẓar / al-ʿaql): The faculty that integrates sensory data and testimony, infers the unseen from the seen, and discerns the wisdom (ḥikma) vs. foolishness (safah) of actions.

Key Philosophical Divergences Between Māturīdism and Ashʿarism Although later Ottoman classifiers listed scores of differences between the two Sunnī schools, four profound philosophical disagreements separate Māturīdī metaphysics and ethics from classical Ashʿarism: 1. Moral Epistemology (Ḥusn wa-Qubḥ ʿAqliyyān): Whereas Ashʿarīs adopted divine command theory — holding that nothing is intrinsically good or evil prior to scriptural revelation and that knowing God is obligatory only after a prophet arrives — al-Māturīdī and al-Nasafī upheld a moderate moral realism. Reason (ʿaql), they argued, independently recognizes the intrinsic goodness (ḥusn) of gratitude to the Benefactor, truthtelling, and justice, and the intrinsic evil (qubḥ) of ingratitude, lying, and oppression; therefore, every sane adult is rationally obligated to know the Creator even if no prophet has ever reached them. 2. Divine Wisdom (Ḥikma) in Action: While Ashʿarīs denied that God's actions have rational purposes (aghrāḍ / ḥikam) lest God be 'perfected' by an external end, the Māturīdīs insisted that every divine act is grounded in objective Wisdom (ḥikma) — defined as 'placing each thing in its rightful place' (waḍʿ al-shayʾ fī mawḍiʿihi) — and that acting in vain (ʿabath) is impossible for the Wise Creator.

3. Takwīn (Bringing-into-Being) as an Eternal Attribute: Against the Ashʿarī view that God's act of creating (takwīn) is identical to the temporally originated creature (al-mukawwan) or a mere relation, the Māturīdīs maintained that Takwīn is a real, eternal eighth substantive attribute of God alongside Power and Will, through which contingent things enter existence at their appointed times without introducing change into the divine essence. 4. Twofold Agency and Partial Choice (al-Ikhtiyār al-Juzʾī): Refining the theory of human action beyond Ashʿarī kasb, Māturīdī thinkers analyzed every voluntary act through two aspects: God creates the ontological reality of the act (khalq al-fiʿl), while the human agent exercises a real, non-created relational intention or partial resolve (al-ʿazm al-muṣammam / al-ikhtiyār al-juzʾī) that directs created power toward obedience or sin, thereby grounding genuine moral desert. Through al-Nasafī's Tabṣirat al-Adilla, Najm al-Dīn ʿUmar al-Nasafī's (d. 1142) al-ʿAqāʾid al-Nasafiyya, and the later commentary on it by Saʿd al-Dīn al-Taftāzānī (d. 1390), this Māturīdī synthesis of realist epistemology, rational wisdom, and Sunnī orthodoxy became the dominant philosophical theology of Central Asia, the Ottoman Empire, and the Mughal-Indian subcontinent.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “The pathways through which knowledge of the realities of things is attained are three: the healthy senses (alʿiyān), authentic reports (al-akhbār), and rational investigation (al-naẓar). Whoever rejects rational investigation has no way to establish his own rejection other than through rational investigation itself — and thus his very denial proves the necessity of reason.” — Abū Manṣūr al-Māturīdī, Kitāb al-Tawḥīd (The Book of Divine Unity)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How does al-Bāqillānī's three-premise atomist proof of the temporal origination of the world (ḥudūth al-ʿālam) lead directly to occasionalism, and how does it differ from Avicenna's modal proof from contingency? 2. Why did Imām al-Ḥaramayn al-Juwaynī shift the Ashʿarī cosmological proof toward the Principle of Particularization (takhṣīṣ), and how does this argument address the Peripatetic claim that the world flows eternally from God by necessity? 3. Compare the positions of the Muʿtazila, the Ashʿarīs, and the Māturīdīs on whether human reason can independently discern moral good and evil (al-ḥusn wa-l-qubḥ al-ʿaqliyyān). How does al-Māturīdī's concept of divine Wisdom (ḥikma) mediate between the other two schools? 4. How does the Māturīdī distinction between God's creation of the act (khalq) and the human agent's partial choice or resolve (al-ʿazm al-muṣammam / al-ikhtiyār al-juzʾī) attempt to avoid the charge of fatalism (jabr) often leveled against Ashʿarī acquisition (kasb)?
