Chapter 13Al-Ghazālī: The Proof of Islam

The Crisis and the Cure

Abū Ḥāmid Muḥammad ibn Muḥammad al-Ghazālī (c. 1058–1111) was born in Ṭūs, in northeastern Iran, and died there after a life that has become the iconic drama of Islamic intellectual history. He rose with meteoric speed through the ranks of scholarship, securing by the age of thirty-three the most prestigious professorship in the Seljuk empire, at the Niẓāmiyya madrasa in Baghdad. He mastered fiqh (jurisprudence), kalām (theology), and the doctrines of the falāsifa so thoroughly that he could write a devastating critique of their metaphysics. And then, in 1095, at the pinnacle of his fame, he broke down. A spiritual and intellectual crisis — described in his autobiographical al-Munqidh min al-Ḍalāl (The Deliverer from Error) — left him unable to speak, unable to teach, paralysed by doubt about the foundations of knowledge itself. He abandoned his post, gave away his wealth, and disappeared into the life of a wandering Sufi, spending eleven years in seclusion, prayer, and spiritual discipline. When he emerged, he had become something new: not merely a theologian or a jurist, but a mujaddid (renewer) of the faith, a man who had personally suffered the limits of reason and had found in Sufi experience a certainty that syllogisms could not provide. He wrote his masterpiece, Iḥyāʾ ʿUlūm al-Dīn (The Revival of the Religious Sciences), a forty-book summa that integrated law, theology, ethics, and mysticism into a comprehensive guide to the spiritual life. And he wrote Tahāfut al-Falāsifa (The Incoherence of the Philosophers), the most influential philosophical critique in Islamic history — a book that charged al-Fārābī and Avicenna with heresy on three counts and that set the terms of debate for the next eight centuries. Al-Ghazālī is a towering and ambiguous figure. To his admirers, he is the Proof of Islam (Ḥujjat al-Islām), the man who purified the faith from Hellenistic contamination and showed the limits of reason while vindicating Sufi gnosis. To his detractors, he is the man who killed philosophy in the Islamic world, whose assault on the falāsifa inaugurated a long decline into dogmatic slumber. Neither caricature survives serious examination. Al-Ghazālī did not kill philosophy; he transformed it, forcing later philosophers — including Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, Ibn Rushd, alṬūsī, and Mullā Ṣadrā — to engage with his criticisms and to refine their own arguments. And his own thought was far more philosophical, and far more complex, than the polemical caricature of the Tahāfut suggests. This chapter examines the arc of al-Ghazālī's intellectual life: his crisis of doubt, his philosophical critique, his occasionalism, and the philosophical mysticism that crowns his later work.

1. From Philosophy to Doubt to Sufism: The Intellectual Itinerary Al-Ghazālī's autobiography, al-Munqidh min al-Ḍalāl, is one of the most compelling documents of medieval philosophy. It is not a straightforward biography but a philosophical narrative of the soul's search for certainty. AlGhazālī describes how, as a young man, he was driven by a "thirst for the true nature of things" — a desire that was not academic but existential. He needed to know, not merely to believe, and he could not rest with second-hand conviction. He surveyed four paths to knowledge, subjecting each to a sceptical interrogation. The first was taqlīd — the unquestioning acceptance of authority, of parents, teachers, and tradition. This he found intellectually worthless: one's birth in a particular community is an accident, not an argument. The second was kalām, the dialectical theology of the Ashʿarī school. Kalām, he concluded, was useful for defending dogma against heretics but could not provide genuine certainty, because its premises were accepted on faith and its arguments were merely probable. The third was philosophy (falsafa), which he studied with intense dedication for two years. The philosophers, he argued, were guilty of grave metaphysical errors, but their logic and mathematics were sound and indispensable. The fourth was Sufism (taṣawwuf), which claimed that certainty was not a matter of argument but of dhawq — direct tasting, immediate experience — and that the soul could achieve a knowledge of God beyond the reach of reason. After his crisis, al-Ghazālī embraced Sufism not as a rejection of reason but as its completion. Reason, he held, can bring one to the threshold of the divine but cannot cross it. The eye of the intellect sees that there is a realm beyond its reach, just as the eye of the body sees colours but cannot hear sounds. Sufi practice — prayer, fasting, solitude, the invocation of God's name — purifies the soul and opens the "eye of the heart" (ʿayn al-qalb), through which a direct knowledge of spiritual realities becomes possible. This knowledge is not irrational; it is suprarational. The philosopher who stops at syllogism is like a man who trusts only the sense of touch and denies the evidence of sight and hearing.

2. The Incoherence of the Philosophers: The Three Charges The Tahāfut al-Falāsifa is a philosophical polemic of remarkable energy and sophistication. Its purpose, al-Ghazālī declares in the introduction, is to examine the teachings of the Muslim Peripatetics — especially al-Fārābī and Avicenna — and to show that in certain crucial matters they have failed to meet their own standard of demonstrative proof. They claimed for their doctrines the certainty of syllogistic demonstration; al-Ghazālī proposes to demonstrate that their arguments are dialectical at best and sophistical at worst. He divides their teachings into three categories. In physics and logic, he finds little objectionable; their errors are minor and do not touch religion. In mathematics, he finds nothing objectionable at all, and he warns readers against rejecting mathematics merely because philosophers study it. But in metaphysics, he identifies seventeen doctrines that are either unproven or false, and three that are so dangerous that their proponents must be declared unbelievers (kuffār). Those three are: The eternity of the world. The philosophers held that the universe had no temporal beginning, flowing eternally from God by necessity. This, al-Ghazālī charged, contradicts the Qur'anic doctrine of creation and makes God an involuntary cause rather than a free Creator. If the world is eternal, then miracles are impossible, because every event is part of a necessary causal chain that cannot be interrupted. God's knowledge of particulars. The philosophers held that God knows particulars only "in a universal way" — through knowing the universal causes, not the particular events as temporally distinct items. Al-Ghazālī

charged that this effectively denies God's omniscience as affirmed in the Qur'an, which asserts that not a leaf falls without God's knowledge. Bodily resurrection. The philosophers held that the afterlife is purely spiritual — the soul's intellectual bliss or torment, not a physical return to the body. Al-Ghazālī charged that this contradicts the explicit Qur'anic promise of bodily resurrection and physical reward and punishment. On these three points, al-Ghazālī declared, the philosophers have not just erred; they have contradicted the clear meaning of revelation, and their position constitutes unbelief. On the other seventeen doctrines — including the emanation scheme, the philosophers' claim that creation is necessary, and their denial of God's knowledge of particulars in their particularity — they are merely incorrect, heretical (bidʿa), but not guilty of outright apostasy. The argumentative strategy of the Tahāfut is noteworthy. Al-Ghazālī does not set out to prove the opposite of the philosophers' doctrines using scripture or theology. Instead, he adopts their own philosophical methods — logical analysis, conceptual distinction, counter-example — and attempts to show that their arguments are insufficient. He does not claim that eternity is false; he claims the philosophers have not demonstrated it. His sceptical conclusion is that philosophy cannot attain certainty on metaphysical questions, and that these questions are ultimately the province of revelation and spiritual experience.

3. Causality and Occasionalism One of the most philosophically rich sections of the Tahāfut is its seventeenth discussion, on causality. Al-Ghazālī challenges the philosophers' assumption that the connection between a cause and its effect is necessary. When fire touches cotton and the cotton burns, what do we observe? We observe the cotton in contact with the fire, and then we observe it burning. We do not observe a "necessity" linking the two events. The connection is a matter of habit (ʿāda), not rational demonstration. It is God who, by His will and power, creates the burning at the moment of contact with the fire. The fire is not the cause; God is the cause, and He creates the burning in conjunction with the presence of the fire as a matter of His customary practice. This is occasionalism: the view that all causal power belongs to God alone, and that created things are merely occasions for God's direct creative action. Al-Ghazālī's version, developed in the Ashʿarī tradition, is radical but also carefully calibrated. He does not deny that events occur in regular patterns. The world is governed by God's ʿāda, His custom, which He has freely chosen to maintain. But this custom is not a metaphysical necessity; God could, if He willed, create the burning without the fire or prevent the fire from burning. Miracles are simply God's suspension of His custom on particular occasions to authenticate a prophet. This occasionalism had profound consequences. It made natural science possible — because God's custom is regular, we can study its patterns — but it also made natural science dependent on theology, because the ultimate explanation of every event is God's will, not created causal powers. Al-Ghazālī's position was sharply criticised by Ibn Rushd, who argued that it destroyed the very possibility of knowledge: if there is no necessary connection between cause and effect, then the scientist can never be confident that the same cause will produce the same effect tomorrow. Al-Ghazālī's response, implicit in his works, is that God's custom is reliable because God is wise and does not act arbitrarily; we can trust the regularity of the world not as a metaphysical necessity but as a divine promise.

4. The Niche of Lights: Philosophical Mysticism Al-Ghazālī's philosophical profundity is most evident not in the polemical Tahāfut but in his late mystical work Mishkāt al-Anwār (The Niche of Lights). This short treatise, an extended commentary on the Qur'anic Light Verse ("God is the Light of the heavens and the earth...," Q 24:35), is one of the most daring works of philosophical

mysticism in any language. It develops a metaphysics of light that would influence Suhrawardī's Illuminationism and, through him, the entire later tradition of ḥikma. Al-Ghazālī argues that light is not merely a physical phenomenon but the fundamental reality of existence. The true light is God, who is the light of all lights, the source of all visibility and all being. Everything that exists reflects divine light in varying degrees. The human soul, when purified, becomes a polished mirror in which God's light shines without obstruction. The supreme attainment of the mystic is fanāʾ — annihilation — in which the soul's own selfhood is so completely effaced that only God's light remains visible. At this station, the mystic realises the truth of the testimony of faith: lā ilāha illā Allāh, there is no god but God — there is no reality but the One. The Mishkāt is remarkable for its philosophical sophistication and for its willingness to push spiritual epistemology to its limits. Al-Ghazālī distinguishes between the everyday self, the rational self, and the innermost secret (sirr) that can witness God directly. He discusses the possibility that the universe is arranged in a hierarchy of lights — the visible light of the sun, the intellectual light of the soul, the angelic light of the celestial intelligences, and the uncreated light of God — each illuminating the level below it. The language is Neoplatonic in its structure, but alGhazālī gives it an Islamic and Sufi colouring that is entirely his own. The Mishkāt also contains passages of such extreme monism that some later readers accused al-Ghazālī of veering into the "unity of being" (waḥdat al-wujūd) associated with Ibn ʿArabī. Al-Ghazālī's defenders have argued that his monism is always qualified: it is an experience of the mystic, not an ontological claim about the identity of God and the world. But the ambiguity itself is productive; it shows that al-Ghazālī, for all his caution in the Tahāfut, was prepared to follow philosophical and spiritual reasoning into territories that bordered on the unsayable.

5. The Legacy of the Proof of Islam Al-Ghazālī's influence on Islamic thought is almost impossible to measure. His Iḥyāʾ ʿUlūm al-Dīn became one of the most widely read books in Islamic history, second only to the Qur'an and the ḥadīth collections in many regions. It redefined the relationship between law, theology, and Sufism, making interior spiritual purification the heart of Islam and legitimising Sufism for the orthodox scholars who had previously suspected it. Through the Iḥyāʾ, alGhazālī created a mainstream Sunnī piety that was at once legally exact, theologically Ashʿarī, and mystically deep — a synthesis that has endured to the present. The Tahāfut had a more complex legacy. It forced the philosophical tradition to defend itself, and it generated a genre of tahāfut literature — refutations and counter-refutations — that continued for centuries. Ibn Rushd's Tahāfut al-Tahāfut (The Incoherence of the Incoherence) was the most famous response, but Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī's theological engagement with Avicenna, al-Ṭūsī's defence of Avicennan philosophy, and the later synthesis of philosophy and theology in Mullā Ṣadrā all bear the mark of al-Ghazālī's challenge. Philosophy did not die after alGhazālī; it changed, and part of the change was that it had to address al-Ghazālī's objections with greater rigour and self-awareness. In the West, al-Ghazālī's Tahāfut was translated into Latin and influenced the sceptical currents of late medieval philosophy. His critique of causality has been compared to Hume's, and his autobiographical narrative of doubt has found readers from Montaigne to modern existentialists. The image of the great scholar who abandons everything to seek certainty in his own soul is permanently compelling, and al-Ghazālī's version of it — passionate, literate, intensely intellectual — remains among the most powerful in world literature.

6. From Baghdad to the Verification of Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī Al-Ghazālī died in Ṭūs in 1111, reconciled to the Sufi path and convinced that he had done what he could to revive the faith and to draw the philosophical sting. But his attack on the falāsifa could not go unanswered, and the response came not from a defender of the philosophers but from a fellow theologian who absorbed philosophy more deeply than al-Ghazālī himself had done. Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī (c. 1149–1209), the greatest Sunnī philosophertheologian of the post-classical period, took up al-Ghazālī's challenge and transformed it into a new, more rigorous synthesis of Avicennism and Ashʿarism. His method of taḥqīq — the systematic verification of every argument — and his monumental critique of Avicenna's Ishārāt would define the terms of philosophical theology for the next six centuries. The next chapter turns to this towering figure, the "Imām of the sceptics," whose work represents the mature Sunnī engagement with the philosophical tradition.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “The connection between what is habitually believed to be a cause and what is habitually believed to be an effect is not necessary according to us (al-iqtirān bayna mā yuʿtaqadu fī al-ʿāda sababan wa-mā yuʿtaqadu musabbaban laysa bi-ḍarūrī ʿindanā)... Rather, each of two distinct things is neither the other nor implied in the other; their conjunction proceeds from the prior decree of God, who creates them side by side, not because the conjunction is necessary in itself and incapable of separation.” — Abū Ḥāmid al-Ghazālī, Tahāfut al-Falāsifa (The Incoherence of the Philosophers, Discussion XVII)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. Why did al-Ghazālī compose Maqāṣid al-Falāsifa (The Intentions of the Philosophers) before publishing Tahāfut al-Falāsifa (The Incoherence of the Philosophers), and why did he single out only three of the twenty philosophical discussions — the eternity of the world, God's lack of knowledge of particulars, and the denial of bodily resurrection — as grounds for unbelief (takfīr)? 2. Analyze al-Ghazālī's critique of necessary natural causation in the Seventeenth Discussion of the Tahāfut using the example of cotton and fire. In what ways does al-Ghazālī's argument anticipate David Hume's critique of causal necessity, and how do their ultimate metaphysical conclusions differ? 3. How did al-Ghazālī defend the neutrality and indispensability of Avicennan logic in al-Qisṭās al-Mustaqīm, Miʿyār al-ʿIlm, and the prologue to al-Mustaṣfā min ʿIlm al-Uṣūl, declaring that 'whoever does not know logic has no reliable confidence in his sciences'? 4. In al-Munqidh min al-Ḍalāl (Deliverance from Error), how does al-Ghazālī overcome his radical skeptical crisis concerning sensory and rational first principles, and how does he synthesize Sufi interior ethics with Sunnī law in Iḥyāʾ ʿUlūm al-Dīn?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Al-Ghazālī, Abū Ḥāmid. The Incoherence of the Philosophers (Tahāfut al-Falāsifa). Translated by Michael E. Marmura. 2nd ed. Louisville, KY: Fons Vitae, 1980. Al-Ghazālī, Abū Ḥāmid. The Niche of Lights (Mishkāt al-Anwār). Translated by David Buchman. Provo, UT: Brigham Young University Press, 1998. Griffel, Frank. Al-Ghazālī's Philosophical Theology. Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2009.

Chapter 14Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī: The Philosopher-Theologian and the Turn to Verification

The Imām of the Sceptics

Fakhr al-Dīn Muḥammad ibn ʿUmar al-Rāzī (c. 1149–1209) was born in Rayy, the ancient city near modern Tehran that had already given the Islamic world the physician-philosopher Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī three centuries earlier. By the time of his death in Herat, he had written over a hundred works on theology, philosophy, logic, Qur'anic exegesis, jurisprudence, medicine, and the occult sciences, and he had decisively altered the relationship between kalām and falsafa in the Sunnī world. His contemporaries called him "the Imām of the sceptics" (imām al-mushakkikīn) — a title that acknowledged both the probing, relentless quality of his mind and the unease it provoked in traditionalist circles. He was, after al-Ghazālī, the most important Sunnī philosophertheologian of the post-classical period, and his influence on the madrasa curriculum from Cairo to Calcutta persisted for six centuries. Al-Ghazālī had attacked the falāsifa and then turned to Sufism; al-Rāzī absorbed philosophy so thoroughly that his own theology became a kind of Avicennan kalām, a dialectical system that used the logical tools of Avicenna to defend Ashʿarī doctrine while simultaneously subjecting the philosophers' central proofs to devastating internal critique. His method of taḥqīq — a rigorous "verification" of arguments that demanded the scholar examine every premise and consider every objection — became the standard of intellectual seriousness in the post-classical tradition. In al-Rāzī, the boundary between philosophy and theology all but dissolves. He is a theologian who philosophises and a philosopher who worships, and his monumental commentary on Avicenna's al-Ishārāt wa-lTanbīhāt remains one of the most penetrating critiques of Avicennan metaphysics ever written. This chapter examines al-Rāzī's life, his method, his critique of Avicenna, his own philosophical theology, and his legacy for later Islamic thought.

1. Life and Intellectual Milieu Al-Rāzī received his early education from his father, the preacher and scholar Ḍiyāʾ al-Dīn al-Makkī, and then studied at the Niẓāmiyya madrasa in Nishapur under the pupils of al-Juwaynī and al-Ghazālī. He travelled extensively through Persia, Khwārazm, Transoxiana, and Afghanistan, debating with philosophers, Muʿtazilīs, and Karramī literalists, and attracting the patronage of Khwārazm-Shāhs and Ghurid sultans. A famous anecdote records his debate in Herat with a prominent Muʿtazilī scholar, whom he reduced to silence; the defeated scholar reportedly died of chagrin. Such stories, whether true or embroidered, capture al-Rāzī's reputation as a fearsome dialectician. His intellectual project was shaped by two forces. The first was the post-Ghazālian imperative: any Sunnī theologian who wished to be taken seriously had to master Avicennan logic and metaphysics, because al-Ghazālī himself had shown that ignorance of philosophy left theology defenceless. The second was the rise of the Shīʿī Avicennan tradition, particularly in the eastern Islamic lands, which claimed Avicenna as a forerunner of Shīʿī wisdom. To combat this claim, al-Rāzī needed to demonstrate that the Avicennan system was philosophically flawed and that Sunnī theology could offer a superior rational account of God, the world, and the soul.

His major works fall into three phases. The early phase produced his al-Maḥṣūl fī ʿIlm Uṣūl al-Fiqh, a pathbreaking work of legal theory that applied Avicennan logic and semantics to the principles of jurisprudence — itself a philosophical act of immense consequence. The middle phase saw his great theological summa, the al-Maṭālib alʿĀliya min al-ʿIlm al-Ilāhī (The High Inquiries in Divine Science), a nine-book treatise on metaphysics that is one of the largest works of philosophical theology in Islamic history. The late phase produced his unfinished Sharḥ alIshārāt, the most important Sunnī engagement with Avicenna's masterpiece, and his vast Qur'anic commentary, Mafātīḥ al-Ghayb (Keys of the Unseen), which weaves theology, philosophy, law, and mysticism into a single exegetical tapestry.

2. The Method of Taḥqīq: Verification as a Virtue Al-Rāzī's most distinctive contribution to Islamic intellectual culture was his formalisation of taḥqīq — a method of inquiry that demanded the scholar subject every argument to systematic doubt and verification. The muḥaqqiq (verifier) does not accept a doctrine on the authority of a school or a teacher; he identifies all possible objections to a proposition, evaluates the arguments for and against each, and reaches a conclusion based on a balance of reasons. If no conclusion is attainable, he openly acknowledges his uncertainty. This willingness to live with suspended judgement, and to record it in a published text, was new in Islamic philosophical writing and struck some readers as dangerously sceptical. Al-Rāzī applies this method relentlessly in the al-Maṭālib al-ʿĀliya. On almost every major question — the existence and attributes of God, the eternity or creation of the world, the nature of the soul, the reality of prophecy — he presents a full survey of the positions of the falāsifa, the Muʿtazila, the Ashʿarīs, and other schools, then subjects each to a battery of objections. His own position often emerges as the least vulnerable, but he rarely claims demonstrative certainty. The existence of God, for example, he defends through a modified argument from contingency, but he acknowledges that the argument relies on premises that a determined sceptic could challenge. Reason, he concludes, can establish the plausibility of theism and the coherence of prophetic claims, but it cannot, on its own, produce the kind of perfect certainty that the falāsifa had promised. That certainty comes only through the combination of rational probability, the testimony of prophecy, and the spiritual experience of the saint. This epistemic modesty placed al-Rāzī at a certain distance from both the dogmatic theologians, who claimed certainty for their creedal statements, and the Peripatetic philosophers, who claimed certainty for their metaphysical deductions. He is a rationalist who knows the limits of reason, a sceptic who uses scepticism in the service of faith. His method would be inherited and refined by later figures such as al-Ījī, al-Jurjānī, and alTaftāzānī, becoming the dominant mode of philosophical theology in the late medieval Islamic world.

3. The Critique of Avicenna in the Sharḥ al-Ishārāt Al-Rāzī's commentary on Avicenna's al-Ishārāt wa-l-Tanbīhāt is a landmark in the history of Islamic philosophy. Avicenna had written the Ishārāt as a dense, allusive, and deliberately difficult text — a guide for advanced students who had already mastered the Peripatetic curriculum. Al-Rāzī's commentary treats the text not as a set of truths to be transmitted but as a series of arguments to be tested. He quotes a passage from Avicenna, reconstructs its logical structure, and then raises every serious objection he can think of — drawing on Ashʿarī theology, Muʿtazilī atomism, and independent logical analysis. The result is less a "commentary" in the traditional sense than a philosophical cross-examination. His most important critical points include:

The proof of the Necessary Existent. Avicenna's argument that the set of all possible beings requires a necessary cause outside itself is, al-Rāzī argues, question-begging. Why must the set of all possible beings itself require a cause? The set is not an additional entity over and above its members; it is merely the collection of individual possible beings, each of which has its own cause. Avicenna has not demonstrated that the collection as a whole needs a single external cause rather than a series of simultaneous or successive causes. Al-Rāzī's critique here is so sharp that later defenders of Avicenna, notably al-Ṭūsī, had to reformulate the proof to save it. God's knowledge of particulars. Avicenna's doctrine that God knows particulars "in a universal way" is, al-Rāzī argues, a failure to account for God's omniscience as required by the Qur'an. If God knows a particular eclipse only through its universal cause, He does not know it as this eclipse happening now, in its concrete particularity. Al-Rāzī develops an alternative Ashʿarī account: God's knowledge is an eternal, single, undifferentiated act that nevertheless encompasses every particular event in its particularity — a mystery, but one that revelation asserts and that philosophy cannot disprove. The eternity of the world. Al-Rāzī concedes that Avicenna's arguments for the eternity of the world are philosophically formidable, but he argues that they are not decisive. Drawing on al-Ghazālī and the Ashʿarī occasionalist tradition, he points out that the concept of an eternal series of past events involves paradoxes of infinity that reason cannot fully resolve. The question of the world's eternity, he concludes, is one of those matters where reason reaches an aporia, and where the believer follows the guidance of scripture. The nature of the soul. Avicenna's argument from the "floating man" for the soul's substantial immateriality is powerful, al-Rāzī acknowledges, but it does not by itself establish the soul's pre-existence or its independent subsistence after death. A purely rational psychology can demonstrate that the soul is not identical with the body, but not that it is naturally immortal. Immortality is a gift of God, known through revelation, not a theorem of metaphysics. Al-Rāzī's commentary on the Ishārāt became the standard Sunnī lens through which Avicenna was read, and it generated a response — al-Ṭūsī's defence — that itself became a classic. The Rāzī-Ṭūsī debate was studied in madrasas for centuries, and it established the model of philosophical commentary as an exercise in critical thinking rather than passive exposition.

4. God, Creation, and the Limits of Reason in the Al-Maṭālib The al-Maṭālib al-ʿĀliya is al-Rāzī's mature philosophical theology. Its nine books cover being and non-being, existence and essence, God's existence, attributes, and acts, the creation of the world, the nature of the soul, and prophecy and eschatology. The architecture of the work is Avicennan — it proceeds from ontology to natural theology to psychology — but the content is a sustained attempt to defend Ashʿarī doctrines within an Avicennan framework. Al-Rāzī's position on the ontological question of existence and essence is instructive. He accepts the Avicennan distinction between existence and essence as logically coherent, but he denies the metaphysical conclusion Avicenna draws — that God is the Necessary Existent whose essence is existence. For al-Rāzī, God's existence is a fact about God, not God's very nature; God's essence is hidden and unknowable, and our talk of "necessary existence" is a description of how God relates to the world, not a definition of what God is. This preserves the Ashʿarī emphasis on divine transcendence and mystery while using Avicennan language. On the question of the world's creation, al-Rāzī develops a sophisticated version of the kalām cosmological argument. The world, he argues, is composed of atoms and accidents that come into existence after not existing.

Every atom and every accident requires a cause for its coming to be. Since an infinite regress of temporal causes is impossible, there must be a first cause that is itself uncaused, eternal, and possessed of will and power — God. This argument does not claim demonstrative certainty; al-Rāzī acknowledges that the possibility of an eternal world without a temporal beginning is logically conceivable. But the kalām argument, combined with the testimony of revelation, provides sufficient rational grounds for belief in temporal creation. His doctrine of divine attributes is a careful middle way. Against the Muʿtazila, he affirms that God has real attributes — knowledge, power, life, will, speech, hearing, sight — that are distinct from His essence. Against the anthropomorphists, he insists that these attributes are entirely unlike human qualities and are known only through their effects. Against the philosophers, he argues that the divine attributes are not reducible to God's essence; there is a real, though incomprehensible, multiplicity in the Godhead that does not compromise unity. This position became standard Ashʿarī teaching. Al-Rāzī's eschatology is similarly ambivalent. He affirms bodily resurrection as a matter of faith, but he acknowledges that the philosophical arguments for a purely spiritual afterlife are strong. His solution is to argue that God, being omnipotent, can recreate the body after its dissolution, and that the Qur'anic descriptions of paradise and hell are true in ways that human reason cannot fully fathom. The philosophical difficulty of bodily resurrection is not a reason to reject it but an indication of the limits of philosophy.

5. The Integration of Logic and Theology One of al-Rāzī's most enduring legacies was the thoroughgoing integration of Avicennan logic into Sunnī theological method. His al-Maḥṣūl in legal theory and his theological works treat logic not as a foreign instrument but as the natural language of serious argument. He writes syllogisms, analyses definitions, distinguishes between essential and accidental predications, and examines modal propositions as a matter of course. This integration was controversial — some traditionalists accused him of corrupting the religious sciences with Greek logic — but it prevailed. After al-Rāzī, the Sunnī madrasa curriculum could not function without logic. His influence on the discipline of uṣūl al-fiqh was particularly transformative. By bringing Avicennan semantics and the theory of definition into the analysis of legal language, he raised the theoretical sophistication of jurisprudence to a new level. Questions about the meaning of scriptural terms, the structure of analogy (qiyās), and the relationship between literal and metaphorical language were now framed in the vocabulary of Avicennan logic. This philosophical turn in legal theory was one of the main avenues through which philosophy continued to thrive in the Sunnī world long after the demise of the independent falsafa tradition.

6. The Rāzian Turn and the Post-Classical Tradition Al-Rāzī's synthesis of Avicennism and Ashʿarism defined the intellectual landscape of the later Islamic east. His students and commentators — among them Quṭb al-Dīn al-Miṣrī, Sirāj al-Dīn al-Urmawī, and the brilliant logician Athīr al-Dīn al-Abharī — spread his methods through the madrasas of Persia, Iraq, and Anatolia. The great fourteenth-century theologians ʿAḍud al-Dīn al-Ījī (d. 1355) and al-Sayyid al-Sharīf al-Jurjānī (d. 1413) developed their own systems on Rāzian foundations, and their works became the standard advanced texts of Sunnī kalām. Al-Rāzī's Qur'anic commentary, the Mafātīḥ al-Ghayb, is a monument of philosophical exegesis. It is not merely a collection of philological notes and legal rulings but a sustained attempt to read the Qur'an in the light of reason and science. Al-Rāzī brings to every verse the full resources of Aristotelian physics, Avicennan metaphysics, and Ashʿarī theology. He discusses, for example, the cosmology of the Throne Verse (Q 2:255) in terms of Ptolemaic astronomy, the embryology of the creation of man (Q 23:13–14) in terms of Galenic medicine, and the problem of

evil in the story of Job in terms of theodicy. His commentary is, in effect, a summa of the sciences, unified by the conviction that the Qur'an and reason are in perfect harmony. The "Rāzian turn" represents a permanent change in Islamic intellectual life. Before al-Rāzī, theology and philosophy existed in tension; after him, they existed in a creative symbiosis. The theologian who could not philosophise was no longer credible; the philosopher who ignored theology was no longer relevant. This mutual interpenetration would characterise the tradition from the School of Isfahan to the Ottoman learned establishment to the South Asian madrasas, and it is one of the reasons why the narrative of philosophical decline after al-Ghazālī is unsustainable. Al-Rāzī ensured that philosophy did not merely survive al-Ghazālī's attack but flourished in new institutional forms and new intellectual syntheses.

7. The Critique from Within: Ibn Taymiyya and the Rejection of Aristotelian Logic Al-Rāzī's synthesis of Avicennism and Ashʿarism was not universally welcomed. The most formidable critic of the entire philosophical-theological enterprise came from within the Ḥanbalī tradition, in the person of Taqī al-Dīn Aḥmad ibn Taymiyya (1263–1328). Ibn Taymiyya was a jurist and theologian, a fierce defender of the Sunnī tradition against what he saw as heretical innovations, and a thinker whose philosophical criticism was so penetrating that even his opponents acknowledged its force. His work is not philosophy in the conventional sense, but it is philosophical criticism of the highest order, and it constitutes a permanent challenge to the foundations of the Avicennan-Aristotelian tradition. No comprehensive history of Islamic philosophy can ignore him, even if it does not endorse him. Ibn Taymiyya's most sustained philosophical work is Darʾ Taʿāruḍ al-ʿAql wa-l-Naql (Averting the Conflict between Reason and Revelation), a multi-volume treatise in which he argues that the apparent conflict between reason and revelation is an illusion. The conflict, he contends, is not between reason and revelation but between a particular corrupted philosophical conception of reason and the authentic teaching of the Prophet. He targets the falāsifa and their Ashʿarī followers, arguing that the Aristotelian logic and metaphysics they inherited are not neutral tools but a specific, flawed worldview that distorts the true nature of knowledge and reality. His critique of Aristotelian logic is remarkably original. He argues that the syllogism, the core of the logical method, does not produce new knowledge. The conclusion of a syllogism is already contained in its premises, so the syllogism merely makes explicit what was implicit. It cannot, therefore, be the foundation of knowledge, as the philosophers claim. The true foundations of knowledge are two: the witness of the senses (ḥiss) and the report of reliable testimony (khabar), including the testimony of the prophets. The fiṭra, the primordial human capacity for knowledge, grasps necessary truths directly, without the mediation of syllogistic reasoning. Logic, for Ibn Taymiyya, is an elaborate apparatus that obscures the simplicity of genuine knowledge and leads to sophistry. This critique has been compared to the later critiques of formal logic by Francis Bacon and John Stuart Mill, and there is a genuine parallel: Ibn Taymiyya's insistence that knowledge is grounded in experience and testimony, not in deductive reasoning from first principles, anticipates the empiricist tradition. His critique of Avicennan metaphysics focuses on two central doctrines: the distinction between existence and essence and the proof of the Necessary Existent. He argues that the distinction between existence and essence, on which the proof depends, is a conceptual error. Existence is not something added to essence; to say that something exists is not to attribute a property to it but to affirm that the thing itself is real. The philosophers have hypostasised a mental distinction into a real one, and their entire metaphysics rests on this mistake. The proof of the Necessary Existent, he argues, is similarly flawed: the argument from contingency assumes that every possible being requires a cause, but this assumption cannot be demonstrated. Moreover, even if there is a necessary being,

the philosophers have not shown that this being is the God of the Qur'an: an all-knowing, all-powerful, personal creator who hears prayers and holds human beings accountable. The God of the philosophers is a mere abstract principle; the God of the Qur'an is a living Lord. Ibn Taymiyya did not reject reason; he rejected a specific philosophical conception of reason. His own epistemology is grounded in the fiṭra, the innate human disposition to know the truth, and in the conjunction of sense, reason, and revelation. He argues that the Qur'an and the Prophetic tradition are the true sources of knowledge about God, the world, and the human soul, and that the philosophers' attempts to transcend revelation are doomed to failure. His influence on the modern Islamic world has been immense, and his arguments continue to be debated by philosophers and theologians today. His inclusion in a comprehensive history of Islamic philosophy is not an endorsement of his positions but an acknowledgment that the tradition includes its critics as well as its practitioners. The dialogue between philosophy and its critics is part of the story, and Ibn Taymiyya is the most powerful critic the tradition ever produced.

8. The Post-Rāzian Synthesis in Sunnī Kalām: Al-Āmidī, al-Ījī, al-Taftāzānī, and al-Jurjānī Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī did not close the book of Sunnī philosophical theology; he inaugurated its golden age. In the two centuries between al-Rāzī's death in 1209 and the death of al-Sayyid al-Sharīf al-Jurjānī in 1413, a succession of encyclopedic Sunnī philosopher-theologians transformed the madrasa curriculum from Cairo and Damascus to Shiraz and Samarqand into an arena of high-level analytical metaphysics: Sayf al-Dīn al-Āmidī (1156–1233): Born in Diyarbakır and active in Cairo and Damascus, al-Āmidī mastered Avicennan philosophy alongside Shāfiʿī jurisprudence. In Abkār al-Afkār fī Uṣūl al-Dīn (Virgin Thoughts on the Principles of Religion) and al-Nūr al-Bāhir fī al-Ḥikam al-Zawāhir, he subjected both Avicenna and al-Rāzī to forensic critique, while his al-Iḥkām fī Uṣūl al-Aḥkām recast the entire science of Islamic legal theory in the mold of Avicennan formal logic and epistemology. ʿAḍud al-Dīn al-Ījī (c. 1281–1355): A chief judge and philosopher in Ilkhanid and Muzaffarid Shiraz, al-Ījī composed Kitāb al-Mawāqif fī ʿIlm al-Kalām (The Book of Stations in the Science of Theology). Of its six stations (mawāqif), the first four are devoted entirely to epistemology, general ontology (al-umūr al-ʿāmma — existence, essence, necessity, contingency, unity, and causality), accidents, and substances before turning to theology proper in the final two stations. Saʿd al-Dīn al-Taftāzānī (1322–1390) & al-Sayyid al-Sharīf al-Jurjānī (1340–1413): Serving at the court of Tīmūr (Tamerlane) in Samarqand, where they engaged in famous public philosophical disputations, alTaftāzānī and al-Jurjānī produced the definitive works of post-classical Sunnī thought. Al-Taftāzānī's Sharḥ alMaqāṣid and Sharḥ al-ʿAqāʾid al-Nasafiyya bridged Ashʿarī and Māturīdī metaphysics, while al-Jurjānī's monumental Sharḥ al-Mawāqif (Commentary on al-Ījī's Stations) and his philosophical lexicon Kitāb al-Taʿrīfāt became the supreme advanced textbooks of Sunnī ontology and epistemology across the Ottoman, Safavid, and Mughal empires for five hundred years. In the hands of al-Ījī, al-Taftāzānī, and al-Jurjānī, the section on 'General Matters' (al-umūr al-ʿāmma) became a shared philosophical laboratory where theologians and philosophers analyzed mental existence (al-wujūd aldhihnī), the ontological status of non-existent possibles (al-maʿdūmāt al-mumkina), the liar paradox (jadhr alaṣamm), and the truth-maker problem (nafs al-amr — 'the thing in itself' or objective fact-of-the-matter, formalized by Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī and refined by al-Jurjānī).

9. From the East to the West With al-Rāzī's synthesis in place, and with the challenge of Ibn Taymiyya registered, we can now turn to the far west of the Islamic world, where a distinctive Andalusian tradition was producing its own responses to the philosophical and theological questions of the age. Al-Ghazālī's Tahāfut had been read in al-Andalus, and the philosophers there felt its challenge acutely. Three generations of Andalusian thinkers would take up that challenge: the first, Ibn Bājja, would explore the solitary life of the philosopher in an imperfect city; the second, Ibn Ṭufayl, would dramatise the ascent of reason without revelation in a philosophical novel of luminous beauty; and the third, Ibn Rushd — Averroes — would mount a direct and systematic defence of Aristotelian philosophy against al-Ghazālī's critique, and in doing so would become the most important Muslim philosopher in the Western imagination. The next chapter examines the two thinkers who prepared the way for Averroes: Ibn Bājja and Ibn Ṭufayl.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “Let no partisan of Avicenna imagine that true philosophical allegiance consists in blindly swallowing whatever the Shaykh wrote, nor let any partisan of the theologians imagine that wisdom consists in rejecting his words out of hand; rather, the station of verification (maqām al-taḥqīq) requires that we formulate every argument on both sides in its strongest possible syllogistic form and follow the proof wherever necessity leads.” — Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, Sharḥ al-Ishārāt wa-l-Tanbīhāt and al-Maṭālib al-ʿĀliya

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. What is the method of 'exhaustive verification' (taḥqīq) developed by Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī in Sharḥ al-Ishārāt, al-Mabāḥith al-Mashriqiyya, and al-Maṭālib al-ʿĀliya, and how did it transform Sunnī kalām from defensive apologetics into constructive philosophical inquiry? 2. Why did al-Rāzī reject the Peripatetic theory of perception as the literal 'imprinting' (inṭibāʿ) of forms in the mind, proposing instead that knowledge is a direct relational state (iḍāfa maḥḍa) between the knowing soul and the known object? 3. Examine Taqī al-Dīn ibn Taymiyya's nominalist and empiricist critique of Avicennan metaphysics and Aristotelian logic in al-Radd ʿalā al-Manṭiqiyyīn and Darʾ Taʿāruḍ al-ʿAql wa-l-Naql. Why does Ibn Taymiyya argue that universal concepts exist only in the mind while only concrete particulars exist in the external world? 4. How did post-Rāzian Sunnī philosopher-theologians — especially al-Āmidī, al-Ījī, al-Taftāzānī, and al-Jurjānī — structure the 'General Ontology' (al-umūr al-ʿāmma) sections of their works, and how did they deploy the concept of nafs al-amr?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Shihadeh, Ayman. The Teleological Ethics of Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī. Leiden: Brill, 2006. Ibn Taymiyya, Taqī al-Dīn. Ibn Taymiyya Against the Greek Logicians (Jahd al-Qarīḥa fī Tajrīd al-Naṣīḥa). Translated by Wael B. Hallaq. Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1993. Hoover, Jon. Ibn Taymiyya's Theodicy of Perpetual Optimism. Chapter 15Andalusian Beginnings: Ibn Bājja and Ibn Ṭufayl

Philosophy at the Edge of the Islamic World

While al-Ghazālī was writing his Tahāfut in the east, and Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī was refining his method of verification, philosophy in the Islamic west — al-Andalus and the Maghreb — was taking its first independent steps. Greek and Arabic philosophical texts had reached Muslim Spain by the late tenth century, and a tradition of Peripatetic thought was developing there, nourished by the libraries of Córdoba and the patronage of enlightened rulers. But the Andalusian philosophers faced a distinctive intellectual climate. The Mālikī jurists were powerful and often hostile to falsafa; the Almoravid and later Almohad dynasties combined political ambition with religious reformism, alternately persecuting and patronising philosophers. In this environment, philosophy was a more fragile and embattled enterprise than it had been in Baghdad or Bukhara. Two remarkable thinkers, Ibn Bājja and Ibn Ṭufayl, bridged the gap between the Fārābīan-Avicennan tradition and the towering figure of Ibn Rushd. They were not system-builders on the scale of Avicenna, but they were original and provocative philosophers in their own right — one a theorist of the philosopher's alienation from a corrupt society, the other the author of the most famous philosophical novel in the Islamic tradition. Together they prepared the soil from which Averroes would grow. This chapter examines their lives, their major works, and their contributions to the perennial problem of the relation between the philosopher and the community.

1. Ibn Bājja: The Solitary Philosopher Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn Yaḥyā ibn al-Ṣāʾigh ibn Bājja (c. 1085–1138), known to the Latin West as Avempace, was born in Saragossa, in northeastern al-Andalus, and died in Fez, possibly poisoned by rivals envious of his learning. He was a polymath — a physician, an astronomer, a musician, a poet, and a philosopher — who served as vizier to Almoravid governors and produced a body of philosophical work that, though largely incomplete and scattered, represents the first flowering of authentic Peripatetic philosophy in the Islamic west. His most important work is Tadbīr al-Mutawaḥḥid (The Governance of the Solitary), a treatise on the condition of the philosopher who lives in an imperfect city — a city that does not share his values or recognise his wisdom. Ibn Bājja takes up a problem left unresolved in al-Fārābī's political philosophy: what is the philosopher to do if the virtuous city does not exist? Al-Fārābī had described the ideal state in which the philosopher-prophet rules and all citizens cooperate toward true happiness. But he had also acknowledged that such a state might not be attainable. Ibn Bājja explores the consequences of this failure in sober, even tragic, terms. He distinguishes four kinds of human beings: the masses, who pursue bodily pleasures; the men of letters and jurists, who pursue honour and reputation; the men of science, who pursue theoretical knowledge but remain bound to society; and the truly solitary (mutawaḥḥid), who pursues the perfection of his intellect in isolation from the corrupting influences around him. The solitary philosopher is not a hermit by choice but by necessity. He lives among people who do not understand him, who may even persecute him, and he must navigate a society that offers no support for the life of the mind. Yet Ibn Bājja's solitary is not a passive victim. He practises a kind of inner emigration: he withdraws his soul from the pursuits of the city while continuing to live in it physically. He governs himself — his appetites, his emotions, his time — according to the dictates of reason. His ultimate goal is the ittiṣāl, the conjunction or union with the Active Intellect, which is the perfection of the human soul. This union, Ibn Bājja believed, is attainable in this life,

not only after death; the philosopher who achieves it becomes, in a sense, a purely intellectual being, no longer dependent on the body or the society that surrounds it. Ibn Bājja's theory of intellectual ascent is deeply Neoplatonic. He describes a hierarchy of spiritual forms that the soul must traverse: from the forms of material bodies, through the intelligible forms abstracted from matter, to the Active Intellect itself, which is the ultimate object of contemplation and the source of all knowledge. His language is dense and technical, and his work remained incomplete at his death, but its influence was considerable. Ibn Ṭufayl and Ibn Rushd both read him carefully, and his critique of the imperfections of existing political societies sharpened the Andalusian philosophical tradition's awareness of the tensions between the philosopher and the commonwealth.

2. Ibn Ṭufayl: The Philosophical Novel Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Ṭufayl al-Qaysī (c. 1105–1185) was a physician, vizier, and courtier at the Almohad court in Marrakesh. He served as chief physician to the caliph Abū Yaʿqūb Yūsuf and was the patron who introduced the young Ibn Rushd to the caliph, setting the stage for Averroes's great commentaries. But Ibn Ṭufayl's own claim on philosophical immortality rests on a single slim book: Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān (Alive, Son of Awake), a philosophical novel of such originality and charm that it has been translated into most major languages and continues to inspire readers today. The title borrows the name of Avicenna's allegorical guide, but Ibn Ṭufayl's Ḥayy is a very different kind of work. It tells the story of a child who grows up alone on a deserted equatorial island, either spontaneously generated from the fermenting mud (a theory Ibn Ṭufayl takes seriously) or cast ashore as an infant like Moses. The boy, Ḥayy, is raised by a gazelle, and as he grows, he uses his unaided reason — no revelation, no teacher, no society — to climb the entire ladder of knowledge. The narrative traces Ḥayy's intellectual development through seven-year stages. By seven, he has learned to cover his nakedness and use tools. By fourteen, he has discovered the principles of physics and biology by dissecting his gazelle-mother's body to find the source of life. By twenty-one, he has grasped the hierarchy of the natural world and the unity of the cosmos. By twenty-eight, he has abstracted from the material world to the concept of a single, necessary cause — the Necessary Existent — through a reasoning process that mirrors Avicenna's argument from contingency. By thirty-five, he has achieved the very summit of intellectual and spiritual perfection: a direct, ecstatic vision of the divine light, in which his individual selfhood is dissolved in the contemplation of the One. In the final section of the book, Ḥayy encounters another human being, Absāl (spelled Asāl or Absāl in the Arabic manuscripts, deliberately echoing the contemplative figure from Avicenna's recital Salāmān wa-Absāl), a devout contemplative from a neighbouring island who has come to the uninhabited island seeking spiritual solitude. When Asāl teaches Ḥayy to speak, Ḥayy discovers that the truths he has reached through reason alone are exactly the same truths that Asāl's religion teaches — the existence of God, the immortality of the soul, the need for moral discipline, the promise of an afterlife — only couched in symbols and laws for the multitude. Ḥayy and Asāl travel to Asāl's island, where Ḥayy attempts to teach the people the pure truth as he has experienced it. But the attempt fails; the people are incapable of understanding philosophical truth without the veil of symbols, and Ḥayy realises that religion, with its rituals and narratives, is the necessary medicine for the masses. He and Asāl return to the deserted island to live out their days in solitary contemplation. The philosophical power of Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān lies in its demonstration, through imaginative narrative, of the central thesis of Peripatetic rationalism: that human reason, unaided by revelation, can attain complete knowledge of God and the universe. The book is an argument for the autonomy of philosophy, written in a form that embodies its

argument — the reader follows Ḥayy's reasoning step by step, experiencing the ascent of the intellect as a journey. And yet Ibn Ṭufayl is careful not to denigrate religion. Religion is a mercy, a condescension of truth to the capacities of the multitude. The philosopher does not despise it; he understands it, and he sees its necessity. The harmony of philosophy and religion is preserved, but the hierarchy is clear: philosophy is the truth; religion is the truth in a form that ordinary people can grasp. The novel is also a sustained meditation on the relationship between the individual and society. Ḥayy's perfect knowledge is achieved in solitude. Society, far from being necessary for human flourishing — as al-Fārābī had argued — appears in Ibn Ṭufayl's narrative as an obstacle to it. The philosopher can reach the highest perfection without a city, without laws, without a prophet. The implication is radical: the ideal life is the life of the solitary contemplative, not the life of the political animal. This vision, which echoes Ibn Bājja's governance of the solitary but is dramatised with far greater literary power, would have profound resonance in later Islamic and European thought. Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān was translated into Latin in the seventeenth century and became a favourite of European Enlightenment thinkers, who saw in Ḥayy a vindication of natural religion and the sufficiency of reason. It influenced John Locke's theory of the mind as a blank slate and inspired Daniel Defoe's Robinson Crusoe. But its deepest significance lies in its place within the Islamic philosophical tradition: it is the most eloquent statement of the thesis that reason and revelation are two paths to the same summit, and that the philosopher who climbs the path of reason reaches the peak no less surely than the prophet.

3. Deepening the Andalusian Vision: Spiritual Forms in Ibn Bājja and Septenary Epistemology in Ibn Ṭufayl To grasp the full philosophical weight of Ibn Bājja's Tadbīr al-Mutawaḥḥid (The Governance of the Solitary) and Risālat al-Ittiṣāl (Epistle on Conjunction), one must examine his technical theory of spiritual forms (al-ṣuwar alrūḥāniyya). Between purely material forms embedded in matter and completely universal intelligible forms residing in the Active Intellect, Ibn Bājja identifies three ascending grades of intermediate spiritual forms: (1) forms residing in the common sense (al-ḥiss al-mushtarak), (2) forms residing in the imaginative faculty (al-takhayyul), and (3) forms residing in the memory (al-dhākira). This hierarchy underpins Ibn Bājja's sharp philosophical critique of al-Ghazālī's al-Munqidh min al-Ḍalāl. AlGhazālī, Ibn Bājja argues, mistook the dazzling imaginative visions experienced by Sufi mystics in solitude — which belong merely to the intermediate spiritual forms of the internal senses — for the ultimate conjunction (ittiṣāl) with the divine intelligible realm. Authentic felicity is attained not by bypassing demonstrative science for emotional ecstasy, but by ascending methodically through theoretical physics and metaphysics until the human acquired intellect (al-ʿaql al-mustafād) strips away all particular imaginative attachments and becomes identical with universal intelligible truth. In corrupt regimes — whether timocratic, oligarchic, democratic, or tyrannical — such solitary philosophers are 'strangers' (ghurabāʾ) and 'weeds' (nawābit). Like medicinal herbs sprouting unbidden in a field of thorns, they are alienated from their immediate society, yet they represent the only seeds from which a future Virtuous City could ever grow. Ibn Ṭufayl's Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān transforms this theory of intellectual ascent into a rigorous developmental epistemology structured around seven-year biological and cognitive cycles (septenaries): First Septenary (Ages 1–7): Infancy and affective bonding with the maternal gazelle; acquisition of sensory coordination and practical tool-use out of bodily need.

Second & Third Septenaries (Ages 7–21): Death of the gazelle and empirical dissection; Ḥayy conducts anatomical vivisection and comparative physiology to discover the vital animal spirit (al-rūḥ al-ḥayawānī) seated in the heart, mastering fire and natural causality. Fourth Septenary (Ages 21–28): Transition from natural science to ontology; analyzing the multiplicity and unity of bodies, Ḥayy discovers the hylomorphic distinction between bodily extension and substantial form, and ascends via the impossibility of an infinite causal regress to the immaterial Necessary Creator. Fifth Septenary (Ages 28–35): Astronomical and cosmological reflection; contemplating the celestial spheres, Ḥayy weighs the arguments for the eternity of the world vs. temporal creation ex nihilo and demonstrates that both hypotheses lead identically to the existence of an incorporeal, omnipotent Agent. Sixth & Seventh Septenaries (Ages 35–50): Imitation of the three orders of being and ecstatic contemplation (mushāhada); recognizing that his own essence is an immaterial substance akin to the celestial intellects, Ḥayy practices ecological stewardship (eating only ripe fruit so as not to destroy plant species), circular meditative motion, and total intellectual absorption until he witnesses the divine truth through what Avicenna called Eastern Wisdom (al-ḥikma al-mashriqiyya). By showing that Ḥayy's autodidactic reason and contemplative vision arrive at the exact inner truth (bāṭin) that Absāl had sought inside revealed scripture, while Salāmān's literalist society requires outward sensory parables (ẓāhir) and legal deterrence, Ibn Ṭufayl provides both a defense of natural reason and a sobering sociological diagnosis of why pure philosophy cannot be preached indiscriminately in the public square.

Andalusian Mystical-Philosophical Monism: Ibn Masarra (883–931) and Ibn Sabʿīn (1217–1270) Frame-working the Peripatetic tradition of Ibn Bājja, Ibn Ṭufayl, and Ibn Rushd at the beginning and end of Andalusian intellectual history stood two remarkable thinkers who fused Neoplatonic philosophy with mystical monism: Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masarra al-Jabalī (883–931) of Córdoba and Quṭb al-Dīn ʿAbd alḤaqq Ibn Sabʿīn (1217–1270) of Murcia: Ibn Masarra (883–931) and Inferential Contemplation (Iʿtibār): Retreating with his disciples to a mountain hermitage in the Sierra of Córdoba two centuries before Ibn Bājja, Ibn Masarra authored Risālat alIʿtibār (Epistle on Contemplative Inference) and Kitāb Khawāṣṣ al-Ḥurūf (Book of the Properties of Letters). In Risālat al-Iʿtibār, Ibn Masarra anticipates Ibn Ṭufayl's Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān by demonstrating step by step how unaided rational contemplation (iʿtibār), ascending up the ladder of natural signs from plants and elements to the Universal Soul and Universal Intellect, arrives at the exact same truth that prophetic revelation announces from above. Ibn Sabʿīn (1217–1270), The Sicilian Questions, and Absolute Unity (al-Waḥda al-Muṭlaqa): Born in Murcia and active Across North Africa and Mecca, Ibn Sabʿīn gained international fame when the Hohenstaufen Emperor Frederick II of Sicily sent four classical philosophical puzzles to the Almohad court — concerning the eternity of the world, the object and preconditions of metaphysics, the Aristotelian categories, and the immortality of the soul. In al-Masāʾil al-Ṣiqilliyya (The Sicilian Questions), the young Ibn Sabʿīn expounded and critiqued the positions of Aristotle, al-Fārābī, Avicenna, al-Ghazālī, and Ibn Rushd. In his magnum opus Budd al-ʿĀrif (The Escape of the Gnostic), Ibn Sabʿīn went beyond both Peripateticism and standard Sufism to formulate a radical ontology of Absolute Unity (al-waḥda al-muṭlaqa), summarized in his watchword Allāh faqaṭ ('God alone'): pure, undifferentiated Being belongs unconditionally to the One, and the gnostic (al-muḥaqqiq) unites the logical rigor of the philosopher with the annihilating realization of absolute unity.

4. The Road to Averroes Ibn Bājja and Ibn Ṭufayl prepared the Andalusian stage for the greatest philosophical mind the Islamic west would ever produce. Ibn Ṭufayl, in his capacity as court physician to the Almohad caliph Abū Yaʿqūb Yūsuf, personally recommended Ibn Rushd to the caliph's service, and the caliph commissioned Ibn Rushd to write clear and comprehensive commentaries on Aristotle. The story is famous: when Ibn Rushd was presented to the caliph, the caliph asked him, "What is the opinion of the philosophers about the heavens? Are they eternal or created?" Ibn Rushd, fearing a trap, hesitated, but the caliph began discussing the question with Ibn Ṭufayl in a display of philosophical erudition that put Ibn Rushd at ease. From that moment, Averroes's career as the Commentator began. The next chapter turns to Ibn Rushd: his monumental commentaries on Aristotle, his rebuttal of al-Ghazālī, his defence of the harmony of philosophy and scripture, and his extraordinary dual legacy as the last great Muslim philosopher of the medieval West and the most influential Muslim thinker in the history of Latin Europe.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “When Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān and Absāl landed upon the inhabited island governed by Salāmān and began to disclose the esoteric truths of pure contemplation to its citizens, the people recoiled from them in incomprehension; and Ḥayy realized with certainty that the vast majority of mankind are served best by the outward parables, ethical commands, and legal boundaries brought by the Prophet, and that demanding pure demonstrative philosophy from everyone would only unsettle their lives without lifting them to the station of the blessed.” — Ibn Ṭufayl, Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. Why does Ibn Bājja describe the genuine philosopher living inside an imperfect, non-virtuous regime as a 'weed' (nābita) or 'stranger' (gharīb) in Tadbīr al-Mutawaḥḥid, and how does this depart from al-Fārābī's activist ideal of the philosopher-legislator? 2. How does Ibn Bājja's three-tier taxonomy of 'spiritual forms' (al-ṣuwar al-rūḥāniyya) ground both his theory of conjunction with the Active Intellect (ittiṣāl) and his philosophical critique of al-Ghazālī's Sufism? 3. Trace the seven-year developmental septenaries of Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān on his equatorial island. How does Ibn Ṭufayl show that empirical observation, anatomical dissection, causal deduction, and mystical contemplation form a single continuous path? 4. What is the significance of the final encounter between Ḥayy, the contemplative seeker Absāl, and the literalist ruler Salāmān? Does the ending of Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān express philosophical optimism about human reason or sociological pessimism about political society?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Ibn Ṭufayl, Abū Bakr. Ibn Tufayl's Hayy Ibn Yaqzan: A Philosophical Tale. Translated by Lenn E. Goodman. Updated ed. Translated by Lawrence Berman in Medieval Political Philosophy: A Sourcebook, edited by Ralph Lerner and Muhsin Mahdi, 122–133. Ithaca, NY: Cornell University Press, 1963. Montada, Josep Puig. Press, 2005. Conrad, Lawrence I., ed. The World of Ibn Ṭufayl: Interdisciplinary Perspectives on Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān. Leiden: Brill, 1996. Harvey, Steven. Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 1992.
