Chapter 16Ibn Rushd (Averroes): The Defender of Philosophy

The Commentator and the Judge

Abū al-Walīd Muḥammad ibn Aḥmad ibn Rushd (1126–1198), known to the Latin world as Averroes, was born in Córdoba into a distinguished family of Mālikī jurists. His grandfather had been the chief judge of Córdoba; Ibn Rushd himself would serve as judge (qāḍī) in Seville and Córdoba before becoming chief physician to the Almohad caliph Abū Yaʿqūb Yūsuf and later his son Abū Yūsuf Yaʿqūb al-Manṣūr. He was, by profession, a jurist and a physician; his philosophical work was a labour of love and, ultimately, of intellectual obligation. That philosophical work is astonishing in its scope and influence. Ibn Rushd wrote commentaries on almost all of Aristotle's surviving works — three levels of commentary (short, middle, and long) for different audiences — earning him the title al-Shāriḥ, the Commentator, by which Dante and the Latin scholastics would know him. He wrote original treatises on metaphysics, physics, psychology, and medicine. He defended philosophy against alGhazālī's Tahāfut in his own Tahāfut al-Tahāfut (The Incoherence of the Incoherence), a work of ferocious dialectical skill. And he wrote the Faṣl al-Maqāl (The Decisive Treatise), a legally framed argument for the compatibility of philosophy and Islamic law that remains one of the boldest statements of the philosopher's right to exist in a religious society. Ibn Rushd's life ended in tragedy. In the last years of his career, he fell from favour — perhaps because of political intrigues, perhaps because the caliph needed to distance himself from philosophy to placate the Mālikī jurists. He was exiled to Lucena, near Córdoba, his books were burned, and he died in Marrakesh in 1198, broken and isolated. But his intellectual legacy had only just begun. Within a generation of his death, his Aristotelian commentaries were being translated into Latin and Hebrew, and they would transform the intellectual culture of three civilisations.

1. The Harmony of Philosophy and Scripture: The Decisive Treatise Ibn Rushd's Faṣl al-Maqāl is a legal opinion (fatwā) in form and a philosophical manifesto in substance. Its central question is: does Islamic law permit, prohibit, or command the study of philosophy? Ibn Rushd's answer is that it not only permits but commands it — for those who are capable. He argues that the Qur'an repeatedly calls upon human beings to reflect on creation, to use their reason, to draw analogies, and to seek knowledge. This rational reflection (iʿtibār) is precisely what philosophy (falsafa) is. Philosophy is the systematic study of the universe through demonstrative syllogism, the only method that yields certainty. If the Qur'an commands rational reflection, and philosophy is the most perfect form of such reflection, then the study of philosophy is a religious obligation for those who possess the intellectual capacity — just as jurisprudence is an obligation for those who possess the legal capacity. But what if the conclusions of philosophy appear to contradict the literal meaning of scripture? Ibn Rushd's answer is bold: apparent contradictions arise from misinterpretation, not genuine conflict. The Qur'an, he argues, contains

verses of different levels of clarity. Some are clear and unambiguous (muḥkamāt) and must be taken literally; others are ambiguous (mutashābihāt) and allegorical interpretation (taʾwīl) is not only permitted but required. The philosopher is precisely the person best qualified to carry out this allegorical interpretation, because he understands the demonstrative truths and can see how the scriptural symbols point to them. When scripture speaks of God "descending" or "sitting on the Throne," these are allegories for truths about divine causality and governance that the philosopher understands in their intellectual form. Crucially, Ibn Rushd restricts allegorical interpretation to the scholarly elite. The masses, the jurists, and the theologians who have not mastered demonstrative science must accept the literal meaning of scripture and not be exposed to philosophical allegories, which would confuse them and threaten their faith. Al-Ghazālī's great error, in Ibn Rushd's view, was not that he criticised the philosophers — that was his right — but that he aired philosophical disputes in a popular book, the Tahāfut, thereby sowing doubt among the masses who were not equipped to handle it. The philosopher must practise intellectual discretion; truth is one, but it must be communicated differently to different audiences. This theory of the three classes of people — the rational elite who seek demonstrative truth, the dialectical theologians who are satisfied with probable arguments, and the rhetorical multitude who require symbolic narratives — is the cornerstone of Ibn Rushd's political philosophy. It is deeply elitist, but it is also a sophisticated attempt to carve out a protected space for philosophy within an Islamic society without undermining the faith of the ordinary believer.

2. The Incoherence of the Incoherence: Answering Al-Ghazālī Ibn Rushd's Tahāfut al-Tahāfut is a point-by-point refutation of al-Ghazālī's Tahāfut al-Falāsifa. Its method is to quote al-Ghazālī's arguments verbatim and then to reply to each one, often with considerable sarcasm. The tone is combative, but the philosophical substance is profound. Ibn Rushd does not merely defend the philosophers; he attempts to clarify what Aristotle actually taught, over against both al-Ghazālī's polemical distortions and, at times, Avicenna's Platonising deviations. On the eternity of the world, Ibn Rushd argues that al-Ghazālī has misunderstood Aristotle. The world, for Aristotle, is not created at a moment in time, but it is eternally dependent on God as its cause. God's creative act is eternal, not a temporal beginning. This does not make God an involuntary agent; God's freedom consists in His acting in accordance with His perfect nature, not in choosing between alternatives. There is no contradiction between the Qur'an and this Aristotelian doctrine, Ibn Rushd argues, because the Qur'an does not specify how the world was created; the verses that speak of creation in six days are allegorical and do not preclude an eternal creative act. On God's knowledge of particulars, Ibn Rushd affirms that God knows particulars, but he develops a subtle account of divine knowledge that differs from both Avicenna and al-Ghazālī. God's knowledge, he argues, is the cause of all existents; God knows things by being their cause. This knowledge is neither universal in the Avicennan sense nor temporal in the human sense; it is a unique mode of knowledge that belongs to the divine essence and that we can only describe by analogy. On bodily resurrection, Ibn Rushd takes a position that has been much debated. In the Tahāfut al-Tahāfut, he appears to affirm a purely spiritual afterlife, though he is cautious in his language. In his Faṣl al-Maqāl, he suggests that the resurrection of the body is a symbolic representation of a spiritual truth, and that the masses should accept the literal sense. This is, of all his positions, the one that has attracted the most criticism from Muslim scholars, and the charge of heresy has followed him for centuries.

The most philosophically significant section of the Tahāfut al-Tahāfut is Ibn Rushd's defence of causality against alGhazālī's occasionalism. He argues that the denial of necessary causal connections between things is both philosophically incoherent and theologically disastrous. If fire has no nature that inclines it to burn, and God creates burning at each moment, then the world has no stability or order. Knowledge becomes impossible, because you can never be sure what will follow from what. Even the knowledge of God's custom is impossible, because custom itself presupposes stable natural kinds that behave in regular ways. The world, Ibn Rushd insists, is a cosmos, an ordered whole in which things have real natures and real causal powers given to them by God. God creates the world in such a way that it operates according to intrinsic principles, and the regularity we observe is a sign of divine wisdom, not a limitation on divine power. This position — the affirmation of secondary causality and natural teleology — would become foundational for the Latin scholastics, especially Thomas Aquinas.

3. The Great Commentaries and Latin Averroism Ibn Rushd's commentaries on Aristotle are, in their scope and precision, one of the great intellectual achievements of the Middle Ages. He wrote over thirty-eight commentaries, covering the entire Aristotelian corpus. The "long commentaries" (tafāsīr) on the Metaphysics, On the Soul, Physics, On the Heavens, and other works became standard texts in European universities after their translation into Latin in the thirteenth century. For Thomas Aquinas, Ibn Rushd was simply the Commentator, the indispensable guide to Aristotle's meaning. But Ibn Rushd's interpretation of Aristotle was controversial. On several key points, he advanced doctrines that went beyond Aristotle and that brought him into conflict with Christian orthodoxy. The most famous is the doctrine of the unity of the intellect: Ibn Rushd argued that the material intellect — the human capacity for knowledge — is a single, eternal, separate substance shared by all human beings, not a personal faculty individual to each person. Individual human beings participate in this single intellect through their imaginations, but the intellect itself is one and immortal. Personal immortality, on this view, is severely compromised; what survives death is the universal intellect, not the individual soul with its memories and personality. This doctrine was fiercely debated in Paris and Oxford. Thomas Aquinas wrote a treatise against it, De Unitate Intellectus contra Averroistas (On the Unity of the Intellect against the Averroists), and the church condemned it as heretical. Yet the doctrine persisted among a group of thinkers known as the "Latin Averroists," including Siger of Brabant, who argued that philosophy and revelation might reach opposite conclusions — a position known as the "double truth" theory. It should be stated clearly that Ibn Rushd himself did not hold the double truth theory; no serious scholar now believes he did. He consistently maintained that truth is one, and that the apparent conflict between philosophy and scripture is resolvable through proper allegorical interpretation. The "double truth" was a Latin misinterpretation, a radicalisation of his thought that made Averroes a symbol of rationalism and heterodoxy in the Western imagination.

4. Political Theory and the Commentary on Plato's Republic Ibn Rushd also wrote a commentary on Plato's Republic, making him one of the few Muslim philosophers to engage directly with Platonic political philosophy (Aristotle's Politics, as noted earlier, was not fully available in Arabic). In this commentary, he adapted Plato's ideal state to the conditions of the Islamic world, drawing on al-Fārābī's earlier synthesis. He argued that the ideal state is one in which philosophers rule, or at least in which rulers are guided by philosophy. But he was realistic about the distance between the ideal and the real. Existing Islamic states, he observed, were far from the Platonic paradigm; they were more like timocracies (rule by honour) or even tyrannies. The philosopher's role, in such imperfect states, is to advise the ruler where possible, to maintain his own

intellectual freedom, and to avoid the corruption that comes with political ambition. Ibn Rushd's political philosophy is thus less utopian than al-Fārābī's and more focused on the practical navigation of imperfect regimes.

5. The Double Legacy Ibn Rushd's legacy is, paradoxically, far greater in the West than in the Islamic world. In Europe, he was "Averroes," the Commentator, whose works helped spark the thirteenth-century Aristotelian revolution and whose name became synonymous with philosophical rationalism. The Averroist controversies shaped the development of scholastic philosophy and the emergence of modern science; thinkers from Aquinas to Galileo and Spinoza engaged with his ideas. In the Islamic world, by contrast, Ibn Rushd's influence was more limited. The political persecution he suffered at the end of his life signalled a broader eclipse of Peripatetic philosophy in the Islamic west. After the Reconquista, the intellectual centre of gravity shifted eastward, where the Avicennan tradition — and the Rāzian synthesis of Avicennism and Ashʿarism — had already absorbed and responded to many of Ibn Rushd's concerns. His works were read by a few later scholars, but his great commentaries were largely forgotten in Arabic until modern times. Yet Ibn Rushd's importance for the history of Muslim philosophy is inestimable. He was the last and greatest representative of the pure Peripatetic tradition in Islam — a philosopher who, with unparalleled fidelity to Aristotle, defended the harmony of reason and revelation against the most formidable theological critic they had ever faced. He made the case, with legal precision and philosophical passion, that the pursuit of demonstrative truth is not a foreign intrusion but a duty laid upon the Muslim by the Qur'an itself. And he did so at great personal cost, embodying the very ideal of the solitary philosopher that Ibn Bājja had described. In Ibn Rushd, the Andalusian Peripatetic tradition speaks its final word — a word that would echo through Europe for centuries even as it faded from its native soil.

6. The Turn to Illumination With Ibn Rushd, the Peripatetic tradition in the Islamic west reaches its terminus. But in the east, while al-Ghazālī and Ibn Rushd were debating causality and eternity, and while Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī was forging his new synthesis, a very different philosophical movement was gathering force — one that would claim not to destroy the Peripatetic synthesis but to transcend it. Its founder was Shihāb al-Dīn Suhrawardī, the Master of Illumination (Shaykh alIshrāq), and his philosophy of light would open a new chapter in the history of Islamic ḥikma. To him we now turn.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “Since it has been established that the Revealed Law (al-sharʿ) has made it obligatory to reflect upon existing things by means of the intellect and to consider them, and since consideration (iʿtibār) is nothing other than extracting the unknown from the known — which is syllogistic demonstration (al-qiyās al-burhānī) — it is obligatory for us to pursue philosophical inquiry into existing things; for truth does not contradict truth (al-ḥaqq lā yuḍāddu al-ḥaqq), but rather accords with it and bears witness to it.” — Ibn Rushd (Averroes), Faṣl al-Maqāl fī mā bayna al-Ḥikma wa-l-Sharīʿa min al-Ittiṣāl (The Decisive Treatise)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. In Faṣl al-Maqāl (The Decisive Treatise), how does Ibn Rushd issue a formal legal ruling (fatwā) proving that the study of Aristotelian demonstrative philosophy is religiously obligatory (wājib bi-l-sharʿ) for those who possess the requisite intellectual capacity?

2. How does Ibn Rushd classify humanity according to the three modes of assent — demonstrative (burhānī), dialectical (jadalī), and rhetorical (khaṭābī) — and why does he charge al-Ghazālī and the mutakallimūn with harming both religion and philosophy by disclosing allegorical interpretations (taʾwīl) to the general public? 3. Examine Ibn Rushd's defense of intrinsic causal natures against al-Ghazālī's occasionalism in the Seventeenth Discussion of Tahāfut al-Tahāfut. Why does Ibn Rushd argue that denying necessary causal powers (quwā / ṭabāʾiʿ) in things destroys both the specific essences of things and our rational proof of the Wise Creator? 4. Why did Ibn Rushd's doctrine of the 'unity of the material intellect' in his Long Commentary on Aristotle's De Anima ignite the 'Latin Averroist' controversy at the University of Paris in the thirteenth century?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Ibn Rushd (Averroes). Decisive Treatise and Epistle Dedicatory (Faṣl al-Maqāl). Translated by Charles E. Butterworth. Part V: Illuminationism and Systematic Mystical Ontology

Chapter 17Shihāb al-Dīn Suhrawardī: The School of Illumination

The Master of Light

Shihāb al-Dīn Yaḥyā ibn Ḥabash al-Suhrawardī (c. 1154–1191) was born in the village of Suhraward in northwestern Iran and died, at the age of thirty-six, not from illness or old age but by execution — the fate, his followers would later say, of a martyr of wisdom. He studied in Marāgha and Isfahan, absorbing both the Peripatetic philosophy of Avicenna and the spiritual teachings of Sufism, and he travelled through Anatolia and Syria, finally settling in Aleppo under the patronage of the Ayyubid prince al-Malik al-Ẓāhir. There, his brilliance, his unorthodox teachings, and perhaps his political indiscretions earned him the enmity of the city's orthodox jurists. They denounced him to Saladin, who ordered his death. Suhrawardī was executed or died in prison in 1191. He has been known ever since as al-Shaykh al-Maqtūl, the Slain Master, or al-Shaykh al-Ishrāq, the Master of Illumination. Suhrawardī's philosophy is a deliberately radical departure from the Peripatetic mainstream. He did not reject Aristotle or Avicenna outright, but he claimed that their philosophy — the philosophy of discursive reason (baḥth) — was incomplete and that the true philosophy was one in which discursive reason and direct spiritual vision (dhawq, kashf) were united. He called this new synthesis Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq, the Philosophy of Illumination, and his masterwork of the same name is the founding text of a tradition that would profoundly reshape Islamic thought in Iran and beyond. Where the Peripatetics had thought in terms of existence, essence, and syllogism, Suhrawardī thought in terms of light, darkness, and presence. His was a philosophy of vision — both the eye's vision and the soul's — and it claimed to recover an ancient wisdom that stretched back to Hermes and the sages of ancient Persia, a wisdom more ancient and more complete than the Greek.

1. The Philosophy of Light and Darkness The fundamental reality, for Suhrawardī, is not being but light (nūr). Light is self-manifest, self-evident; it requires no definition, because it is the principle by which everything else is defined and seen. The universe is a hierarchy of lights and darknesses, with the Light of Lights (Nūr al-Anwār) at its summit — God, the ultimate source of all illumination, who is pure, self-subsistent, and infinitely rich. From the Light of Lights emanates the First Light, a pure intellectual substance, and from it, through a process of procession and reflection, a descending series of dominating lights (anwār qāhira) — roughly corresponding to the angelic intellects of the Peripatetic tradition. But Suhrawardī's language is radically different from that of al-Fārābī and Avicenna. He deliberately avoids the technical vocabulary of Aristotelian metaphysics. Instead, he writes in a visionary idiom drawn from Persian angelology, Sufi symbolism, and Zoroastrian light imagery. The intellects are no longer merely "intellects" but "lights" with names drawn from Persian mythology: Bahman, Isfandārmudh, and so on. The universe is a living, luminous presence, not a mechanical cascade of logical necessitations. Below the realm of pure lights lies the world of barzakh — the intermediary realm, the imaginal world — which is composed not of light but of darkness that has received illumination. This includes the celestial spheres, the stars,

and the material world in which we live. Matter itself is not evil, as in Gnosticism, but is a state of darkness that becomes beautiful and good when illuminated by the higher lights. The human soul is a light that has become trapped in the barzakh of the body; its journey is to purify itself — through knowledge, asceticism, and spiritual practice — and ascend back to the world of pure light from which it came.

2. Knowledge by Presence The epistemological centre of Suhrawardī's philosophy is the distinction between knowledge by presence (al-ʿilm alḥuḍūrī) and knowledge by representation (al-ʿilm al-ḥuṣūlī). The Peripatetics, he argued, had an impoverished theory of knowledge because they reduced all knowledge to representation: the mind knows an object by having a mental form that represents the object. But this leads to a regress, and it cannot account for the direct, immediate knowledge that the soul has of itself — the knowledge Avicenna had illustrated with his "floating man" experiment. Knowledge by presence, by contrast, is direct, intuitive, and unmediated. The soul knows its own existence and its own states not through any intermediary form but by being present to itself. Similarly, the soul can know the higher intelligences and the Light of Lights through a process of illumination, in which the light of the intelligible world is directly present to the purified soul. This knowledge is not the acquisition of forms but an encounter, a vision, a tasting (dhawq). The philosopher who has reached this stage knows God not as the conclusion of a syllogism but as a presence more intimate than his own self. Suhrawardī's insistence on knowledge by presence had profound implications for his method. He believed that philosophy could not be merely a deductive system; it had to include the spiritual practices that purify the soul and open it to illumination. He prescribed a regimen of asceticism — forty-day retreats, fasting, reduced sleep, invocation (dhikr) — as an essential part of the philosophical life. The true sage is both a ḥakīm (philosopher) and a ʿārif (mystic), and his knowledge is both discursive and illuminative. Suhrawardī himself claimed to have received the wisdom of Illumination in a series of visionary experiences, including a dream in which Aristotle appeared to him and confirmed that the Peripatetic philosophy was only a preparation for the higher wisdom.

3. The Imaginal World One of Suhrawardī's most influential and original doctrines is his theory of the ʿālam al-mithāl — the world of images, the imaginal realm. This is an intermediate ontological plane between the purely intellectual world of lights and the physical world of bodies. It is a world of suspended forms (al-ṣuwar al-muʿallaqa), in which spiritual realities take on sensible shapes — not in a crude physical sense but in a subtle, luminous matter. The imaginal world is the realm of symbolic visions, prophetic dreams, and the bodily forms assumed by angels and spirits. It is the realm in which the soul undergoes its afterlife experiences — the pleasures of Paradise and the torments of Hell — before the final resurrection. It is also the realm in which the mystical ascent occurs: the soul, in its visionary journey, travels not through physical space but through the landscapes of the imaginal world, encountering symbolic figures, cities, and events that correspond to its inner states. This doctrine provided a philosophical framework for understanding the symbolic and visionary dimensions of religion. The descriptions of Paradise and Hell in the Qur'an, the Prophet's nocturnal journey (miʿrāj), the visions of the Sufi saints — all could be understood as events occurring in the imaginal world, which is real in its own order without being physical. Suhrawardī thus preserved the literal truth of these scriptural accounts for the masses while giving the philosopher a means of interpreting them as symbolic realities. The ʿālam al-mithāl would become a cornerstone of later Iranian philosophy, richly developed by Mullā Ṣadrā and his followers.

4. The Rejection of Peripatetic Essentialism Suhrawardī's philosophy of illumination entailed a fundamental critique of the Avicennan metaphysics of existence and essence. Avicenna had held that existence is something added to essence, and that God is the being in whom existence and essence coincide. Suhrawardī found this unintelligible. Existence, he argued, is not a real property that things have; it is merely a concept in the mind. To say "Socrates exists" is not to attribute a distinct property, "existence," to Socrates; it is simply to say that Socrates is an instance of the nature "human." Existence, for Suhrawardī, is a purely mental construct, and the real constituents of reality are essences — quiddities, "whatnesses" — which differ in their degrees of perfection and light. This position was deeply controversial. The later tradition, particularly Mullā Ṣadrā, would argue that Suhrawardī was wrong, and that the primacy belongs not to essence but to existence (aṣālat al-wujūd). But Suhrawardī's critique forced the subsequent tradition to clarify its own position, and the debate between the "essentialists" (followers of Suhrawardī) and the "existentialists" (followers of Mullā Ṣadrā) became one of the central philosophical debates of later Islamic thought.

5. The Revival of Ancient Wisdom Suhrawardī's claim to be recovering an ancient wisdom, older than the Greeks, is integral to his project. In the Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq, he traces the lineage of illuminationist wisdom from Hermes (identified with the Qur'anic Idrīs) through the sages of ancient Persia — figures like Kayūmars, Farīdūn, and Kay Khusraw — to Plato and the Neoplatonists, and finally to the Sufi masters of his own day. He presents himself as the reviver of this eternal wisdom, not the inventor of a new school. The Illuminationist sage is the heir of the prophets as well as the philosophers; his knowledge is the culmination of a single, universal tradition of light. This idea of a philosophia perennis — a perennial philosophy, an eternal wisdom transmitted through all the great traditions — would have a long career in Islamic thought, influencing the Shīʿī theosophy of the Safavid period and, in the modern era, the Traditionalism of thinkers like Seyyed Hossein Nasr. Suhrawardī was the first Muslim philosopher to articulate this vision so explicitly and so comprehensively, and his work gave later generations a framework for integrating philosophy, mysticism, and the symbolic reading of scripture.

6. The Ishrāqī School and Its Influence Suhrawardī's execution cut his life short, but his influence grew steadily after his death. His works were studied, commented upon, and developed by a succession of philosophers, particularly in Shīʿī Iran. Quṭb al-Dīn al-Shīrāzī (d. 1311), a student of Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī, wrote the most important commentary on the Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq, making it accessible to a wide readership. Jalāl al-Dīn al-Dawānī (d. 1502) and later Mīr Dāmād (d. 1631) were deeply influenced by Suhrawardī's integration of discursive philosophy and mystical illumination. But it was Mullā Ṣadrā (1571–1640), the greatest philosopher of the Safavid period, who brought Suhrawardī's legacy into full integration with the Peripatetic and Akbarian traditions. Mullā Ṣadrā accepted Suhrawardī's doctrine of the imaginal world and his emphasis on knowledge by presence, but he argued for the primacy of existence over essence, against Suhrawardī. His Transcendent Philosophy (al-Ḥikma al-Mutaʿāliya) is, in part, a grand synthesis in which the illuminative wisdom of Suhrawardī is placed on a new ontological foundation. Through Mullā Ṣadrā, Suhrawardī's ideas remain alive in the philosophical curriculum of Shīʿī madrasas to this day.

7. Suhrawardī's Critique of Peripatetic Logic and Ontology: Essential Definition and Mental Considerations (Iʿtibārāt ʿAqliyya) Although later textbooks often summarize the debate between Suhrawardī and Mullā Ṣadrā as a contest between the 'primacy of essence' (aṣālat al-māhiyya) and the 'primacy of existence' (aṣālat al-wujūd), it is vital to recognize that the phrase aṣālat al-māhiyya was coined retroactively by Mullā Ṣadrā in the seventeenth century. In Part One of Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq, Suhrawardī launches a far more radical nominalist and phenomenological assault on two cornerstones of Avicennan Peripateticism: the theory of essential definition (al-ḥadd al-tāmm) and the real distinction between essence and existence.

The Impossibility of Aristotelian Essential Definition Aristotle and Avicenna held that all scientific knowledge begins with definitions composed of proximate genus (jins qarīb) and specific difference (faṣl), which capture the complete essence of a thing. Suhrawardī dismantles this claim through a devastating dilemma: to define a concrete reality such as 'human' or 'horse,' one would have to enumerate every essential constituent and the unique specific difference that distinguishes it from all other realities. Yet how is that specific difference known? If it is known through ordinary features accessible to everyone, it is not a hidden scientific discovery; if it is an occult essential property unknown to prior experience, the inquirer cannot know what to look for without already knowing the thing's essence (a variation of Meno's paradox), nor can one ever be certain that no further hidden essential constituent has been overlooked. All genuine definitions, Suhrawardī concludes, begin and end with direct sensory or intuitive acquaintance (mushāhada) with simple realities — above all, self-evident Light (nūr).

Existence, Necessity, and Contingency as Mental Considerations (Iʿtibārāt ʿAqliyya) Equally incisive is Suhrawardī's refutation of the Avicennan claim that existence (wujūd) is a real ontological constituent added to quiddity (māhiyya) in the external world. In a famous infinite-regress argument (burhān altasalsul), Suhrawardī reasons as follows: if a contingent horse becomes existent in the external world only by having a real entity called 'existence' added to its essence, then that added 'existence' must itself exist; if it exists through a second existence, an infinite regress of existences ensues, whereas if it exists by its own essence without needing an added existence, then there is no reason why the concrete quiddity itself cannot exist directly through its Efficient Maker without any intermediate ontological glue. Consequently, concepts such as 'existence' (wujūd), 'necessity' (wujūb), 'contingency' (imkān), 'unity' (waḥda), and 'substantiality' (jawhariyya) are mental considerations (iʿtibārāt ʿaqliyya) — second-order intellectual predicates generated by the mind when comparing concrete things, having no independent referent in extra-mental reality. Extra-mental reality consists not of abstract 'essences' plus 'existence,' but of a continuous, dynamic spectrum of self-manifesting Light (al-nūr al-ẓāhir li-nafsihi wa-l-muẓhir li-ghayrihi) graded according to intensity and weakness (al-tashkīk fī al-nūr).

Longitudinal (Ṭūlī) and Latitudinal (ʿArḍī) Orders of Lights and Platonic Archetypes Replacing Avicenna's slender chain of ten celestial intellects, Suhrawardī constructs a vast, luminous cosmology governed by the Principle of the Noblest Contingency (qāʿidat imkān al-ashraf): if a lower, baser contingent thing exists in the physical world, its nobler immaterial counterpart must already have been brought into being prior to it. From the Light of Lights (Nūr al-Anwār) emanates the First Proximate Light (al-Nūr al-Aqrab, identified with the Zoroastrian Bahman), followed by two interconnected hierarchies:

The Longitudinal Order of Mothers (al-Ṭabaqa al-Ṭūliyya / al-Ummahāt): A vertical causal chain of dominant immaterial lights multiplying through countless radiant reflections (ishrāqāt) and mutual witnessings (mushāhadāt). The Latitudinal Order of Lords of Species (al-Ṭabaqa al-ʿArḍiyya / Arbāb al-Anwāʿ): A horizontal array of co-equal immaterial lights corresponding to the Platonic Forms (al-muthul al-aflāṭūniyya) and Zoroastrian angelic archetypes (such as Khurdād, Murdād, and Urdībihisht), each governing and sustaining a specific natural species in the physical world as its 'Theurgic Lord of the Talisman' (rabb al-ṭilasm).

8. From Light to Unity Suhrawardī's philosophy of light is a world away from the syllogistic caution of al-Kindī, the political architecture of al-Fārābī, or the rigorous demonstration of Ibn Rushd. It is a philosophy in which vision, symbol, and spiritual experience take priority over definition and deduction. And yet it is continuous with the earlier tradition: Suhrawardī read Avicenna deeply, and his project was to fulfil what he believed Avicenna had only hinted at in his Eastern philosophy. He is the bridge between the Peripatetic synthesis and the full flowering of mystical ontology that would culminate in the works of Ibn ʿArabī, Rūmī, and Mullā Ṣadrā. With Suhrawardī, we have moved decisively from the philosophy of being to the philosophy of light, from the syllogism to the vision. The next chapters will explore the further reaches of this transformation: the mystical ontology of Ibn ʿArabī, the poetic philosophy of Rūmī, and the revolutionary philosophy of history articulated by Ibn Khaldūn. The story of Islamic philosophy is about to take another turn — from the solitary intellect to the unity of all being, from the lamp of reason to the sun of the heart.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “If there is in existence something that needs neither definition nor explanation, it is that which is manifest in itself; and nothing is more manifest than Light (al-nūr), whereas nothing is richer in need of explanation than that which is obscure. Therefore, the reality of Light is that which is manifest in its own essence and makes other things manifest by its essence (al-ẓāhir fī nafsihi al-muẓhir li-ghayrihi); and your own self-awareness is a pure immaterial Light present to itself without any mediating mental form.” — Shihāb al-Dīn al-Suhrawardī, Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq (The Philosophy of Illumination, Part II.1)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How does Suhrawardī argue in Part One of Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq that the Aristotelian-Avicennan theory of essential definition (al-ḥadd al-tāmm by genus and specific difference) is logically circular and epistemically impossible? 2. Explain Suhrawardī's infinite-regress argument proving that existence (wujūd), necessity (wujūb), and contingency (imkān) are 'mental considerations' (iʿtibārāt ʿaqliyya) rather than concrete constituents added to essences in the external world. 3. Contrast 'knowledge by representation' (al-ʿilm al-ḥuṣūlī) with Suhrawardī's 'knowledge by presence' (al-ʿilm al-ḥuḍūrī). Why must all representational knowledge ultimately rest upon unmediated presential selfawareness? 4. How does Suhrawardī's ontology of Light (nūr) and Darkness (ghasaq) replace Avicenna's ten-intellect cosmology with a vast longitudinal (ṭūlī) and latitudinal (ʿarḍī) hierarchy of Lights and Lords of Species (arbāb al-anwāʿ)?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Suhrawardī, Shihāb al-Dīn. The Philosophy of Illumination (Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq). Edited and translated by John Walbridge and Hossein Ziai. Provo, UT: Brigham Young University Press, 1999. Ziai, Hossein. Knowledge and Illumination: A Study of Suhrawardī's Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq. Atlanta: Scholars Press, 1990. Walbridge, John. The Leaven of the Ancients: Suhrawardī and the Heritage of the Greeks. Albany: State University of New York Press, 2000. Marcotte, Roxanne D. Suhrawardi and the School of Illumination. London: Routledge, 2019. Aminrazavi, Mehdi. Suhrawardi and the School of Illumination. Richmond: Curzon Press, 1997.

Chapter 18Ibn ʿArabī, the Unity of Being, and the Systematic Akbarian School

The Greatest Master

Muḥyī al-Dīn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn al-ʿArabī (1165–1240) was born in Murcia, in al-Andalus, and died in Damascus, leaving behind a body of work so vast and so difficult that it has inspired devotion, controversy, and bewilderment for eight centuries. His followers called him al-Shaykh al-Akbar, the Greatest Master, and credited him with unveiling the deepest secrets of existence. His critics, both medieval and modern, accused him of pantheism, heresy, and obscurantism. What no one disputes is that Ibn ʿArabī was one of the most original and influential mystical thinkers in the history of religion — a philosopher of the unseen whose ideas about the unity of being, the perfect human, and the creative imagination reshaped the intellectual landscape of Islam, from Anatolia to India and beyond. Ibn ʿArabī is often classified as a Sufi rather than a philosopher, but this distinction is misleading. He was not a faylasūf in the Peripatetic sense; he did not write commentaries on Aristotle or compose syllogistic proofs for the existence of God. But he was a philosopher in the deeper sense: a thinker who developed a comprehensive and systematic account of reality, knowledge, and the human soul, and who articulated that account in a technical vocabulary of extraordinary precision. His philosophy is a mystical ontology — an account of being rooted in spiritual vision — and it stands as one of the great intellectual achievements of the Islamic tradition. This chapter examines his central doctrines: the unity of being, the perfect human, the creative imagination, and his hermeneutics of the Qur'an.

1. Waḥdat al-Wujūd: The Unity of Being The phrase most closely associated with Ibn ʿArabī is waḥdat al-wujūd, the unity of being. He himself did not use this exact expression; it was coined by later disciples to encapsulate his metaphysics. But the idea it expresses is the axis around which his entire thought turns: being (wujūd) is one. There is only one reality, one existence, and that is God, the Absolute, the Real (al-Ḥaqq). Everything other than God — the cosmos, the natural world, the human soul, all forms and all things — has no independent being of its own. It exists only as a manifestation, a selfdisclosure (tajallī), of the one divine reality. This is not pantheism, if pantheism means that every individual thing is God. Ibn ʿArabī was careful to maintain a distinction between the one Being and its many manifestations. The cosmos is not identical with God; it is like a shadow cast by God, or a wave on the ocean of divine existence. The wave is nothing other than the ocean, but the

ocean is not exhausted by the wave. The world is He/not He (huwa lā huwa): it reveals the divine without ever becoming identical with the divine essence. This delicate balance between immanence and transcendence is the heart of Ibn ʿArabī's ontology. The origin of the cosmos, in Ibn ʿArabī's vision, is divine love. God, in His solitude, was a "hidden treasure" that longed to be known, as a famous sacred tradition (ḥadīth qudsī) says. This longing moved the divine reality to manifest itself, to disclose its perfections in an infinity of forms. The cosmos is thus a theophany (tajallī), a continuous divine self-revelation, renewed at every instant. Creation is not a one-time event in the past but an ongoing, instantaneous effusion of being from God. At every moment, the world is being created anew; there is no persistence of being except through the constant creative act of the Real. This doctrine of perpetual creation (khalq jadīd) had profound implications. It dissolved the Aristotelian distinction between substance and accident, between the permanent and the changing. In the theatre of being, nothing endures by itself; every form is a new garment that the Real puts on and takes off in an endless display of beauty. The world is a dream, a divine imagination, a succession of images through which the hidden Source makes itself visible to itself.

2. The Perfect Human: Al-Insān al-Kāmil If the cosmos is God's self-disclosure, the human being is the purpose and summit of that disclosure. Ibn ʿArabī developed the concept of the Perfect Human (al-insān al-kāmil), the fully realised human being who actualises all the divine names and attributes and who serves as the mirror in which God contemplates His own perfection. The Perfect Human is not simply any virtuous person. He is the microcosm who recapitulates within himself the entire macrocosm. He contains the spiritual realities of all the prophets, all the angels, all the natural elements, all the divine names. He is the barzakh — the isthmus, the intermediary — between the unmanifest divine essence and the manifest world. Without him, the cosmos would be a body without a soul. Through him, God's creative purpose is fulfilled. In one sense, the Perfect Human is a universal spiritual reality, the Muḥammadan Reality (al-ḥaqīqa almuḥammadiyya), which is the eternal archetype of all prophethood and the first manifestation of God's light. In another sense, the Perfect Human is embodied in specific individuals — the prophets, the great saints, the "poles" (aqṭāb) around whom the spiritual hierarchy of the universe revolves. The greatest of these, for Ibn ʿArabī, is the Prophet Muḥammad, who is the most complete locus of divine self-disclosure and the model for all spiritual perfection. The path to becoming a Perfect Human — or, more realistically, to approaching that station — is the path of Sufi discipline: purification of the soul, the invocation of God's names, the cultivation of the virtues that correspond to the divine attributes, and the progressive annihilation of the ego-self (nafs) so that the true Self, the divine self, may shine through. The goal is not extinction but baqāʾ — subsistence in God, a state in which the mystic has died to his own attributes and lives through the attributes of the Real.

3. Imagination as Creative Faculty One of Ibn ʿArabī's most original and far-reaching contributions is his philosophy of the imagination (khayāl). Imagination, for Ibn ʿArabī, is not mere fancy or daydreaming. It is a cosmic faculty, a creative power, and an ontological plane — the very principle that mediates between the unseen world of spirit and the visible world of bodies. The imagination is the faculty that perceives the imaginal world (ʿālam al-mithāl), which Suhrawardī had

described and which Ibn ʿArabī richly elaborates. It is the power by which the soul gives form to the formless, and it is, at its highest, the power by which God manifests His own creative self-disclosure. The universe itself, on this view, is an act of divine imagination. The forms we perceive are not illusions in the sense of being false; they are real symbols, real presences, that embody spiritual meanings. The Prophet's visions, the mystic's experiences, the symbols of ritual, the verses of scripture — all are theophanies perceived by the imagination. To reduce them to literal statements or abstract concepts is to miss their reality; to interpret them imaginally is to enter into the creative act of divine self-revelation. Ibn ʿArabī gave particular attention to dreams and visions. The dream, he taught, is a privileged access to the imaginal world, and the true dream-vision (ruʾyā) is a mode of prophecy that continues even after the closure of legislative prophethood. The mystic learns to "interpret" dreams not by imposing a fixed code of symbols but by entering into the imaginal state with the waking consciousness of a realised soul. The dream-world and the wakingworld are two sides of a single reality, and the sage moves between them with ease. This philosophy of imagination had a profound influence on later Islamic aesthetics, particularly in Persian poetry and miniature painting. The works of Rūmī, Ḥāfiẓ, and the great Persian miniaturists can be read as explorations of the same imaginal realm that Ibn ʿArabī mapped in his metaphysical treatises.

4. Qur'anic Hermeneutics and the Unity of Religions Ibn ʿArabī's approach to the Qur'an is deeply philosophical. He reads scripture not as a codex of laws and historical reports but as a living symbolic text, every verse of which is a door to infinite meanings. The Qur'an, he says, has multiple levels of meaning, corresponding to the multiple levels of the human soul, and its deepest meanings are disclosed only to the perfected saint whose soul has become a polished mirror of the divine names. His method of interpretation is radically polysemic. Every verse, every word, every letter can bear an endless number of true meanings, because the Word of God is infinite and the understanding of the saint is an ongoing disclosure, never exhausted. He practiced this hermeneutic in his monumental al-Futūḥāt al-Makkiyya (The Meccan Openings) and his shorter Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam (The Bezels of Wisdom), where each prophet — Adam, Abraham, Moses, Jesus, Muḥammad — is treated as a unique facet of the divine wisdom, a distinct word of God, a living symbol through which a particular aspect of the divine nature is manifested. This hermeneutic also grounded Ibn ʿArabī's famous and controversial attitude toward other religions. In his poetry, he could write: My heart has become capable of every form: a pasture for gazelles, a cloister for monks, a temple for idols, a Kaʿba for the pilgrim, the tablets of the Torah, the book of the Qur'an. I follow the religion of Love: whatever way Love's camels take, that is my religion and my faith. This is not a loosening of commitment to Islam but a universalising of its deepest principle. The Real is worshipped in all sincere acts of worship, even if the forms differ. The dogmas and rituals of other faiths are not mere errors; they are limited but genuine manifestations of the same divine light that shines most perfectly in the Muḥammadan revelation. The Perfect Human, who has realised waḥdat al-wujūd, sees God in every belief and every form, and he worships God where God is, which is everywhere.

5. The Akbarian Legacy Ibn ʿArabī's influence on subsequent Islamic thought can scarcely be overstated. His works became the subject of an enormous commentary tradition, starting with his stepson and foremost interpreter, Ṣadr al-Dīn al-Qūnawī (d.

1274), who systematised his teachings and linked them to the Avicennan philosophical tradition. His Miftāḥ alGhayb is a key text of philosophical Sufism, and it became the bridge by which Ibn ʿArabī's ontology entered the madrasa curriculum. ʿAbd al-Karīm al-Jīlī (d. c. 1424) , author of al-Insān al-Kāmil, expanded the concept of the Perfect Human in a philosophical direction that was widely read in the Ottoman lands. Dāwūd al-Qayṣarī (d. 1350) wrote an influential commentary on the Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam that was studied in Ottoman madrasas for centuries. Persian poets from Fakhr al-Dīn al-ʿIrāqī to Shabistarī gave his metaphysics a lyrical form. Ottoman sultans sponsored the study of the Fuṣūṣ, and the Akbarian tradition became an integral part of the curriculum in the great learning centres of Anatolia, Syria, and Egypt. In the Shīʿī world, Ibn ʿArabī was read with particular intensity. The Safavid philosophers, including Mīr Dāmād and Mullā Ṣadrā, incorporated his ideas into their own systems, often in creative and critical ways. Mullā Ṣadrā's doctrine of the primacy of existence and his account of substantial motion are, in part, responses to the challenges raised by Ibn ʿArabī's ontology. The debate over whether Ibn ʿArabī was a pantheist or an orthodox mystic continues, but the depth of the engagement testifies to his enduring power. Ibn ʿArabī also travelled beyond the Muslim world. His ideas influenced Christian mystics in Spain, Jewish kabbalists, and, in the modern era, Western scholars of mysticism such as Henry Corbin, who made the "imaginal world" a key category in the study of comparative religion. He is today one of the most widely read and studied Muslim authors globally, a philosopher of unity for a fragmented age.

6. The Five Divine Presences (al-Ḥaḍarāt al-Ilāhiyya al-Khams) and the Systematic Akbarian School While Ibn ʿArabī himself wrote in a torrent of visionary exegesis, paradox, and poetic symbol across the Futūḥāt and the Fuṣūṣ, his disciples transformed his teachings over the next two centuries into a rigorous, formal metaphysical discipline known as Theoretical Gnosis (al-ʿirfān al-naẓarī / al-ḥikma al-ʿirfāniyya), complete with its own technical vocabulary, deductive demonstrations, and madrasa textbooks. At the heart of this systematization stands the ontology of the Five Divine Presences (al-ḥaḍarāt al-ilāhiyya al-khams) — the five universal planes across which the One Reality (al-Ḥaqq) discloses Itself (tajallī): 1. The Presence of Exclusive Unity (Ḥaḍrat al-Aḥadiyya / al-Dhāt / Hāhūt): The Absolute Divine Essence considered in Its pure, unconditioned hiddenness ('the Mystery of Mysteries,' ghayb al-ghuyūb), prior to all names, attributes, and relations, concerning which no creature can attain conceptual knowledge. 2. The Presence of Inclusive Oneness and Divinity (Ḥaḍrat al-Wāḥidiyya / al-Ulūhiyya / Lāhūt): The level of the Divine Names and Attributes together with their cognitive correlates in God's eternal knowledge — the Immutable Entities (al-aʿyān al-thābita). Known also as the 'Most Holy Effusion' (al-fayḍ al-aqdas), this is the plane where every possible quiddity subsists eternally in God's knowledge before receiving external manifestation. 3. The Presence of the Invincible Spirit and Pure Intellects (Ḥaḍrat al-Jabarūt / ʿĀlam al-Arwāḥ): The realm of pure, luminous immaterial spirits and separate intellects brought into actual existence through the 'Holy Effusion' (al-fayḍ al-muqaddas). 4. The Presence of the Imaginal Realm (Ḥaḍrat al-Malakūt / ʿĀlam al-Mithāl al-Muṭlaq): The ontological isthmus (barzakh) where spiritual meanings take on subtle, non-material sensible figures, dimensions, and colors. 5. The Presence of the Corporeal World and the All-Comprehensive Human (Ḥaḍrat al-Mulk / al-Nāsūt wa-lInsān al-Kāmil): The visible physical universe culminating in the Perfect Human (al-insān al-kāmil), who

integrates all five presences within a single synthetic mirror.

The Architects of Akbarian Metaphysics: Al-Qūnawī, al-Jandī, al-Qayṣarī, al-Jīlī, and Jāmī Five major thinkers forged this systematic Akbarian tradition and brought it into direct dialogue with Avicennan philosophy and post-classical kalām: Ṣadr al-Dīn al-Qūnawī (1207–1274): Ibn ʿArabī's stepson and foremost disciple in Konya, al-Qūnawī recast his master's vision in the rigorous ontological language of Avicennan metaphysics. In Miftāḥ al-Ghayb (The Key to the Unseen) and Iʿjāz al-Bayān, he defined the subject matter (mawḍūʿ) of metaphysical gnosis (al-ʿilm alilāhī) as the Real Existence (al-wujūd al-ḥaqq) of God, and engaged in a celebrated philosophical correspondence (al-Murāsalāt) with Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī on whether God's reality is identical with absolute existence. Muʾayyad al-Dīn al-Jandī (d. c. 1300) & ʿAbd al-Razzāq al-Kāshānī (d. c. 1335): Al-Qūnawī's student alJandī authored the first complete systematic commentary on the Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam, followed by al-Kāshānī's influential commentary and Sufi lexicon (Iṣṭilāḥāt al-Ṣūfiyya), which standardized Akbarian ontology across Persia and the Arab world. Dāwūd ibn Maḥmūd al-Qayṣarī (d. 1350): Appointed by the Ottoman Sultan Orhan Gazi as the first professor of the earliest Ottoman madrasa in Iznik (Nicaea) in 1336, al-Qayṣarī prefixed his commentary on the Fuṣūṣ with a twelve-chapter philosophical prolegomenon (Muqaddimat al-Qayṣarī). Treating existence (wujūd) as unconditionally self-subsistent, neither a universal concept nor a particular substance, al-Qayṣarī's Muqaddima remains to this day the foundational textbook of theoretical Sufism in Iranian and Anatolian seminaries. ʿAbd al-Karīm al-Jīlī (1365–1424): In his masterpiece al-Insān al-Kāmil fī Maʿrifat al-Awākhir wa-l-Awāʾil (The Perfect Human in the Knowledge of the Last and First Things), al-Jīlī elaborated the descending stages of divine self-disclosure (tanazzulāt) and the cosmic metaphysics of the Muḥammadan Reality. Nūr al-Dīn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Jāmī (1414–1492): The great Timurid poet-philosopher of Herat composed alDurra al-Fākhira (The Precious Pearl) at the request of the Ottoman Sultan Mehmed II the Conqueror. Comparing point-by-point the proofs of the theologians (mutakallimūn), the Peripatetic philosophers (ḥukamāʾ), and the Akbarian Sufis (ṣūfiyya) on eleven fundamental metaphysical questions — including the unity of existence, divine knowledge of particulars, and causal necessity — Jāmī demonstrated that Akbarian ontology had become a full-fledged third metaphysical school alongside kalām and falsafa.

7. From Prose to Poetry Ibn ʿArabī's works, for all their brilliance, are notoriously difficult. He wrote in dense Arabic, often in a visionary style that assumes the reader has already tasted the experiences he describes. But his ideas found a far wider audience through the medium in which Persian culture excelled: poetry. The next chapter turns to the most famous mystical poet in Islamic history — Jalāl al-Dīn Rūmī — who translated the metaphysics of unity into the music of verse and made the love-longing of the soul for God accessible to millions, literate and illiterate, Muslim and nonMuslim alike.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “Glory be to Him who brought all things into manifestation while being identical with their very existence! The cosmos stands between the Real (al-Ḥaqq) and sheer non-existence like an image seen in a polished mirror: in one respect It is He, and in another respect it is not He (huwa lā huwa); and the Perfect Human (al-insān al107

kāmil) is to the Real as the pupil is to the eye through which sight occurs.” — Muḥyī al-Dīn Ibn ʿArabī, Fuṣūṣ alḤikam (The Bezels of Wisdom, Chapter on Adam) and al-Futūḥāt al-Makkiyya

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. Why is it philosophically inaccurate to translate Ibn ʿArabī's doctrine of the Unity of Being (waḥdat al-wujūd) as 'pantheism'? How does the formula 'He / not He' (huwa lā huwa) maintain both divine transcendence (tanzīh) and divine immanence (tashbīh)? 2. What are the 'Immutable Entities' (al-aʿyān al-thābita) in Ibn ʿArabī's ontology, and how does the distinction between the Most Holy Effusion (al-fayḍ al-aqdas) and the Holy Effusion (al-fayḍ al-muqaddas) account for the manifestation of the cosmos? 3. Trace the Five Divine Presences (al-ḥaḍarāt al-ilāhiyya al-khams) as systematized by Ṣadr al-Dīn al-Qūnawī and Dāwūd al-Qayṣarī. Why does the Perfect Human (al-insān al-kāmil) occupy a unique ontological station as the comprehensive isthmus (barzakh) of the cosmos? 4. How did ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Jāmī in al-Durra al-Fākhira compare the positions of the theologians (mutakallimūn), the Peripatetic philosophers (ḥukamāʾ), and the Akbarian Sufis on the relationship between God's existence and essence?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Ibn ʿArabī, Muḥyī al-Dīn. The Bezels of Wisdom (Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam). Translated by R. W. J. Austin. Mahwah, NJ: Paulist Press, 1980. Chittick, William C. The Sufi Path of Knowledge: Ibn al-ʿArabī's Metaphysics of Imagination. Albany: State University of New York Press, 1989. Chittick, William C. The Self-Disclosure of God: Principles of Ibn al-ʿArabī's Cosmology. Albany: State University of New York Press, 1998. Ali, Mukhtar H. The Horizons of Being: The Metaphysics of Ibn al-ʿArabī in the Muqaddimat al-Qayṣarī (Translation and Study of Dāwūd al-Qayṣarī's Prolegomenon). Leiden: Brill, 2020. Jāmī, Nūr al-Dīn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān. Chapter 19Rūmī and Philosophical Poetry

The Poet of Love

Jalāl al-Dīn Muḥammad ibn Muḥammad al-Balkhī al-Rūmī (1207–1273) — known simply as Rūmī — was born in Balkh, in present-day Afghanistan, and died in Konya, in present-day Turkey. He was a jurist, a theologian, a teacher, and by all accounts a respectable member of the religious establishment. Then, in 1244, he met a wandering dervish named Shams al-Dīn of Tabriz, and his life was turned inside out. The relationship between Rūmī and Shams — a volcanic fusion of spiritual intensity, affection, and utter absorption — shattered Rūmī's scholarly composure and unlocked in him a flood of poetic inspiration. For the remaining twenty-nine years of his life, he poured out an immense body of verse: the Mathnawī-yi Maʿnawī (The Spiritual Couplets), a six-book philosophicalmystical poem of some twenty-six thousand lines that is often called the "Qur'an in Persian"; the Dīwān-i Shams-i

Tabrīzī, a collection of ecstatic odes; and a smaller body of prose works, including the Fīhi mā fīhi (In It Is What Is In It), a record of his discourses. Rūmī is not a philosopher in the strict sense. He did not write systematic treatises, he did not argue syllogistically, and he often mocked the pretensions of the professional philosophers. But Rūmī's poetry is saturated with philosophical ideas — the metaphysics of unity, the nature of the soul, the problem of evil, the psychology of love, the relationship between form and meaning — and he presented these ideas in a way that ordinary human beings could feel, sing, and live. To exclude him from a history of Muslim philosophy would be to miss the most effective philosophical communicator in the Islamic tradition. This chapter explores the core philosophical themes of Rūmī's poetry and asks what it means to do philosophy in the medium of verse.

1. Love, Annihilation, and the Path of Return The single great theme of Rūmī's poetry is love (ʿishq). Love is not a sentiment or a preference; it is the fundamental metaphysical force, the power that drives the whole cosmos, the gravitational pull that draws every particle of creation back to its divine origin. The Mathnawī opens with the song of the reed flute, which is the soul's lament of separation from its source: "Listen to the reed as it tells its tale, complaining of separation." The reed has been cut from the reedbed; the soul has been exiled from the divine presence. All human longing, all desire, all restlessness, is the soul's memory of its true home. The path of life is the path of return. The vehicle of return is love, which Rūmī understands as an ontological electricity. Love is not something we have; it is something that has us. It annihilates the ego, dissolves the boundaries of the self, and burns away everything that is not God. Rūmī's beloved master Shams is, in his poetry, a living symbol of this annihilating love — the human form that reveals the divine beauty with such intensity that the lover's selfhood is consumed. The relationship between Rūmī and Shams is a dramatic enactment of the relationship between the soul and God, and Rūmī's poetry is its record. The technical term for this annihilation is fanāʾ — the passing away of the individual self in the overwhelming presence of the divine. But Rūmī, like Ibn ʿArabī, teaches that fanāʾ is followed by baqāʾ — subsistence in God, a return to the world with a new self that is no longer the ego but the self that God has given. The perfected saint does not disappear; he becomes the locus through which God's attributes flow into the world. He is at once completely annihilated and completely alive, the servant who has become the friend of God.

2. The Core Philosophical Themes of the Mathnawī The Mathnawī is not a disorganised outpouring of emotion. It is a carefully structured work of didactic poetry that proceeds through stories, parables, dialogues, and odes to unfold a comprehensive spiritual vision. The philosophical content of the Mathnawī can be organised around several core themes. The One and the Many. Rūmī's ontology is a poetic expression of waḥdat al-wujūd. "You suppose you are the drop," he says, "but you are the ocean." The multiplicity of the world is an illusion produced by the veils of perception; behind the forms, there is only the One. But Rūmī does not give the reader a dry metaphysical statement. He tells the story of the elephant in the dark: a group of blind men each touch a different part of the elephant — trunk, tusk, leg, ear — and each declares the elephant to be something different. Their dispute is the human condition; the elephant is the divine reality, which the partial perceptions of the senses and the mind cannot grasp. Philosophy that argues without tasting, without seeing, is one of the blind men. The Soul's Ascent. The human soul is a multi-layered reality that can ascend through the mineral, vegetable, animal, and human levels to the angelic and beyond. "I died as mineral and became a plant; I died as plant and rose

to animal; I died as animal and I was a man. Why should I fear? When was I less by dying?" The soul's evolution is not a doctrine of reincarnation in the earthly sense but a spiritual climb through degrees of being toward the divine. The purpose of life is to continue this ascent through knowledge, love, and purification until the soul becomes a mirror for the divine attributes. The Problem of Evil. Rūmī treats evil as an appearance, a privation, a necessary part of the total picture. A painting requires shadow to show light; the body requires pain to know health; the soul requires difficulty to grow strong. "God created pain and suffering," he writes, "so that joy might be known through its opposite." This is the classic Neoplatonic theodicy, but Rūmī gives it psychological and narrative depth. He tells the story of a chickpea boiling in a pot, crying out in pain, and the cook answering: "I am not torturing you; I am making you tasty. You were raw; now you are being cooked. You must pass through the fire to become food for the soul." The metaphor is culinary, but the philosophy is serious: suffering is a refining fire, a call to transcendence, and the world's evils are ingredients in a dish whose ultimate flavour is joy. Reason and Love. Perhaps the most persistent philosophical theme in Rūmī is the relationship between reason (ʿaql) and love (ʿishq). Reason, for Rūmī, is a noble and necessary faculty — "God's greatest gift" — but it is limited. Reason can describe the shore; it cannot swim the ocean. It can point to the moon; it cannot become the moonlight. Love is the faculty that crosses the boundaries that reason cannot cross. It is supra-rational, not irrational; it completes reason, it does not destroy it. The philosopher who relies on reason alone is like a donkey carrying a load of books; the lover who has no reason is a madman. The perfect human integrates both, but the ruling principle is love. Form and Meaning. Rūmī constantly distinguishes between the outer form (ṣūrat) and the inner meaning (maʿnā). The ritual prayer is a form whose meaning is the soul's presence with God; the fast is a form whose meaning is the purification of desire; the scripture is a form whose meaning is the living word that speaks to the heart. The man who fights over the forms of religion while ignoring their meaning is the man who carries a ladder on his shoulder and never climbs it. The philosopher's conceptual systems are also forms; they are useful only if they lead beyond themselves to the direct experience of meaning. This hermeneutic, deeply influenced by Sufi and Akbarian thought, is one of Rūmī's most valuable philosophical contributions. Free Will and Determinism. Rūmī wrestles with the classic theological problem of free will and divine predestination and refuses to resolve it intellectually. He offers parables on both sides — stories of people who succeed through determined effort, stories of people whose efforts fail and who must surrender to God's will — and concludes that the answer cannot be captured in a doctrine. The mature soul lives in a state of "poised tension," acting as if everything depends on effort while knowing that everything depends on God. This is not philosophical evasion; it is a practical wisdom that recognises the limits of propositional knowledge.

3. Comparison with Technical Falsafa: A Lay Philosophy? Rūmī's relationship to the technical falsafa of the Peripatetics is complex. He knew the tradition; his teacher Burhān al-Dīn al-Tirmidhī had studied Avicenna and the philosophers. But Rūmī rarely engages with them directly, and when he does, it is usually to chastise them for spiritual blindness. In one famous passage, he compares the philosopher to a man who exhaustively studies the properties of water — its chemical composition, its boiling point, its taste — but never drinks when thirsty. The knowledge of the philosophers is a knowledge of definitions and demonstrations, but it is not the knowledge of taste (dhawq), and without taste, it remains a description of a feast without the eating.

And yet, Rūmī's poetry does philosophical work. It addresses the same questions that falsafa addresses — God, cosmos, soul, ethics, epistemology — and it provides answers that are intellectually coherent, emotionally compelling, and practically transformative. The Mathnawī is a philosophical summa in poetic form, a Shifāʾ written in verse. Its medium is not the syllogism but the story, the symbol, the metaphor, and the rhythm, which are, for Rūmī, superior instruments for conveying truth about the human condition. The syllogism convinces the mind; the story changes the heart. This makes Rūmī the philosopher of the people, the thinker who succeeds in bridging the gap between the intellectual elite and the ordinary believer. Where Avicenna's Ishārāt could be read only by the trained scholar, the Mathnawī could be read aloud in the bazaar, sung at gatherings, memorised by children, and its philosophical content absorbed almost without the hearer's awareness. Rūmī is the most successful populariser of philosophical ideas in Islamic history, and the enduring global appeal of his poetry — today he is the best-selling poet in America — testifies to the power of his method.

4. The Mevlevi Order and the Whirling Dervishes Rūmī's philosophical poetry was not meant to be merely read. It was meant to be lived. After his death, his followers organised themselves into the Mevlevi Order, a Sufi brotherhood whose central ritual is the samāʿ — a ceremony of music, poetry, and whirling dance that enacts the soul's journey of annihilation and subsistence. The whirling dervish, with one hand raised to heaven and the other pointed to the earth, becomes a living symbol of the perfect human: receiving from God and transmitting to creation, turning on the axis of love. The samāʿ is philosophy in motion, a bodily and musical performance of the metaphysics of unity. It is, in its own way, as rich a philosophical practice as the writing of a treatise. The Mevlevi Order spread through Anatolia and the Ottoman world, influencing Ottoman art, music, and politics. The great Ottoman calligraphers, miniaturists, and composers were often Mevlevis, and their aesthetic production is an extension of Rūmī's philosophical vision. The order was suppressed in the early Turkish Republic but has survived and been revived as a cultural and spiritual practice, a testament to the enduring power of Rūmī's synthesis.

5. The Poet and the World Rūmī died in Konya in 1273, and his funeral, it is said, was attended by Muslims, Christians, Jews, and Zoroastrians — his poetry had already crossed the borders of creed. His invitation, "Come, come, whoever you are, come again," has become a global motto of spiritual welcome. But Rūmī was not a syncretist or a relativist. He was a deeply committed Muslim who believed that the truths he expressed in poetry were the truths of the Qur'an, the truths of the Prophet, the truths that the saints of all traditions had glimpsed but that Islam had stated most perfectly. His universalism was rooted in his particularism; his door was wide open because he was so deeply at home in his own house. With Rūmī, the mystical-ontological current of Islamic philosophy reaches its maximum accessibility. From Ibn ʿArabī's dense metaphysical treatises, which required a specialised intellect to penetrate, the tradition moves into the realm of music, dance, and story, where the same ideas can be felt in the body and the heart. The unity of being becomes a tune you can hum; the perfect human becomes a friend you can love; the path of return becomes a road you can walk.

6. The Turn to History and Society The mystical poetry of Rūmī represents one direction in which post-Avicennan Islamic thought developed. But there was another direction, equally original and perhaps more surprising: the philosophy of history and society. In the fourteenth century, a North African scholar named Ibn Khaldūn would invent a new science — the science of human society — and would produce, in his Muqaddimah, the most sophisticated philosophy of history that the medieval world had ever seen. The next chapter turns to this towering figure, the philosopher of ʿaṣabiyya and the cycles of civilisation.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “The Peripatetic logician walks upon wooden legs, and a wooden leg is exceedingly unsteady! Intellect (ʿaql) is like an official who swims carefully near the shore, whereas Love (ʿishq) is the boundless ocean in which the swimmer is drowned and reborn; I died as mineral and became a plant, I died as plant and rose to animal, I died as animal and I was a human — why then should I fear? When was I ever diminished by dying?” — Jalāl al-Dīn Muḥammad al-Balkhī al-Rūmī, Mathnawī-yi Maʿnawī (Books I & III)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. In what sense is Rūmī's Mathnawī-yi Maʿnawī a work of genuine philosophy rather than merely devotional verse, and how does narrative parable function as an epistemological vehicle for truths that resist syllogistic formulation? 2. Explain Rūmī's distinction between 'particular, calculating reason' (ʿaql-i juzʾī) and 'Universal Intellect' (ʿaql-i kullī) illuminated by ontological Love (ʿishq). Why does Rūmī compare the deductive philosopher to a person walking on wooden stilts? 3. How does Rūmī's famous parable of the elephant in the dark room illustrate his epistemology of perspective, spiritual blindness, and the limits of sensory and sectarian dogmatism? 4. Analyze Rūmī's poetic account of the soul's ascending evolution from mineral to plant, animal, human, and angelic consciousness. How did twentieth-century thinkers like Muḥammad Iqbāl reinterpret this passage as a philosophy of creative becoming?
