Part VI: The Philosophy of History and Society

Chapter 20Ibn Khaldūn: The Philosophy of History and the Science of Society

The Scholar of the Maghreb

Walī al-Dīn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Khaldūn (1332–1406) was born in Tunis into a family of Andalusian Arab origin that had produced scholars and administrators for generations. His life spanned the turbulent politics of North Africa and Islamic Spain, where he served a succession of rulers as diplomat, vizier, and adviser — often in dangerous conditions, sometimes imprisoned, always observing the rise and fall of dynasties with the eye of a naturalist watching the cycles of the seasons. In his later years, he withdrew from political life to write his masterpiece, the Kitāb al-ʿIbar (Book of Lessons), a universal history prefaced by a long methodological and theoretical introduction called the Muqaddimah (Prolegomenon). It is this introduction — a self-contained treatise of some five hundred pages — that has given Ibn Khaldūn his reputation as one of the greatest social philosophers of any age. Ibn Khaldūn claimed to have founded a new science (ʿilm mustaqill), the science of human society (ʿilm al-ʿumrān al-basharī). This science was to be the study of the laws governing the formation, development, and decay of civilisations. It would be empirical, based on historical observation rather than philosophical deduction; it would be causal, seeking the necessary relations between social phenomena; and it would be critical, providing criteria by which historical reports could be verified or rejected. The Muqaddimah is the realisation of this programme, and it has no real parallel in the medieval world. This chapter examines Ibn Khaldūn's philosophy of history, his theory of ʿaṣabiyya, his economics, his epistemology, and his place between the Aristotelian and modern traditions.

1. The New Science of ʿUmrān Ibn Khaldūn's first move is to break with the conventions of both the historians and the philosophers. Historians, he argues, are mostly uncritical compilers who repeat whatever they hear without examining its truth. They report

impossible events — armies of millions, cities of gold — without considering whether the natural conditions of human life could have produced them. Philosophers, for their part, have written about politics, but they have done so in an ideal and deductive mode, describing the "virtuous city" that ought to exist rather than the actual societies that do exist. Ibn Khaldūn proposes something new: a science that studies human society as it actually is, with the same empirical rigour that the natural sciences bring to their subjects. This science of ʿumrān (a term that covers civilisation, social life, and settled habitation) proceeds from a fundamental axiom: human beings are by nature political animals who cannot survive in isolation. They must cooperate to produce food, shelter, and defence. This cooperation gives rise to social forms — family, tribe, city, state — which develop according to laws that are as regular as the laws of nature. Ibn Khaldūn's task is to discover these laws by observing the historical record and abstracting the recurring patterns. The Muqaddimah is organised around this task. It contains extensive discussions of geography and climate, of the difference between nomadic and sedentary life, of the origin of the state, of the nature of kingship and the caliphate, of the arts and sciences, of economic production and taxation, and of the causes of the rise and fall of dynasties. Through all these topics, Ibn Khaldūn seeks to identify the causal connections — the ṭabāʾiʿ al-ʿumrān, the natural dispositions of civilisation — that explain why societies are as they are and why they change in the ways they do.

2. ʿAṣabiyya: The Motor of History The central concept of Ibn Khaldūn's theory is ʿaṣabiyya, a term that is difficult to translate but can be glossed as "group cohesion," "social solidarity," or "tribal spirit." ʿAṣabiyya is the bond that holds a group together — the shared sense of kinship, honour, and mutual obligation that makes collective action possible. It is strongest in nomadic, tribal societies (badāwa), where the harsh conditions of desert life require intense cooperation and produce a spirit of courage, endurance, and independence. History, for Ibn Khaldūn, is driven by the interaction between two kinds of society: the nomadic world of badāwa and the sedentary world of ḥaḍāra. Nomadic groups, whose ʿaṣabiyya is strong, periodically conquer the settled civilisations and found new dynasties. The conquerors are attracted by the luxuries and culture of the cities, and over the course of a few generations, they become softened by sedentary life. Their ʿaṣabiyya weakens; they become dependent on mercenaries, on elaborate bureaucracies, on wealth that they no longer produce. The dynasty decays, becoming corrupt, oppressive, and militarily feeble, until it is overthrown by a new wave of nomads with fresh ʿaṣabiyya. The cycle repeats. Ibn Khaldūn mapped this cycle with a precision that remains startling. He argued that a dynasty rarely lasts longer than three or four generations — about a hundred and twenty years — before its vigour is exhausted. The first generation retains its desert toughness; the second still remembers it; the third has known only luxury; the fourth has lost all connection to the founding spirit and is ripe for replacement. This is not a moralistic narrative of virtue and vice but a causal account of the sociological effects of environment, wealth, and power on human character. Crucially, ʿaṣabiyya is not, for Ibn Khaldūn, a purely destructive or primitive force. It is the necessary precondition for political authority. Without it, no state can be founded or maintained. The ruler's task is to channel ʿaṣabiyya, to temper it with religion and law, and to create a stable political order before the inevitable decline sets in. Religion plays a key role in this process: it can augment and elevate ʿaṣabiyya, transforming tribal loyalty into a shared commitment to a higher cause, and it can provide the normative framework that holds a diverse empire together after the initial cohesion of conquest has begun to fray.

3. The Epistemology of History Ibn Khaldūn's most important methodological contribution is his theory of historical criticism. He argued that the historian's primary task is not to collect reports but to evaluate their truth. The criterion of truth is the conformity of the report to the laws of ʿumrān. If a report describes an event that could not have occurred given the known regularities of social life — for example, a city of impossible size or an army of impossible number — the historian must reject it, no matter how reliable the chain of transmitters appears to be. The possibility of the event, as determined by the new science of society, is the ultimate touchstone. This was a radical proposal. The dominant method of Islamic historiography had been the isnād system, in which reports were evaluated by the credibility of the chain of transmitters. Ibn Khaldūn did not discard this method, but he insisted that it was insufficient. The veracity of the chain cannot guarantee the truth of a report that contradicts the known order of the world. The historian must be a philosopher of society, not merely a collector of chains. He must understand what is possible for human beings in their various conditions before he judges what particular human beings did. Ibn Khaldūn's epistemology also extended to the relationship between reason and revelation. He accepted the basic positions of Ashʿarī theology on the limits of human reason in metaphysical matters, but he argued that within the realm of the observable world — nature and society — reason can attain genuine knowledge of causal laws. His God does not violate these laws through miracles in history; the affairs of the world proceed according to the regularities that God has established. This is not occasionalism; it is the affirmation of a relative autonomy of the causal order, at least for the purposes of historical and social explanation. Ibn Khaldūn is, in this respect, closer to Ibn Rushd than to al-Ghazālī, though he is careful not to stray into theological controversy.

4. Economic Thought: The Labour Theory of Value and the Dynamics of State Finance Ibn Khaldūn is also one of the first philosophers to develop a systematic economic theory. He articulated a labour theory of value: the value of goods, he argued, is determined by the amount of labour invested in their production. A thing that requires more labour to produce is more valuable than one that requires less, all else being equal. The division of labour is the engine of productivity, and the growth of cities depends on the specialisation and cooperation of labourers, artisans, and merchants. His analysis of state finance is remarkably modern. He observed that in the early stages of a dynasty, taxation is light, and the state's revenues, though modest, are sufficient because the demands of the ruler are few. As the dynasty ages, the ruler's expenditures increase — luxurious living, elaborate bureaucracy, military expansion, monuments — and the state raises taxes to meet them. Higher taxes discourage production, leading to a shrinking tax base, which in turn leads to higher rates, which further depress production. The cycle ends in fiscal crisis, which weakens the state and hastens its fall. This analysis has been compared to the Laffer curve and to modern theories of fiscal policy, and it testifies to Ibn Khaldūn's sensitivity to the unintended consequences of state action. He also discussed supply and demand, price levels, the dangers of state interference in commerce, and the economic basis of urban-rural relations. The Muqaddimah can be read as a treatise on political economy long before that discipline existed as a separate field. Its empirical orientation and its search for causal regularities make it a precursor to the modern social sciences in a way that the political philosophy of al-Fārābī or the ethical treatises of Ibn Miskawayh are not.

5. Religion, Prophecy, and Kingship Ibn Khaldūn's treatment of religion is sociological rather than theological. He analyses prophecy as a social force that transforms communities by uniting them under a single moral vision. A prophet, by virtue of his claim to divine authority, can overcome the factionalism of tribes and create a superior form of ʿaṣabiyya — a solidarity based on shared belief rather than blood. The early Islamic conquests, he argues, were made possible by the combination of Arab tribal solidarity and the unifying force of the new religion. Without the religion, the tribes would have remained divided; without the tribes, the religion would have had no military vehicle. The two reinforced each other to produce a historical explosion. But Ibn Khaldūn does not claim that prophecy is merely a social function. As a believing Muslim, he affirms the truth of the Prophet's mission. His point is a methodological one: the social effects of religion can be studied empirically, just as the effects of climate or economics can be studied. The divine origin of religion is a matter of faith; its worldly consequences are a matter of science. He also analysed the transformation of the caliphate into kingship (mulk). The original caliphate, he argues, was a rational and religious institution in which the ruler governed according to the Sharīʿa and with the consent of the community. Over time, as the Arab empire expanded and ʿaṣabiyya changed, the caliphate became a hereditary monarchy on the Persian and Byzantine model — a natural development, Ibn Khaldūn suggests, given the dynamics of power and group solidarity. He describes this transformation without passing moral judgement; his interest is in the causal mechanisms, not in ideal forms.

6. Ibn Khaldūn's Place: Between Aristotle and Modern Sociology Ibn Khaldūn has often been called the father of sociology, and the title is not entirely anachronistic. He consciously set out to create a new empirical science of society, distinct from both the deductive politics of the philosophers and the uncritical chronicle of the historians. His emphasis on causal explanation, on the regularities of social life, on the material and environmental factors that shape human behaviour, and on the critical evaluation of sources — all this places him, intellectually, closer to Montesquieu, Adam Smith, or Émile Durkheim than to any medieval predecessor. And yet Ibn Khaldūn is also a man of his time. His science of society is embedded in a theological framework. He accepts the createdness of the world, the limitation of human reason in matters of the unseen, and the ultimate sovereignty of God over history. His empiricism is not secularism; it is a division of labour between what reason can know (the observable world) and what faith must accept (the invisible world). This division was itself a philosophical stance, one that allowed him to pursue a genuinely scientific study of society without coming into conflict with religious orthodoxy. Ibn Khaldūn's influence has been more durable than his immediate readership might suggest. He was read by Ottoman historians and by some European thinkers in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries. In the twentieth century, he was claimed by Arab nationalists, by Marxists, by modernisers, and by traditionalists — each finding in his work a precursor of their own intellectual project. His stature today is secure, and his Muqaddimah remains a living text, capable of speaking to economists, sociologists, and philosophers of history in ways that the more strictly metaphysical texts of the tradition may not.

7. The Return to the East Ibn Khaldūn wrote in the Maghreb, and his intellectual legacy, though recognised in the Islamic east, was never fully integrated into the madrasa curriculum there. The east had its own philosophical developments, centred in the

Shīʿī lands of Iran and Iraq, where the Avicennan, Illuminationist, and Akbarian traditions were being woven into a new synthesis. The next chapter turns to one of the great architects of that synthesis: Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī, the philosopher, theologian, and scientist who revived Avicennan philosophy after al-Ghazālī's critique, answered the objections of Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, and laid the foundations for the School of Isfahan.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “History, in its outward appearance, is no more than accounts of political events, dynasties, and occurrences of the remote past; yet in its inner reality (fī bāṭinihi), it is rational investigation (naẓar), verification (taḥqīq), and the subtle explanation of the causes and origins of existing things. Therefore it is rooted firmly in philosophy and deserves to be counted as one of its branches, through an independent science whose subject is human civilization and social organization (al-ʿumrān al-basharī).” — Ibn Khaldūn, Al-Muqaddima (Prolegomena to the Book of Lessons)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. Why did Ibn Khaldūn argue that traditional isnād criticism (evaluating the moral probity of transmitters) is insufficient for establishing historical truth without a prior 'science of human civilization' (ʿilm al-ʿumrān albasharī) that tests whether a reported event is sociologically and physically possible? 2. Explain the dialectical relationship between nomadic/pastoral society (al-ʿumrān al-badawī) and sedentary urban civilization (al-ʿumrān al-ḥaḍarī) in the Muqaddima. Why does the very triumph of a dynasty in founding a luxurious city plant the seeds of its four-generation decay? 3. What is 'group solidarity' (ʿaṣabiyya) in Ibn Khaldūn's political sociology, and how does prophetic religion transform tribal ʿaṣabiyya into a supra-tribal civilizational force? 4. Why did Ibn Khaldūn write a chapter 'In Refutation of Philosophy and the Corruption of Its Students' near the end of the Muqaddima? How can a thinker who rejected Neoplatonic metaphysical deduction simultaneously claim that his new science of history is 'one of the branches of philosophy'?

Part VII: The Later Shīʿī and Ottoman Philosophical Traditions

Chapter 21Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī: Theologian, Philosopher, Scientist

The Polymath of Maragha

Naṣīr al-Dīn Muḥammad ibn Muḥammad al-Ṭūsī (1201–1274) was one of the most versatile minds of the Islamic tradition. He was a Shīʿī theologian of the first rank, the author of the most influential Twelver Shīʿī creed, the Tajrīd al-Iʿtiqād (The Abstract of Belief). He was a philosopher who defended Avicenna against the critiques of Fakhr alDīn al-Rāzī and wrote the most important commentary on Avicenna's al-Ishārāt wa-l-Tanbīhāt. He was an astronomer who directed the great observatory at Maragha, under the patronage of the Mongol Ilkhāns, and whose astronomical models corrected and advanced the Ptolemaic system in ways that would later be used by Copernicus.

He was a mathematician, a logician, a mineralogist, and the author of the most widely read work of philosophical ethics in the Persian tradition, the Akhlāq-i Nāṣirī (Nasirean Ethics). Al-Ṭūsī's career unfolded under the shadow of the Mongol invasions. He was in his teens when Genghis Khan's armies swept across Central Asia, and he spent much of his early life moving between Ismāʿīlī fortresses in the mountains of northern Iran, serving as a scholar and astrologer for the Ismāʿīlī rulers. When the Mongols under Hülegü destroyed the Ismāʿīlī state and then sacked Baghdad in 1258, al-Ṭūsī had already transferred his services to the new conquerors. He became Hülegü's chief astrologer and adviser, and he persuaded the Mongol ruler to fund the construction of a magnificent observatory — the first institution of its kind, with a library, a staff of astronomers, and precision instruments — at Maragha. He died in Baghdad in 1274, having produced a corpus of work that spanned the full range of the sciences and that shaped the subsequent course of both philosophical and scientific inquiry in Islam. This chapter examines al-Ṭūsī's philosophical theology, his defence of Avicenna, his ethical masterpiece, and his contributions to logic and science. He is the bridge between the classical Avicennan tradition and the later Shīʿī philosophical renaissance, and his synthesis of philosophy, theology, and mysticism set the template for the great Safavid thinkers to come.

1. Tajrīd al-Iʿtiqād: A Philosophical Creed Al-Ṭūsī's Tajrīd al-Iʿtiqād is a concise statement of Twelver Shīʿī theology, but it is a theology written in the language and method of philosophy. Al-Ṭūsī does not begin with scripture, as a traditional theologian might; he begins with ontology. The first section of the Tajrīd is devoted to the most abstract questions of being, existence, essence, and causality. God is introduced as the Necessary Existent, in a direct appropriation of Avicenna's metaphysical proof. The attributes of God — knowledge, power, will, life, speech — are argued for through rational analysis of what the Necessary Existent must be, rather than through citation of scripture. This method was revolutionary in Shīʿī theology. It effectively replaced the Muʿtazilī framework that had dominated earlier Shīʿī kalām with a fully Avicennan metaphysics. Al-Ṭūsī reinterprets Shīʿī doctrines — the Imamate, the infallibility of the Imams, the occultation of the Twelfth Imam — within this philosophical framework, giving them a rational foundation that appealed to the intellect while satisfying the demands of piety. The Tajrīd became the standard theological text in Shīʿī madrasas, and its commentary tradition — spanning centuries and including works by such luminaries as al-Ḥillī, al-Qūshjī, and beyond — is enormous. Al-Ṭūsī's theology is notable for what it does not do. It does not attack the falāsifa; it embraces them. It incorporates the Avicennan doctrine of emanation, the intellects, the Active Intellect, and the soul's intellectual ascent — all doctrines that al-Ghazālī had condemned. Al-Ṭūsī, as a Shīʿī thinker, was not bound by al-Ghazālī's Ashʿarī strictures, and he felt free to integrate philosophy and theology in a way that Sunnī theologians after alGhazālī had been wary of doing. The Tajrīd is a monument to a confident Shīʿī rationalism, a declaration that the Imam's community is the true home of philosophical wisdom.

2. The Defence of Avicenna: Commentary on the Ishārāt Al-Ṭūsī's most important philosophical work is his commentary on Avicenna's al-Ishārāt wa-l-Tanbīhāt. The Ishārāt was the advanced textbook of Avicennan philosophy, a dense and allusive text that required unpacking. A century earlier, Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, the great Sunnī theologian-philosopher, had written a commentary that was in fact a sustained critique of Avicenna's positions. Al-Rāzī had charged Avicenna with inconsistency, question-

begging, and failure to prove his central claims — particularly the proof of the Necessary Existent, the nature of divine knowledge, and the theory of emanation. Al-Ṭūsī undertook to answer al-Rāzī point by point. His commentary — known as the Sharḥ al-Ishārāt — quotes al-Rāzī's objections and then dismantles them, clarifying Avicenna's arguments, distinguishing between their valid and invalid forms, and defending the coherence of the Avicennan system. The Sharḥ al-Ishārāt is not a slavish repetition of Avicenna; al-Ṭūsī sometimes adjusts or improves the arguments. But its overall effect was to rescue the Ishārāt from al-Rāzī's critique and to establish it as the central text of philosophical instruction in the Shīʿī world. For centuries, students of philosophy in Iran studied the Ishārāt through the lens of al-Ṭūsī's commentary, and the debate between al-Rāzī and al-Ṭūsī became a staple of madrasa education. Al-Ṭūsī's defence of Avicenna also included original philosophical contributions. He refined Avicenna's argument for the Necessary Existent, making it more rigorous by explicitly ruling out the possibility of a circle or infinite regress of possible beings. He clarified the concept of the possible in itself and the necessary through another, distinctions that became standard in later Islamic metaphysics. And he addressed the problem of God's knowledge of particulars — one of the three charges al-Ghazālī had brought — arguing that Avicenna's position, properly understood, does not deny God's knowledge of particulars but affirms it in a manner befitting a timeless intellect.

3. Akhlāq-i Nāṣirī: Practical Philosophy Al-Ṭūsī's Akhlāq-i Nāṣirī is the most famous work of philosophical ethics in the Persian language. It was written early in his career, during his time with the Ismāʿīlīs, and its title — "The Nasirean Ethics" — was originally addressed to the Ismāʿīlī governor Nāṣir al-Dīn ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. Al-Ṭūsī later revised it, removing the specifically Ismāʿīlī material and adding a dedication to the Mongol ruler. The Akhlāq-i Nāṣirī is structured in three parts, corresponding to the three domains of practical philosophy as defined by Aristotle: ethics (tahdhīb al-akhlāq), economics or household management (tadbīr al-manzil), and politics (siyāsat al-mudun). The first part, on ethics, is heavily indebted to Ibn Miskawayh's Tahdhīb al-Akhlāq, which al-Ṭūsī reorganises, clarifies, and expands. He presents the tripartite soul, the four cardinal virtues, the doctrine of the mean, the nature of happiness, and the practical discipline of character refinement. But al-Ṭūsī also adds discussions of the Sufi path, the stations of the spiritual journey, and the relationship between philosophical perfection and the Imamate — additions that reflect his Shīʿī and mystical commitments. The second part, on household management, covers the roles and responsibilities of family members, the education of children, the duties of servants, and the practical wisdom of running a household — topics that Ibn Miskawayh had treated only cursorily. The third part, on politics, draws on al-Fārābī's Virtuous City and Ibn Sīnā's political philosophy, but adds discussions of the necessary qualities of the ruler, the types of regimes, and the place of the Shīʿī Imam in the political order. The Akhlāq-i Nāṣirī became a standard text in the Persianate world. It was taught in madrasas from Isfahan to Istanbul to Delhi, and it influenced subsequent works of ethical and political philosophy, including Dawānī's Akhlāq-i Jalālī and Kınalızāde's Akhlāq-i ʿAlāʾī. Its combination of Greek virtue ethics, Islamic piety, and Persian courtly culture produced a distinctively Islamic humanism that shaped the moral education of generations of administrators, scholars, and rulers.

4. Logic, Mathematics, and the Maragha Observatory Al-Ṭūsī's contributions to logic and the mathematical sciences were as significant as his contributions to philosophy and theology. In logic, he wrote extensively on the syllogism, on modal logic, on the distinction between

the subject matter and the principles of a science, and on the relationship between logic and grammar. His logical writings refined the Avicennan system and made it more teachable. In mathematics, his work on the parallel postulate — the problem of whether Euclid's fifth postulate could be proven from the others — was of lasting importance. Al-Ṭūsī's attempted proof, though ultimately unsuccessful, was a major advance, and it influenced later mathematicians in the Islamic world and, through them, European work in non-Euclidean geometry. But al-Ṭūsī's most famous scientific achievement was the Maragha Observatory. Built in 1259, it brought together astronomers and mathematicians from across the Islamic world, including the brilliant Quṭb al-Dīn al-Shīrāzī, and produced the most accurate astronomical tables of the age. The "Maragha School" developed new mathematical models for planetary motion that challenged Ptolemy's system, particularly his use of the equant point, which violated the principle of uniform circular motion. Al-Ṭūsī invented the "Ṭūsī couple" — a mathematical device using two circles to generate linear motion from circular motions — which solved one of the major difficulties in Ptolemaic planetary theory. Versions of this model appear in the work of Copernicus, raising the still-debated question of the Maragha School's influence on the European scientific revolution. Al-Ṭūsī's legacy in science and philosophy illustrates the integration of the sciences and the humanities that was characteristic of the Islamic philosophical tradition. He did not compartmentalise his life into "science" and "philosophy"; for him, the study of the heavens, the refinement of the soul, and the defence of Avicenna were parts of a single project of wisdom. The observatory at Maragha was a philosophical institution as much as a scientific one.

5. Technical Foundations of the Rāzī–Ṭūsī Debate: Modulation of Existence (Tashkīk) and the Rule of the One To understand why Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī's Ḥall Mushkilāt al-Ishārāt (Resolving the Difficulties of the Pointers) and Tajrīd al-Iʿtiqād (The Catharsis of Doctrine) reshaped the entire subsequent history of Islamic metaphysics, we must examine two technical disputes in which al-Ṭūsī answered Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī's skepticism and paved the way for Mullā Ṣadrā:

Univocity (Ishtirāk Maʿnawī) versus Modulation (Tashkīk) of Existence In his works, Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī argued that 'existence' (wujūd) is predicated univocally (bi-l-ishtirāk al-maʿnawī, with a single shared conceptual meaning) of both the Necessary Existent and contingent beings, just as 'nonexistence' (ʿadam) has a single conceptual meaning. Yet if 'existence' has the exact same simple meaning in God and creatures, how can God's existence be identical with His essence while creatures' existence is superadded to their essences? Al-Ṭūsī resolved this dilemma by reviving and formalizing the doctrine of analogical gradation or modulation (al-tashkīk): while the mental concept of existence is shared, existence is not a uniform genus divided evenly among its instances like 'humanity' among Zayd and ʿAmr. Rather, like light or number, existence applies to its instances by priority and posteriority (bi-l-taqaddum wa-l-taʾakhkhur), sufficiency and need (bi-l-ghinā wa-l-faqr), and intensity and weakness (bi-l-shidda wa-l-ḍaʿf). The existence of the Necessary First Cause is self-subsistent, uncaused, and infinitely intense, whereas the existence of contingent things is derivative and dependent. Three and a half centuries later, Mullā Ṣadrā would transfer this Ṭūsian modulation from the concept of existence to the extra-mental reality of existence itself (tashkīk al-wujūd).

Defending the Rule of the One (Qāʿidat al-Wāḥid) Against Theological Voluntarism Against al-Ghazālī's and al-Rāzī's rejection of the Peripatetic axiom 'From the One, insofar as it is utterly one, only one can proceed directly' (al-wāḥid lā yaṣduru ʿanhu illā al-wāḥid), al-Ṭūsī mounted a rigorous modal defense. If a strictly simple, undivided First Cause directly produced two distinct effects A and B without any intermediary, then the aspect (ḥaythiyya) by which It is the source of A must either be identical to or distinct from the aspect by which It is the source of B. If the two aspects are identical, then producing A would be identical to producing B, which is absurd; if the two aspects are distinct within the essence of the First Cause, then the First Cause would be internally composite (murakkab), requiring a prior cause for its composition. Thus, multiplicity can arise in the universe only through the mediated relational aspects (iʿtibārāt) that emerge in the First Intellect when it contemplates its Cause, its own necessity through its Cause, and its intrinsic contingency in itself.

From Marāgha to Isfahan: ʿAllāma al-Ḥillī and the Fifteenth–Sixteenth-Century School of Shiraz Between al-Ṭūsī's death in 1274 and Mīr Dāmād's rise in late sixteenth-century Isfahan, the torch of philosophical theology and metaphysics was carried forward first by al-Ṭūsī's brilliant Twelver Shīʿī student al-ʿAllāma Jamāl al-Dīn al-Ḥasan ibn Yūsuf ibn al-Muṭahhar al-Ḥillī (1250–1325) — whose commentary on his teacher's Tajrīd al-Iʿtiqād (Kashf al-Murād fī Sharḥ Tajrīd al-Iʿtiqād) canonized Avicennan-Ṭūsian modal metaphysics in Shīʿī seminary education — and then by the vibrant School of Shiraz in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries: Jalāl al-Dīn al-Dawānī (1426–1502): Chief scholar of Aq Qoyunlu Shiraz and author of the famous Persian ethical manual Akhlāq-i Jalālī, the Illuminationist commentary Shawākil al-Ḥūr fī Sharḥ Hayākil al-Nūr, and the Old and New Glosses on al-Ṭūsī's Tajrīd. Blending Avicennan demonstration, Illuminationism, and Akbarian mysticism under the banner of the 'Tasting of Theosophy' (dhawq al-taʾalluh), al-Dawānī argued that only God is a true, real individual instance (fard ḥaqīqī) of absolute Being (al-wujūd al-muṭlaq), whereas contingent quiddities are merely related to Being ('existent,' mawjūd) by an accidental connection (intisāb). Ṣadr al-Dīn al-Dashtakī (1425–1498) and Ghiyāth al-Dīn Manṣūr al-Dashtakī (d. 1542): Al-Dawānī's formidable Shirazi rivals. Across decades of fierce public disputation and competing super-glosses (ṭabaqāt aljalāliyya wa-l-ṣadriyya) that were studied from Istanbul to Mughal Delhi, Ṣadr al-Dīn and his son Ghiyāth alDīn debated al-Dawānī on mental existence (al-wujūd al-dhihnī), the modulation of existence, and above all the Liar Paradox (shubhat al-jadhr al-aṣamm, 'the Paradox of the Mute Root' — 'Everything I say at this moment is false'), developing sophisticated semantic distinctions between object-language and metalanguage. Shams al-Dīn Muḥammad ibn Aḥmad al-Khafrī (d. 1535): A pupil of Ṣadr al-Dīn al-Dashtakī in Shiraz and Kashan, al-Khafrī united advanced mathematical astronomy (al-Takmila fī Sharḥ al-Tadhkira, demonstrating that multiple non-Ptolemaic planetary models can be mathematically equivalent representations of the same physical reality) with deep glosses on the Ilāhiyyāt of al-Tajrīd, directly shaping the philosophical curriculum inherited by Mīr Dāmād and Mullā Ṣadrā.

6. The Bridge to the School of Isfahan Al-Ṭūsī died in 1274, having absorbed the shock of the Mongol conquest and having succeeded in transplanting the philosophical tradition into the new political order. His synthesis of Avicennan metaphysics, Shīʿī theology, and practical ethics became the intellectual foundation for the later development of philosophy in Iran. His students — most notably al-Ḥillī and Quṭb al-Dīn al-Shīrāzī — carried his legacy forward, commenting on his works, extending his arguments, and spreading his influence through the madrasas of Iraq and Persia.

But the full flowering of the Shīʿī philosophical renaissance would come three centuries later, under the Safavid dynasty, when a new generation of thinkers — Mīr Dāmād, Shaykh Bahāʾī, and above all Mullā Ṣadrā — would build on al-Ṭūsī's foundations to create what they called the "Transcendent Philosophy" (al-Ḥikma al-Mutaʿāliya). The next chapter turns to the first of these Safavid philosophers, Mīr Dāmād, the founder of the School of Isfahan, and his doctrine of eternal origination.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “What the eminent commentator [Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī] has practiced in his commentary on the Pointers (alIshārāt) is not commentary at all, but demolition and calumny (jarḥ); for we have demonstrated that 'existence' (al-wujūd) is predicated of its instances not by uniform equality like a generic quiddity, but by analogical gradation (bi-l-tashkīk) in priority and posteriority, necessity and contingency, and intensity and weakness.” — Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī, Ḥall Mushkilāt al-Ishārāt (Resolving the Difficulties of Avicenna's Pointers)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How did Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī's point-by-point response to Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī in Ḥall Mushkilāt al-Ishārāt rescue the Avicennan philosophical system in the thirteenth century and establish the Rāzī–Ṭūsī debate as the core of the later madrasa curriculum? 2. Explain al-Ṭūsī's theory of the 'analogical modulation of existence' (tashkīk al-wujūd). How does it solve alRāzī's dilemma regarding how 'existence' can have a single conceptual meaning (ishtirāk maʿnawī) across both God and contingent creatures? 3. Why is al-Ṭūsī's Tajrīd al-Iʿtiqād (The Catharsis of Doctrine) considered a watershed text in the history of Shīʿī thought, and why did both Sunnī scholars (such as al-Qūshjī, al-Dawānī, and al-Jurjānī) and Shīʿī scholars (such as al-Ḥillī and al-Lāhījī) write major commentaries on its ontological chapters? 4. How did al-Ṭūsī expand Ibn Miskawayh's individual virtue ethics into a comprehensive tripartite practical philosophy — ethics (akhlāq), household economics (tadbīr-i manzil), and political philosophy (siyāsat-i mudun) — in the Akhlāq-i Nāṣirī?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Al-Ṭūsī, Naṣīr al-Dīn. The Nasirean Ethics (Akhlāq-i Nāṣirī). Translated by G. M. Wickens. London: George Allen & Unwin, 1964. Al-Ṭūsī, Naṣīr al-Dīn. Contemplation and Action: The Spiritual Autobiography of a Muslim Scholar (Sayr wa-Sulūk). Edited and translated by S. J. Badakhchani. London: I.B. Tauris / Institute of Ismaili Studies, 1998. Chapter 22Mīr Dāmād and the School of Isfahan

The Philosopher of Eternal Origination

Sayyid Muḥammad Bāqir ibn Muḥammad al-Dāmād (c. 1561–1631), known as Mīr Dāmād, was the first great philosopher of the Safavid renaissance and the teacher of the greatest Persian philosopher of them all, Mullā Ṣadrā. He was born in Astarābād in northern Iran (hence his nisba al-Astarābādī) and spent his formative youth studying in Mashhad as the maternal grandson of the great Safavid jurist al-Muḥaqqiq al-Thānī Shaykh ʿAlī al-Karakī (d. 1534, whence his father's title Dāmād, "Son-in-Law") and the pupil of his maternal uncle Shaykh ʿAbd al-ʿĀlī alKarakī (d. 1585), before residing in Qazvin and Kashan. After Shāh ʿAbbās I transferred the Safavid capital to Isfahan in 1598, Mīr Dāmād settled in the new imperial metropolis and became the dean of the circle of scholars, jurists, poets, and mystics that made seventeenth-century Isfahan one of the intellectual wonders of the early modern world. Mīr Dāmād was a jurist, a theologian, and a poet in Persian and Arabic, but his enduring fame rests on his attempts to resolve one of the most intractable problems of Islamic philosophy: the relationship between the eternal God and the temporal world. The problem was this. Avicenna had argued that God, the Necessary Existent, is eternal and unchanging, and that His creative act must therefore be eternal. If God created the world in time, that would imply a change in God — a decision, a transition from not-creating to creating — which contradicts divine simplicity. Yet the Qur'an and the theological tradition insisted that the world had a temporal beginning. Al-Ghazālī had charged the philosophers with heresy on precisely this point. Avicenna's position — eternal creation — seemed to many to be a denial of the scriptural truth. Mīr Dāmād proposed a startlingly original solution. He argued for a third category between eternity and temporality, which he called ḥudūth dahrī — "eternal origination" or "atemporal origination." His doctrine is dense and technical, but it transformed the metaphysical landscape of Safavid philosophy and set the stage for Mullā Ṣadrā's even more radical breakthroughs. This chapter examines Mīr Dāmād's metaphysics, his synthesis of Avicennism and Illuminationism, and his role as the founder of the School of Isfahan.

1. Time, Eternity, and Ḥudūth Dahrī To understand Mīr Dāmād, one must first understand the three ontological categories he deployed: sarmad (pure eternity), dahr (atemporal duration), and zamān (time). Sarmad is the mode of being of God. It is pure, unchanging eternity, without before or after, without succession or change. God exists in sarmad, and His existence is identical with His essence. Nothing is prior to God, and God is prior to everything else, but this priority is not temporal; it is ontological. Zamān is ordinary time — the succession of moments that we experience in the physical world, the beforeand-after of motion and change. Time, for Mīr Dāmād, is a feature of the material universe. It is measured by the movement of the celestial spheres, and it applies only to bodies in motion. Dahr is the intermediate plane. It is the mode of being of the immaterial intellects — the separate intelligences that emanate from God and govern the cosmos. Dahr is not temporal; it has no before or after in the physical sense. But it is not purely eternal in the way God is eternal, because the intellects are caused beings, and their mode of being involves a kind of priority and posteriority that is not time. Mīr Dāmād describes dahr as the "container" of the intellectual world, just as time is the "container" of the physical world.

Mīr Dāmād's central claim is that the world — meaning the chain of intellects, souls, and celestial bodies — is muḥdath dahrī (originated in perpetuitas / atemporal duration): it comes into existence after real non-existence in the atemporal mode of dahr, rather than in measurable physical time (zamān). This means that the world is coeternal with God (in the sense that God never existed without the world), but the world is also originated (because it depends entirely on God for its being and is posterior to God ontologically). The world has no beginning in time; it has a beginning in dahr, which is a kind of origination that does not involve temporal succession. This was a brilliant attempt to have it both ways: to preserve the Avicennan eternity of creation (and thus protect God's immutability) while affirming that the world is originated and dependent (and thus satisfying the theological demand). Mīr Dāmād's formulation was so subtle and original that it became known as the "third path" between the theologians and the Peripatetics. It did not convince everyone — his own student Mullā Ṣadrā would eventually reject it — but it established Mīr Dāmād as the most original metaphysician of his generation.

2. Synthesis of Avicennism and Illuminationism Mīr Dāmād was an Avicennan in his basic philosophical commitments. He revered Avicenna and wrote commentaries on his works. But he was also deeply influenced by Suhrawardī's Illuminationist philosophy, and his writings are a sustained attempt to synthesise the two traditions. He called his philosophy al-ḥikma al-yamāniyya — "Yemeni wisdom," a term that alluded not to tribal ancestry (Mīr Dāmād was a Ḥusaynī Sayyid from Astarābād, descendant of the Prophet through Imām al-Ḥusayn), but to the celebrated Prophetic hadith "Faith is Yemeni and wisdom is Yemeni" (al-īmān yamān wa-l-ḥikma yamāniyya) and to a rich symbolic philology: in Arabic, Yaman shares its root with yumn (blessing/felicity) and al-ayman (the right-hand side — the side of the blessed in the Qur'an and the direction from which Moses perceived the sacred fire on the "right slope of the Mount," al-jānib alayman). Facing the rising sun of the sacred East (al-mashriq, the fountainhead of Suhrawardī's Illumination), the south/Yemen lies on the right hand of light and prophetic revelation (al-ḥikma al-yamāniyya al-īmāniyya), in contrast to the occident (al-maghrib) of purely discursive Greek Peripateticism. From Avicenna, Mīr Dāmād took the framework of the Necessary Existent, the distinction between existence and essence, the emanation of intellects, and the rational method of demonstration. From Suhrawardī, he took the language of light and illumination, the theory of knowledge by presence, the importance of imaginal experience, and the conviction that philosophy must include spiritual practice. Mīr Dāmād believed that the Peripatetic and Illuminationist methods were complementary, not opposed. The philosopher begins with reason and demonstration (baḥth) but must ascend to direct vision (dhawq) through purification and spiritual discipline. The perfect sage, for Mīr Dāmād, is both a master of syllogism and a friend of God. This synthesis produced a distinctive philosophical style — learned, allusive, mystical, and difficult. Mīr Dāmād wrote in a dense and often obscure Arabic that assumed the reader was already steeped in both the Avicennan and the Illuminationist traditions. His works demand patience and initiation. But for those who persevere, they offer a unified vision of the cosmos as a living chain of intellects, radiating from the divine light and calling the soul back to its luminous origin.

3. The "Seal of the Philosophers" and the Circle of Isfahan Mīr Dāmād was known to his contemporaries as the "Third Teacher" (after Aristotle and al-Fārābī) and as the "Seal of the Philosophers" (khātam al-ḥukamāʾ). The latter title was not meant to suggest that philosophy was ending with him — quite the contrary — but that he embodied the culmination of the previous traditions and the synthesis of their wisdom. His students referred to him as "the teacher" (al-muʿallim) without qualification, and his influence in Safavid Isfahan was enormous.

Mīr Dāmād was at the centre of a remarkable intellectual circle that included Shaykh Bahāʾ al-Dīn al-ʿĀmilī (Shaykh Bahāʾī), the polymath theologian, poet, and architect who designed much of the city of Isfahan; Sayyid Aḥmad al-ʿAlawī al-ʿĀmilī (d. 1644/1650), Mīr Dāmād's cousin, son-in-law, and foremost commentator (author of Sharḥ al-Qabasāt, the Avicennan commentary Miftāḥ al-Shifāʾ, and the interfaith philosophical apology Miṣqal-i Ṣafā); along with Mīr Abū al-Qāsim Findiriskī (d. 1640) and a host of poets, calligraphers, and mystics. This circle made Isfahan a crucible of philosophical creativity, where the worlds of the madrasa and the Sufi lodge, the court and the library, met and fertilised each other. Among Mīr Dāmād's students was a brilliant young Shīrāzī named Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm, later known as Mullā Ṣadrā. Mullā Ṣadrā studied with Mīr Dāmād, absorbed his synthesis of Avicennism and Illuminationism, and then went beyond it. He would reject his master's doctrine of ḥudūth dahrī and propose a far more radical theory — the primacy of existence over essence and the idea of substantial motion — that would give birth to the school of Transcendent Philosophy. Mīr Dāmād, the founder of the School of Isfahan, was thus the immediate intellectual progenitor of the greatest philosopher of later Islam.

4. Mīr Dāmād's Legacy Mīr Dāmād died in 1631, while accompanying Shāh Ṣafī on a pilgrimage to Najaf. His works continued to be studied in the Shīʿī madrasas, but they were eventually overshadowed by the more powerful and systematic works of his student Mullā Ṣadrā. The subtlety of Mīr Dāmād's metaphysics made his writings difficult, and Mullā Ṣadrā's critiques were often seen as decisive. Yet Mīr Dāmād's thought has never lost its place entirely. In recent decades, there has been a revival of interest in his works, as philosophers seek to understand the full range of intellectual options available in the Safavid period and to recover the roots of later developments. Mīr Dāmād represents the mature flowering of the Islamic philosophical tradition in its Shīʿī form — a tradition that had absorbed Peripatetic logic, Avicennan metaphysics, Illuminationist vision, and Sufi spirituality, and that was producing thinkers of remarkable originality. His doctrine of eternal origination, whatever its ultimate philosophical merits, was a daring attempt to solve a problem that had bedevilled the tradition for half a millennium. And his role as the teacher of Mullā Ṣadrā ensures that his name will always be linked to the most glorious moment of the later philosophical renaissance.

5. Inside Kitāb al-Qabasāt: Why Mīr Dāmād Defended Aṣālat al-Māhiyya and Real Priority in Dahr In his masterwork Kitāb al-Qabasāt (The Book of Blazing Brands) and its companion al-Ufuq al-Mubīn (The Clear Horizon), Mīr Dāmād did not merely coin a poetic phrase with ḥudūth dahrī ('atemporal origination'); he constructed an intricate modal ontology grounded in the primacy of quiddity (aṣālat al-māhiyya) and a fierce critique of both theological 'imaginary time' (zamān mawhūm) and Peripatetic essential causation. Why did Mīr Dāmād find Avicenna's and al-Ṭūsī's doctrine of merely 'essential origination' (ḥudūth dhātī) theologically and philosophically inadequate? According to Avicenna, the world's contingency means only that its essence requires a cause, while its non-existence precedes its existence 'by essence' (bi-l-dhāt), never in actual reality (fī al-wāqiʿ / fī nafs al-amr), since the world coexists with God throughout all eternity. Mīr Dāmād argued that a 'non-existence' that is strictly simultaneous with existence in actual reality is a merely conceptual figment (amr iʿtibārī) that cannot ground true creaturely origination: if a thing has always existed alongside God without ever actually being preceded by real non-existence (ʿadam ṣarīḥ), it is not genuinely brought into being (majʿūl).

Conversely, Mīr Dāmād rejected the kalām view that before creating the physical cosmos, God waited through an infinite stretch of 'imaginary time' (zamān mawhūm), since time is an accident of physical motion and cannot exist prior to bodies. Instead, in the container of dahr (atemporal duration, which surrounds all immaterial intellects and the totality of physical time), the entire created order is preceded by an actual, real non-existence (al-ʿadam al-ṣarīḥ al-dahrī) — an ontological chasm or 'anterior rupture' (infikāk dahrī) that is neither measurable by clock-time (zamān) nor reducible to a mere mental comparison (dhāt). Furthermore, holding that existence is an abstract concept while concrete quiddities (māhiyyāt) are what the Creator directly makes (al-majʿūl bi-l-dhāt), Mīr Dāmād argued that the Maker brings the essence itself out of real atemporal non-existence into dahr. Alongside Mīr Dāmād and Shaykh Bahāʾī, the broader School of Isfahan encompassed strikingly diverse philosophical temperaments: Mīr Abū al-Qāsim Findiriskī (d. 1640), an ascetic Avicennan philosopher who traveled extensively in Mughal India, studied Sanskrit texts and the Yoga-Vāsiṣṭha, and wrote a celebrated philosophical ode (Qaṣīda-yi Ṣināʿiyya) alongside a treatise on motion (Risāla-yi Ṣināʿiyya); and Mullā Rajab ʿAlī Tabrīzī (d. 1669), who rejected Mullā Ṣadrā's primacy and modulation of existence to found a rival Safavid school defending the equivocity of existence (al-ishtirāk al-lafẓī li-l-wujūd) and the primacy of essence.

6. The Stage for Transcendent Philosophy With Mīr Dāmād, the School of Isfahan takes its place as a major chapter in the history of Islamic philosophy. The tradition has now passed through its Peripatetic synthesis, its mystical and Illuminationist turns, its engagement with the challenges of al-Ghazālī and the responses of Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī and Ibn Rushd, the philosophy of history of Ibn Khaldūn, and the theological-philosophical integration of al-Ṭūsī. It is now poised for its greatest later synthesis — the Transcendent Philosophy of Mullā Ṣadrā, who will take up the primacy of existence, the doctrine of substantial motion, and the unity of the knower and the known, and who will claim to have achieved what Avicenna hinted at, Suhrawardī envisioned, and Ibn ʿArabī described: a unified vision of being, knowledge, and salvation. The next chapter turns to this culminating figure, the greatest philosopher of later Islam, whose works are still taught and debated in the madrasas of Qom and beyond, and who represents the fullest expression of the wisdom tradition that we have been tracing through the centuries.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “The container of the unchanging Creator with the unchanging is pure eternity (al-sarmad); the container of the unchanging intelligible order with the changing corporeal order — preceded by real, explicit non-existence in the fact-of-the-matter — is atemporal duration (al-dahr); and the container of the changing physical order with the changing is measurable time (al-zamān). Thus the entire cosmos is originated in atemporal duration (muḥdath dahrī), neither preceded by imaginary physical time nor merely contingent by conceptual comparison.” — Mīr Muḥammad Bāqir al-Dāmād, Kitāb al-Qabasāt (The Book of Blazing Brands)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. Define Mīr Dāmād's three ontological containers of reality: sarmad (pure eternity), dahr (atemporal duration / perpetuitas), and zamān (measurable physical time). Which level of existence corresponds to each container? 2. Why did Mīr Dāmād reject both Avicenna's doctrine of merely 'essential origination' (ḥudūth dhātī) and the kalām theologians' doctrine of 'temporal origination' (ḥudūth zamānī) preceded by 'imaginary time' (zamān mawhūm)?

3. How does Mīr Dāmād's commitment to the 'primacy of quiddity' (aṣālat al-māhiyya) support his theory of atemporal origination (ḥudūth dahrī) in Kitāb al-Qabasāt, and what did he mean by calling his synthesis 'Yemeni Wisdom' (al-ḥikma al-yamāniyya)? 4. Compare the philosophical approaches of Mīr Dāmād, Shaykh Bahāʾī, Mīr Findiriskī, and Mullā Rajab ʿAlī Tabrīzī within the seventeenth-century School of Isfahan.

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Mīr Dāmād, Muḥammad Bāqir. Kitāb al-Qabasāt (The Book of Blazing Brands). Chapter 23Mullā Ṣadrā: Transcendent Philosophy

The Sage of Shīrāz

Ṣadr al-Dīn Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Qawāmī al-Shīrāzī (1571–1635/1636, traditionally 1640), known universally as Mullā Ṣadrā and honored in the seminary tradition as Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn ('Foremost of the Theosophers'), is the culminating figure of the later Islamic philosophical tradition — a thinker whose ambition, originality, and sheer synthetic power place him in the company of Avicenna and al-Fārābī. He was born in Shīrāz, studied in Isfahan under Mīr Dāmād and Shaykh Bahāʾī, and then withdrew for a period of rigorous spiritual retreat in the village of Kahak, near Qom. During this seclusion, he experienced the visionary openings that, he claimed, gave him the keys to the deepest mysteries of metaphysics. When he returned to public life, he began to write the works that would transform the philosophical landscape of Shīʿī Islam. His magnum opus, al-Ḥikma al-Mutaʿāliya fī al-Asfār al-ʿAqliyya al-Arbaʿa (The Transcendent Philosophy in the Four Intellectual Journeys), is a vast summa in which the entire previous tradition — Peripatetic, Illuminationist, Akbarian, and theological — is subsumed, critiqued, and raised to a new synthesis. Mullā Ṣadrā called his system Transcendent Philosophy (al-Ḥikma al-Mutaʿāliya). By this he meant a philosophy that transcends the limitations of both pure reason and pure mysticism, a wisdom that unites discursive proof (burhān), spiritual vision (ʿirfān), and the Qur'anic revelation (qurʾān) in a single coherent whole. The word "transcendent" also signals his most fundamental metaphysical commitment: the primacy of existence (aṣālat alwujūd) — the doctrine that what is real is not the static essences of things but the dynamic, graded, flowing reality of existence itself. This chapter examines the core doctrines of Mullā Ṣadrā's system: the primacy of existence, substantial motion, the unity of the knower, the known, and knowledge, and the soul's journey from matter to God.

1. The Primacy of Existence The foundational move of Mullā Ṣadrā's philosophy is his resolution of the debate between the essentialists and the existentialists. Suhrawardī had argued that existence is a mere mental concept, and that the real constituents of reality are essences — quiddities. Avicenna, while treating existence as something real added to essence, had not fully clarified the ontological priority. Mullā Ṣadrā cuts through the debate with a single, revolutionary thesis: existence is the primary reality, and essences are nothing but the limitations and determinations that existence assumes. Existence (wujūd), for Mullā Ṣadrā, is not a property that things have; it is the very act of being by which things are. It is a dynamic, flowing, self-manifesting reality. Essences — what we call "humanity," "horse," "stone" — are not real entities standing alongside existence; they are the boundaries, the modes, the particular delimitations that the single ocean of existence takes. Existence is like light; essences are like the coloured panes of glass through which the light shines, producing different colours without the light itself being anything other than light. The primacy of existence means that the universe is not a collection of fixed kinds but a single, continuous, graded manifestation of being. This doctrine has the immediate consequence that God — who is pure existence, without any limiting essence — is not merely the highest being but the very ground and reality of all being. The entire cosmos is God's existence manifesting itself in degrees of intensity and limitation. This is not pantheism, for Mullā Ṣadrā carefully maintains that God's essence remains absolutely transcendent and that the limited existences of creatures are not identical with God's infinite existence. But it is a radical statement of divine immanence: everything that exists does so only by participating, in a limited mode, in the single reality that God is. The relationship between God and the world is that of the infinite to its own finite modalities.

2. Substantial Motion The second pillar of Mullā Ṣadrā's system is the doctrine of substantial motion (al-ḥaraka al-jawhariyya). Aristotle and the entire Peripatetic tradition had held that change occurs only in the categories of quantity, quality, and place — accidents. The substance of a thing — its fundamental nature — was thought to be stable and unchanging; a horse does not become a dog, an acorn is still an oak in substance even if it grows in size. Change was accidental, not substantial. Mullā Ṣadrā denies this. He argues that substance itself is in constant, continuous motion. Nothing in the universe remains the same for two instants. Every being is a flowing, evolving reality, transforming from lower to higher, from less intense existence to more intense existence. The acorn is not a static substance that happens to acquire the accidents of size and shape; it is a single continuum of existence that moves from the level of mineral, through the level of plant, to the level of tree, and eventually, through consumption and transformation, to the level of animal and human. The entire natural world is a river of substantial change. This motion is not chaotic; it is teleological. Every being moves toward its perfection, toward a fuller and more intense realisation of existence. The universe is a graduated ascent from the dimmest existence of prime matter to the luminous existence of the celestial intellects and finally to the infinite intensity of the Necessary Existent. Mullā Ṣadrā calls this the tashkīk al-wujūd — the systematic ambiguity or gradation of existence. Existence is not a univocal concept; it is a reality that varies in intensity, like light that can be bright or dim, but is everywhere the same principle. The hierarchy of the cosmos is not a ladder of different kinds of being but a single continuum of existence intensifying and perfecting itself.

Substantial motion also solves a problem that bedevilled earlier philosophers: how does something new come into being? If substance is stable, then the emergence of a new form — a new species, a new soul — requires a miraculous intervention. For Mullā Ṣadrā, the new emerges naturally because the substance itself is always in the act of becoming. The soul, as we shall see, is not a static substance added to the body; it is the body's own existence refined and intensified to the level of intellect.

3. The Unity of the Knower, the Known, and Knowledge Mullā Ṣadrā's epistemology is a direct consequence of his metaphysics of existence. Knowledge, for him, is a mode of existence. When I know something, my existence becomes united with the existence of the object in a special way. The knower, the known, and the act of knowing are not three separate things; they are three aspects of a single reality. Knowledge is not the representation of an object by a mental image; it is the presence of the known to the knower, a union of existences. This doctrine has radical implications. It means that all knowledge is a form of self-knowledge, because to know something is to actualise its existence within one's own being. The soul, in the act of knowing, becomes what it knows. When I contemplate a geometric theorem, my intellect becomes that theorem in an intelligible mode. When I know God, my soul participates — in its finite way — in the divine existence that is its ultimate object. The goal of philosophy is not to acquire correct propositions but to become more existent, to intensify one's being, to unify oneself with the highest intelligible realities. The intellect, for Mullā Ṣadrā, is not a passive receiver of forms but an active, creative principle. The human soul, through its intellectual activity, elevates itself through the degrees of existence. It begins at the level of the senses, rises through the imagination, and culminates in the pure intellect that is one with the Active Intellect — and ultimately, in the state of the perfected sage, one with the divine reality itself. This ascent is the existential meaning of the Qur'anic journey to God. Mullā Ṣadrā reads the miʿrāj of the Prophet, the Hadith of the Hidden Treasure, and the verses on the soul's return as descriptions of the very process he has mapped in his metaphysics.

4. The Soul's Journey: Origination from Matter, Subsistence in Spirit Mullā Ṣadrā's psychology is built on the principle of substantial motion. The human soul, he argues, is not an immaterial substance that descends into the body and exists independently from the start, as Plato and the earlier theologians had held. It is brought into existence with the body and is at first a thoroughly material and bodily thing. But through its own developmental motion, it gradually actualises higher levels of being — vegetative, animal, and finally intellectual. The soul, in its essence, is a trajectory, a becoming. This means, critically, that the soul is not a pre-existing entity trapped in matter; it genuinely emerges from matter through a process of existential intensification. The body is the soil in which the soul grows. At the moment of birth, the soul is merely a potentiality; through sensation, imagination, and intellectual effort, it becomes actual and eventually immaterial. By the time of death, the soul has — or has not — reached a level of independence from the body. The soul that has actualised its intellect becomes a self-subsistent immaterial substance capable of surviving the dissolution of the body. The soul that has not achieved this maturity remains bound to the material world and undergoes a kind of purgatorial refinement. Mullā Ṣadrā's eschatology thus becomes a natural account of the afterlife. Hell is not an arbitrary punishment but the soul's inevitable state of deprivation, its clinging to bodily attachments that can no longer be satisfied. Heaven is the soul's natural condition of intellectual illumination and union with the divine. The bodily resurrection is real, but it occurs in the imaginal body — a subtle body made of the soul's own imaginative forms — rather than in the

gross physical body. This preserves the literal truth of the Qur'anic descriptions while giving them a philosophically coherent interpretation.

5. Qur'anic Hermeneutics and the Synthesis of the Three Wisdoms Mullā Ṣadrā believed that the Qur'an contains, in symbolic and allusive form, the deepest truths of philosophy. His major works contain extensive Qur'anic exegesis, and he wrote independent commentaries on several chapters and verses. His method is to read the Qur'an alongside the works of Avicenna, Suhrawardī, Ibn ʿArabī, and the Shīʿī Imams, showing that all these sources converge on a single truth. The Triunity of Mullā Ṣadrā's method — burhān (demonstrative proof), ʿirfān (mystical unveiling), and qurʾān (scripture) — is not a patchwork but a unity. Philosophy without spirituality is blind; spirituality without philosophy is prone to error; both require the divine guidance of revelation. The perfect sage, for Mullā Ṣadrā, is the one who has integrated all three, who can demonstrate the truth by syllogism, taste it in ecstatic vision, and find it confirmed in the holy text. This integration is the meaning of "transcendent philosophy." He offered particularly profound interpretations of the Light Verse, the Throne Verse, and the Sura of the Resurrection, reading them as expositions of the primacy of existence, substantial motion, and the soul's return. His hermeneutics is a model of philosophically informed scriptural reading that has influenced Shīʿī thought to the present day.

6. The Ṣadrian Legacy Mullā Ṣadrā died in Basra (in 1045 AH / 1635–1636 CE according to manuscript colophons established by modern scholarship, or 1050 AH / 1640 CE in traditional biographies) while making his seventh pilgrimage on foot to Mecca. His immediate legacy was carried forward by his two eminent sons-in-law, Mullā Muḥsin Fayḍ Kāshānī (1598– 1680) and ʿAbd al-Razzāq al-Lāhījī (d. 1662), alongside the Neoplatonic-Ṣadrian thinker Qāḍī Saʿīd Qummī (1639– 1696), and was revived in the late eighteenth and nineteenth centuries by Mullā ʿAlī Nūrī (d. 1831) in Isfahan, Mullā Hādī Sabzavārī (1797–1873) in Sabzavār, and the School of Tehran led by Mullā ʿAbd Allāh Zunūzī (d. 1841) and his son Āqā ʿAlī Mudarris Ṭihrānī (1818–1889, author of Badāyiʿ al-Ḥikam). By the nineteenth century, Sabzavārī's versified summary of Ṣadrian philosophy, the Manẓūma, was a standard textbook in Shīʿī madrasas, and Mullā Ṣadrā's influence was pervasive. In the twentieth century, the great Iranian philosopher ʿAllāma Ṭabāṭabāʾī revived the study of Mullā Ṣadrā and introduced his thought to a new generation. Through Ṭabāṭabāʾī and his students, Mullā Ṣadrā has become one of the most studied philosophers in contemporary Iran, and his works are being translated, debated, and applied to contemporary philosophical problems — from the nature of existence to the philosophy of mind to the dialogue between Islam and the West. Mullā Ṣadrā's achievement is a monument to the vitality of the philosophical tradition in Islam. Far from dying after al-Ghazālī or Averroes, the tradition produced, in the seventeenth century, a thinker of world-historical stature who reorganized metaphysics around the dynamic reality of existence and offered a comprehensive account of being, knowledge, and the soul that remains powerful and challenging today.

7. Mapping the Four Intellectual Journeys (al-Asfār al-Arbaʿa) and the Rule of Simple Reality Mullā Ṣadrā's magnum opus, al-Ḥikma al-Mutaʿāliya fī al-Asfār al-ʿAqliyya al-Arbaʿa (The Transcendent Philosophy concerning the Four Intellectual Journeys of the Soul), is organized not according to the traditional Aristotelian

division of physics and metaphysics, but according to the four stages of the philosophical-spiritual traveler's (sālik) ontological transformation: 1. The First Journey — From Creation to the Truth (Min al-Khalq ilā al-Ḥaqq): Passing through the veils of sensory multiplicity and conceptual quiddities to arrive at the Primacy, Unity, and Gradation of Existence (aṣālat wa-waḥdat wa-tashkīk al-wujūd). This section constitutes Mullā Ṣadrā's general ontology (al-umūr alʿāmma), covering existence, mental existence, contingency, necessity, causality, and substantial motion (alḥaraka al-jawhariyya). 2. The Second Journey — By the Truth within the Truth (Bi-l-Ḥaqq fī al-Ḥaqq): Contemplating the concrete determinations of being and the Aristotelian categories (substance, quantity, quality, relation, matter, and form) in light of substantial motion, establishing how the physical cosmos is continually renewed at every instant. 3. The Third Journey — From the Truth to Creation with the Truth (Min al-Ḥaqq ilā al-Khalq bi-l-Ḥaqq): Special Metaphysics (al-ilāhiyyāt bi-l-maʿnā al-akhaṣṣ), proving the existence and absolute simplicity of the Necessary Existent, the divine attributes of Knowledge, Power, and Will, divine providence, and the emanation of the intelligible and imaginal worlds. 4. The Fourth Journey — With the Truth in Creation (Fī al-Khalq bi-l-Ḥaqq): Philosophical anthropology, psychology, and eschatology ('the science of the soul and the Return,' ʿilm al-nafs wa-l-maʿād), tracing the soul as 'bodily in origination and spiritual in survival' (jismāniyyat al-ḥudūth wa-rūḥāniyyat al-baqāʾ) through its sensory, imaginal, and intellectual stations to the bodily resurrection.

The Rule of Simple Reality (Qāʿidat Basīṭ al-Ḥaqīqa) and the Eleven Principles of Bodily Resurrection To reconcile the absolute unity of God with the multiplicity of His attributes and creatures without falling into either dualism or illusionism, Mullā Ṣadrā formulates his celebrated Rule of Simple Reality (qāʿidat basīṭ alḥaqīqa kull al-ashyāʾ wa-laysa bi-shayʾ minhā): 'That which is utterly simple in its reality is, by virtue of its unity, all things, while being none of them in their finite privations and boundaries.' Because the Necessary Existent is pure, unconditioned Existence (ṣirf al-wujūd) unmixed with any limiting quiddity (māhiyya) or non-existence (ʿadam), nothing positive in any creature's existence can be absent from Him; if any perfection of being were absent from the Simple Reality, its essence would be compounded of existence and privation, contradicting its absolute simplicity. Finally, in the Fourth Journey and in al-Ḥikma al-ʿArshiyya (The Wisdom of the Throne), Mullā Ṣadrā lays down eleven ontological principles to solve the problem that had defeated Avicenna in the Tahāfut debate: bodily resurrection (al-maʿād al-jismānī). Because the identity of a human body depends not on its transient, decomposing elemental matter (which changes continuously throughout earthly life via substantial motion), but on its enduring formal soul (al-nafs al-nāṭiqa), and because the perfected soul possesses a creative imaginative power (quwwat al-khayāl al-munfaṣil) ontological in nature, at death the soul projects its own real, subtle imaginal body (al-jism al-mithālī / al-ukhrawī). This resurrected body is identical in personal form to the earthly body — explaining the literal truth of Qur'anic descriptions of Paradise and Hell — yet free from elemental corruption.

8. The Post-Ṣadrian Tradition Mullā Ṣadrā's death was not the end of the tradition but a new beginning. The subsequent centuries saw the consolidation of his school, its spread to India and beyond, and its continuing vitality in the face of modern

challenges. The next chapter traces the course of Post-Ṣadrian thought — the systematisation of Sabzavārī, the philosophical renaissance in Qom, the Ottoman philosophical tradition that sustained the Avicennan-Rāzian heritage in the Sunnī world, and the continuing legacy of Transcendent Philosophy into the contemporary era.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “In earlier days I used to be a passionate defender of the primacy of quiddity (aṣālat al-māhiyya) and the purely conceptual status of existence, following the Shaykh of Illumination [Suhrawardī] and my revered teacher [Mīr Dāmād], until my Lord guided me to the luminous demonstration that Existence (al-wujūd) is the sole fundamental reality (aṣīl) in the external world, that it is a single reality graded by intensity and weakness (tashkīk), and that quiddities are nothing but the mental silhouettes and boundaries of specific degrees of existence.” — Mullā Ṣadrā (Ṣadr al-Dīn al-Shīrāzī), Al-Ḥikma al-Mutaʿāliya fī al-Asfār al-ʿAqliyya al-Arbaʿa (The Four Journeys, I.1)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. What proofs does Mullā Ṣadrā advance in the First Journey of the Asfār and in Kitāb al-Mashāʿir to overturn his teacher Mīr Dāmād's position and establish the Primacy of Existence (aṣālat al-wujūd) over quiddity? 2. Explain how Mullā Ṣadrā's doctrine of the Analogical Gradation of Existence (tashkīk al-wujūd) allows him to reconcile the unity of being (waḥdat al-wujūd) with the real multiplicity of degrees of existence (al-kathra fī alwaḥda). 3. Why did Aristotle and Avicenna deny that motion can occur within the category of substance (jawhar), and how does Mullā Ṣadrā's principle of Substantial Motion (al-ḥaraka al-jawhariyya) ground his thesis that the human soul is 'bodily in origination and spiritual in survival' (jismāniyyat al-ḥudūth wa-rūḥāniyyat al-baqāʾ)? 4. How does Mullā Ṣadrā unite his theory of the identity of the intellect, the intelligizer, and the intelligible (ittiḥād al-ʿāqil wa-l-maʿqūl) with his eleven principles of the imaginal body (al-jism al-mithālī) to provide a philosophical demonstration of bodily resurrection?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Mullā Ṣadrā (Ṣadr al-Dīn al-Shīrāzī). The Book of Metaphysical Penetrations (Kitāb al-Mashāʿir).
