Chapter 24Post-Ṣadrian Thought and the Ottoman Philosophical Tradition

The Tradition Continues

Mullā Ṣadrā's Transcendent Philosophy was not a closed system that ended philosophical inquiry. It was a living framework that invited elaboration, criticism, and extension. In the centuries between his death in 1640 and the twentieth century, a chain of philosophers — some famous, some obscure — kept the tradition alive and adapted it to new circumstances. The Ṣadrian school spread from Isfahan to Qom, to Najaf, to India, and eventually to the modern university classrooms of Tehran and beyond. It survived the collapse of the Safavid dynasty, the political upheavals of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, and the profound intellectual challenge of European modernity. This chapter surveys the major developments in Post-Ṣadrian philosophy in Iran, then turns to the parallel tradition of philosophical inquiry in the Ottoman Empire — a tradition that is often neglected in surveys of Islamic philosophy but that sustained the Avicennan-Rāzian heritage in the Sunnī world for six centuries. It continues with the systematising work of Hādī Sabzavārī, the rise of the school of Qom under ʿAllāma Ṭabāṭabāʾī, the controversial philosophical engagements of Ruhollah Khomeini, and the continuing vitality of the Ṣadrian tradition in contemporary Iran and beyond.

1. Sabzavārī and the Systematisation of Transcendent Philosophy Mullā Hādī Sabzavārī (1797–1873) was the most important philosopher of the Qajar period and the man who did more than anyone else to consolidate and popularise Mullā Ṣadrā's teachings. He was born in Sabzavār in northeastern Iran, studied in Isfahan and Mashhad, and returned to his native city, where he taught for decades and attracted students from across the Persianate world. Sabzavārī's masterpiece is the Manẓūma (The Poem), a versified summary of the entire Transcendent Philosophy, accompanied by an extensive prose commentary. The Manẓūma covers logic, metaphysics, psychology, and eschatology, all within a rigorously Ṣadrian framework. Its purpose was pedagogical: to provide students with a clear, memorisable, and systematic exposition of the Transcendent Philosophy. In this, it succeeded brilliantly. The Manẓūma became the standard textbook of ḥikma in the Shīʿī madrasas, and its commentary tradition has continued to the present. Sabzavārī was not a slavish follower. He clarified ambiguities, resolved tensions, and, at key points, departed from Mullā Ṣadrā when he judged it necessary. His most important original contribution is his refinement of the proof of the Necessary Existent. He constructed a new version of the burhān al-ṣiddīqīn — the proof of the veracious — that he claimed was more rigorous and more illuminating than Avicenna's or Mullā Ṣadrā's versions. The proof proceeds from the sheer fact of existence itself: existence is a reality, and its reality cannot come from nothing or depend on an impossibility; it must derive from a reality that is existence in its pure, necessary, and infinite mode. This is an argument that moves from the concept of existence directly to the Necessary Existent, without the intermediate step of distinguishing necessity from possibility in individual beings. Sabzavārī also deepened the Ṣadrian account of the soul's journey, developing a rich psychology of the spiritual stations and their corresponding existential intensifications. He was himself a practicing mystic, and his philosophy was infused with the purifying ethos of the Sufi path. For Sabzavārī, philosophy was an existential discipline, a way of life, and his Manẓūma was as much a spiritual manual as a textbook. Simultaneously in the Qajar capital, the School of Tehran — founded by Mullā ʿAbd Allāh Zunūzī (d. 1841), Mullā ʿAlī Mudarris Nūrī, and above all Āqā ʿAlī Mudarris Ṭihrānī (Zunūzī) (1818–1889) — brought Ṣadrian philosophy into its first direct encounter with modern

European thought. In his Persian masterpiece Badāyiʿ al-Ḥikam (Marvels of Wisdom, written in response to seven philosophical questions posed by the Qajar prince Badīʿ al-Mulk Mīrzā regarding Kantian and modern European philosophy) and his treatise Sabīl al-Rashād, Āqā ʿAlī Mudarris deepened Mullā Ṣadrā's theory of substantial motion and bodily resurrection, directly training the teachers of ʿAllāma Ṭabāṭabāʾī and Ruhollah Khomeini.

2. The School of Qom: ʿAllāma Ṭabāṭabāʾī The twentieth century witnessed a remarkable revival of Ṣadrian philosophy in the city of Qom, under the leadership of a figure of immense piety, learning, and intellectual charisma: Sayyid Muḥammad Ḥusayn Ṭabāṭabāʾī (1904–1981), known universally as ʿAllāma Ṭabāṭabāʾī. He was born in Tabriz, studied in Najaf under the leading scholars of the day, and moved to Qom in 1945, where he began a teaching career that would transform the intellectual life of the Iranian Shīʿī world. ʿAllāma Ṭabāṭabāʾī's most important philosophical work is Uṣūl-i Falsafa-yi Riʾālīsm (The Principles of the Philosophy of Realism), a series of essays written in Persian and accompanied by the extensive commentaries of his brilliant student Murtaḍā Muṭahharī. The work is a defence of philosophical realism against both medieval nominalism and modern Western idealism and scepticism. It argues for the primacy of existence, the objectivity of essence, and the capacity of the human mind to know reality as it is — all positions deeply rooted in the Ṣadrian tradition but now articulated in dialogue with European philosophers such as Kant, Hegel, and Berkeley. What made Ṭabāṭabāʾī's achievement unique was his dual competence. He was a master of the classical Islamic sciences — fiqh, uṣūl, tafsīr, ḥikma — and he was also deeply engaged with modern Western philosophy, which he had studied through Persian translations and through discussions with European-educated interlocutors. He brought these two worlds into a genuine conversation. He did not simply reject Western philosophy; he interrogated it, critiqued it from a Ṣadrian standpoint, and sought to demonstrate the enduring relevance of Islamic ḥikma to contemporary problems. His monumental Qur'anic commentary, al-Mīzān fī Tafsīr al-Qurʾān (The Balance in the Interpretation of the Qur'an), is also a work of philosophy. It employs the method of interpreting the Qur'an through the Qur'an, but at crucial points it draws on Ṣadrian concepts to explicate the meaning of verses about God, the soul, and the afterlife. The Mīzān is perhaps the most significant synthesis of Tafsīr and ḥikma since the works of Mullā Ṣadrā himself, and it is studied with reverence across the Shīʿī world. ʿAllāma Ṭabāṭabāʾī's students included some of the most influential thinkers of contemporary Iran: Murtaḍā Muṭahharī, the philosopher of revolution and education; Ḥusayn Naṣr, the perennialist philosopher and historian of Islamic science; and a generation of clerics, professors, and intellectuals who have carried the Ṣadrian tradition into the modern university and the public square.

3. Ruhollah Khomeini: The Philosopher as Revolutionary Among the students who attended Ṭabāṭabāʾī's philosophy lectures in Qom was a senior jurist and theologian named Ruhollah Khomeini (1902–1989). Khomeini is known to the world as the leader of the Islamic Revolution in Iran, but he was also a deeply trained philosopher in the tradition of Mullā Ṣadrā. His philosophical works — particularly his Taʿlīqa ʿalā Sharḥ al-Manẓūma (Commentary on the Manẓūma) and his Miṣbāḥ al-Hidāya ilā alKhilāfa wa al-Wilāya (The Lamp of Guidance to the Vicegerency and the Guardianship) — are serious and original contributions to the Ṣadrian tradition. Khomeini accepted the core doctrines of Transcendent Philosophy: the primacy of existence, substantial motion, the gradation of being, and the soul's journey to God. But he placed a distinctive emphasis on the political

implications of these doctrines. For Khomeini, the perfected sage is not merely a contemplative recluse but the rightful ruler of the community, the walī al-amr, whose guardianship (walāya) over the people is a participation in the divine authority of the Prophet and the Imams. The doctrine of wilāyat al-faqīh (the guardianship of the jurist), which became the constitutional principle of the Islamic Republic of Iran, was thus grounded, in Khomeini's own mind, in a Ṣadrian metaphysical framework — even if its legal and political articulation departed from the classical tradition in significant ways. Khomeini also wrote poetry and mystical discourses in which he expressed, in vivid and sometimes ecstatic language, the Ṣadrian vision of the soul's annihilation in God and its subsistence through the divine attributes. These works reveal a dimension of his personality that is obscured by his political image: Khomeini was, in his inner life, a mystic who practiced the discipline of fanāʾ and who saw the political struggle as an extension of the spiritual path. The relationship between philosophy and revolution in Khomeini's thought is a subject of ongoing debate. Some critics argue that his political activism was a betrayal of the contemplative ethos of the ḥikma tradition; others argue that he actualised the latent political potential of Ṣadrian thought, which had always linked spiritual perfection with social responsibility. Whichever position one takes, Khomeini's career demonstrates that the Ṣadrian tradition was not a museum piece but a live philosophical force capable of shaping history.

4. Philosophy in the Ottoman Empire: The Forgotten Sunnī Tradition The narrative of Islamic philosophy after the Mongol period has, until recently, been dominated by the Iranian and Shīʿī tradition. The Ottoman Empire (c. 1299–1922), which at its height governed the Balkans, Anatolia, the Levant, Egypt, the Hijaz, and much of North Africa, sustained a vigorous philosophical culture that is only now being recovered by scholars. The Ottoman madrasa curriculum included logic (manṭiq), philosophical theology (kalām), and wisdom (ḥikma) as compulsory subjects. The great Ottoman scholars wrote super-commentaries on al-Rāzī, alṬūsī, and al-Kātibī; they debated Avicennan metaphysics, Akbarian ontology, and Ṣadrian existentialism; and they produced original works of ethics, political philosophy, and philosophy of science. The neglect of this tradition in modern scholarship is a function of linguistic barriers (Ottoman Turkish is now read by few) and of the old orientalist narrative that positioned the Ottomans as cultural epigones. The reality is more interesting: the Ottoman philosophical tradition was a creative synthesis of Persian, Arabic, and Turkish elements, and it produced thinkers of genuine originality. Mullā Fanārī (d. 1431) is a natural starting point. Shams al-Dīn Muḥammad ibn Ḥamza al-Fanārī (1350–1431), known as Mullā Fanārī (Molla Fenârî), was born in Ottoman Anatolia, where he received his initial training in the rational and religious sciences under ʿAlāʾ al-Dīn al-Aswad and Jamāl al-Dīn al-Aqsarāʾī before travelling to Mamluk Cairo to study under the eminent Ḥanafī-Māturīdī scholar Akmal al-Dīn al-Bābartī; returning to Bursa, he served as chief judge and became the first Shaykh al-Islām of the Ottoman state. His major work, Miṣbāḥ al-Uns (The Lamp of Intimacy), is a commentary on the Miftāḥ al-Ghayb of Ṣadr al-Dīn al-Qūnawī, and it is a landmark in the Ottoman reception of Ibn ʿArabī. Fanārī synthesised the Akbarian doctrine of waḥdat al-wujūd with the Avicennan distinction between existence and essence, producing a philosophical mysticism that was both metaphysically rigorous and spiritually evocative. His Fuṣūl al-Badāʾiʿ is a treatise on the principles of jurisprudence that integrates Akbarian metaphysics into legal theory. Fanārī's work set the intellectual tone for the Ottoman learned establishment for two centuries. Taşköprüzāde Aḥmad ibn Muṣṭafā (d. 1561) , known as Isḥāqī, was the author of Miftāḥ al-Saʿāda wa Miṣbāḥ al-Siyāda (The Key of Happiness and the Lamp of Leadership), a monumental encyclopaedia of the sciences that

surveys the entire intellectual tradition from the Greeks through Avicenna to al-Rāzī, al-Ṭūsī, and al-Dawānī. The Miftāḥ is not merely a catalogue but a philosophical defence of the sciences: Taşköprüzāde argues that every branch of knowledge, including philosophy and the mathematical sciences, has a legitimate place in the Islamic curriculum and that the study of these sciences is a religious obligation for the community. He also wrote alShahāʾid al-Sharʿiyya, a treatise on philosophical theology that engages with the Rāzī-Ṭūsī debate, and al-Risāla fī al-ʿIlm, a philosophical analysis of the nature of knowledge and its acquisition. Kātib Çelebī (d. 1657) , known as Ḥājjī Khalīfa, was the greatest bibliographer and one of the most philosophically engaged scholars of the later Ottoman period. His Kashf al-Ẓunūn ʿan Asāmī al-Kutub wa-l-Funūn (The Removal of Doubts concerning the Names of Books and the Sciences) is a vast bio-bibliographical dictionary of some fifteen thousand works in the Islamic sciences, and its extensive introductions to each discipline are in effect short treatises on the philosophy of science. Kātib Çelebī was deeply concerned by the rise of an anti-intellectual current among Ottoman preachers who denounced logic, philosophy, and the mathematical sciences as corrupting innovations. His Mīzān al-Ḥaqq fī Ikhtiyār al-Aḥaqq (The Balance of Truth in the Choice of the More True) is a defence of the rational sciences and a plea for intellectual tolerance, written in a clear, accessible Turkish that addressed the educated layman as much as the scholar. His Irshād al-Ḥayārā ilā Tārīkh al-Yūnān wa-l-Rūmān (Guide for the Perplexed to the History of the Greeks and Romans) is a pioneering work of comparative intellectual history. Kātib Çelebī represents the Ottoman philosophical tradition at its most open and cosmopolitan, engaged with European learning while remaining firmly rooted in the Islamic heritage. The Ottoman madrasa curriculum. The Ottoman madrasas, particularly those of the Süleymaniye complex in Istanbul, taught a curriculum that included logic, ādāb al-baḥth (the rules of disputation), philosophical theology, and ḥikma at the advanced levels. The texts studied included al-Abharī's Īsāghūjī, al-Kātibī's Shamsiyya, al-Ṭūsī's Tajrīd, al-Ījī's Mawāqif with al-Jurjānī's commentary, and the works of al-Dawānī. The Ottoman scholarly class (ʿulemā) was deeply imbued with the Avicennan-Rāzian tradition of philosophical theology, and the great Ottoman legal scholars — Shaykh al-Islām Ebussuʿūd Efendi (d. 1574), for example — routinely used the tools of this tradition in their legal reasoning. The decline of the madrasa curriculum in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, and the subsequent abandonment of the philosophical sciences in favour of purely legal and exegetical studies, is a complex phenomenon that requires careful historical analysis. It was not the result of a single thinker or event but of institutional, political, and economic factors that continue to be debated by historians. The Ottoman ethical tradition. The akhlāq literature, which began with Ibn Miskawayh and al-Ṭūsī, continued in the Ottoman world with works such as Kınalızāde Ali Çelebī's (d. 1572) Akhlāq-i ʿAlāʾī . Kınalızāde was a judge and scholar who combined the Persian tradition of philosophical ethics with Ottoman political realities. His Akhlāq-i ʿAlāʾī is structured like al-Ṭūsī's Akhlāq-i Nāṣirī — ethics, household management, and politics — but it integrates the Akbarian concept of the Perfect Human and the Ṣadrian doctrine of substantial motion with the practical concerns of Ottoman governance. It is one of the most comprehensive works of Islamic practical philosophy in the early modern period, and it demonstrates that the akhlāq tradition remained alive and creative well after the medieval period. The Ottoman engagement with modernity. In the nineteenth century, Ottoman intellectuals confronted the intellectual challenge of European modernity. Thinkers like Namık Kemal (1840–1888) , Ziya Paşa (1829–1880) , and the Young Ottomans drew on both Islamic philosophical concepts and European political philosophy to articulate a vision of constitutional government and Islamic reform. The Tanzimat reforms, and the subsequent debates between the modernists, the Islamists (represented by figures like Mehmed Akif Ersoy (1873–1936) , the poet of the Turkish national anthem and a committed Muslim thinker), and the Westernists, were all philosophical

debates in disguise: what is the nature of human flourishing? What is the purpose of the state? How can Islam and modernity be reconciled? These questions were debated in Turkish newspapers, journals, and books, and they produced a philosophical culture that is only now being studied by modern scholars. The recovery of the Ottoman philosophical tradition is one of the most exciting frontiers of contemporary Islamic studies. It challenges the notion that philosophy in the Islamic world was a purely Persian and Shīʿī affair after the Mongol invasions and demonstrates that the Sunnī world continued to produce original philosophical work well into the modern period. It also complicates the received narrative of decline. The Ottomans were not merely the "sick man of Europe" but a civilisation that sustained and transformed the classical philosophical heritage, and their intellectual achievements deserve to be integrated into the larger history of Islamic philosophy.

5. Continuing Traditions in India and Beyond The Ṣadrian tradition was not confined to Iran and the Ottoman lands. It spread to India, where Shīʿī scholars had long maintained intellectual ties with the centres of learning in Persia and Iraq. In the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, Indian scholars such as ʿAbd al-ʿAlī Bahr al-ʿUlūm and the family of ʿUlamāʾ-i Farangī Maḥall studied and taught Ṣadrian texts, integrating them with the local traditions of kalām and Sufism. In the twentieth century, Indian philosophers like Sayyid Vahīd Akhtar and others continued to publish on Mullā Ṣadrā, bringing the Transcendent Philosophy into dialogue with Hindu thought and with modern European ideas. The Ṣadrian influence also extended to the Arab world, particularly to the Shīʿī communities of Iraq, Lebanon, and the Gulf, where the works of Mullā Ṣadrā, Sabzavārī, and Ṭabāṭabāʾī are studied in the ḥawzas (seminaries) of Najaf and Qom. In the contemporary diaspora, Shīʿī intellectuals in Europe and North America are producing a new generation of scholarship on Ṣadrian philosophy, often in engagement with continental and analytic traditions. The Journal of Islamic Philosophy, the works of scholars like Parviz Morewedge, Sajjad Rizvi, and Ibrahim Kalin, and the translation projects of institutions like the Islamic College in London testify to the continuing global vitality of this tradition.

6. The Endurance of the Wisdom Tradition Post-Ṣadrian thought demonstrates that Islamic philosophy did not end with the classical period. It continued, adapted, and remains alive — a tradition of ḥikma that has proven remarkably resilient in the face of political upheaval, colonial domination, and intellectual challenge. The Transcendent Philosophy, with its emphasis on the spiritual transformation of the knower, offers a vision of philosophy that is at once intellectually rigorous and existentially demanding, and it continues to attract seekers and scholars across the world. The Ottoman tradition, long neglected, is now being recovered as a vital chapter in this story. The Sunnī philosophical theology of the Ottoman madrasas, the Akbarian synthesis of Mullā Fanārī, the encyclopaedic vision of Taşköprüzāde and Kātib Çelebī, and the ethical humanism of Kınalızāde all testify to the creativity and persistence of the philosophical impulse in the Sunnī world. Together with the Ṣadrian tradition in Iran and India, they demonstrate that the wisdom tradition of Islam is not the property of any single sect or region but a shared inheritance of the entire Muslim community. The next stage of our story moves from the specifically Shīʿī and Ottoman contexts to the broader Islamic world. We turn now to the Indian subcontinent in the eighteenth century, where a thinker of towering intellect — Shāh Walī Allāh al-Dihlawī — was forging a new philosophical synthesis, one that would bring together the legal, theological, philosophical, and mystical traditions of Sunnī Islam and lay the foundations for modern Islamic reform.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “Its [existence's] notion is one of the best-known things, / Yet its deepest reality lies in the extremity of hiddenness; / Existence with us is fundamental in reality (aṣīl), / While quiddity is a secondary mental boundary (iʿtibārī), according to the clear proof.” — Mullā Hādī Sabzavārī, Ghurar al-Farāʾid / Sharḥ-i Manẓūma (The Metaphysics of Sabzavārī)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How did Mullā Hādī Sabzavārī's didactic philosophical poem Ghurar al-Farāʾid and his commentary Sharḥ-i Manẓūma systematize Mullā Ṣadrā's Transcendent Philosophy for the Qajar and modern Iranian seminary curriculum? 2. Examine Mullā Shams al-Dīn al-Fanārī's (Molla Fenârî's) Miṣbāḥ al-Uns. How did the first Ottoman Shaykh alIslām synthesize Ṣadr al-Dīn al-Qūnawī's Akbarian ontology with Post-Rāzian kalām and Avicennan logic? 3. What do Taşköprüzāde's encyclopedia Miftāḥ al-Saʿāda, Kınalızāde's ethical-political treatise Akhlāq-i ʿAlāʾī, and Kātib Çelebī's Mīzān al-Ḥaqq reveal about the vitality of the rational sciences (maʿqūlāt) in the Ottoman Empire from the fifteenth to the seventeenth century? 4. How did ʿAllāma Muḥammad Ḥusayn Ṭabāṭabāʾī revive the teaching of Mullā Ṣadrā in twentieth-century Qom through Bidāyat al-Ḥikma, Nihāyat al-Ḥikma, and his Qur'anic commentary al-Mīzān?

Part VIII: Early Modern South Asian Metaphysics, the Maʿqūlāt Tradition, and Shāh Walī Allāh

Chapter 25Shāh Walī Allāh al-Dihlawī, Shaykh Aḥmad Sirhindī, and the South Asian Rationalist (Maʿqūlāt) Tradition

The Sage of Delhi

Quṭb al-Dīn Aḥmad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥīm, known as Shāh Walī Allāh (1703–1762), was born in Delhi, the capital of the declining Mughal Empire. The India of his youth was a world in crisis: the empire was fragmenting under the pressure of regional revolts and Maratha incursions, the intellectual life of the Muslim elite had become sclerotic, and the great traditions of scholarship seemed to have lost their creative energy. Shāh Walī Allāh set himself the task of intellectual and spiritual renewal — tajdīd — and he pursued it with a scope and originality that make him the most important Muslim thinker of the Indian subcontinent and one of the most significant philosophers of the pre-modern Islamic world. He studied in Delhi under his father, a distinguished scholar and Sufi master, and then made the pilgrimage to Mecca, where he spent over a year studying with the leading teachers of the day. On his return, he began to write — in Arabic and Persian — works of exegesis, theology, philosophy, law, and mysticism that sought to synthesise the entire intellectual heritage of Islam into a unified system that could speak to the crisis of his age. His magnum opus,

Ḥujjat Allāh al-Bāligha (The Conclusive Argument from God), is one of the most remarkable works of Islamic thought ever written: a systematic account of the nature of religion, society, and the spiritual life that draws on kalām, falsafa, fiqh, and Sufism, and that presents itself as the definitive demonstration of the wisdom underlying the Sharīʿa. This chapter examines Shāh Walī Allāh's philosophical synthesis: his theory of the stages of civilisation, his metaphysics of the subtle spiritual centres, his philosophy of law and religion, and his enduring influence on modern South Asian Islam.

1. The Conclusive Argument: A Theory of Religion and Society The Ḥujjat Allāh al-Bāligha is structured as a journey from the universal principles of human nature and society to the specific details of Islamic law and spirituality. Shāh Walī Allāh begins with a universal anthropology: human beings are social animals endowed with an innate moral sense (fiṭra) and with a capacity for spiritual development that unfolds through the refinement of the subtle centres (laṭāʾif) of the soul. He argues that God, in His wisdom, has sent prophets to every community to guide them to the perfection of which they are capable. The prophets are not mere lawgivers; they are physicians of the soul, who diagnose the spiritual diseases of their people and prescribe the rituals, laws, and ethical principles that can restore them to health. The Sharīʿa, on this view, is a comprehensive programme of spiritual therapy, designed to cultivate every faculty of the human being — intellect, will, emotion, and body — and to bring them into harmony with the divine purpose. What distinguishes Shāh Walī Allāh's approach is his attempt to show, through rational argument, the wisdom (ḥikma) underlying the specific provisions of Islamic law. He does not merely assert that prayer, fasting, pilgrimage, and the penal laws are divine commands; he explains, in terms of the nature of the human soul and the needs of society, why these particular institutions are the most appropriate means for achieving human felicity. This is a philosophical defence of the Sharīʿa that is without precedent in its scope and power. Shāh Walī Allāh also develops a theory of civilisation that echoes Ibn Khaldūn's but gives greater weight to spiritual factors. Civilisations, he argues, pass through stages of growth, maturity, and decline, and the key variable is the quality of the moral and spiritual life of the ruling elite. The decline of the Mughal Empire, in his analysis, was the result of a spiritual disease — a loss of iḥsān (spiritual excellence) among the ruling class and the scholarly class — that could only be cured by a revival of authentic, experiential knowledge of God.

2. The Subtle Spiritual Centres and Ibn ʿArabī's Legacy Shāh Walī Allāh was steeped in the Sufi tradition of the Naqshbandī and Qādirī orders, and his spiritual psychology is one of his most original contributions. He taught that the human soul contains, beyond the ordinary faculties of reason, imagination, and appetite, a series of subtle spiritual centres (laṭāʾif, singular laṭīfa) — the heart (qalb), the spirit (rūḥ), the innermost secret (sirr), the deeply hidden (khafī), and the most deeply hidden (akhfā). These centres are progressively activated through spiritual practice (invocation, meditation, ethical purification) and become the loci of direct knowledge of God. The activation of these centres corresponds to the stages of the Sufi path, and Shāh Walī Allāh described their nature, functions, and interrelations with a precision that reflects both his own spiritual experience and his synthesis of Ibn ʿArabī's metaphysical categories with the more sober psychology of the Indian Naqshbandī tradition.

On the vexed question of waḥdat al-wujūd (the unity of being) and waḥdat al-shuhūd (the unity of witnessing), Shāh Walī Allāh adopted a mediating position that has become influential. He argued that the difference between Ibn ʿArabī's "unity of being" and the more orthodox "unity of witnessing" (associated with the Indian master Shaykh Aḥmad Sirhindī) is ultimately verbal, reflecting different stages of spiritual experience. In the state of annihilation (fanāʾ), the mystic experiences the One in all things and all things as the manifestation of the One; in the state of subsistence (baqāʾ), the mystic returns to the world and sees God and the world as distinct, but sees the world as entirely dependent on God. Both experiences are true at their respective levels, and neither exhausts the reality of the divine. This irenic resolution was characteristic of Shāh Walī Allāh's synthetic temper.

3. Hermeneutics and Legal Theory: The Philosophy of Uṣūl al-Fiqh Shāh Walī Allāh's most important intellectual legacy was in the field of legal theory. He believed that the Sharīʿa, properly understood, was the perfect expression of divine wisdom, but that its interpretation had been corrupted by two tendencies: the blind imitation of earlier jurists (taqlīd) and the excessive rationalism of the theologians who disconnected law from its spiritual roots. He proposed a new hermeneutics — a method of interpreting scripture that took into account the historical context of revelation, the linguistic conventions of the first audience, the universal principles of human nature, and the spiritual purposes underlying legal commands. This method, he argued, would allow jurists to extract the timeless principles of the Sharīʿa from the time-bound expressions of the texts and to apply them to new situations without abandoning the tradition. He also argued for the harmonisation of the four Sunnī schools of law. The differences among the schools, he claimed, were largely the result of differing interpretive methods and local circumstances, not of fundamental disagreement about the purposes of the Sharīʿa. A renewed ijtihād (independent legal reasoning), informed by a deep knowledge of the prophetic tradition and the spiritual sciences, could bridge these differences and restore the unity of the Muslim community. This programme placed Shāh Walī Allāh at the centre of the eighteenth-century Islamic revival. He was not a modernist in the sense of advocating the wholesale reinterpretation of the Sharīʿa to suit contemporary sensibilities, but he opened the door to a more flexible and principled jurisprudence that later reformers, from the Indian Ahl-i Ḥadīth movement to the Egyptian modernists, would push further than he himself might have approved.

4. Translation of the Qur'an and the Democratisation of Knowledge One of Shāh Walī Allāh's most consequential acts was his translation of the Qur'an into Persian, the language of the educated elite in Mughal India. At the time, many scholars opposed translating the sacred text, fearing that it would lead to misinterpretation and a diminishment of the reverence due to the Arabic original. Shāh Walī Allāh disagreed. He argued that the message of the Qur'an was intended for all humanity, and that making it accessible to those who did not know Arabic was a religious duty. The translation was an act of intellectual democratisation, a blow against the monopoly of the Arabic-trained scholarly class on the meaning of scripture. His sons and disciples extended this work, producing translations into Urdu and other vernaculars, and the movement he inspired contributed to the spread of Qur'an literacy among the Muslim masses of South Asia. The translation project was not only a religious act but a philosophical one: it reflected his conviction that knowledge — especially the knowledge of God's word — was a right, not a privilege, and that the renewal of the community depended on an educated and spiritually engaged laity.

5. Political Thought and the Ideal of the Just Caliphate Shāh Walī Allāh lived through the devastating sack of Delhi by the Persian conqueror Nādir Shāh in 1739, an event that shattered the prestige of the Mughal dynasty and plunged North India into chaos. In response, he wrote a series of political tracts calling on the Muslim rulers of India to unite and to restore justice. His political thought was both realistic and idealistic. He argued that the primary purpose of government is the establishment of justice and the maintenance of public order. In the absence of just government, the social contract dissolves, and society falls into a war of all against all — a Hobbesian insight that he articulated in distinctly Islamic terms. The Sharīʿa provides the moral framework within which government must operate, but it does not specify a single form of government. The ideal form is the just caliphate, but in its absence, any ruler who maintains justice and protects the Muslim community is legitimate. This political theory was a subtle negotiation between the utopian vision of a righteous caliphate and the grim realities of eighteenth-century India. It allowed Shāh Walī Allāh to be both a principled critic of Mughal corruption and a pragmatic advocate of Muslim political survival. His letters to the various warlords and princes of the day are among the most remarkable political documents of the pre-modern Islamic world.

6. Legacy: From Deoband to Modern Reform Shāh Walī Allāh's intellectual influence after his death was enormous. His sons — Shāh ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, Shāh ʿAbd alQādir, and Shāh Rafīʿ al-Dīn — continued his teaching and writing, and his grandson Shāh Muḥammad Ismāʿīl led a militant reform movement against what he saw as syncretic practices among Indian Muslims. The Deoband school, founded in 1867, drew deeply on the Walī Allāhī tradition, as did the Ahl-i Ḥadīth movement, with its emphasis on the direct study of the Qur'an and Ḥadīth and its rejection of taqlīd. In the twentieth century, Shāh Walī Allāh was claimed as a forerunner by both the Muslim modernists and the Islamist revivalists. His emphasis on ijtihād and the rational defence of the Sharīʿa made him a hero to modernists like Sayyid Aḥmad Khān; his passionate commitment to the moral renewal of the community made him a hero to revivalists like Abū al-Aʿlā Mawdūdī. His work continues to be read and debated in madrasas and universities across South Asia, and his philosophical synthesis remains one of the most impressive achievements of pre-modern Islamic thought.

7. Shaykh Aḥmad Sirhindī and the Metaphysics of Waḥdat al-Shuhūd (Unity of Witnessing) To appreciate the historical achievement of Shāh Walī Allāh's eighteenth-century synthesis, one must step back to the two towering intellectual movements of seventeenth-century Mughal India that preceded him: the Naqshbandī-Mujaddidī spiritual metaphysics of Shaykh Aḥmad Sirhindī (1564–1624) and the rationalist (maʿqūlāt) philosophy of the Jaunpūrī and Farangī Maḥall schools. Born in Sirhind in the Punjab during the reign of the Mughal Emperor Akbar and imprisoned briefly at Gwalior Fort by Emperor Jahāngīr, Shaykh Aḥmad Sirhindī — revered across South and Central Asia as Mujaddid-i Alf-i Thānī ('Renovator of the Second Islamic Millennium') — articulated in his 536 collected Persian letters (Maktūbāti Imām-i Rabbānī) the most systematic Sunnī critique and reformulation of Akbarian mysticism ever written. Having personally traversed the ecstatic stations of Ibn ʿArabī's waḥdat al-wujūd ('Unity of Being') under his father and his Naqshbandī master Khwāja Bāqī Billāh, Sirhindī argued that the mystic's experience of absolute oneness (tawḥīd wujūdī) is not the final summit of realization, but an intermediate stage of spiritual intoxication (sukr).

Just as a person gazing into the blazing midday sun ceases to see the stars in the sky — not because the stars have ceased to exist or have become identical with the sun, but because the overwhelming radiance of the sun eclipses them from the beholder's vision — the mystic overwhelmed by divine love experiences Waḥdat al-Shuhūd ('Unity of Witnessing'): he sees nothing but the One. When the mystic advances further from intoxication to the station of prophetic sobriety (ṣaḥw) and servitude (ʿubūdiyya), he realizes that creation is neither identical with God's Essence nor a direct emanation of God's own Existence. Rather, the contingent essences of creatures are constituted by specific privations (aʿdām makhṣūṣa) upon which the reflection or shadow (ẓill) of the Divine Attributes falls; the shadow depends utterly on the Origin (Aṣl) at every instant, yet the shadow is never the Origin itself. It was this sharp ontological conflict between the followers of Ibn ʿArabī and the followers of Sirhindī in Delhi that Shāh Walī Allāh sought to reconcile in his epistle Fayṣalat Waḥdat al-Wujūd wa-l-Shuhūd.

8. The South Asian Maʿqūlāt Tradition: Mullā Maḥmūd Jaunpūrī, Muḥibb Allāh al-Bihārī, and the Dars-i Niẓāmī Simultaneously with the School of Isfahan in Safavid Iran, Mughal and post-Mughal North India became the world's most vibrant center of Sunnī Peripatetic and dialectical philosophy — a tradition known in Indo-Persian scholarship as the Rational Sciences (al-ʿulūm al-ʿaqliyya or maʿqūlāt). Launched in the sixteenth century when the Shirazi philosopher Fatḥ Allāh Shīrāzī (d. 1589) introduced the works of al-Dawānī, Mirzā Jān Bāghnawī, and the School of Shiraz to the court of Emperor Akbar, this movement was consolidated by Mullā ʿAbd al-Salām Lāhawrī (d. 1627) and reached its philosophical zenith in four landmark figures: Mullā Maḥmūd Jaunpūrī (1606–1652): Patronized by Emperor Shāh Jahān and Prince Shāh Shujāʿ, Jaunpūrī authored al-Shams al-Bāzigha (The Rising Sun, his monumental commentary on his own physics/metaphysics compendium al-Ḥikma al-Bāligha) and al-Farāʾid fī Sharḥ al-Fawāʾid on philosophical rhetoric. A contemporary of Mullā Ṣadrā and disciple of Mīr Dāmād's circle, Jaunpūrī mounted a rigorous Peripatetic critique of his teacher Mīr Dāmād's doctrine of atemporal origination (ḥudūth dahrī), defending instead temporal origination while analyzing corpuscular physics, optics, time, and the soul-body problem with unmatched analytical clarity. For over two centuries, al-Shams al-Bāzigha stood alongside Mullā Ṣadrā's Sharḥ al-Hidāya (known in India as the Ṣadrā) as one of the two supreme textbooks of philosophy across South Asia. Qāḍī Muḥibb Allāh al-Bihārī (d. 1707): Chief judge of the Mughal Empire under Aurangzeb ʿĀlamgīr, alBihārī authored two masterpieces that revolutionized the madrasa curriculum: Sullam al-ʿUlūm (The Ladder of the Sciences), the most advanced and densely argued treatise on post-Avicennan formal and modal logic ever written in the Islamic world, and Musallam al-Thubūt on the philosophical foundations of jurisprudence (uṣūl al-fiqh). Mullā Niẓām al-Dīn Sihālawī (1677–1748) and the Farangī Maḥall School: Granted the European merchant mansion ('Farangī Maḥall') in Lucknow by Emperor Aurangzeb in 1695 after the martyrdom of his father Mullā Quṭb al-Dīn, Mullā Niẓām al-Dīn designed the celebrated Dars-i Niẓāmī curriculum. Rather than memorizing encyclopedic legal manuals, the Dars-i Niẓāmī trained students' analytical faculties through deep reading of the hardest texts of Avicennan logic (Sullam al-ʿUlūm, Sharḥ al-Shamsiyya, Mīr Zāhid), RāzianJurjānian philosophical theology (Sharḥ al-Mawāqif), and natural philosophy/metaphysics (al-Shams alBāzigha and Mullā Ṣadrā's Sharḥ al-Hidāya). His son, ʿAbd al-ʿAlī Bahr al-ʿUlūm ('Ocean of the Sciences,' 1731– 1810), authored prized super-commentaries on both Sullam al-ʿUlūm and Rūmī's Mathnawī. ʿAllāma Faḍl-i Ḥaqq Khayrābādī (1797–1861) and the Khayrābādī School: The last great master of classical Indo-Islamic falsafa, Faḍl-i Ḥaqq Khayrābādī authored al-Hadiyya al-Saʿīdiyya fī al-Ḥikma alṬabīʿiyya (on natural philosophy), al-Mirqāt (on logic), and al-Rawḍ al-Majūd (on ontology and the critique of

Mullā Ṣadrā). Exiled to the Andaman Islands by the British colonial authorities for his intellectual leadership during the 1857 Uprising, where he died in captivity, Khayrābādī symbolizes the enduring vitality of the South Asian rationalist tradition right up to the threshold of colonial modernity.

9. The Dawn of Modernity Shāh Walī Allāh died in 1762, as the eighteenth century was giving way to the modern age. The Islamic world was on the cusp of a series of intellectual and political shocks — colonialism, the scientific revolution, the rise of the nation-state — that would challenge its philosophical traditions in unprecedented ways. The next chapter turns to the first wave of modern Muslim thinkers: the reformers and renaissance figures of the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries who sought to revive Islamic thought in the face of European power and to articulate a philosophy that could be both Muslim and modern.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “The supreme science among the sciences of religious certainty is the Science of the Secrets of Religion (ʿilm asrār al-dīn), which investigates the rational wisdoms (al-ḥikam), universal human benefits (al-maṣāliḥ), and civilizational stages (al-irtifāqāt) underlying the divine commandments; just as a skilled physician prescribes remedies matched to the constitution of the patient, the Wise Lawgiver has revealed laws calibrated to the four progressive stages of human social development.” — Shāh Walī Allāh al-Dihlawī, Ḥujjat Allāh al-Bāligha (The Conclusive Argument from God)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. Explain Shaykh Aḥmad Sirhindī's critique of pantheistic interpretations of Ibn ʿArabī in the Maktūbāt and his distinction between the 'Unity of Being' (waḥdat al-wujūd) and the 'Unity of Witnessing' (waḥdat al-shuhūd). How did Shāh Walī Allāh later mediate between these two doctrines? 2. What were the major philosophical texts and innovations of the seventeenth- and eighteenth-century South Asian maʿqūlāt and Dars-i Niẓāmī tradition, particularly Mullā Maḥmūd Jaunpūrī's al-Shams al-Bāzigha and Muḥibb Allāh al-Bihārī's Sullam al-ʿUlūm? 3. Trace the four progressive stages of human socio-political organization (al-irtifāqāt al-arbaʿa) in Shāh Walī Allāh's Ḥujjat Allāh al-Bāligha. How does his theory compare with Ibn Khaldūn's science of civilization? 4. Why did almost every competing movement in modern South Asian Islam — from the traditionalist Deoband seminary and the Ahl-i Ḥadīth to the Aligarh modernists and Muḥammad Iqbāl — claim Shāh Walī Allāh as its intellectual progenitor?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Shāh Walī Allāh al-Dihlawī. The Conclusive Argument from God: Shāh Walī Allāh of Delhi's Ḥujjat Allāh al-Bāligha. Translated by Marcia K. Hermansen. Leiden: Brill, 1996. Friedmann, Yohanan. Shaykh Aḥmad Sirhindī: An Outline of His Thought and a Study of His Image in the Eyes of Posterity. Montreal: McGill-Queen's University Press, 1971. Part IX: Modern and Contemporary Philosophical Engagements

Chapter 26Reform and Renaissance: Nineteenth- and Early Twentieth-Century Thinkers

The Shock of the New

The nineteenth century opened with the Islamic world in a state of profound crisis. The Ottoman Empire, the last great Muslim superpower, was visibly in decline, its territories being carved up by European colonial powers. Iran and Central Asia had fallen into political fragmentation and economic dependency. The Indian subcontinent was under British rule. The scientific and industrial revolutions had transformed European military and economic

power, and the intellectual currents of the Enlightenment — rationalism, empiricism, historicism — were challenging the traditional foundations of religious belief worldwide. Muslim intellectuals responded to this crisis in a variety of ways. Some retreated into a defensive rigidity, rejecting everything associated with the coloniser. Others embraced European models wholesale, calling for the abandonment of tradition. But a significant and influential group sought a third path: they argued that Islam was fundamentally compatible with reason, science, and progress, and that the renewal of Muslim societies required not the rejection of the Islamic heritage but its critical recovery. These thinkers — Jamāl al-Dīn al-Afghānī, Muḥammad ʿAbduh, Ṭāhā Ḥusayn, and others — laid the intellectual foundations for Islamic modernism and initiated a philosophical conversation with modernity that continues to this day. This chapter examines the lives, ideas, and legacy of the major figures of the Islamic reform and renaissance movement of the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries. It explores their arguments for reason and reform, their engagement with European philosophy, and their attempts to articulate an Islamic response to the challenges of modernity.

1. Al-Afghānī and the Call for Rational Revival Sayyid Jamāl al-Dīn al-Afghānī (1838–1897) was a political activist, a public intellectual, and a brilliant orator who travelled across the Islamic world — from Afghanistan to Iran, to Egypt, to Istanbul, to Paris — calling on Muslims to unite, to modernise, and to reclaim the rational spirit of their own classical heritage. His biography is shrouded in mystery and self-mythologisation: he claimed to be an Afghan (perhaps to avoid being identified as a Shīʿī Persian, as he likely was), and his movements were often dictated by political intrigue and expulsion. Al-Afghānī was not a systematic philosopher. He wrote little, and his most famous publications — the journal alʿUrwa al-Wuthqā (The Firmest Bond), co-edited with his Egyptian disciple Muḥammad ʿAbduh, and his polemical al-Radd ʿalā al-Dahriyyīn (The Refutation of the Materialists) — are works of advocacy rather than analysis. But his influence was immense, because he articulated a vision that many Muslims found compelling: Islam, properly understood, is a religion of reason and action, and the decline of Muslim societies is the result of abandoning that spirit, not of adhering to it. In The Refutation of the Materialists (originally composed in Persian in Hyderabad, India, in 1881 under the title Ḥaqīqat-i Madhhab-i Nīcharī wa Bayān-i Ḥāl-i Nīchariyyān before Muḥammad ʿAbduh translated it into Arabic as al-Radd ʿalā al-Dahriyyīn), al-Afghānī targeted both the Indian "Naturalist" (Nīcharī) school of Sayyid Aḥmad Khān and European materialists from Democritus to Darwin and the positivists, arguing that reductionist materialism erodes the metaphysical foundations of moral accountability and social solidarity. But he also criticised the complacent literalism and fatalism of the traditionalist scholars. The true Islam, he argued, is the Islam of the philosophers and the scientists — the Islam that had led the world in knowledge and that had inspired the European Renaissance. The Muslims had fallen behind not because Islam was incompatible with science but because they had abandoned the rational Qur'anic imperative to study nature and seek wisdom. Al-Afghānī's call for iṣlāḥ (reform) had a profound impact on two generations of Muslim activists and thinkers. He inspired the Egyptian nationalist movement, the Iranian Constitutional Revolution, and the early pan-Islamic movement that sought to unite Muslims against colonial domination. His vision of a politically engaged, rationalist, and anti-colonial Islam would be taken up and developed by his most famous student, Muḥammad ʿAbduh.

2. Muḥammad ʿAbduh: Reason, Revelation, and Social Reform Shaykh Muḥammad ʿAbduh (1849–1905) was the most important and influential Muslim reformer of the late nineteenth century. He was born in the Nile Delta, studied at al-Azhar in Cairo, and fell under al-Afghānī's spell when the two met in 1871. Exiled from Egypt for his political activities, he spent time in Beirut and Paris, and on his return he began a career as a teacher, a judge, and eventually the Grand Mufti of Egypt — the highest religious legal authority in the land. ʿAbduh was a man of remarkable intellectual breadth. He was trained in the classical Islamic sciences but read widely in European philosophy, politics, and science. His most important works — Risālat al-Tawḥīd (The Theology of Unity), his commentaries on the Qur'an, and his legal opinions (fatāwā) — sought to demonstrate that Islam, when understood correctly, is a religion of reason, progress, and tolerance, and that the obstacles to Muslim advancement are not inherent to the faith but the products of historical corruption and intellectual stagnation. The theology of ʿAbduh centres on the concept of tawḥīd — divine unity. But for ʿAbduh, tawḥīd was not only a metaphysical doctrine; it was a liberating social principle. If God alone is sovereign, then no human being has the right to enslave another, either politically or intellectually. Tawḥīd eliminated the blind submission to tradition (taqlīd) that had paralysed the Muslim mind, and it authorised the free exercise of reason (ʿaql). The Qur'an, ʿAbduh argued, calls human beings to reflect on nature, to seek knowledge, and to govern their affairs through consultation (shūrā). Islam, properly understood, is democratic, rationalist, and progressive. ʿAbduh's most important philosophical contribution is his theory of the relationship between reason and revelation. He argued that Islam is the "religion of nature" (dīn al-fiṭra): its teachings are in perfect accord with the innate reason of the healthy human soul. Revelation does not impose arbitrary demands; it confirms and elevates what reason already knows. When reason and revelation appear to conflict, the conflict is illusory. Either the scriptural text has been misinterpreted — and must be reinterpreted allegorically — or the rational claim is not genuinely demonstrative but merely speculative. ʿAbduh thus opened the door to a flexible, historically sensitive interpretation of the Qur'an that could accommodate scientific discoveries and modern social norms. In practical terms, ʿAbduh's reform programme was extensive. He called for the reform of al-Azhar, introducing modern sciences and European languages into the curriculum. He advocated the improvement of the status of women, arguing that polygamy and unilateral divorce were not essential to Islam but concessions to historical conditions that could be reformed. He called for parliamentary government, the rule of law, and the separation of religious and political authority. He saw no contradiction between being a devout Muslim and a modern citizen; indeed, he saw modernity as the fulfilment of Islam's deepest ethical and rational impulses. ʿAbduh's influence was channelled through his student Muḥammad Rashīd Riḍā (1865–1935), who edited the journal al-Manār (The Lighthouse) and who popularised his teacher's ideas across the Muslim world. Over time, Riḍā's own thought took a more conservative and literalist turn, but the ʿAbduhian synthesis of reason and revelation remained the founding vision of Islamic modernism, and it would inspire reformers from Morocco to Indonesia.

3. Ṭāhā Ḥusayn and Cartesian Doubt in an Islamic Context Ṭāhā Ḥusayn (1889–1973) was an Egyptian literary critic, historian, and philosopher who embodied the radical potential of the ʿAbduhian movement — and also the limits of its acceptance. Blind since childhood, he studied at al-Azhar and then at the newly founded Egyptian University, before earning a doctorate in literature at the

Sorbonne in Paris. He returned to Egypt as a champion of the European Enlightenment and a fierce critic of what he saw as the intellectual backwardness of the traditional Islamic institutions. In 1926, Ḥusayn published Fī al-Shiʿr al-Jāhilī (On Pre-Islamic Poetry), a work of literary criticism that applied the methods of European historical criticism to the corpus of classical Arabic poetry. He argued that much of the poetry attributed to the pre-Islamic period was in fact forged in later centuries for political and religious reasons, and that the Qur'an itself must be studied as a historical document situated in its milieu. The book caused a storm of controversy and was banned; Ḥusayn was tried for apostasy (he was acquitted) and forced to revise and republish the book under a new title. The philosophical significance of Ḥusayn's work lay in its application of Cartesian doubt — the systematic questioning of all received knowledge — to the Islamic tradition. Ḥusayn argued that the Muslim intellectual must not be afraid to doubt, because doubt is the path to certainty. The unexamined tradition is a prison; rational inquiry is the key. This was a direct extension of ʿAbduh's critique of taqlīd, but applied with a radicalism that ʿAbduh himself had avoided. Ḥusayn subjected the sacred texts and the foundational narratives of Islam to the same critical scrutiny that European scholars had applied to the Bible, and the result was a crisis of authority that has not been resolved to this day. In later years, Ḥusayn turned from literary criticism to social and political commentary, advocating for education, democracy, and the rights of women. His autobiography, al-Ayyām (The Days), is a classic of modern Arabic literature and a moving testament to the power of reason and education to overcome adversity. He remains a symbol of the secular, liberal strand of Arab modernism — a thinker who believed that Islam could and must accommodate the full rigour of modern critical thought, even if that meant fundamentally reinterpreting the tradition.

4. The Arab Awakening and Other Voices The ʿAbduh-ʿAfghānī circle was not the only source of reformist energy in the nineteenth-century Arab world. In Tunisia, Khayr al-Dīn al-Tūnisī (1822–1890) wrote Aqwam al-Masālik fī Maʿrifat Aḥwāl al-Mamālik (The Surest Path to Knowledge of the Conditions of Nations), a comparative study of European and Islamic political institutions that argued for constitutional government and educational reform. In Syria, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Kawākibī (1855– 1902) wrote Ṭabāʾiʿ al-Istibdād (The Characteristics of Tyranny), a brilliant analysis of political oppression that drew on both Islamic sources and European political theory. Before them, Rifāʿa Rāfiʿ al-Ṭahṭāwī (1801–1873) had already introduced Egyptian intellectuals to French Enlightenment thought, translating works of philosophy, political theory, and science. These thinkers shared a common conviction: that the salvation of the Islamic world lay not in rejecting modernity but in selectively appropriating it, in reinterpreting the Islamic heritage in a way that recovered its rational and progressive spirit, and in reforming the political and educational institutions that were holding Muslim societies back. Their project was a philosophical one, even when it did not take the form of systematic philosophy, because it concerned the most fundamental questions of knowledge, value, and human flourishing.

5. The Unfinished Project of Reform By the early twentieth century, the lines of debate were clearly drawn. On one side stood the traditionalists, who viewed all innovation as a deviation from the authentic path. On the other side stood the radical Westernisers, who viewed Islam itself as an obstacle to progress. Between them stood the reformers, who believed that Islam possessed

the inner resources to respond to modernity and that the renewal of Muslim societies required both a return to the foundational sources and a critical engagement with the ideas of the modern world. The reformers did not achieve all they had hoped. The colonial grip on the Islamic world was broken only through decades of struggle, and post-colonial states often adopted authoritarian, nationalist, or socialist ideologies that were as hostile to liberal reformism as they were to traditionalism. The intellectual space for a genuinely free and critical Islamic philosophy remained narrow. But the reformers articulated a permanent possibility: the possibility of being a devout Muslim and a committed rationalist, a lover of tradition and a citizen of the modern world. That possibility would be taken up and radicalised in the twentieth century by figures like Muḥammad Iqbāl, the subject of our next chapter, who would fuse the ʿAbduhian spirit of reform with the resources of European philosophy and Sufi poetry to create a new vision of the Muslim self in the modern age.

6. Toward the Reconstruction of Religious Thought The nineteenth-century reformers cracked open the door. The twentieth century would see it pushed wide open by philosophers who engaged directly with Hegel, Nietzsche, Bergson, and Heidegger, writing in the global language of philosophy while drawing on the Qur'an, Rūmī, and Mullā Ṣadrā. The next chapter turns to the greatest of these philosophical poets of Islamic modernism: Muḥammad Iqbāl, whose Reconstruction of Religious Thought in Islam remains the most daring and original attempt to articulate an Islamic philosophy for the modern age.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “Islam was the first religion to address the rational intellect (al-ʿaql), calling upon human beings to examine the book of the universe alongside the revealed Book, liberating minds from the shackles of blind imitation (taqlīd), and establishing that wherever an apparent expression of scripture conflicts with a definitive rational demonstration, scripture must be interpreted in harmony with what reason has proved.” — Shaykh Muḥammad ʿAbduh, Risālat al-Tawḥīd (The Theology of Unity)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. Compare Jamāl al-Dīn al-Afghānī's response to Ernest Renan's 1883 Sorbonne lecture ('Islam and Science') with his Persian treatise The Refutation of the Materialists (al-Radd ʿalā al-Dahriyyīn). How did al-Afghānī adjust his philosophical rhetoric for European and Muslim audiences? 2. How did Shaykh Muḥammad ʿAbduh revive Muʿtazilī and classical philosophical themes in Risālat al-Tawḥīd to argue that Islam is the religion of reason, natural science, and human moral agency? 3. Evaluate Sayyid Aḥmad Khān's thesis in nineteenth-century India that 'the Word of God (Revelation) can never contradict the Work of God (the Laws of Nature).' What philosophical criticisms did al-Afghānī and traditionalist scholars level against his naturalism (nechariyya)? 4. How did Ṭāhā Ḥusayn apply Cartesian methodological doubt in Fī al-Shiʿr al-Jāhilī (On Pre-Islamic Poetry, 1926), and what boundaries did the ensuing controversy set for historical criticism in twentieth-century Arab thought?

Chapter 27Muḥammad Iqbāl: The Reconstruction of Religious Thought

The Poet-Philosopher of the East

Sir Muḥammad Iqbāl (1877–1938) was born in Sialkot, in present-day Pakistan, and died in Lahore, leaving behind a body of work that straddles poetry and philosophy, East and West, tradition and modernity. He studied at Government College in Lahore, then at Cambridge and Munich, where he earned a doctorate in philosophy with a dissertation on The Development of Metaphysics in Persia. He returned to British India as a barrister, a poet, and a public intellectual, and he became the spiritual father of the idea of Pakistan. But Iqbāl was far more than a political thinker. He was a philosopher of the self and a religious visionary who attempted a grand synthesis of Islamic theology, Sufi metaphysics, and modern European philosophy. His Reconstruction of Religious Thought in Islam — a series of lectures delivered in 1928–29 and later expanded — is the most original work of philosophical theology produced by a Muslim in the twentieth century. Iqbāl's thought is built around a single, luminous concept: the khūdī, the self or ego. From this centre, he developed a metaphysics of becoming, a critique of classical Sufism, a new interpretation of ijtihād, and a vision of the Muslim community as a spiritual democracy. He wrote his philosophy in English prose and his deepest intuitions in Persian and Urdu verse. He was at once a modern mind and a lover of Rūmī; a reader of Nietzsche and Bergson and a disciple of the Qur'an. This chapter examines the major themes of Iqbāl's philosophical reconstruction and its place in the story of Muslim thought.

1. The Philosophy of the Ego The keystone of Iqbāl's thought is the doctrine of khūdī. The self, for Iqbāl, is not a passive substance or an illusion to be dissolved; it is a centre of free, creative activity, a spark of the divine life, whose purpose is to intensify its own individuality. "The ultimate aim of the ego," he wrote, "is not to see something, but to be something." The Qur'anic story of Adam is the story of the emergence of the human ego as God's vicegerent on earth. The fall is not a sin but the birth of freedom; the tree represents the transition from instinct to consciousness. Man is the creature who has accepted the "trust" of personality, which the heavens and the mountains refused. Iqbāl's conception of the ego is profoundly influenced by the Qur'anic emphasis on action, responsibility, and individuality. The ego is not a fixed thing but a process, a continual act of self-creation. It becomes more real as it absorbs more of the world — mastering nature, shaping society, deepening its relationship with God. The fully realised ego is the Perfect Man (insān-i kāmil), not one who has lost himself in the divine unity but one who has attained the highest degree of individual uniqueness, and who, like a beloved servant of God, can command the world. Iqbāl reverses the traditional Sufi ideal of fanāʾ (annihilation) into a philosophy of baqāʾ (subsistence) as a strengthened, God-centred self. This philosophy of the ego carries strong echoes of Nietzsche's will to power and Bergson's creative evolution, but Iqbāl reinterprets them through the Qur'an. Nietzsche's Superman is an atheist who rebels against God; Iqbāl's

Perfect Man is a believer who becomes God's partner in creation. "God is the ultimate Ego," he wrote, "the absolute Individual." The human ego is not God, but it can mirror God's creative activity and share in God's freedom.

2. A Process Metaphysics of God and World Iqbāl presents a dynamic, process-oriented conception of God and the universe. The God of the Qur'an, he argues, is not the static, unmoved mover of Aristotle but a living, creative power who is constantly bringing new possibilities into being. The world is not a finished product but an ongoing act of divine creativity, and the human ego is a coworker with God in the shaping of the future. "The world is not to be seen as a completed fact," he wrote, "but as something in the making." Iqbāl draws on the Ashʿarī theology of atomism and occasionalism but radicalises it. The atoms of the Ashʿarīs, which disintegrate and are recreated at every instant, suggest a universe of continuous creation. Iqbāl adds to this the Bergsonian idea of time as real and creative. Time is not a series of static instants but a continuous flow, and God's creative activity operates within this flow, producing genuine novelty. The future is open, not predetermined; God knows it not as a fixed script but as the field of real possibilities. This allows Iqbāl to affirm both divine omniscience and human freedom. God's relation to the world is that of an all-inclusive Self to a finite self. The world is not identical with God — Iqbāl rejects pantheism — but it lives in God and derives its reality from God's sustaining presence. The purpose of creation is the emergence of free, conscious personalities who can enter into a personal relationship with their Creator. Prayer, for Iqbāl, is the dynamic interaction between the finite ego and the infinite Ego, an act in which the human self becomes more fully itself by opening to the divine self.

3. Critique of Classical Sufism and Peripatetic Stagnation Iqbāl was a devoted reader of Rūmī, and he called Rūmī his spiritual guide. But he launched a sharp critique against what he saw as the passive, world-denying tendencies of much later Sufism. The Persian Sufi poets, he charged, had idealised fanāʾ as a loss of individuality, a drowsy absorption into the divine that drained the energy of the Muslim community and made them incapable of historical action. Plato's philosophy of eternal forms, through the influence of Neoplatonism, had taught Muslims to look away from the concrete world and to seek reality in an abstract beyond. Iqbāl wanted to reverse the direction: to sanctify action, to affirm the body, to engage history. He called his spiritual project khūdī precisely to invert the Sufi doctrine of self-annihilation. The real saint, he argued, is not the one who sits in a corner and says "I am God" but the one who, having found God, returns to the world to build, to create, to lead. The Prophet Muḥammad was the supreme model: a statesman, a general, a lawgiver, and a mystic, all in one. The "night of ascension" was not an escape from the world but a recharging for the struggle of the day. Iqbāl's critique was not of Sufism as such but of the quietism and antinomianism that had, in his view, corrupted it. He also diagnosed the intellectual stagnation of the Muslim world as a consequence of the closure of the gates of ijtihād — independent legal reasoning. The intellectual spirit of the earliest centuries of Islam, he argued, was dynamic, critical, and open. It was the spirit of the Muʿtazila, of al-Fārābī, of Ibn Rushd. The constriction of this spirit, and the dominance of taqlīd, had left Muslims unable to respond creatively to modernity. Iqbāl's philosophy was thus a call for intellectual revival: a recovery of the Qur'anic spirit of inquiry and a new engagement with the thought of the modern world.

4. Law, Ijtihād, and the Modern Muslim Community The most practical chapter of the Reconstruction is its discussion of ijtihād. Iqbāl argued that the Sharīʿa is not a closed code but a living tradition of principles whose application must evolve with time. The classical jurists had developed methods of analogical reasoning (qiyās) and consensus (ijmāʿ) that served their age. Those methods must now be revived and adapted, drawing on the resources of modern philosophy, science, and social thought. Iqbāl proposed that the power of ijtihād be exercised collectively by a legislative assembly — a proposal that moved the centre of legal authority from the individual jurist to the democratic community, an idea that prefigured the political vision of Pakistan. For Iqbāl, the purpose of law is not the mechanical enforcement of rules but the cultivation of the ego. Good law enables human beings to become more fully themselves, to realise their potential as God's vicegerents. The goal of the Islamic state is to create the conditions in which individuals can develop their khūdī to the highest degree. This means education, freedom of conscience, economic justice, and a public sphere animated by the principles of tawḥīd (the oneness of God, which implies the equality of all humans before God) and risāla (prophethood, which implies a community founded on revealed moral guidance). Iqbāl was not a secular liberal; he believed that the modern state needed a spiritual foundation, and that Islam provided the most complete one. Iqbāl's vision of the Muslim community was thus simultaneously conservative and revolutionary. It conserved the core spiritual energies of the Qur'an and the prophetic example, but it called for a radical break with the institutional and intellectual ossification of the preceding centuries. His famous call for a separate Muslim state in northwestern India was not simply a political demand; it was the expression of his philosophical conviction that the Muslim ego required a space in which it could freely develop without being absorbed into a larger non-Muslim identity.

5. Iqbāl's Legacy: Poetry, Politics, Philosophy Iqbāl's influence on the Muslim world, particularly in South Asia and Iran, has been profound. His poetry — the Asrār-i Khūdī (Secrets of the Self), the Rumūz-i Bīkhūdī (Mysteries of Selflessness), the Payām-i Mashriq (Message of the East), and the great Urdu masterpieces Bāng-i Darā and Bāl-i Jibrīl — made him the most beloved poet of the Urdu-speaking world and a major figure in Persian literature. His poetry is philosophical verse of the highest order, accessible to the masses and dense with thought for the educated reader. His political legacy as the spiritual father of Pakistan is complex. Some have claimed him as a secular nationalist, others as a theocratic visionary. The truth is that Iqbāl's political thought resists simple labels. He was both a democrat and a mystic, a constitutionalist and a lover of the Prophet. The tension between these strands in his legacy is the tension of modern Islam itself. Philosophically, Iqbāl opened a space that had not previously existed: the space of an Islamic philosophy that was simultaneously Qur'anic and modern, deeply traditional and unapologetically engaged with Nietzsche, Bergson, and Whitehead. His Reconstruction remains a living book, studied in universities from Lahore to Kuala Lumpur to Chicago, and his ideas have influenced movements for Islamic renewal across the globe. He was not a systembuilder like Avicenna or Mullā Ṣadrā, but he was a visionary who gave a new direction to Muslim philosophical thought — away from the abstract contemplation of essences and toward the dynamic affirmation of the self in history.

6. Inside the Seven Lectures of The Reconstruction of Religious Thought in Islam: Serial Time, Pure Duration, and Process Metaphysics To see how Muḥammad Iqbāl moves beyond poetic inspiration to technical philosophical argument, it is instructive to examine the structure of his seven lectures in The Reconstruction of Religious Thought in Islam (delivered in Madras, Hyderabad, and Aligarh in 1929 and expanded with a seventh lecture in the 1934 Oxford University Press edition): 1. Lecture I ('Knowledge and Religious Experience') & Lecture II ('The Philosophical Test of the Revelations of Religious Experience'): Engaging Kant's Critique of Pure Reason, Einstein's theory of relativity, Alfred North Whitehead's critique of 'the bifurcation of nature,' and William James's psychology of religion, Iqbāl argues that mystical/prophetic intuition (qalb / fuʾād) is not an irrational blind feeling, but a higher, synthetic mode of cognitive experience that grasps reality as an indivisible organic whole where analytic reason (ʿaql juzʾī) slices it into static spatial segments. 2. Lecture III ('The Conception of God and the Meaning of Prayer'): Drawing simultaneously on Henri Bergson's Creative Evolution and the Iraqi Sufi philosopher ʿAyn al-Quḍāt al-Hamadānī (d. 1131), Iqbāl distinguishes sharply between serial time (spatialized clock-time broken into past, present, and future, corresponding to the world of measured creation, khalq) and pure duration (dahr) (an organic, interpenetrating flow in which the past operates within the present and the future is an open, unrealized creative possibility, corresponding to the divine command, amr). Because the future is genuinely open rather than a pre-recorded cinematic reel awaiting projection, divine omniscience does not mean foreknowing predetermined events like a spectator, but knowing all real possibilities within God's infinite creative life. 3. Lecture IV ('The Human Ego — His Freedom and Immortality'): Engaging Ashʿarī atomism alongside modern quantum physics and emergent evolution, Iqbāl argues that the human ego (khūdī) emerges out of the colony of sub-egos constituting matter, yet transcends mechanism through directive intention (amriyya). Personal immortality is not a passive metaphysical birthright guaranteed automatically to every soul (as in Plato), but a moral and ontological achievement won through ego-sustaining tension and creative action. 4. Lecture V ('The Spirit of Muslim Culture'): Presenting one of the most original arguments in modern Islamic thought, Iqbāl argues that the Prophet Muḥammad stands at the watershed between the ancient world (governed by uncritical reliance on supernatural oracle and deductive Greek speculation) and the modern world (governed by inductive, empirical reason). Precisely by declaring the Finality of Prophethood (Khatm al-Nubuwwa), Islam abolished humanity's psychic dependence on new supernatural revelations and threw the human intellect back upon its own critical resources — thereby giving birth to the experimental and historical sciences in figures like al-Bīrūnī, Ibn al-Haytham, and Ibn Khaldūn. 5. Lecture VI ('The Principle of Movement in the Structure of Islam') & Lecture VII ('Is Religion Possible?'): Translating process metaphysics into jurisprudence and political philosophy, Iqbāl reinterprets consensus (ijmāʿ) as a dynamic parliamentary legislative assembly and calls for the transfer of ijtihād from isolated conservative jurists to elected Muslim legislatures capable of reconstructing social and legal norms for a changing world.

7. The Road to the Contemporary Iqbāl died in 1938, a decade before the birth of Pakistan. The world he left behind was on the brink of World War II, decolonisation, and the emergence of new Muslim nation-states. The philosophical debates of the second half of the twentieth century would unfold in the shadow of these political transformations, and they would engage not only

with the classical Islamic heritage but with the full range of modern Western thought — existentialism, Marxism, hermeneutics, feminism, postcolonialism. The next chapter surveys this landscape of contemporary Muslim philosophy: the Traditionalists, the Iranian intellectuals, the Arab and Francophone critics, the feminist thinkers, the Marxist and liberationist currents, the analytic philosophers, and the new generation of thinkers who are reshaping the conversation today.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “The Ultimate Ego exists in pure duration wherein change ceases to be a succession of alien states and becomes continuous creation... To exist in pure duration is to be a self, and to be a self is to be able to say 'I am.' Only that truly exists which can say 'I am'; and the degree of the intuition of 'I-amness' (khūdī) determines the place of a thing in the scale of being.” — Sir Muḥammad Iqbāl, The Reconstruction of Religious Thought in Islam (Lecture II)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How does Muḥammad Iqbāl define the self or ego (khūdī) in Asrār-i Khūdī and The Reconstruction of Religious Thought in Islam, and why does he criticize both Neoplatonic-pantheistic Sufism (fanāʾ as dissolution of individuality) and Nietzsche's atheistic Übermensch? 2. Explain Iqbāl's distinction between 'serial time' (spatialized clock-time governing khalq) and 'pure duration' (dahr governing amr). How does this distinction reshape his understanding of divine omniscience, human free will, and continuous creation? 3. Why does Iqbāl argue in Lecture V ('The Spirit of Muslim Culture') that the Islamic doctrine of the Finality of Prophethood (Khatm al-Nubuwwa) marks the historical birth of the modern inductive and empirical intellect? 4. How does Iqbāl reconceive ijtihād as 'the principle of movement in the structure of Islam' in Lecture VI, and what institutional form does he propose for consensus (ijmāʿ) in a modern 'spiritual democracy'?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Iqbāl, Sir Muḥammad. The Reconstruction of Religious Thought in Islam. Edited and annotated by M. Saeed Sheikh, with a new introduction by Javed Majeed. Stanford: Stanford University Press, 2013. Iqbāl, Sir Muḥammad. The Secrets of the Self (Asrār-i Khūdī). Translated by Reynold A. Nicholson. London: Macmillan, 1920. Diagne, Souleymane Bachir. Islam and Open Society: Fidelity and Movement in the Philosophy of Muhammad Iqbal. Translated by Melissa McMahon. Dakar: CODESRIA, 2010. Schimmel, Annemarie. Gabriel's Wing: A Study into the Religious Ideas of Sir Muhammad Iqbal. Leiden: Brill, 1963. Sevea, Iqbal Singh. The Political Philosophy of Muhammad Iqbal: Islam and Nationalism in Late Colonial India. Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 2012.
