Chapter 28Twentieth-Century Philosophical Revival in the Seminary and Academy: Seyyed Hossein Nasr, Muḥammad Bāqir al-Ṣadr, Murtaḍā Muṭahharī, Syed Muhammad Naquib al-Attas, and Ismāʿīl Rājī al-Fārūqī

The Plural Landscape of Modern Muslim Thought

The period from the mid-twentieth century to the present has been a time of extraordinary intellectual ferment in the Muslim world. Decolonisation produced new nation-states and new universities. Migration brought large Muslim communities into the heart of Europe and North America. The globalisation of media and scholarship made it impossible for Muslim thinkers to remain isolated from any current of world thought. The result has been a proliferation of philosophical voices — some defending the classical traditions, others radically reinterpreting them, others seeking a middle path. Across this chapter and the next (Chapters 28 and 29), we survey the major figures and currents of twentieth- and twenty-first-century Muslim philosophy. Chapter 28 examines the philosophical revival rooted in the classical metaphysics of the seminary (ḥawza) and the university academy: the Traditionalism of Seyyed Hossein Nasr, the epistemological and inductive revolution of Muḥammad Bāqir al-Ṣadr in Najaf, the Ṣadrian realism of Murtaḍā Muṭahharī in Qom and Tehran, and the epistemic decolonization projects of Syed Muhammad Naquib al-Attas and Ismāʿīl Rājī al-Fārūqī. Chapter 29 then turns to scriptural hermeneutics, critical theory, feminist philosophy, trusteeship ethics (Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān), and the emerging field of analytic Islamic philosophy.

1. Traditionalism: Seyyed Hossein Nasr and the Perennial Philosophy The most visible spokesman for the classical Islamic intellectual tradition in the modern West is Seyyed Hossein Nasr (b. 1933). An Iranian philosopher who studied at MIT and Harvard, Nasr returned to Iran and taught at the University of Tehran before the Islamic Revolution forced him into exile in the United States. He is a scholar of Suhrawardī, Mullā Ṣadrā, and Islamic science, and he is the leading exponent of the Traditionalist or Perennialist school associated with the French thinker René Guénon and the Swiss German philosopher Frithjof Schuon. Traditionalism holds that the great religions are diverse manifestations of a single eternal wisdom — the philosophia perennis — which has been revealed in different forms to different peoples across history. The core of this wisdom is metaphysical: the Absolute and Infinite Reality (God, the One) is the source of all relative and finite existence, and the purpose of human life is the return to that source through spiritual discipline, contemplation, and the cultivation of virtue. The modern world, for the Traditionalists, is an anomaly — a civilisation that has severed itself from the divine and is hurtling toward ecological and spiritual catastrophe. The only remedy is the recovery of sacred knowledge and the reintegration of the sciences and society into a theocentric framework. Nasr has argued that the Islamic intellectual tradition — from Avicenna to Mullā Ṣadrā — is precisely such a sacred science, and that its revival is urgently needed. He has written extensively on the environmental crisis, critiquing the desacralisation of nature and calling for a renewed sense of the cosmos as a divine book. He has also engaged in interfaith dialogue, defending the authenticity of multiple religious traditions from a philosophical standpoint. Nasr's Traditionalism has been influential among a segment of Muslim intellectuals in the West and in the Islamic world, particularly in its insistence that modernity is not a neutral framework but a particular, and deeply problematic, metaphysical stance. Critics of Nasr argue that his Traditionalism is ahistorical, romantic, and politically quietist. By idealising the premodern synthesis and dismissing modernity as a monolithic error, it fails to engage with the real intellectual and social challenges of the present. Nasr's defenders reply that the crisis he diagnoses — the spiritual vacuum of a

purely secular and consumerist civilisation — is real, and that the wisdom of the ḥikma tradition is a necessary medicine precisely because it refuses to accept modernity's own terms.

2. Muḥammad Bāqir al-Ṣadr (1935–1980): Epistemology, Subjective Probability, and the Solution to Hume's Problem of Induction While Seyyed Hossein Nasr articulated the Traditionalist vision in Tehran and Washington, an extraordinary philosophical renaissance was unfolding inside the classical Shīʿī seminaries (ḥawzāt ʿilmiyya) of Najaf and Qom. Born in Kāẓimiyya near Baghdad into a celebrated family of scholars and executed in 1980 at the age of forty-five by the regime of Saddam Hussein, Grand Ayatollah Sayyid Muḥammad Bāqir al-Ṣadr (1935–1980) was arguably the most analytically gifted philosopher and legal theorist produced by the twentieth-century Arab seminary tradition. Writing in Najaf during the 1950s and 1960s, when dialectical materialism and Soviet Marxism were sweeping Iraqi universities, the twenty-four-year-old al-Ṣadr published Falsafatunā (Our Philosophy, 1959), followed two years later by his monumental socio-economic treatise Iqtiṣādunā (Our Economics, 1961).

Falsafatunā: Rationalist Realism Against Empiricism and Dialectical Materialism In Falsafatunā, al-Ṣadr conducts a systematic forensic audit of the two dominant philosophical rivals of the twentieth century: Anglo-French positivist empiricism (from John Locke and David Hume to the Vienna Circle) and Marxist-Leninist dialectical materialism. Against radical empiricism, al-Ṣadr demonstrates that sensory experience alone can never generate universal causal laws or the principle of non-contradiction without prior rational first principles (al-awwaliyyāt al-ʿaqliyya); if the principle of causality itself were merely an empirical generalization, any attempt to prove causality from experience would beg the question. Against dialectical materialism, al-Ṣadr dissects the Hegelian-Marxist claim that internal contradiction (tanāquḍ dākhilī) is the engine of motion in nature. Drawing on Mullā Ṣadrā's substantial motion (al-ḥaraka al-jawhariyya), he shows that motion is the continuous ontological passage from potentiality (quwwa) to actuality (fiʿl) — and potentiality and actuality are not simultaneous logical contradictories that violate the law of non-contradiction, but complementary gradations of a dynamic substance.

Al-Usus al-Manṭiqiyya li-l-Istiqrāʾ: Solving the Problem of Induction Al-Ṣadr's crowning purely philosophical achievement is his 1972 treatise al-Usus al-Manṭiqiyya li-l-Istiqrāʾ (The Logical Foundations of Induction), in which he tackled the most notorious problem in modern philosophy of science: David Hume's skeptical challenge to induction. How can observing a finite number of instances (such as a thousand pieces of iron expanding when heated) ever justify a universal causal law or objective certainty about unobserved future cases? Al-Ṣadr rejected both the classical Aristotelian claim that induction relies on a hidden syllogism ('nature always acts uniformly') and Bertrand Russell's and John Maynard Keynes's postulation of unproven a priori postulates. Instead, al-Ṣadr constructed a two-stage epistemological theory of induction (al-madhhab al-dhātī fī al-maʿrifa, 'the Subjectivist-Realist Theory of Knowledge'): 1. The Objective Stage of Deductive Probability Calculus (al-Marḥala al-Tawāldiyya al-Mawḍūʿiyya): Formulating an original axiomatic theory of probability based on an epistemic 'indifference class' of atomic knowledge-states, al-Ṣadr proves through inverse probability (analogous to Bayes's theorem, though derived independently from foundational logical axioms) that each successful experimental observation without a counter-instance exponentially diminishes the probability of accidental coincidence and drives the objective probability of a real causal connection asymptotically toward 1.

2. The Subjective Stage of Epistemic Closure (al-Marḥala al-Tawāldiyya al-Dhātiyya): Even after a million observations, pure mathematical probability remains fractionally short of 1, leaving a tiny gap short of absolute certainty (yaqīn). Al-Ṣadr argues that it is a fundamental natural law of human cognition that the human mind is constitutionally incapable of sustaining serious doubt when the probability of an accidental coincidence shrinks below an infinitesimal threshold; the mind spontaneously bridges the infinitesimal gap and transforms high asymptotic probability into categorical objective certainty. Using this same two-stage inductive mechanism, al-Ṣadr constructs a rigorous modern design proof for the existence of the Wise Creator from the convergent teleological harmonies of the physical cosmos.

3. Murtaḍā Muṭahharī (1919–1979): Epistemological Realism and the Revitalization of Mullā Ṣadrā In Iran, the foremost philosophical collaborator and disciple of ʿAllāma Muḥammad Ḥusayn Ṭabāṭabāʾī (see Chapter 24) was Ayatollah Murtaḍā Muṭahharī (1919–1979), professor of philosophy at the University of Tehran until his assassination in May 1979. When Ṭabāṭabāʾī composed the fourteen philosophical essays of Uṣūl-i Falsafa wa Rawish-i Riʾālīsm (The Principles of Philosophy and the Method of Realism, 1953) to defend Islamic metaphysical realism against European idealism and Soviet materialism, it was Muṭahharī who wrote the monumental fivevolume analytical commentary that brought Mullā Ṣadrā into direct conversation with Descartes, Kant, Hegel, William James, Henri Bergson, and Bertrand Russell. Muṭahharī's most influential philosophical contribution built upon Ṭabāṭabāʾī's distinction between Real Concepts (iʿtibārāt ḥaqīqiyya) and Conventional / Practical Concepts (iʿtibārāt bi-l-maʿnā al-akhaṣṣ — 'ought' propositions constructed for action): Epistemological Realism Against Kantian Constructivism: Analyzing the problem of perceptual error (why a straight stick appears bent in water), Muṭahharī showed that the ability to detect and correct sensory illusions proves rather than refutes the mind's access to objective external reality (nafs al-amr). Divine Justice (ʿAdl-i Ilāhī) and Theodicy: In his celebrated treatise ʿAdl-i Ilāhī, Muṭahharī synthesized Mullā Ṣadrā's ontology of graded existence with a systematic theodicy, arguing that moral and natural evils are privations (aʿdām) inseparable from the existence of a dynamic material world governed by universal causal laws and moral growth. Philosophical Ethics and Social Justice: Against both rigid legal literalism and uncritical Westernization, Muṭahharī grounded Islamic social law in natural law and the teleological perfection of the human soul.

4. Syed Muhammad Naquib al-Attas (b. 1931) and Ismāʿīl Rājī al-Fārūqī (1921–1986): Decolonizing Epistemology and the Islamization of Knowledge Parallel to the seminary revival and Nasr's critique of secular scientism, two major twentieth-century philosophers from Malaysia and Palestine launched systematic programs to decolonize modern education and reconstruct the social and natural sciences on Islamic metaphysical and ethical foundations:

Syed Muhammad Naquib al-Attas: Metaphysics of Islam, Adab, and the Dewesternization of Knowledge Born in Bogor, Java, into a distinguished Baʿalawī Sayyid family and founder of the International Institute of Islamic Thought and Civilization (ISTAC) in Kuala Lumpur, the Malaysian philosopher and metaphysician Syed Muhammad Naquib al-Attas (b. 1931) authored Islam and Secularism (1978) and Prolegomena to the Metaphysics of Islam (1995). Drawing deeply on classical Ashʿarī-Māturīdī kalām, al-Ghazālī, Ibn ʿArabī, Jāmī, and

the Malay-Indonesian philosophical mystics Hamzah Fanṣūrī and Nūr al-Dīn al-Rānīrī, al-Attas argues that knowledge (ʿilm) is never culturally or metaphysically neutral. Every scientific and educational system is infused with a tacit worldview (ruʾyat al-islām li-l-wujūd). For al-Attas, the crisis of the modern Muslim world is fundamentally an epistemological crisis: the loss of adab (fuqdān al-adab). In al-Attas's technical ontology, adab is not mere social etiquette; it is the cognitive and practical recognition of the proper place of every thing in the hierarchical order of being and existence, culminating in the recognition of God's rightful place as Lord. Education in Islam is therefore properly called taʾdīb (instilling adab so as to produce a good human being), rather than mere tarbiya (biological training to produce a compliant citizen of the nation-state). The 'Islamization of Contemporary Knowledge' begins by isolating the secularizing, reductionist elements injected into the modern human and natural sciences and re-anchoring those sciences in a realist ontology of degrees of existence.

Ismāʿīl Rājī al-Fārūqī: Tawḥīd as Methodological Principle and Ethical Axiology Whereas al-Attas grounded his project in Akbarian-Ghazālian metaphysics, the Palestinian-American philosopher Ismāʿīl Rājī al-Fārūqī (1921–1986) — trained in phenomenology and axiology (analyzing Max Scheler and Nicolai Hartmann in his doctoral work on Christian Ethics and On Arabism), professor at Temple University, and cofounder of the International Institute of Islamic Thought (IIIT) in 1981 — approached the Islamization of Knowledge through ethical rationalism and the methodology of Tawḥīd. In Al-Tawḥīd: Its Implications for Thought and Life (1982), al-Fārūqī argued that tawḥīd is not merely a theological creed about God's oneness, but a comprehensive epistemological and methodological principle encompassing five unities: the unity of God, the unity of creation (which guarantees the intelligibility and law-governed regularity of nature), the unity of truth and knowledge (which rules out any ultimate contradiction between revelation waḥy and reason ʿaql), the unity of life and moral responsibility, and the unity of humanity.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “When empirical science observes that a phenomenon B repeatedly follows phenomenon A across thousands of trials under varied conditions, the growth of our certainty in a causal law is neither a deductive syllogism from a pre-existing major premise nor a blind psychological habit, as Hume imagined. Rather, it is an objective deductive accumulation of probability value that converges toward unity, followed by a natural epistemic closure in which the human mind bridges the vanishingly small margin of improbable coincidence to attain rational certainty.” — Muḥammad Bāqir al-Ṣadr, al-Usus al-Manṭiqiyya li-l-Istiqrāʾ (The Logical Foundations of Induction, 1972)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How does Muḥammad Bāqir al-Ṣadr's two-stage theory of induction in al-Usus al-Manṭiqiyya li-l-Istiqrāʾ attempt to overcome David Hume's skeptical critique of causal inference without assuming an unproven a priori postulate of the uniformity of nature? 2. Why did Murtaḍā Muṭahharī and ʿAllāma Ṭabāṭabāʾī distinguish between 'real concepts' (iʿtibārāt ḥaqīqiyya) and 'conventional/practical concepts' (iʿtibārāt ʿamaliyya), and how does this distinction shape their critique of both Kantian idealism and Marxist historical materialism? 3. Evaluate Seyyed Hossein Nasr's thesis in Man and Nature and Knowledge and the Sacred that the modern global ecological crisis is the direct result of the seventeenth-century desacralization of nature and the eclipse of metaphysical wisdom (ḥikma).

4. Compare Syed Muhammad Naquib al-Attas's metaphysics of adab and 'dewesternization of knowledge' with Ismāʿīl Rājī al-Fārūqī's methodology of tawḥīd. Where do these two visions of decolonizing Islamic epistemology converge, and where do they diverge on Sufism and metaphysics?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Al-Ṣadr, Muḥammad Bāqir. Our Philosophy (Falsafatunā). Translated by Shams C. Inati. London: Muhammadi Trust / KPI, 1987. Al-Ṣadr, Muḥammad Bāqir. The Logical Foundations of Induction (al-Usus al-Manṭiqiyya li-l-Istiqrāʾ). Cairo / London: Islamic Philosophy Series, 2019. Ṭabāṭabāʾī, Muḥammad Ḥusayn, and Murtaḍā Muṭahharī. The Principles of Philosophy and the Method of Realism (Uṣūl-i Falsafa wa Rawish-i Riʾālīsm). 5 vols. London: ICAS Press. Nasr, Seyyed Hossein. Knowledge and the Sacred. The Gifford Lectures. Albany: State University of New York Press, 1989. Nasr, Seyyed Hossein. Islamic Philosophy from Its Origin to the Present: Philosophy in the Land of Prophecy. Albany: State University of New York Press, 2006. Al-Attas, Syed Muhammad Naquib. Prolegomena to the Metaphysics of Islam: An Exposition of the Fundamental Elements of the Worldview of Islam. Kuala Lumpur: ISTAC, 1995. Al-Fārūqī, Ismāʿīl Rājī. Al-Tawḥīd: Its Implications for Thought and Life. Herndon, VA: International Institute of Islamic Thought, 1982.

Chapter 29Hermeneutics, Critical Theory, Trusteeship Ethics, and Analytic Islamic Philosophy: Fazlur Rahman, Sharīʿatī, Surūsh, Arkoun, Abū Zayd, Wadud, Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, and the Analytic Turn

1. Fazlur Rahman (1919–1988): From Avicenna and Mullā Ṣadrā to the Double Movement Hermeneutic Alongside the revival of classical metaphysics in the seminary and the Traditionalist critique of secularism, a major stream of late twentieth- and early twenty-first-century Muslim philosophers turned their attention to philosophical hermeneutics, critical social theory, indigenous moral philosophy, and analytic metaphysics. The foundational bridge between classical falsafa and modern ethical hermeneutics was built by Fazlur Rahman (1919–1988). Born in Hazara in present-day Pakistan, educated at Punjab University and Oxford University (where he wrote his doctoral dissertation on Avicenna's Psychology under Simon van den Bergh and Sir Hamilton Gibb), and serving from 1969 until his death in 1988 as Harold H. Swift Distinguished Service Professor of Islamic Thought at the University of Chicago, Rahman produced landmark philological and philosophical studies on Prophecy in Islam: Philosophy and Orthodoxy (1958, analyzing al-Fārābī's and Avicenna's theories of prophetic intellection and imagination) and The Philosophy of Mullā Ṣadrā (1975, the first rigorous English analytical monograph on the Asfār). In the second phase of his career — crystallized in Islam and Modernity: Transformation of an Intellectual Tradition (1982) and Major Themes of the Qur'an (1980) — Rahman diagnosed what he saw as the root failure of both conservative traditionalists and piecemeal modernists: their 'atomistic' (tajzīʾī) approach to scripture, which isolates individual verses from the overarching moral worldview of the Qurʾān. Drawing critically on philosophical

hermeneutics while rejecting subjective relativism, Rahman formulated his celebrated Double Movement Theory of ethical and legal interpretation: 1. The First Movement — From the Present Concrete Situation Back to the Qur'anic Revelation: The interpreter first investigates the concrete historical, socio-economic, and cultural macro-context of seventhcentury Arabia in which a specific Qur'anic ruling was revealed, distilling from those particular historical rulings their underlying universal moral principles, values, and objectives (ratio legis / maqāṣid) — above all, socio-economic justice and human dignity (taqwā). 2. The Second Movement — From those Universal General Principles Back to the Contemporary Present: Having extracted the universal moral objective from its seventh-century historical vessel, the philosopherethicist carefully analyzes the sociological, economic, and institutional realities of the present world in order to embody those universal Qur'anic principles in new concrete legal and social norms.

2. Iranian Intellectuals: Shariati and Soroush The Islamic Revolution of 1979 was one of the most consequential political events of the twentieth century, and it was preceded and followed by a vigorous philosophical debate in Iran about the nature of Islam, society, and the modern state. Two figures embody the poles of this debate: ʿAlī Sharīʿatī (1933–1977), the revolutionary ideologue, and ʿAbd al-Karīm Surūsh (b. 1945), the liberal reformer. ʿAlī Sharīʿatī was a sociologist and activist who studied in Paris and returned to Iran to deliver fiery lectures that drew huge crowds of students and intellectuals. His thought is a synthesis of Shīʿī theology, Third World liberation ideology, and French existentialism. He presented Islam not as a set of rituals but as a revolutionary ideology of justice, liberation, and commitment (taʿahhud). The prophets, he argued, were not contemplative mystics but social revolutionaries who fought against tyranny and exploitation. The Shīʿī Imams, particularly Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī, were martyrs of the struggle for justice. The annual mourning for Ḥusayn was not a passive ritual but a training ground for revolutionary self-sacrifice. Sharīʿatī's philosophy was not systematic, and his academic critics have pointed to his misreadings of history and his selective use of texts. But his impact was enormous. He gave a generation of young Iranians a language with which to fuse their religious identity with their revolutionary aspirations. He died in exile in England under suspicious circumstances, a year before the Revolution he had helped to inspire. At the opposite end of the ideological spectrum stands ʿAbd al-Karīm Surūsh, a philosopher and theologian who has been called the Luther of Islam. Surūsh studied chemistry in Iran and the philosophy of science in London before returning to Iran after the Revolution to teach at the University of Tehran. In the 1990s, he published a series of books and articles that launched a devastating critique of the clerical establishment's claim to absolute religious authority. His key philosophical contribution is the theory of the "contraction and expansion of religious knowledge" (qabḍ wa basṭ-i tiʾūrīk-i sharīʿat). Surūsh argues that religious knowledge is a human, historical product distinct from revelation itself. The Qur'an is divine and unchanging, but our understanding of the Qur'an is shaped by the categories, questions, and sciences of the age in which we live. As human knowledge — philosophy, natural science, social science — evolves, so too does our interpretation of scripture. There is no such thing as a fixed, final, absolute theology. All religious knowledge is provisional, fallible, and subject to revision. This is not to deny the truth of religion but to recognise that our access to that truth is always mediated by human reason and culture.

The political conclusion Surūsh draws is that no one — no cleric, no jurist, no institution — can claim to possess the definitive interpretation of Islam. Religious authority must be democratised. The state must not enforce a particular theology. The religious community is a community of interpretation, in which all believers have a right to participate. Surūsh's ideas have been profoundly influential among Iranian reformers and among Muslim intellectuals globally, and they have earned him exile, threats, and ongoing controversy. Iranian intellectual life since the Revolution has been astonishingly rich. Thinkers like Muḥammad Mujtahid Shabistarī have drawn on Heidegger and Gadamer to argue for a hermeneutical approach to the Qur'an. Muḥsin Kadīvar has developed philosophical arguments for democracy from within the tradition of Shīʿī jurisprudence. The Islamic Republic, far from extinguishing philosophy, created the conditions for a philosophical renaissance — often in opposition to the state itself.

3. Arab and Francophone Critics: Arkoun and Abu Zayd In the Arab world and in Francophone North Africa, another group of intellectuals applied the tools of modern critical theory — structuralism, poststructuralism, anthropology, hermeneutics — to the Islamic tradition. Two of the most important are Mohammed Arkoun (1928–2010), an Algerian-born professor at the Sorbonne, and Naṣr Ḥāmid Abū Zayd (1943–2010), an Egyptian scholar who spent the last years of his life in exile in the Netherlands. Mohammed Arkoun called his project "Islamic reason and the critique of Islamic reason," deliberately echoing Kant. He argued that the classical Islamic intellectual tradition had become closed — that it had erected a set of orthodoxies that blocked genuine critical thought about the historical formation of the tradition. He proposed to "deconstruct" the Islamic heritage, using the tools of linguistics, anthropology, and history to uncover the historical conditions under which the texts were produced and to show that they were the products of human struggles for power and meaning, not simply a timeless divine deposit. Arkoun distinguished between the "Qur'anic discourse" — the original oral revelation experienced by the Prophet Muḥammad — and the "closed official corpus" that later generations constructed. The closed corpus, he argued, had been elevated to a status that silenced critical inquiry. The task of the modern Muslim intellectual was to reopen the corpus, to rethink the unthought, to recover the plural voices that had been suppressed by orthodoxy. Arkoun's work is dense and difficult, and his audience has been primarily academic, but his challenge to the silence of the tradition has been a permanent provocation. Naṣr Ḥāmid Abū Zayd was a literary critic and Qur'anic scholar who applied the methods of hermeneutics and semiotics to the Qur'an. He argued that the Qur'an, as a text in the Arabic language, must be studied as a literary and historical document. It employed the linguistic conventions of its time, it was addressed to a specific audience, and its meanings are not exhausted by a single literal reading. The Qur'an is a "message" that becomes a "text" in the act of interpretation, and interpretation is always conditioned by the interpreter's historical situation. Abū Zayd's most controversial claim was that the Qur'an, once revealed, entered the realm of human culture and could be studied using the methods of the humanities. This claim was held by an Egyptian court, in 1995, to constitute apostasy, and Abū Zayd was forcibly divorced from his wife (under Egyptian law, a Muslim woman cannot be married to an apostate). He and his wife left Egypt for the Netherlands, where he taught at Leiden University until his death. The case became an international symbol of the persecution of free thought in the Muslim world, and Abū Zayd's work — Mafhūm al-Naṣṣ (The Concept of the Text) — is studied widely as an example of an intellectually rigorous and existentially courageous Islamic humanism.

4. Feminist Reconsiderations: Mernissi and Wadud Feminist Muslim thinkers have transformed the intellectual landscape by asking a question that classical philosophy and theology had largely ignored: what does Islam require and promise for women? Their work is both a critique of patriarchal interpretations of Islam and a constructive retrieval of egalitarian resources within the Qur'an and the prophetic tradition. Fatema Mernissi (1940–2015) was a Moroccan sociologist and feminist whose Beyond the Veil and The Veil and the Male Elite are foundational texts of Islamic feminism. She analysed the historical construction of gender roles in Islamic societies, arguing that the Prophet Muḥammad's original message was radically egalitarian and that the restrictions on women were products of later social and political conditions, not of revelation itself. Mernissi used the tools of social science and critical history to deconstruct the patriarchal narratives that had become enshrined as orthodoxy, and she called for a reclamation of women's agency within an Islamic framework. Amina Wadud (b. 1952) is an African-American Muslim theologian whose Qur'an and Woman is a landmark of feminist Qur'anic interpretation. She reads the Qur'an through the lens of gender justice, arguing that the text's foundational principles — the equal creation of men and women from a single soul, the equal moral accountability of both sexes, and the responsibility of all believers to enjoin right and forbid wrong — require the rejection of patriarchy. Wadud does not discard the Qur'an; she excavates it, finding within it a message of liberation that later exegetes buried. In 2005, she led a mixed-gender Friday prayer congregation in New York City, an act that provoked intense controversy and solidified her place as a public philosopher of gender and authority. The work of these and other feminist thinkers — including Riffat Hassan, Asma Barlas, and Kecia Ali — has forced a fundamental reconsideration of the philosophical anthropology of Islam. They have argued that the traditional hierarchical metaphysics, in which man is the summit of creation and woman is a derivative, has no necessary foundation in the Qur'an and is a cultural imposition that has distorted the ethical core of the tradition. Their work is not a footnote to Islamic philosophy; it is a transformative contribution to the ongoing reconstruction of Muslim thought.

5. Marxist, Liberationist, and Critical Engagements The contemporary Muslim engagement with modernity has not been limited to liberal reformism, traditionalism, or feminist hermeneutics. A significant stream of Muslim thought has drawn on Marxist, liberationist, and critical theory traditions to articulate a vision of social justice and political transformation grounded in Islamic principles. This current is often ignored in surveys of Islamic philosophy, which tend to focus on the liberal and traditionalist poles, but it represents a vital and creative intellectual movement that has had considerable influence in the Muslim world. Maḥmūd Amīn al-ʿĀlim (1922–2009) was an Egyptian philosopher and intellectual who engaged deeply with Marxist thought while remaining rooted in his Islamic and Arab identity. His major work, al-Muʿjiza aw alMaḥkama al-Kubrā (The Miracle or the Great Tribunal), is a philosophical treatise that defends the rationality of revelation and the possibility of a rational faith against both secular materialism and traditionalist obscurantism. Al-ʿĀlim was not a Marxist in any orthodox sense, but he drew on Marxist historical analysis and critical theory to critique the social and economic injustices of the modern Arab world. His work is a reminder that the engagement with European philosophy in the modern Islamic world has been far more diverse than the standard narrative of "liberalism vs. fundamentalism" suggests.

Anwar ʿAbd al-Malik (1924–2012) was a major Egyptian philosopher and sociologist whose work engaged with Marxist theory, dependency theory, and the critique of capitalism. He wrote extensively on the philosophy of history, the sociology of culture, and the political economy of the Arab world. His work is not explicitly Islamic in a religious sense, but it is grounded in a deep understanding of the Islamic heritage and the challenges facing Muslim societies in the modern era. He represents a secular, leftist current in Arab thought that has been largely forgotten by scholars of Islamic philosophy but that is essential for understanding the full range of intellectual life in the Muslim world. Ali Shariati was discussed above in the context of Iranian intellectuals, but his thought belongs equally in the Marxist-liberationist context. Shariati synthesised Shīʿī theology with a Marxist-inspired analysis of class struggle and exploitation, presenting Islam as a revolutionary ideology of liberation. His influence on the Iranian Revolution and on subsequent movements of Islamic social justice has been immense. The Palestinian liberation theology movement — particularly the work of theologians like Naim Ateek (an Anglican priest, but drawing on Islamic concepts of justice) and the broader discourse of sumūd (steadfastness) — has engaged with the religious dimensions of the struggle for justice in the Holy Land. While not strictly Islamic philosophy, this discourse has influenced Muslim thinkers who see in the Palestinian cause a paradigm of the Qur'anic command to "stand firm in justice" (Q 4:135). The Nigerian scholar and activist Ibrahim El-Zakzaky and the Islamic Movement of Nigeria, though highly controversial, represent a stream of contemporary Islamic political thought that draws on both Shīʿī and Sunnī traditions of social justice and resistance to oppression. Their intellectual framework is grounded in a reading of the Qur'an and the prophetic tradition that emphasises the liberation of the oppressed (mustaḍʿafūn) as a central religious imperative. These and other currents of Muslim thought — the critical theory of the Frankfurt School as engaged by Muslim intellectuals like Jalal al-e Ahmad (1923–1969) in Iran, the anti-colonial philosophy of Frantz Fanon as read through an Islamic lens, the Latin American liberation theology that has influenced some Muslim thinkers in the Philippines and Indonesia — are part of the global conversation that constitutes contemporary Islamic philosophy. They deserve a place in any comprehensive survey, not because one must agree with their conclusions but because they are part of the living, contested, and creative tradition of Muslim thought.

6. Turkish New Thought and Western Integration Turkey, with its unique history of secularisation and its ongoing negotiation between Islam and the West, has produced a distinct philosophical culture. In the early republican period, thinkers like Nurettin Topçu (1909–1975) attempted to fuse Islamic spirituality with a communitarian and anti-capitalist critique of modernity. Topçu, influenced by French existentialism and the thought of Henri Bergson, argued that the modern nation-state had destroyed the organic spiritual community and that a renewed Islamic ethics could ground a just social order. In the late twentieth and early twenty-first centuries, Turkish philosophers have increasingly engaged with continental and analytic philosophy. The journal İslâm Araştırmaları Dergisi and centres such as the İSAM foundation have produced scholarly work on the Islamic philosophical heritage while also addressing topics like the philosophy of science, bioethics, and political theory. Turkish diasporic intellectuals in Germany, such as Navid Kermani, have explored the aesthetic and spiritual dimensions of the Qur'an, bringing a literary-philosophical sensibility to bear on the sacred text.

The broader phenomenon of Muslims living in the West — whether as migrants, refugees, or converts — has created a new intellectual reality: a "Western Islam" that is being philosophically articulated in English, French, and German, and that is in constant dialogue with secularism, liberalism, feminism, and postcolonial theory. Thinkers like Tariq Ramadan have attempted to define a European Muslim identity that is both fully Muslim and fully European, drawing on the resources of Islamic jurisprudence and ethics to address the challenges of citizenship, pluralism, and secular law. The philosophical conversation is no longer dominated by the categories of the madrasa; it is happening in universities, seminars, and online forums, and it is producing a global Islamic philosophy that is still, in many ways, finding its shape.

7. Analytic Islamic Philosophy: The New Frontier One of the most significant developments in the contemporary academic world is the emergence of what has been called "analytic Islamic philosophy" — an approach that brings the tools and methods of analytic philosophy to bear on the Islamic philosophical tradition. This is not merely a matter of studying Avicenna or al-Ghazālī using the techniques of modern logic and conceptual analysis; it is a constructive philosophical project that seeks to articulate Islamic philosophical positions in dialogue with contemporary analytic metaphysics, epistemology, philosophy of religion, and philosophy of mind. The work of Mohammad Saleh Zarepour on Avicenna's philosophy of time and modality is exemplary. His book Avicenna on Time (2023) is a rigorous analysis of Avicenna's theory of time, causation, and the relationship between the temporal and the eternal, using the resources of contemporary analytic philosophy to clarify and evaluate Avicenna's arguments. Jari Kaukua's work on Avicennan psychology and its implications for the philosophy of mind is similarly important. Seyed N. Mousavian and others have engaged with Avicennan logic and semantics in dialogue with contemporary formal logic. The Oxford Studies in Medieval Philosophy and journals like Vivarium, Arabic Sciences and Philosophy, and Philosophy East and West regularly publish work in this vein. The analytic approach has its critics. Some scholars worry that it decontextualises Islamic philosophy, stripping it of its historical, religious, and cultural specificity in the name of a "universal" rationality that is, in fact, the rationality of the contemporary Anglophone academy. Others argue that the analytic method is particularly well suited to the argumentative, logical, and systematic character of much of the Islamic philosophical tradition, and that it enables a genuine philosophical engagement that is more than mere historical exposition. Both positions have merit, and the debate between them is itself a sign of the vitality of the field. What is clear is that analytic Islamic philosophy has opened new avenues for the tradition. It has made Avicenna and his successors participants in contemporary debates about causation, modality, consciousness, and the nature of God. It has trained a new generation of scholars who are equally at home in the texts of the classical tradition and the arguments of contemporary philosophy. It has created a space in which Islamic philosophy can be studied not only as a historical artefact but as a living contribution to the philosophy of the present. For the future of Islamic philosophy, this is an encouraging sign. The tradition has always been defined by its encounter with other traditions — Greek, Persian, Indian, Christian, and Jewish. The encounter with contemporary analytic philosophy is simply the latest chapter in a long history of creative engagement. If the past is any guide, that encounter will produce new syntheses, new arguments, and new insights that we can only begin to imagine.

8. Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān (Taha Abdurrahman, b. 1944): Critique of Derivative Modernity and the Philosophy of Trusteeship (Fiqh al-Iʾtimān) Widely recognized across the contemporary Arab world as its most original living systematic philosopher, the Moroccan logician, philosopher of language, and moral theorist Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān (b. 1944) — educated at Mohammed V University in Rabat and the Sorbonne, where he wrote two doctoral dissertations on the philosophy of language and the logic of natural argumentation — mounted a profound two-front critique against both Western secular modernity and what he terms the 'imitative rationalism' of modern Arab secularists (such as Muḥammad ʿĀbid al-Jābirī and Arkoun). In Tajdīd al-Manhaj fī Taqwīm al-Turāth (Renewing the Method for Evaluating the Heritage, 1994), al-Ḥaqq al-ʿArabī fī al-Ikhtilāf al-Falsafī (The Arab Right to Philosophical Difference, 2002), Suʾāl al-Akhlāq (The Question of Ethics, 2000), and Rūḥ al-Ḥadātha (The Spirit of Modernity, 2006), Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān advances three interconnected philosophical doctrines: 1. The Spirit of Modernity vs. Its Western Historical Application: One must distinguish between the universal 'Spirit of Modernity' (rūḥ al-ḥadātha — based on maturity/autonomy, critical inquiry, and universality) and the particular 'Western Reality of Modernity' (wāqiʿ al-ḥadātha), which deformed that spirit by severing reason from revelation, reducing nature to an object of domination, and stripping ethics of transcendence. Authentic Islamic modernity cannot be achieved by translating French or German secularism into Arabic; it must grow endogenously from within Islamic ethical and linguistic categories. 2. The Three Ranks of Reason (al-ʿAql al-Mujarrad, al-ʿAql al-Musaddad, and al-ʿAql al-Muʾayyad): Against the impoverished Enlightenment reduction of reason to 'Abstract / Instrumental Reason' (al-ʿaql al-mujarrad — which calculates means without moral ends), Ṭāhā develops a three-tier epistemology: above Abstract Reason stands 'Guided Reason' (al-ʿaql al-musaddad, reason oriented by ethical action and divine law), and at the summit stands 'Divinely Supported / Expanded Reason' (al-ʿaql al-muʾayyad, reason illuminated by spiritual purification and living moral experience, drawing on al-Ghazālī and Sufi praxis). 3. Trusteeship Ethics (al-Iʾtimāniyya / Fiqh al-Iʾtimān): Against René Descartes's 'I think, therefore I am' — which reduces the human essence to solitary theoretical cognition — Ṭāhā argues that the defining universal property of humanity is not abstract logic but ethicity (al-akhlāqiyya): 'There is no human without ethics, and no ethics without religion.' Grounded in the Primordial Covenant (al-mīthāq, Q 7:172) and the Offering of the Trust (al-amāna, Q 33:72), the Trusteeship Paradigm holds that we do not possess absolute sovereign ownership (tamalluk) over our bodies, wealth, political power, or the earth; everything we hold is a divine deposit (wadīʿa / amāna) entrusted to us for the care of creation.

9. A Living Conversation This survey of contemporary voices has been necessarily brief, and many important figures have been omitted. What it demonstrates is that Muslim philosophy is not a closed canon but a living and contentious conversation. The debates of today — over the nature of religious knowledge, the relationship between Islam and the state, the rights of women, the meaning of secularism, and the possibility of a global Islamic humanism — are extensions of the debates that have run through this entire book. The Muʿtazila asked what reason can know of God; today's thinkers ask what reason can know of the text. Al-Fārābī asked how the philosopher should relate to the city; today's thinkers ask how the Muslim should relate to the secular state. Ibn Sīnā asked how God creates; today's thinkers ask how God creates in a world of quantum mechanics and evolution. The questions evolve, but the

philosophical impulse — to seek truth through reason, to reconcile faith and intellect, to live wisely — remains constant. The final chapter turns to the ethical and political legacy of this long tradition: the living inheritance of Islamic philosophical ethics, the ideals of the just city, the challenges of democracy and pluralism, and the urgent task of constructing a Muslim philosophy for a globalised and ecologically fragile world. 1. The Spirit of Modernity vs. Its Western Historical Application: One must distinguish between the universal 'Spirit of Modernity' (rūḥ al-ḥadātha — based on maturity/autonomy, critical inquiry, and universality) and the particular 'Western Reality of Modernity' (wāqiʿ al-ḥadātha), which deformed that spirit by severing reason from revelation, reducing nature to an object of domination, and stripping ethics of transcendence. Authentic Islamic modernity cannot be achieved by translating French or German secularism into Arabic; it must grow endogenously from within Islamic ethical and linguistic categories. 2. The Three Ranks of Reason (al-ʿAql al-Mujarrad, al-ʿAql al-Musaddad, and al-ʿAql al-Muʾayyad): Against the impoverished Enlightenment reduction of reason to 'Abstract / Instrumental Reason' (al-ʿaql al-mujarrad — which calculates means without moral ends), Ṭāhā develops a three-tier epistemology: above Abstract Reason stands 'Guided Reason' (al-ʿaql al-musaddad, reason oriented by ethical action and divine law), and at the summit stands 'Divinely Supported / Expanded Reason' (al-ʿaql al-muʾayyad, reason illuminated by spiritual purification and living moral experience, drawing on al-Ghazālī and Sufi praxis). 3. Trusteeship Ethics (al-Iʾtimāniyya / Fiqh al-Iʾtimān): Against René Descartes's 'I think, therefore I am' — which reduces the human essence to solitary theoretical cognition — Ṭāhā argues that the defining universal property of humanity is not abstract logic but ethicity (al-akhlāqiyya): 'There is no human without ethics, and no ethics without religion.' Grounded in the Primordial Covenant (al-mīthāq, Q 7:172) and the Offering of the Trust (al-amāna, Q 33:72), the Trusteeship Paradigm holds that we do not possess absolute sovereign ownership (tamalluk) over our bodies, wealth, political power, or the earth; everything we hold is a divine deposit (wadīʿa / amāna) entrusted to us for the care of creation.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “Western modernity confused the universal Spirit of Modernity — which calls humanity out of blind tutelage into critical maturity and creative renewal — with its own defective historical application, which severed reason from transcendent moral trusteeship. True philosophical creativity begins only when we transition from 'Abstract Reason' (al-ʿaql al-mujarrad), which knows how to manipulate things while forgetting their moral worth, to 'Guided and Supported Reason' (al-ʿaql al-musaddad wa-l-muʾayyad), which recognizes that the cosmos is a Trust (amāna) placed in our care.” — Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, Rūḥ al-Ḥadātha (The Spirit of Modernity, 2006)

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. Explain the two steps of Fazlur Rahman's 'Double Movement' hermeneutical theory. How does it attempt to avoid both ahistorical literalism on the one hand and arbitrary secular relativism on the other? 2. How does ʿAbd al-Karīm Surūsh's distinction between 'religion itself' (dīn) and 'human religious knowledge' (maʿrifat-i dīnī) draw upon modern philosophy of science, and what traditional theological objections have Iranian seminary scholars raised against it? 3. Why does Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān argue that 'ethicity' (al-akhlāqiyya) rather than abstract rationality (alʿaqlāniyya al-mujarrada) is the defining essence of the human being, and how does his hierarchy of Abstract, Guided, and Supported Reason critique Enlightenment philosophy?

4. In what ways have twenty-first-century analytic philosophers used Avicenna's Flying Man argument, Suhrawardī's knowledge by presence (al-ʿilm al-ḥuḍūrī), and the post-classical concept of nafs al-amr to address contemporary problems in philosophy of mind and truth-maker theory?

Primary Texts in Translation & Further Reading Rahman, Fazlur. Islam and Modernity: Transformation of an Intellectual Tradition. Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 1982. Rahman, Fazlur. Reason, Freedom, and Democracy in Islam: Essential Writings of Abdolkarim Soroush. Translated and edited by Mahmoud Sadri and Ahmad Sadri. Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2000. Arkoun, Mohammed. The Unthought in Contemporary Islamic Thought. London: Saqi Books, 2002. Wadud, Amina. Qur'an and Woman: Rereading the Sacred Text from a Woman's Perspective. 2nd ed. Oxford: Oxford University Press, 1999. Hallaq, Wael B. Reforming Modernity: Ethics and the New Human in the Philosophy of Abdurrahman Taha. New York: Columbia University Press, 2019. Chapter 30Islamic Ethics and Political Philosophy: A Living Tradition

The Unfinished City

From the moment al-Fārābī imagined the Virtuous City, ruled by a philosopher-prophet who translated demonstrative truth into laws and symbols for the multitude, Islamic philosophy has been inseparable from the question of how human beings should live together. The ethical and political tradition that began with al-Kindī's therapy of the soul and al-Fārābī's hierarchy of regimes did not end with the classical period. It continued through Ibn Miskawayh's refinement of character, al-Ṭūsī's household management, Ibn Khaldūn's cycles of civilisation, Shāh Walī Allāh's theory of the just caliphate, and Iqbāl's democratic ijtihād. Today, in a world of nation-states, global capitalism, climate crisis, and unprecedented pluralism, the central questions of Islamic political and ethical philosophy are more pressing than ever. Can the ideals of the virtuous city be translated into the language of modern democracy? What does the Sharīʿa's vision of justice require in an age of human rights and international law? What does it mean to be a steward (khalīfa) of a planet in peril? This chapter explores the living tradition of Islamic ethics and political philosophy, tracing its continuities and its contemporary applications.

1. From the Perfect City to Modern Pluralism Al-Fārābī's virtuous city was governed by a single vision of the good life, known with certainty by the ruling philosopher and communicated through religion to the masses. Ibn Rushd's defence of philosophy rested on a sharp distinction between the demonstrative elite and the rhetorical multitude. This classical model assumed a

society in which religious and philosophical truth coincided, and in which the ruler was responsible for the spiritual and moral formation of the citizens. It was, in a sense, a theocracy of reason. Modern Muslim political thought has had to grapple with the reality of pluralism: the fact that nation-states include multiple religious communities, that even within Muslim societies there is deep disagreement about the meaning of Islam, and that the global order is built on the principle of state sovereignty rather than prophetic authority. The challenge has been to articulate a political philosophy that is authentically Islamic but that can function in a world of difference. Several responses have emerged. One, represented by the thinkers of the Muslim Brotherhood and the Pakistani Islamist Abū al-Aʿlā Mawdūdī, has been to call for the restoration of an Islamic state governed by the Sharīʿa, in which non-Muslims are protected but not equal, and in which secularism is rejected as a Western import. Another, represented by liberal reformers like ʿAbduh, Surūsh, and the Sudanese thinker Mahmoud Mohamed Taha, has argued that the true spirit of the Sharīʿa is democratic, egalitarian, and pluralistic, and that an Islamic state must guarantee equal citizenship, freedom of conscience, and the peaceful rotation of power. A third, more pragmatic position, articulated by historical experience as much as by theory, is the acceptance of the modern nation-state as a de facto framework within which Muslims can live according to their faith without aspiring to clerical rule. The Qur'anic concept of shūrā (consultation) has been a central resource for democratic arguments. Shūrā is commanded in the Qur'an (Q 42:38) as a principle of communal decision-making, and modern Muslim democrats have argued that it provides the religious basis for representative government. The classical caliphate, they suggest, was not a divine form of government but a historically conditioned institution; its essential principles — justice, consultation, accountability — can be realised through democratic mechanisms. This reinterpretation has not been uncontested, but it has become a major strand of contemporary Muslim political philosophy, advanced by thinkers like Khaled Abou El Fadl, who argues that the Qur'anic vision is one of a "faith-based democratic state" in which law is grounded in a substantive moral vision but is produced through the free deliberation of the community.

2. Human Rights and the Maqāṣid al-Sharīʿa One of the central legal and philosophical debates of the modern Islamic world has been the relationship between Islamic law and international human rights. Critics charge that elements of classical Sharīʿa — the penal laws, the unequal status of women and non-Muslims, the prohibition of apostasy — are incompatible with the Universal Declaration of Human Rights. Defenders respond that the Sharīʿa, properly understood, was the original charter of human dignity and that its historical formulations can and must be reformed to meet contemporary standards. The key conceptual tool in this debate has been the maqāṣid al-sharīʿa — the purposes or objectives of the divine law. Classical jurists, particularly al-Ghazālī and al-Shāṭibī, identified five universal purposes of the Sharīʿa: the protection of religion (dīn), life (nafs), intellect (ʿaql), lineage or family (nasl), and property (māl). All specific legal rulings, on this theory, are means to these ends, and when a ruling frustrates its purpose, it ought to be reinterpreted or replaced. Modern philosophers and jurists have expanded the list of maqāṣid to include freedom, justice, equality, human dignity, and environmental protection. The Tunisian scholar Muḥammad al-Ṭāhir ibn ʿĀshūr (1879–1973), in his magisterial Maqāṣid al-Sharīʿa al-Islāmiyya, argued that the purpose of the Sharīʿa is human welfare and that the law must evolve in response to the changing conditions of society. The Malaysian philosopher Syed Muhammad Naquib al-Attas and the Iranian philosopher Muhammad Taqi Misbah Yazdi, from different perspectives, have sought to articulate an Islamic conception of human nature and the good that can ground a distinctive account of

human rights — one that acknowledges the divine source of moral obligation while affirming the dignity and inviolability of the human person. The tension between the universal claims of human rights and the particularity of Islamic law remains unresolved, but the maqāṣid approach has created a space for constructive dialogue. It allows Muslim thinkers to argue that the principles of the Sharīʿa are in harmony with the core values of human rights, even if the historical formulations of the law lag behind. The philosophical task is to articulate the metaphysical foundation of human dignity — the idea that every human being is a khalīfa of God, a locus of divine care and responsibility — and to show that this foundation both grounds and goes beyond the secular concept of rights.

3. Environmental Ethics and the Stewardship Paradigm The ecological crisis has become, in the twenty-first century, the most urgent moral and philosophical challenge facing humanity, and Muslim thinkers have begun to articulate an Islamic environmental ethics that draws on the deep resources of the philosophical and mystical traditions. The core concept is khalīfa — the human being as God's steward or vicegerent on earth. The Qur'anic verse "It is He who has made you successors (khalāʾif) upon the earth" (Q 6:165) has become a foundational text for an Islamic ecotheology. The stewardship paradigm implies that the natural world is not a resource to be exploited without limit but a trust (amāna) from God, for which human beings will be held accountable. Every creature, from the largest galaxy to the smallest ant, is a sign (āya) of God and a community (umma) like ourselves. The destruction of species, the pollution of the seas, and the destabilisation of the climate are not merely imprudent; they are violations of the divine order and the rights of other creatures. Classical Sufi metaphysics, with its vision of the cosmos as a living, breathing manifestation of the divine names, provides a spiritual foundation for this ethics that is both intellectually robust and emotionally compelling. Thinkers like Seyyed Hossein Nasr, Fazlun Khalid, and Ibrahim Özdemir have been at the forefront of articulating this environmental philosophy. Nasr, in works like Religion and the Order of Nature, has argued that the ecological crisis is ultimately a crisis of the modern desacralised worldview, and that Islam, with its emphasis on the unity of being and the integration of science and spirituality, offers a necessary corrective. The Islamic Foundation for Ecology and Environmental Sciences (IFEES) has worked to translate these principles into practical action, from reforestation projects in East Africa to the promotion of renewable energy. The philosophical challenge is to integrate the stewardship paradigm with the demands of economic development and the reality of global climate politics. But the resources are there, in the Qur'an and in the tradition of ḥikma, and the work of building a Muslim environmental philosophy is one of the most promising frontiers of contemporary Islamic thought.

4. Islamic Bioethics: The Maqāṣid Approach to Medicine and Technology One of the most vibrant areas of contemporary Islamic practical philosophy is bioethics, a field that engages directly with the maqāṣid al-sharīʿa framework and that demonstrates the continuing relevance of the philosophical tradition to pressing contemporary questions. Muslim philosophers, jurists, and physicians have produced a substantial body of work on issues ranging from abortion, contraception, and assisted reproduction to organ transplantation, genetic engineering, end-of-life care, and the ethical implications of artificial intelligence in medicine. The field is not a mere application of pre-modern legal rulings but a creative philosophical engagement with new technologies and new moral dilemmas, grounded in the principles of the Sharīʿa and in the ethical vision of the philosophical tradition.

The maqāṣid framework provides the structural foundation. The preservation of life (ḥifẓ al-nafs), the protection of lineage (ḥifẓ al-nasl), and the promotion of human welfare (maṣlaḥa) are the guiding principles. But the application of these principles to specific cases requires a sophisticated philosophical anthropology: what is a human being? When does personhood begin? What is the relationship between body and soul? What constitutes death? The Islamic philosophical tradition offers rich resources for addressing these questions. Avicenna's psychology, with its account of the soul's emergence from the body and its gradual actualisation, provides a framework for thinking about the moral status of the embryo. Mullā Ṣadrā's doctrine of substantial motion, with its vision of the human being as a dynamic, evolving reality, suggests a graduated account of personhood that is both philosophically coherent and religiously grounded. Ibn ʿArabī's doctrine of the Perfect Human and the divine breath (nafkh) provides a spiritual anthropology that can inform discussions of end-of-life care and the meaning of suffering. The Islamic Organization for Medical Sciences, the Islamic Fiqh Academy, and various national bioethics committees have issued numerous rulings and guidelines on these questions. The debates are robust. On organ transplantation, most Sunnī jurists permit it on the grounds of maṣlaḥa and the principle of charity, while some traditionalists insist on the sanctity of the body and the prohibition of mutilation. On assisted reproduction, the majority permit in-vitro fertilisation using the gametes of a married couple but prohibit third-party donation on the grounds of lineage and the sanctity of marriage. On genetic engineering, the debates centre on whether the technology is a form of interference in God's creation (taghyīr khalq Allāh) or a legitimate extension of the healing mission of medicine. On end-of-life care, the debate is between those who permit the withdrawal of futile treatment and those who insist on the absolute sanctity of life, even in terminal illness. These debates are not merely legalistic; they raise philosophical questions about the nature of human agency, the limits of human intervention in the natural order, and the meaning of divine sovereignty over life and death. The Islamic bioethics tradition, at its best, integrates the insights of the falāsifa, the mutakallimūn, and the Sufis into a coherent ethical framework that is both faithful to revelation and responsive to the challenges of modern medicine. The literature is vast and growing, and it deserves a more prominent place in the study of Islamic philosophy than it currently receives.

5. Economic Justice and the Philosophy of the Good Life The ethical tradition of Islam has never separated individual virtue from social justice. The Qur'an's condemnation of ribā (usury), its insistence on zakāt (obligatory alms) and ṣadaqa (voluntary charity), and its recurring demand that wealth not circulate only among the rich — all these provide a moral framework for economic life that is both critical of unfettered capitalism and attentive to the dignity of labour and property. The philosophical task is to articulate a vision of economic justice that is consistent with the principles of the Sharīʿa and that addresses the realities of the modern global economy. Thinkers like the Pakistani economist Syed Nawab Haider Naqvi have formulated an "Islamic economics" that incorporates goals of efficiency, equity, and freedom within an ethical framework derived from the Qur'an. The focus is not only on the prohibition of interest but also on the positive obligation to create a productive, just, and sustainable economy that serves the maqāṣid of life, intellect, and family. The rise of Islamic finance — banking, investment, and insurance conducted according to Sharīʿa principles — is a practical expression of this vision, though its critics argue that it has often been reduced to a technical compliance exercise rather than a genuine transformation of economic values.

The deeper philosophical question is what it means, in Islamic terms, to live well — not merely to avoid prohibited transactions but to cultivate the soul in the midst of material activity. Ibn Miskawayh's ethics of the middle way, alGhazālī's critique of the love of wealth, and the Sufi tradition's emphasis on contentment (qanāʿa) provide a rich counterpoint to the consumerist ethic of global capitalism. The challenge for contemporary Muslim philosophy is to bring these classical insights into a constructive dialogue with the modern disciplines of economics, sociology, and public policy, without losing the spiritual core that makes them distinctive.

6. The Perennial Task of Philosophy Islamic ethics and political philosophy is not a set of solutions to be implemented but a tradition of questioning that demands continual renewal. The questions that al-Fārābī asked — what is the good life? what is the just regime? what is the purpose of human association? — are as urgent today as they were in tenth-century Baghdad. The answers that the tradition offers are not templates to be copied but resources to be reinterpreted. The living tradition is visible in the work of jurists who struggle to apply the maqāṣid to new biomedical technologies, in the activism of environmentalists who ground their advocacy in Qur'anic language, in the arguments of feminists who claim the egalitarian core of the prophetic message, and in the quiet reflections of ordinary Muslims who ask how the wisdom of the past can illuminate the present. Philosophy, in Islam, has never been only a matter of the mind. It has always been a discipline of the soul, a way of seeing the world and living in it. The ethical and political philosophy of Islam is the practical culmination of the metaphysical and epistemological journey that this book has traced. To think, for a Muslim philosopher, is to be accountable. To know is to be transformed. To live wisely is to return, at last, to the source of all wisdom.

In the Philosopher's Own Words (Primary-Source Excerpt) “The Revealed Law (al-sharīʿa) was instituted in its entirety for the realization of the universal benefits of human beings (maṣāliḥ al-ʿibād) in the immediate life and the hereafter; wherever justice, mercy, wisdom, and universal human welfare are found, there stands the Law of God.” — Abū Isḥāq al-Shāṭibī, Al-Muwāfaqāt fī Uṣūl al-Sharīʿa, and Ibn Qayyim al-Jawziyya, Iʿlām al-Muwaqqiʿīn

Seminar Discussion Questions 1. How did Abū Isḥāq al-Shāṭibī (d. 1388) systematize the theory of the Higher Purposes of the Law (maqāṣid alsharīʿa — preserving religion, life, intellect, lineage/dignity, and property) earlier articulated by al-Juwaynī and al-Ghazālī, and how do contemporary Muslim ethicists apply this framework to human rights? 2. Compare the political philosophies of Abū al-Aʿlā Mawdūdī ('theodemocracy' and divine sovereignty, ḥākimiyya), ʿAlī ʿAbd al-Rāziq (the separation of prophetic religion from temporal kingship), and contemporary theorists of Islamic constitutionalism and shūrā (such as Rāshid al-Ghannūshī and Abdullahi Ahmed AnNa'im). 3. How do classical Islamic philosophical concepts — khalīfa (moral stewardship of the earth), mīzān (cosmic balance), āyāt (natural phenomena as sacred signs of God), and ḥimā (protected ecological zones) — ground contemporary Islamic environmental philosophy? 4. In contemporary Islamic bioethics, how do classical philosophical debates between Avicenna, al-Ghazālī, and Mullā Ṣadrā regarding the ensoulment of the fetus (nafkhat al-rūḥ) and the departure of the soul inform legal and ethical rulings on organ transplantation, brain death, and assisted reproductive technologies?

EpilogueThe Future of Islamic Philosophy: A Global Perspective

A Tradition in Motion

We have travelled from the first translations of Aristotle into Arabic to the philosophical poetry of Iqbāl, from the syllogisms of al-Fārābī to the illuminations of Suhrawardī, from the critiques of al-Ghazālī and Ibn Taymiyya to the verifications of Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, from the feminist hermeneutics of Wadud to the analytic metaphysics of the new generation. The story is not over. The future of Islamic philosophy is being written now, in the classrooms of Qom and Kuala Lumpur, in the seminars of Oxford and Chicago, in the madrasas of Deoband and Najaf, in the essays of young thinkers grappling with the meaning of the Qur'an in an age of artificial intelligence, climate crisis, and global interconnection. This epilogue does not pretend to predict the future. It offers, instead, a set of reflections on the directions in which Islamic philosophy might — and perhaps should — move.

1. Bridging Analytic and Continental Philosophy The academic philosophy of the twenty-first century is often divided between two styles: the analytic tradition, dominant in the English-speaking world, which emphasises logic, argument, and conceptual clarity; and the continental tradition, dominant in much of Europe, which emphasises history, embodiment, and the limits of reason. Islamic philosophy, in its classical form, had elements of both. The Peripatetic tradition was rigorously logical and argued from first principles; the Illuminationist and mystical traditions were attuned to temporality, presence, and the ineffable. The future of Islamic philosophy may lie in its capacity to speak to both sides of the aisle — to engage with the problems of analytic metaphysics (existence, causality, modality) using the tools of Avicennan logic, and to engage with the problems of continental philosophy (the critique of modernity, the hermeneutics of the self, the politics of recognition) using the resources of the ḥikma tradition. A new generation of scholars — including figures like Mohammad Azadpur, Caner Dagli, and Sajjad Rizvi — is already doing this, producing work that is both historically informed and philosophically alive.

2. The Encounter with Science and Technology The classical Muslim philosophers were, as we have seen, deeply engaged with the sciences of their day. Avicenna was a physician, al-Bīrūnī an astronomer, Ibn al-Haytham a pioneer of optics. Today, the sciences have become so specialised and so vast that it is difficult for a philosopher to master them as the classical thinkers did. But the philosophical questions raised by modern science — the nature of consciousness, the origin of the universe, the

implications of evolution for the understanding of human nature, the ethics of artificial intelligence and genetic engineering — are no less pressing. Islamic philosophy has the internal resources to address these questions: a robust dualism or emergentism about the soul, a doctrine of continuous creation that is compatible with an evolving universe, a principle of human dignity rooted in the concept of khalīfa. The task of integrating these resources with the findings of modern science is one of the most exciting frontiers of the tradition.

3. Interfaith Dialogue and Global Philosophy In the medieval period, Muslim philosophers were in sustained dialogue with Christians, Jews, and Sabians. The falāsifa read Greek and Syriac texts, and their works were read by Maimonides and Thomas Aquinas. Today, in a world where religious communities are increasingly interconnected, the revival of that dialogue is both a moral imperative and a philosophical opportunity. Islamic philosophy can contribute to global conversations about the nature of God, the problem of evil, the foundation of ethics, and the relationship between faith and reason. Comparative philosophy — the systematic engagement of Islamic thought with Buddhist, Hindu, Chinese, and indigenous traditions — is a field still in its infancy, and Muslim thinkers are only beginning to participate. The future of Islamic philosophy may not be a purely Muslim affair; it may be part of a global renaissance of wisdom traditions, each learning from the others.

4. Philosophy in Southeast Asia and Africa: A Global Perspective The history of Islamic philosophy is often written as a Middle Eastern and South Asian story, with a chapter on Andalusia and a nod to the Ottoman world. This narrative, while historically grounded, is geographically incomplete. The Muslim world extends from Morocco to the Philippines and from the Volga to the Cape of Good Hope, and philosophical thought has flourished in regions that are often neglected in surveys of the tradition. Southeast Asia. The Malay-Indonesian world produced a remarkable philosophical and mystical tradition that synthesized Ibn ʿArabī's ontology, Avicennan metaphysics, and local wisdom traditions. Hamzah Fanṣūrī (d. c. 1590) , a Malay Sufi poet and mystic, wrote the first Malay-language treatments of waḥdat al-wujūd, and his poetry is a classic of Malay literature. Nūr al-Dīn al-Rānīrī (d. 1658) , a Gujurati scholar who became the chief jurist of Aceh, wrote a famous refutation of Ibn ʿArabī's ontology (Ḥujjat al-Ṣiddīq li-Dafʿ al-Zindīq) and was a key figure in the debates between the "wujūdiyya" and their critics in the Malay world. ʿAbd al-Raʾūf al-Sinkīlī (d. 1693) , the great scholar of Aceh, mediated the Akbarian tradition through his translations and commentaries. These figures were not minor epigones; they were creative participants in the global Islamic conversation, and their work is now being rediscovered by a new generation of scholars. Africa. The Sokoto Caliphate of northern Nigeria (1804–1903) produced a distinctive tradition of Islamic reformist thought. ʿUthmān ibn Fūdī, known as Usman dan Fodio (1754–1817) , was a jurist, theologian, and poet who wrote extensively on the need for social justice, the education of women, and the reform of political authority. His daughter, Nana Asmaʾu (1793–1864) , was a poet and educator whose works in Fulfulde and Hausa addressed philosophical and theological themes for a popular audience. The Sokoto tradition was not a mere replication of Middle Eastern models; it engaged creatively with the Maliki legal tradition, the Sufi heritage, and the specific social conditions of West Africa. The East African coast, too, produced significant philosophical and theological literature in Swahili, including the remarkable Hamziya tradition of Arabic-Swahili poetry and the works of Sheikh Muhyiddin bin Sheikh Muhammad Qahtany of Lamu. The Indian Ocean world. The circulation of ideas across the Indian Ocean — from Arabia to India to Southeast Asia to East Africa — created a cosmopolitan Islamic philosophical culture that is only now being mapped by historians. The Mappila Muslims of Malabar, the Muslim communities of the Coromandel coast, and the merchants

and scholars of Gujarat and Malacca were all participants in this network, producing works of theology, law, and mysticism that drew on the full heritage of the tradition. The recovery of these "oceanic" philosophical currents is one of the most exciting developments in contemporary Islamic studies.

5. Gender, Postcolonialism, and the Critique of Authority The feminist critique of the Islamic tradition, as we have seen, has already transformed the landscape. This critique is part of a broader postcolonial reassessment of Islamic intellectual authority. Who speaks for Islam? Who has the right to interpret the texts? The classical tradition was overwhelmingly the production of elite men in positions of legal and political power. The democratisation of knowledge — through printing, literacy, the internet, and mass education — has broken that monopoly. The future of Islamic philosophy will be shaped by voices that were previously silenced or marginalised: women, subaltern communities, converts, and those living at the intersections of multiple identities. The philosophical task will be to think through the implications of this pluralism not as a threat but as a fulfilment of the Qur'anic vision of a single human family, made into nations and tribes so that they may know one another.

6. Open Questions and New Directions The open questions of Islamic philosophy are as vast as the tradition itself. Can a modern ontology of the self be built on the Ṣadrian concept of substantial motion? Can the maqāṣid framework be developed into a full-fledged philosophy of law that engages with the challenges of human rights and international order? Can the aesthetic theory implicit in the Illuminationist tradition and the poetry of Rūmī be articulated in a way that speaks to contemporary art and literature? Can Islamic mysticism contribute to the emerging field of consciousness studies? These questions will not be answered in a single generation. They are invitations to a journey that is only beginning.

7. A Tradition That Refuses to Die More than once in its history, Islamic philosophy has been declared dead. The orientalists of the nineteenth century said it ended with Ibn Rushd, a victim of al-Ghazālī's triumph. The modernists said that it was buried by the weight of taqlīd and could only be resurrected by abandoning the past. But philosophy refused to die. It continued in the madrasas of Isfahan and Qom, in the ḥawzas of Najaf, in the khanqahs of India, in the universities of Europe and North America, and in the Sokoto caliphate and the Malay courts. It continued because the questions it asks — What is real? What can I know? How should I live? — are the questions of every human being who has ever looked at the stars or heard the Qur'anic command to reflect. The tradition of Muslim philosophy is a record of the human soul seeking its source, using every tool available — reason and vision, logic and love, argument and poetry — to find the truth that is at once the beginning and the end. The future of Islamic philosophy is the future of that seeking. It is a future that belongs not only to professional philosophers but to every person who takes the Qur'an's invitation seriously: to look, to think, to reflect, and to return. The door of ijtihād is open. The lamp of ḥikma is still lit. The journey continues.
