Appendix ALogic and Argumentation in Islamic Philosophy

The Instrument of the Sciences

From its inception, Islamic philosophy placed logic at the very centre of the intellectual enterprise. The first Arabic philosophers inherited Aristotle's Organon — the collection of treatises on categories, interpretation, prior and

posterior analytics, topics, and sophistical refutations — but they also inherited the Alexandrian tradition of studying the Rhetoric and Poetics as parts of the logical corpus. Logic was not one science among many; it was the instrument (āla) that made all other sciences possible, the art that distinguished valid reasoning from fallacy, and the gateway to demonstrative certainty. This appendix provides a concise overview of the major developments in logic and argumentation in the Islamic philosophical tradition, from its Greek origins through its Avicennan transformation to its integration into theology and law.

1. The Reception and Expansion of Aristotle's Organon The early translators rendered the full Organon into Arabic, often with Syriac intermediaries and extensive Greek commentaries. Al-Kindī wrote introductory works on logic, emphasising its role as the tool of philosophy. But it was al-Fārābī who became the first great logician of Islam. He wrote commentaries on the entire Organon, including the Rhetoric and the Poetics, which he treated as the arts of persuasion and imaginative representation respectively. AlFārābī's most important logical innovation was his theory of the five syllogistic arts: demonstrative, dialectical, rhetorical, sophistical, and poetic. Each art corresponded to a different level of certainty and a different audience. Demonstration (burhān) yielded certainty for the expert; dialectic (jadal) yielded probable opinion for the disputant; rhetoric (khaṭāba) produced conviction in the multitude through enthymemes and emotional appeal; poetics (shiʿr) moved the soul through imaginative representation (takhayyīl); and sophistics (mughālaṭa) deceived. This comprehensive framework allowed al-Fārābī to integrate logic with political philosophy and the theory of religion, as we saw in his account of prophecy.

2. Avicenna's Logical Revolution Avicenna transformed the Aristotelian logical tradition in fundamental ways. While he claimed to be merely clarifying Aristotle, his innovations were so profound that later logicians spoke of "Avicennan logic" as a distinct system. His most important moves included: The Subject-Matter of Logic: Second-Order Intelligibles (al-Maʿqūlāt al-Thāniya). In al-Madkhal (Isagoge) and Metaphysics (al-Ilāhiyyāt I.2) of al-Shifāʾ, Avicenna defined the precise subject-matter (mawḍūʿ) of logic not as extra-mental things nor as psychological acts of thinking, but as second-order intelligible intentions (al-maʿqūlāt al-thāniya) — such as 'being a subject,' 'being a predicate,' 'universality,' 'genus,' and 'syllogism' — as they supervene upon first-order concepts (al-maʿqūlāt al-ūlā, such as 'human' or 'horse') insofar as they lead the mind from the known (al-maʿlūm) to the unknown (al-majhūl). Two centuries later, the great revisionist logician Afḍal al-Dīn al-Khūnajī (1194–1248), in Kashf al-Asrār ʿan Ghawāmiḍ al-Afkār (Unveiling the Secrets from the Obscurities of Thoughts), broadened the subject-matter of logic even further to encompass all objects of conception (taṣawwurāt) and assent (taṣdīqāt) insofar as they conduce to new knowledge. The theory of the proposition and its truth-conditions. Avicenna gave a detailed analysis of categorical and hypothetical propositions, the modalities of necessity, possibility, and impossibility, and the conditions under which a proposition is true. He distinguished the truth of a proposition (ṣidq) from its assertoric force, and he explored the logical relations between modal operators with a sophistication unmatched in the Greek tradition. The systematisation of the syllogism. Avicenna reorganised the syllogistic theory, classifying syllogisms into connective (iqtirānī) and repetitive (istithnāʾī) forms, and developing a complex calculus of modal syllogisms. He argued that the validity of a syllogism depends on the formal relations of its terms, not on the existence of the things referred to — another step toward pure formalism.

The Relocation and Revision of the Aristotelian Categories (al-Maqūlāt). Against the Baghdad Peripatetics, Avicenna argued in al-Maqūlāt of al-Shifāʾ and al-Mukhtaṣar al-Awsaṭ that the investigation of Aristotle's ten categories (substance and the nine accidental categories) belongs properly to Metaphysics (First Philosophy) rather than to Logic, since categories classify extra-mental modes of being rather than second-order logical intentions. Building on Avicenna's critique, twelfth-century philosophers — specifically ʿUmar Khayyām, Zayn al-Dīn al-Sāwī (in al-Baṣāʾir al-Naṣīriyya), and Suhrawardī (in al-Talwīḥāt and Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq) — reduced the nine accidental categories to four (quantity kamm, quality kayf, relation iḍāfa, and motion ḥaraka), arguing that the remaining six Aristotelian categories (where, when, position, possession, action, and passion) are merely compound modes of relation. After Avicenna, Aristotle's Organon ceased to be the direct textbook of Arabic logic; instead, logic split into two competing post-Avicennan streams, as modern historians of logic (Tony Street and Khaled El-Rouayheb) have shown: a revisionist Rāzian-Khūnajian stream — led by Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī (d. 1209), Afḍal al-Dīn al-Khūnajī (1194–1248, Kashf al-Asrār and al-Jumal), Athīr al-Dīn al-Abharī (d. 1265, Īsāghūjī), Najm al-Dīn al-Kātibī (d. 1276, al-Risāla al-Shamsiyya), and Sirāj al-Dīn al-Urmawī (d. 1283, Maṭāliʿ al-Anwār), which explored new formal conversion and contraposition rules for modal and conditional propositions — and a neo-classical Avicennan stream led by Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī (1201–1274), which defended Avicenna's original doctrines against al-Rāzī and al-Khūnajī.

3. The Logic-Grammar Debate A distinctive feature of Islamic logic was its long and productive tension with Arabic grammar. Grammar (naḥw) was the most prestigious of the Islamic sciences, the key to understanding the Qur'an and the classical language. Some grammarians, most famously Abū Saʿīd al-Sīrāfī (d. 979), argued that logic was nothing but Greek grammar universalised — an alien system that distorted the divine language of the Qur'an and served no purpose that grammar could not serve better. The famous debate between al-Sīrāfī and the Christian logician Mattā ibn Yūnus in Baghdad in 932 symbolised this conflict. Al-Fārābī responded to this challenge by developing a sophisticated philosophy of language that distinguished between the grammatical rules of a particular language and the universal logical forms that underlie all human thought. Grammar, he argued, is the study of the accidental features of a particular tongue; logic is the study of the necessary laws of intelligible discourse as such. The grammarian and the logician study language at different levels, and both are necessary. This defence did not end the debate, but it established the terms on which logic was justified in an Islamic intellectual context. Later thinkers, particularly al-Rāzī and al-Ṭūsī, integrated grammatical analysis with logical theory, producing a rich tradition of philosophical linguistics.

4. Logic in the Service of Theology The most significant transformation in the post-classical period was the integration of Avicennan logic into Sunnī and Shīʿī theology (kalām). After al-Ghazālī, who endorsed logic as a neutral instrument and wrote treatises on it himself, the study of logic became a standard prerequisite for advanced theological education. Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, the great Sunnī theologian-philosopher, used Avicennan logic to structure his theological works, and his commentaries made logic indispensable for kalām. By the thirteenth century, the logic curriculum in the madrasas was thoroughly Avicennan. Students studied alAbharī's Īsāghūjī and al-Kātibī's Shamsiyya as introductory texts, and they worked through commentaries on the Ishārāt at the advanced level. Logic was valued not only for its philosophical utility but for its role in legal reasoning. The principles of Islamic jurisprudence (uṣūl al-fiqh) adopted logical categories — definition, division, syllogism, analogy — to systematise the methods of deriving legal rulings from scripture. The great jurist-

theologian al-Āmidī (d. 1233) wrote a massive work on legal theory that is also a sophisticated application of Avicennan logic to the Sharīʿa. This integration of logic and law is one of the distinctive achievements of postclassical Islamic thought.

5. Ibn Taymiyya's Critique of Aristotelian Logic No account of logic in Islam would be complete without acknowledging the most formidable critic of the Aristotelian logical tradition: Taqī al-Dīn ibn Taymiyya (1263–1328). His critique of logic, developed in works such as al-Radd ʿalā al-Manṭiqiyyīn (The Refutation of the Logicians) and Darʾ Taʿāruḍ al-ʿAql wa-l-Naql, was remarkably original. He argued that the syllogism does not produce new knowledge, because its conclusion is already contained in its premises. The true foundations of knowledge are the witness of the senses (ḥiss) and the report of reliable testimony (khabar), including the testimony of the prophets. The fiṭra, the primordial human capacity for knowledge, grasps necessary truths directly, without the mediation of syllogistic reasoning. Logic, for Ibn Taymiyya, is an elaborate apparatus that obscures the simplicity of genuine knowledge. This critique has been compared to the later critiques of formal logic by Bacon and Mill, and it represents a permanent challenge to the Aristotelian tradition from within Islam. Whether one accepts or rejects his conclusions, Ibn Taymiyya's arguments force the logician to examine the foundations of his discipline with greater rigour.

6. The Enduring Role of Logic in Sunnī and Shīʿī Jurisprudence The logical tradition in Islam never became an academic specialism divorced from practice. It remained embedded in the study of language, theology, and law. In the Shīʿī madrasas, from the School of Isfahan to present-day Qom, logic is still studied as a compulsory preliminary science. The Shīʿī jurists who developed the theory of wilāyat alfaqīh used the logical tools they had learned as students. In the Sunnī world, the study of logic declined in some regions but never disappeared entirely; it persists in the advanced curricula of al-Azhar, Deoband, and other institutions. The tradition of Islamic logic is not only a historical curiosity. It is a living discipline, with contemporary logicians in Iran and the Arab world working to articulate Avicennan logic in the language of modern formal logic, engaging with Boolean algebra, modal logic, and the philosophy of language. The logical heritage of Islam is one of the bridges by which the classical tradition can enter into dialogue with contemporary analytic philosophy.

7. Technical Deepening: Avicenna's Dhātī vs. Waṣfī Modal Readings and the Science of Ādāb alBaḥth For advanced students of Islamic logic, two technical breakthroughs of the Avicennan and post-Avicennan tradition merit special attention because they anticipate twentieth-century quantified modal logic and formal argumentation theory:

Substantial (Dhātī) versus Descriptional (Waṣfī) Readings of Modal Propositions In his al-Ishārāt and al-Shifāʾ, later codified in Najm al-Dīn al-Kātibī's (d. 1276) al-Risāla al-Shamsiyya, Avicenna discovered that every categorical proposition of the form 'Every J is B' contains an implicit temporal or modal quantifier governing the connection between the subject J and the predicate B. Crucially, a proposition such as 'Necessarily, every writer moves his fingers' is false if read in the substantial reading (al-ʿaqd al-dhātī) — which means: 'Every individual substance that happens at some time to be a writer necessarily moves his fingers at all times as long as his substance (dhāt) exists' (since a writer does not move his fingers while sleeping). Yet the exact same proposition is true if read in the descriptional reading (al-ʿaqd al-waṣfī) — which means: 'Every individual who is a writer necessarily moves his fingers for as long as he continues to be described by the attribute (waṣf) of

writing.' By crossing the dhātī and waṣfī readings with temporal qualifications (perpetual dāʾim, absolute muṭlaq, non-perpetual lā-dāʾim), post-Avicennan logicians constructed a system of thirteen canonical modal propositions (al-muwajjahāt al-thalāth ʿashra) whose conversion and syllogistic mixing rules far exceeded anything in the Greek Aristotelian tradition.

The Formal Theory of Dialectical Disputation (Ādāb al-Baḥth wa-l-Munāẓara) In the late thirteenth and fourteenth centuries, Shams al-Dīn al-Samarqandī (d. c. 1322) and ʿAḍud al-Dīn al-Ījī (d. 1355) invented a new formal meta-discipline that was taught alongside logic in every Ottoman, Safavid, and Mughal madrasa: ʿIlm Ādāb al-Baḥth wa-l-Munāẓara (The Formal Rules of Inquiry and Disputation). Unlike Aristotelian Topics, which catalogued informal dialectical commonplaces, Ādāb al-Baḥth formalized a rigorous three-move dialogical game between a Claimant (muʿallil) who advances a thesis backed by a proof and a Questioner (sāʾil) who tests the proof through strictly regulated moves: Objection / Demand for Proof of a Premise (al-Manʿ / al-Munāqaḍa): Challenging the truth of a specific minor or major premise of the Claimant's syllogism, optionally accompanied by a supportive plausibilitybacking (sanad). Charge of Logical Non-Sequitur or Counter-Instance (al-Naqḍ / al-Takhalluf): Demonstrating that the Claimant's proof is formally invalid, circular (dawr), entails an infinite regress (tasalsul), or fails in a parallel case where the same proof-ground exists without the claimed conclusion. Counter-Proof for the Contradictory Conclusion (al-Muʿāraḍa): Granting the formal structure of the Claimant's premise for the sake of argument while constructing an independent syllogism that proves the exact contradictory of the Claimant's thesis. Because every move and counter-move was governed by strict formal termination rules (maʾāl al-baḥth), a disputation could never degenerate into rhetorical shouting; it had to terminate in either the verification (taḥqīq) of the premise or the exposure of its logical flaw.

8. Conclusion: Logic as a Way of Thinking The story of logic in Islamic philosophy is the story of a sustained commitment to clarity, rigour, and the pursuit of certainty. From al-Fārābī's grand synthesis of the syllogistic arts to Avicenna's formal revolution, from the debates with the grammarians to the integration of logic into law, from Ibn Taymiyya's radical critique to the contemporary engagement with analytic philosophy, Muslim thinkers treated logic as the indispensable discipline of the mind. They did not always agree on its scope or its limits — Sufis like Rūmī would mock the logician who never left his syllogisms to taste the divine, and Ḥanbalīs like Ibn Taymiyya would question its claim to produce knowledge — but even the critics had learned the tools they criticised. The logic of the falāsifa was, at its best, a training in honesty: the discipline of following an argument wherever it leads, of distinguishing what is known from what is merely believed, and of submitting the mind to the discipline of truth. That discipline remains as necessary today as it was in the Baghdad of al-Fārābī. The tradition of Islamic logic is a permanent legacy, not only for Muslims but for all who value the life of reason.

Appendix BGlossary of Arabic and Persian Philosophical Terms

This glossary provides concise definitions of the most important technical terms used in Islamic philosophy. The definitions are intended for the non-specialist reader and are not exhaustive. Terms are listed alphabetically by

their Arabic or Persian form, with plural forms indicated where relevant. This expanded glossary includes ninetyeight technical terms across classical falsafa, kalām, Illuminationism, Akbarian gnosis, Post-Ṣadrian metaphysics, and modern Islamic philosophy. al-ḥaḍarāt al-ilāhiyya al-khams – The Five Divine Presences. In systematic Akbarian metaphysics (al-Qūnawī, al-Qayṣarī), the five universal ontological planes of divine self-disclosure: Hāhūt (Absolute Essence), Lāhūt (Divine Names & Immutable Entities), Jabarūt (Pure Intellects), Malakūt (Imaginal World), and Nāsūt/Mulk (Sensible World & the Perfect Human). al-ḥadd al-tāmm – Complete essential definition. In Peripatetic logic, a definition composed of a thing's proximate genus (jins qarīb) and specific difference (faṣl); critiqued as epistemically impossible by Suhrawardī and Ibn Taymiyya. ṣadr – Chest, breast. In Sufi psychology, the outermost spiritual centre, the locus of surrender to God. ḥads – Intuition. In Avicennan epistemology, the faculty of grasping the middle term of a syllogism immediately, without effort. ḥadīth – Report, tradition. A saying or action of the Prophet Muḥammad, transmitted through a chain of narrators. aṣālat al-māhiyya – The primacy of essence. The opposing doctrine, held by Suhrawardī and his followers, that essences or quiddities are the fundamental reality. aṣālat al-wujūd – The primacy of existence. Mullā Ṣadrā's doctrine that existence, not essence, is the fundamental reality. ʿaql – Intellect, reason. In Peripatetic philosophy, the human rational faculty; in cosmology, the separate intelligences that emanate from God. pl. ʿuqūl . ḥaqīqa – Reality, truth. The essential nature of a thing. ḥaraka jawhariyya – Substantial motion. Mullā Ṣadrā's doctrine that the substance of every material being is in continuous, dynamic transformation. arbāb al-anwāʿ – Lords of Species. In Suhrawardī's Illuminationist cosmology, the latitudinal order of immaterial angelic lights (corresponding to the Platonic Forms) that govern and preserve each natural species in the physical world. al-asfār al-arbaʿa – The Four Intellectual Journeys. In Mullā Ṣadrā's philosophy, the four stages of the philosopher-sage's ontological and spiritual ascent: (1) from creation to the Truth, (2) by the Truth in the Truth, (3) from the Truth to creation with the Truth, and (4) with the Truth in creation. al-aʿyān al-thābita – The Immutable Entities. In Ibn ʿArabī and the Akbarian school, the eternal quiddities or latent possibilities of all creatures subsisting in God's knowledge prior to their external manifestation. baqāʾ – Subsistence. In Sufi terminology, the state of abiding in God after the annihilation of the ego ( fanāʾ ). barzakh – Barrier, isthmus. In Ibn ʿArabī and later mystics, the intermediate realm between the spiritual and the material; in eschatology, the state between death and resurrection. basīṭ al-ḥaqīqa kull al-ashyāʾ – The Rule of Simple Reality: 'That which is simple in its reality is all things, and none of them.' Mullā Ṣadrā's metaphysical principle that pure, unconditioned Existence encompasses all positive

perfections of being without their finite boundaries or privations. burhān – Demonstration, proof. In logic, a syllogism that yields certain knowledge; in philosophy, a rational argument that establishes its conclusion with necessity. burhān al-ṣiddīqīn – The Proof of the Sincere. Avicenna's modal demonstration of the Necessary Existent from the contingency of the totality of contingent beings, later reformulated by Mullā Ṣadrā directly from the reality of existence itself. dahr – Atemporal duration. In Mīr Dāmād's ontology, the mode of being of the immaterial intellects, intermediate between pure eternity ( sarmad ) and temporal succession ( zamān ). ādāb al-baḥth wa-l-munāẓara – The formal science of dialectical inquiry and disputation, codified by Shams alDīn al-Samarqandī and ʿAḍud al-Dīn al-Ījī, governing the permissible logical moves (manʿ, naqḍ, muʿāraḍa) between a claimant and a respondent. dhawq – Taste, direct experience. In Sufism and Illuminationist philosophy, the immediate, non-discursive knowledge of spiritual realities. dhikr – Remembrance, invocation. The Sufi practice of repeating the name of God. falsafa – Philosophy. The tradition of Greek-inspired philosophy in the Islamic world. fanāʾ – Annihilation. The passing away of the ego in the experience of the divine. faqīh – Jurist. A scholar of Islamic law ( fiqh ). pl. fuqahāʾ . faylasūf – Philosopher. A practitioner of falsafa . pl. falāsifa . fiqh – Jurisprudence. The science of Islamic law. fiqh al-iʾtimān – Jurisprudence/Philosophy of Trusteeship (al-iʾtimāniyya). Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān's moral philosophy holding that human beings do not own themselves or nature autonomously, but hold existence, reason, and the earth as a moral Trust (amāna) from God. fiṭra – Primordial nature. The innate human disposition to know God and to distinguish right from wrong. ijmāʿ – Consensus. The agreement of the Muslim community, or of the scholars, on a point of law. ijtihād – Independent reasoning. The effort of a qualified jurist to derive a legal ruling from the sources of the law. al-ikhtiyār al-juzʾī – Partial choice or particular resolve (also al-ʿazm al-muṣammam). In Māturīdī philosophical theology, the uncreated relational intention of the human agent that directs God-given power toward a specific moral act. ḥikma – Wisdom. In Islamic philosophy, the comprehensive science of reality, often used as a synonym for philosophy in its highest sense. ʿilla – Cause. In philosophy, the efficient cause that brings something into existence. pl. ʿilal . ʿilm – Knowledge, science. al-ʿilm al-ḥuṣūlī (or ʿilm ḥuṣūlī) – Knowledge by representation (or correspondence). Discursive knowledge acquired through the mediation of mental forms (ṣuwar dhihniyya) and universal concepts.

al-ʿilm al-ḥuḍūrī (or ʿilm ḥuḍūrī) – Knowledge by presence. Direct, unmediated ontological self-awareness or intuitive presence of the known object to the knower, contrasted with representational knowledge. imām – Leader. In Shīʿī Islam, the divinely appointed leader of the community; in Sunnī Islam, the leader of prayer or the head of state. al-insān al-kāmil – The Perfect or All-Comprehensive Human. In Ibn ʿArabī and ʿAbd al-Karīm al-Jīlī, the microcosmic human archetype who integrates all Divine Names and cosmic levels, serving as the polished mirror of the Real. irtifāqāt – Socio-civilizational stages of cooperative refinement. Shāh Walī Allāh al-Dihlawī's four-stage philosophical sociology in Ḥujjat Allāh al-Bāligha tracing human organization from primitive subsistence to household/urban order, state governance, and universal international order. al-ishtirāk al-lafẓī – Homonymy, verbal equivocity. The doctrine that a term (such as 'existence,' wujūd) shares only a verbal name across different instances while having completely distinct meanings in God and creatures. al-ishtirāk al-maʿnawī – Univocity, conceptual commonality. The doctrine — defended by Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī, and Mullā Ṣadrā — that 'existence' (wujūd) has a single, self-evident conceptual meaning across all its instances. iʿtibārāt ʿaqliyya – Mental considerations or second-order conceptual predicates. Suhrawardī's doctrine that concepts such as existence (wujūd), necessity (wujūb), contingency (imkān), and unity are generated by the comparing mind and have no distinct extra-mental referent. ittiṣāl – Conjunction, union. In Peripatetic philosophy, the soul's attainment of contact with the Active Intellect. jawhar – Substance. In Aristotelian and Avicennan metaphysics, a being that subsists in itself. pl. jawāhir . al-jawhar al-fard – The indivisible atom (also al-juzʾ alladhī lā yatajazzaʾ). In Muʿtazilī and Ashʿarī kalām physics, the dimensionless point-substance out of which all extended physical bodies are composed. kalām – Dialectical theology. The rational defence and elaboration of Islamic doctrine. kasb – Acquisition (also iktisāb). In Ashʿarī theology, the doctrine that God creates every human act while the human agent simultaneously acquires moral and legal responsibility for the act through a created, momentary power. khalīfa – Successor, vicegerent, steward. The human being as God's representative on earth; also, the political successor to the Prophet. khalq jadīd – New creation. In Ibn ʿArabī's ontology, the continuous, instantaneous creation of the world at every moment. khūdī – Self, ego. In Iqbāl's philosophy, the centre of free, creative individuality that is the goal of human development. kumūn – Latency. Al-Naẓẓām's theory that all things are created at once and latent within each other. ḥāl – State. In Muʿtazilī theology, a mode of being that is neither existence nor non-existence but a real attribute of essence. pl. aḥwāl .

al-ʿālam al-ṣaghīr (or ʿālam ṣaghīr) – The microcosm, the human being considered as a miniature mirror of the cosmos. al-ʿālam al-kabīr (or ʿālam kabīr) – The macrocosm, the universe as a whole. ʿālam al-mithāl – The imaginal world, the intermediate ontological plane between the purely intellectual and the physical, in which spiritual realities take on subtle sensible forms. Associated especially with Suhrawardī and Ibn ʿArabī. maʿnā – Meaning, significance. In philosophy of language, the intelligible content of a word or concept; in aesthetics, the spiritual meaning behind the outward form. maqāṣid al-sharīʿa – The purposes of the Sharīʿa. The universal goals of Islamic law, traditionally identified as the protection of religion, life, intellect, lineage, and property. maʿqūlāt – The Rational Sciences (al-ʿulūm al-ʿaqliyya — logic, natural philosophy, mathematics, metaphysics, and philosophical theology), contrasted with manqūlāt (the transmitted scriptural sciences); the centerpiece of the South Asian Dars-i Niẓāmī and Farangī Maḥall tradition. māhiyya – Quiddity, essence. The "whatness" of a thing, that which answers the question "What is it?" milla – Religion, creed. A community defined by its religious law and beliefs. muḥaqqiq – Verifier. A scholar who practices taḥqīq , the rigorous verification of arguments. muḥdath – Originated, brought into existence after non-existence (passive participle of Form IV aḥdatha; contrasted with qadīm, eternal). mumkin al-wujūd – Possible existence. A being whose existence is not necessary in itself; in Avicennan metaphysics, everything other than God. mutakallim – Practitioner of kalām . pl. mutakallimūn . naẓar – Reflection, speculation. Rational inquiry. nafs – Soul, self. The principle of life, sensation, and intellect in the human being. nafs al-amr – The fact-of-the-matter or objective reality in itself. Formalized by Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī and al-Sayyid al-Sharīf al-Jurjānī as the objective truth-maker of true propositions (including mathematical and modal truths) independent of any individual thinker's supposition. qadar – Destiny, predestination. Also, in Muʿtazilī theology, the doctrine of human free will. qāʿidat al-wāḥid – The Rule of the One: 'From the One, insofar as it is utterly one, only one can proceed directly' (al-wāḥid lā yaṣduru ʿanhu illā al-wāḥid). A foundational axiom of Peripatetic and Ṣadrian emanationism. qiyās – Syllogism, analogy. In logic, a deductive argument; in jurisprudence, analogical reasoning from a known case to a new case. al-qudamāʾ al-khamsa – The Five Eternals. Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī's anti-Aristotelian cosmological doctrine that five principles are co-eternal: the Creator, the Universal Soul, Prime Matter, Absolute Space, and Absolute Time.

rūḥ – Spirit. The breath of life; in Sufi psychology, the spiritual faculty that is the locus of the divine breath in the human being. ṣūra – Form, image. The outward appearance or shape of a thing; in aesthetics, the sensible manifestation of an inner meaning. sarmad – Pure eternity. The mode of being of God, without before or after. sharīʿa – The divine law. The comprehensive legal and ethical code revealed to the Prophet Muḥammad. shūrā – Consultation. The Qur'anic principle of communal decision-making. taṣawwuf – Sufism. The mystical tradition of Islam. tajallī – Self-disclosure, theophany. The manifestation of the divine in the world. takhṣīṣ – Particularization. In classical Ashʿarī and Māturīdī kalām (especially al-Juwaynī and al-Ghazālī), the free determination by a willing Agent (al-Mukhaṣṣiṣ) of one specific time, measure, or configuration out of equally possible alternatives. takhayyul – Imagination. The faculty of forming mental images; in al-Fārābī's political philosophy, the power by which the prophet translates intellectual truths into symbolic narratives. tanzīh – Transcendence. The affirmation of God's absolute incomparability with creation. taqlīd – Imitation, conformity. The uncritical acceptance of the authority of a school, a scholar, or a tradition. taḥqīq – Verification. The rigorous, critical examination of arguments, associated especially with Fakhr al-Dīn alRāzī. tashkīk al-wujūd – The gradation of existence. Mullā Ṣadrā's doctrine that existence is a single reality that varies in intensity and perfection. tawḥīd – Unity. The affirmation of the absolute oneness of God. taʾwīl – Allegorical interpretation. The interpretation of scripture in a figurative or symbolic sense. ḥudūth dahrī – Atemporal origination. Mīr Dāmād's doctrine that the world is originated in the atemporal mode of dahr , not in time. al-umūr al-ʿāmma – General Ontology (literally, 'General Matters'). In post-classical Islamic philosophy and kalām (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭūsī, al-Ījī, al-Jurjānī), the foundational division of metaphysics that studies concepts common to all domains of being: existence, essence, necessity, contingency, unity, multiplicity, and causality. al-ṣuwar al-rūḥāniyya – Spiritual forms. In Ibn Bājja's psychology (Tadbīr al-Mutawaḥḥid), the intermediate cognitive forms residing in the common sense, imagination, and memory, standing between corporeal forms and pure universal intelligibles. waḥdat al-shuhūd – The unity of witnessing. The doctrine, associated with Shaykh Aḥmad Sirhindī, that the mystic experiences all things as one in the divine presence, without asserting that they are one in their essence. waḥdat al-wujūd – The unity of being. Ibn ʿArabī's doctrine that all existence is one, the self-disclosure of the one divine reality.

walāya – Guardianship, sainthood. The spiritual authority of the saint; in Shīʿī political thought, the authority of the Imam and, in modern times, of the jurist. wājib al-wujūd – The Necessary Existent. God, in Avicennan metaphysics, the being whose existence is identical with its essence and who cannot not exist. wujūd – Existence, being. The fundamental reality studied by metaphysics. al-wujūd al-dhihnī – Mental existence. The doctrine that quiddities can possess a cognitive mode of existence in the mind — where they do not produce their external physical effects (such as burning for mental fire) — alongside concrete external existence (al-wujūd al-khārijī). āya – Sign, verse. The phenomena of nature and the verses of the Qur'an are both āyāt (signs) of God. zamān – Time. The successive, measurable duration of the material world.

↑ Contents

The Architecture, Perennial Problems, and Living Trajectory of Islamic Philosophy

1. Looking Back Across Fourteen Centuries of Philosophical Inquiry When we survey the fourteen-century arc we have traversed — from the early dialectical assemblies of Basra and the Graeco-Arabic translation workshops of Baghdad to the Peripatetic syntheses of al-Kindī, al-Fārābī, Avicenna, and Ibn Rushd; from the empiricist doubts of Abū Bakr al-Rāzī and the comparative epistemology of al-Bīrūnī to the atomist and modal systems of Sunnī kalām; from the Illuminationist vision of Suhrawardī and the systematic ontology of Ibn ʿArabī's school to the post-classical verification (taḥqīq) tradition across Iran, Ottoman Anatolia, and Mughal India; and from the nineteenth-century reformers and Muḥammad Iqbāl to Muḥammad Bāqir alṢadr, Fazlur Rahman, Seyyed Hossein Nasr, Syed Muhammad Naquib al-Attas, Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, and twentyfirst-century analytic Islamic philosophy — one overarching conclusion becomes unmistakable. Islamic philosophy was never an alien Greek transplant that withered on Muslim soil after the twelfth century. Rather, it is one of the longest, richest, and most internally diverse traditions of systematic rational inquiry in human history. Far from being silenced by al-Ghazālī's Tahāfut al-Falāsifa, philosophical reasoning after the eleventh century underwent what historians of philosophy now recognize as a dual transformation. On the one hand, through Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī, ʿAḍud al-Dīn al-Ījī, Saʿd al-Dīn al-Taftāzānī, and al-Sayyid al-Sharīf al-Jurjānī, Avicennan modal logic and general ontology (al-umūr al-ʿāmma) were permanently naturalized into the very bloodstream of Sunnī and Shīʿī philosophical theology (kalām) and legal theory (uṣūl al-fiqh). On the other hand, through Suhrawardī, Ibn ʿArabī, Ṣadr al-Dīn al-Qūnawī, Dāwūd al-Qayṣarī, Mullā Fanārī, Mīr Dāmād, Mullā Ṣadrā, and Mullā Maḥmūd Jaunpūrī, discursive demonstration (burhān) entered into a creative, critical partnership with intuitive self-awareness (al-ʿilm al-ḥuḍūrī) and spiritual realization (taḥqīq).

2. Five Perennial Philosophical Axes of the Islamic Tradition Stepping back from chronological history to philosophical synthesis, we can identify five perennial conceptual problems that bind the classical, post-classical, and modern chapters of this tradition into a single continuous conversation: 1. Existence (Wujūd), Quiddity (Māhiyya), and Modal Metaphysics: From al-Fārābī and Avicenna onward, the distinction between what a thing is (its essence or quiddity) and the fact that it is (its existence) served as

the central axis of Islamic metaphysics. Every major school staked its identity on how it analyzed this distinction: Avicenna used it to formulate the Proof of the Sincere (Burhān al-Ṣiddīqīn) from contingency to the Necessary Existent; al-Ghazālī and the classical mutakallimūn denied that essence has any ontological status prior to God's creative act; Suhrawardī and Mīr Dāmād argued that existence is a second-order mental consideration (iʿtibār ʿaqlī) while concrete quiddities or degrees of Light are primary; Ibn Taymiyya rejected the extra-mental distinction altogether in favor of concrete particulars; and Mullā Ṣadrā consummated the debate with the Primacy and Modulation of Existence (aṣālat wa-tashkīk al-wujūd). 2. Causality, Divine Freedom, and the Order of Nature: How can we uphold both the intelligible causal regularity required by natural science and the absolute freedom and sovereignty of the Creator? Across the centuries, Muslim thinkers explored every rigorous position on the philosophical spectrum: Muʿtazilī secondary causation (tawlīd), Ashʿarī atomist occasionalism and habitual regularity (ʿāda), Māturīdī wisdomgoverned creation (takwīn) and partial human choice (al-ikhtiyār al-juzʾī), Avicennan and Rushdian essential causal necessity, Ibn ʿArabī's continuous re-creation at every breath (al-khalq al-jadīd), and Iqbāl's BergsonianAshʿarī universe of continuous creative emergence. 3. Epistemology — Demonstration, Experience, and Knowledge by Presence: From al-Māturīdī's triad of sense, testimony, and reason and al-Bīrūnī's experimental methodology to Avicenna's Flying Man, Suhrawardī's Knowledge by Presence (al-ʿilm al-ḥuḍūrī), Mullā Ṣadrā's unity of the intellect and the intelligible (ittiḥād alʿāqil wa-l-maʿqūl), and Muḥammad Bāqir al-Ṣadr's probabilistic solution to induction, Muslim philosophers refused to reduce human cognition to either arid rationalism or narrow sensory positivism. 4. The Nature of the Self and the Soul's Becoming: Whether articulated in Ibn Miskawayh's therapy of the virtues, Avicenna's substantial dualism, Ibn Ṭufayl's autodidactic island sage, Rūmī's evolutionary poetics of love, Mullā Ṣadrā's soul that is 'bodily in origination and spiritual in survival' (jismāniyyat al-ḥudūth warūḥāniyyat al-baqāʾ), Shāh Walī Allāh's subtle spiritual centers (laṭāʾif), or Iqbāl's creative ego (khūdī), anthropology in Islamic philosophy is never static; knowing the truth is inseparable from becoming a transformed moral and spiritual agent. 5. Ethics, Law, and the Virtuous Order of Human Society: From al-Fārābī's Virtuous City and Ibn Rushd's critique of timocracy and tyranny to Ibn Khaldūn's empirical science of civilization (ʿilm al-ʿumrān) and group solidarity (ʿaṣabiyya), Shāh Walī Allāh's socio-historical stages (irtifāqāt), Fazlur Rahman's Double Movement hermeneutic, and Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān's Trusteeship Ethics (fiqh al-iʾtimān), Islamic philosophy has consistently wrestled with how universal rational norms of justice relate to historical communities and revealed law.

3. Unfinished Frontiers for the Next Generation of Scholars and Students For students and researchers entering the field today, the study of Islamic philosophy is not a closed exercise in cataloging the past; it is an open frontier with vast territories still awaiting critical editions, translations, and philosophical engagement: The Manuscript Treasures of the Post-Classical Madrasa: Thousands of unedited Arabic, Persian, and Ottoman Turkish philosophical manuscripts from the thirteenth to the nineteenth centuries — including super-commentaries (ḥawāshī) on al-Kātibī's Shamsiyya, al-Ṭūsī's Tajrīd, al-Jurjānī's Sharḥ al-Mawāqif, Mullā Maḥmūd Jaunpūrī's al-Shams al-Bāzigha, and Muḥibb Allāh al-Bihārī's Sullam al-ʿUlūm — remain unstudied in libraries from Istanbul, Cairo, and Konya to Rampur, Patna, Hyderabad, and Tashkent.

Interreligious Philosophical Networks: The deep philosophical cross-fertilization between Muslim thinkers and their Jewish (Saʿadya Gaʾon, Solomon ibn Gabirol, Baḥya ibn Paqūda, Judah Halevi, Moses Maimonides, Gersonides) and Eastern Christian (Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq, Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī, Ibn Suwār, Ibn al-Ṭayyib, Bar Hebraeus) interlocutors within the shared Arabic philosophical ecosystem invites sustained comparative investigation. Constructive Engagement with Global Philosophy: As analytic and continental philosophy increasingly look beyond Eurocentric canons, the conceptual resources of the Islamic tradition — from Avicennan modal ontology and nafs al-amr truth-maker theory to Akbarian and Ṣadrian accounts of consciousness and Ṭāhā ʿAbd al-Raḥmān's ethics of environmental and technological trusteeship — are poised to play a central role in twenty-first-century philosophical inquiry. Philosophy in the Islamic world began when early Muslim thinkers took seriously the Qur'anic invitation to reflect upon the horizons and upon their own souls, welcoming truth from whatever source it came while testing every claim at the bar of demonstrative reason. Fourteen centuries later, that invitation remains as urgent and alive as ever.
